Foreword

The expansion of United States foreign private investment and trade is the major objective of the Bureau of Foreign Commerce, U. S. Department of Commerce. This handbook on Ecuador, provided as a service to United 

States businessmen in the attainment of this objective, is one of a series of country studies giving basic information on investment and trade. 

The information presented in Investment in Ecuador is not intended to serve in lieu of the legal, financial, and business investigations which businessmen should undertake for themselves. It is believed, however, that this publication may profitably be used as a reference book in such investigations. 

Requests for additional information are invited, and may be addressed to any Field Office of the Department or to the American Republics Division, Bureau of Foreign Commerce, Washington 25, D. C. 

Every effort was made to include basic information available when this study went to press. It is planned to keep the information up to date through reports in the Bureau's World Trade Information Service, which should be consulted for subsequent information on economic, administrative, and legal matters. Current developments are reported in the Department's magazine 

Foreign Commerce Weekly. The World Trade Information Service, issued in three parts, and Foreign Commerce Weekly may be purchased from any Field Office of the Department or from the Superintendent of Documents, U. S. 

Government Printing Office, Washington 25, D. C.

Organization and Credit 

Investment in Ecuador is organized in three parts. Part 1 provides a brief background survey of Ecuador and discusses the role of the Government in business and the climate for private investment. Part 2 examines in detail the important sectors of the Ecuadoran economy. Part 3 reviews the principal provisions of the country's business and tax laws. Appendixes containing a list of United States firms in Ecuador and other supplementary data complete the study.

The handbook was prepared under the general direction of George Wythe, Director, American Republics Division, who was also responsible for the preparation of the first two chapters. The principal author is Kenneth N. Hynes, who carried out the research conducted in Ecuador, prepared the material in part 2 as well as sections of the chapters on labor and on business organization law, and coordinated the study.

The chapters on labor legislation, the tax system, and most of the material on business organization law were prepared by Louis Andre Fernandez of the 

District of Columbia bar. Morton Pomeranz, American Republics Division, prepared chapter III of part 1 and the English translation of the industrial encouragement law, which appears as an appendix. Irene S. Johnson supplied some of the foreign trade statistics, and Rene W. Pinto assisted in preparation of the maps. A former employee of the American Republics Division, Janet 

P. Morrison, helped in the preliminary organization of some basic material. 

The manuscript was reviewed by the American Embassy in Quito, and special mention should be made of the cooperation received from John I. Fishburne, Counselor of Embassy for Economic Affairs, and his staff. Acknowledgment is also made of the assistance given by Norman M. Ward, Chief, Division of Agriculture, United States Overseas Mission in Ecuador.

PART 1 The Investment Outlook

CHAPTER I 

An 

Overall View 

Ecuador experienced a period of its most rapid economic growth in 1950-54 when the national income rose 43.6 percent. But the annual rate of increase fluctuated from a high of 14.7 percent in 

1952 to a low of 4.9 percent in 1953. The median average annual rate of increase for the period was 

9.3 percent. Population increased 12.7 percent in those years (3 percent per year), and prices rose 

13.8 percent; real national income advanced 26.2 percent and real per capita income 11.8 percent.¹

The advance continued, but at a slower pace, in 1955 and 1956, when national income rose 5.8 and 

2.6 percent, respectively. In terms of real per capita income, however, these gains were offset by increasing population and by higher prices as compared with 1954. The real value of income per inhabitant in 1955 and 1956 remained about the same as in 1954.

The general prosperity in 1950-54 was brought about by active exports and favorable terms of trade, and was reinforced by public loans from the International Bank for Reconstruction and

Development (World Bank) and the Export-Im port Bank of Washington (Eximbank), which enabled the Government to carry out an important public works program. There were increases in domestic private capital formation and in foreign investment. In 1955, however, the upward trend was halted, largely by the deterioration in terms of trade and by a decline in the rate of capital formation. Gross investment as a percentage of available goods and services declined from 14.8 in 1954 to 13.9 in 1955 and to 13.3 in 1956.2 

Ecuador is not only one of the smallest of South American countries but also one of the two or three having the least economic development during the 19th century. Unhealthy conditions in the coastal regions and the deficiency of internal and international communications were major obstacles. Unlike the other West Coast countries, Ecuador lacked any important wealth in minerals 

1 Banco Central del Ecuador, Memoria del Gerente General del 

Banco Central del Ecuador Correspondiente al Ejercicio de 1956, Quito, 1957, tables 1 and 2, page IX. Referred to as Memoria from here forward. 

Banco Central del Ecuador, Memoria, 1957, page 20. to pay the cost of port works, railways, and public buildings.

At the turn of the century, cacao was Ecuador's chief export. As the chief producer of high quality cacao, Ecuador for a few decades profited from the rising world demand. Exports reached a peak of about 47,000 metric tons during World War I but declined afterwards owing to the outbreak of cacao plant diseases and the increase in competition from other areas. The low was around 14,000 tons in the 1940's.

The country's other principal exports tagua nuts, coffee, panama hats, and rubber-were inadequate during the 1920's and 1930's to make up for the losses on cacao, but the expansion of coffee exports, the development of petroleum, and the boom in rice and bananas during World War II and postwar years raised the export totals to higher levels.

ECONOMIC STRUCTURE 

The Ecuadoran economy is decidedly of the agricultural-pastoral type. Industry is only incipient, and minerals are of secondary importance. 

Ninety-five percent of Ecuador's exports are of agricultural origin. During 1950-55, agriculture (including animal husbandry, forestry, and fishing) accounted for 37.3 percent of the national income whereas industry contributed 16.1 percent (see table 1). Despite the fact that a large part of the population is engaged in subsistence activities, the commercial sector of the economy is closely connected with export-import activities. In 1954-56, the ratio of the value of exports to national income was 15.6 percent while import and export duties and taxes accounted for 46.8 percent of the revenues of the Central Government, exclusive of cash balances and loans.5 Ecuador is dependent on * Unless otherwise indicated, the weight-ton unit in this study is the metric ton of 2,204.6 pounds.

Provisional data for 1956 indicate that agriculture accounted for 34.5 percent of the national income, and industry for 16.5 percent.

9, 165 imports for most of its supply of capital goods and for various essential foodstuffs. 

The per capita use of mechanical power is very low in Ecuador (0.12 ton coal equivalent as compared with 0.49 for all Latin America), despite considerable increase in mechanization in recent years. Humans and animals (mainly burros) are still the chief means of transport in some areas. 

Some of the principal crops cacao, coffee, bananas are cultivated, harvested, and handled by hand. There are 1,300 tractors in the country, and they are used for opening up new lands and preparing the soil for such crops as sugarcane and rice. In some sections, plows are unknown, and cultivation is carried on by the hoe, pointed stick, or machete.

With the expansion of highways the motor vehicle has become an increasingly important means of transport. Ecuador is fortunate in being approximately self-sufficient in petroleum fuel, although consumption threatens to outstrip production. Increasing demand for electricity for illumination and industrial use has created a shortage in most of the chief cities. Ecuador has a large hydroelectric potential. A five-year plan launched in 1954 would double the present installed electric capacity of about 70,000 to 75,000 kilowatts (kw.) provided the financing of over $50 million is arranged. Coal is not found in commercial quantities, but considerable wood is burned as fuel. 

Since 1950, manufacturing industries have expanded.

NATIONAL INCOME AND PRODUCTION 

The per capita national income of Ecuador is low, but has risen during the past 2 decades from the equivalent of $91 in 1939 to $156 in 1955. 

The estimate for 1956 is $154. In 1954-56, however, real per capita income remained unchanged at $133.

Available information does not permit a clearcut distribution of the population by income classes, but some general conclusions are possible. 

At the bottom in income are the farmworkers of the Sierra, a large part of whom receive virtually no cash income. Somewhat above them are those 

Indians and mestizos who are able to supplement their subsistence economy with earnings from rural crafts or urban employment. In some cases, such as the handicraft artisans of Otavalo, in the Province of Imbabura, they achieve a comfortable standard of living.

About 20,000 men and 5,000 women are employed as operatives in manufacturing industries, and another 5,000 persons are employed in industrial offices. The majority of the women are in the textile and clothing industries. In 1955 factory workers received, on the average, $10 a week and office employees $108 a month. Although real wages in some industries appear to have increased, they have remained stationary or declined in others. 

Wages and salaries accounted for 47.9 percent of the national income in 1954 as against 49.3 percent in 1950 (see table 2). On the other hand,

XII. 

Although the private sector of the economy has expanded vigorously since 1949, public investment has increased at a more rapid rate, rising from 25 percent of total gross private and Government investment in 1950 to 30 percent in 1954 and 38 percent in 1955. The increased public investment, aided by loans of the Eximbank, was devoted to highways, railways, ports, airports, and power development. In 1956, however, public investment was cut back as financing became more difficult, and public participation in total investment declined to 35.5 percent.

Imports of capital goods in 1955 and 1956 accounted for 36 and 39.9 percent, respectively, of total imports (see chapter XII). 

The effectiveness of the large public investment has been reduced by scattering the available funds 

Source: Unpublished data furnished by Jose C. Cardenas, Director_de 

Departmento de Investigaciones Economicas, Banco Central del Ecuador, Quito. among a large number of projects; funds are frequently tied up for a long time before construction is completed. In addition to the Central 

Government and the state enterprises, more than 600 entities, including Provincial councils, municipalities, universities, and ports, receive specific fractional allocation of revenues under various tax measures (see chapter X). This sectionalism naturally hampers the orderly programing of investment budgets. In an effort to bring about greater coordination, the Government in 1954 

established a National Board of Economic Planning and Coordination (Junta Nacional de Planificacion y Coordinacion Economica). Assistance in staffing the Board was given by the United States Government through the International Co operation Administration. The United Nations has also provided technical assistance. Through loans from the Eximbank and the World Bank, the Government has also been able to concentrate resources on high-priority objectives. in- Net receipts of foreign capital in 1946-55, cluding loans and grants (such as relief in connection with the 1949 earthquake), amounted to $61.2 million, according to calculations of the Na tional Board of Economic Planning and Coordi nation. Increases in private foreign capital during that period, estimated at $46.7 million, resulted chiefly from reinvestment of profits. 7 

Junta Nacional de Planificacion y Coordinacion Economica, 

Informe Anual, 1955-1956, Quito, 1956, page 23.

CHAPTER II The Land, People, and Cities 

Ecuador, as its name recalls, straddles the equator. On the west it faces the Pacific Ocean, on the north it borders on Colombia, and on the east and south it touches Peru.

Its land area is not definitely known, but its territory, which includes the distant Galapagos Islands in the Pacific Ocean, is estimated provisionally at 105,000 square miles, approximately the same as the State of Colorado. "It is smaller than any other South American country except Uruguay.

Ecuador is traversed, from north to south, by two great Andean ranges, which support between them a series of high intermont basins or Andean valleys. This highland region, known as the 

Sierra, has long been the most densely populated section of the country. 

Some of the highland basins drain westward into the Pacific, while others empty into tributaries of the Amazon River system. These streams have cut into the volcanic soil to form deep valleys and terraces.

A series of active volcanoes and snowcapped peaks, rising from both ranges of the Andes, form a north-south corridor, or gallery, through the center of the country. The highest of these mountains is Mt. Chimborazo at 20,574 feet.

The coastal region, or Costa, comprises a number of subregions. The northern portion, embracing the Province of Esmeraldas, part of the Province of Manabi, and western portions of the Provinces of Pichincha and Imbabura, is a hilly area of heavy rains and tropical forest. To the south and between this coastal hilly belt and the base of the Andes there is a lowland belt 40 to 50 miles wide drained by the Guayas River and several tributaries. This belt, together with the lowlands along the eastern shore of the Gulf of Guayaquil, is known as the Guayas Lowland. Some of this area is flooded part of the year, but there is fertile alluvial soil on higher ground. Most of this area is devoted to grazing, but it is also the home of the cacao plant. A third subregion is the dry zone, which embraces the western part of the southern Province of El Oro, the peninsula of 

Santa Elena, and the coastal belt of the Province of Manabi.

The region east of the Andes (Oriente) has a tropical climate and abundant rainfall. Most of this region is heavily forested. The sparse population consists chiefly of uncivilized Indians. 

The Galapagos Islands, the nearest of which is situated 580 miles from the coast, form an archipelago of some 12 principal and many smaller islands officially known as the Archipielago de Colon. There is little permanent cultivation on the islands, which are bleak and barren, and only four of the islands have a settled population. 

Rich fishing grounds are found throughout the archipelago.

CLIMATE 

Ecuador lies in a transition zone that shows surprising variations in climate. In the north, there is heavy rainfall, but moisture diminishes farther south, and desert conditions prevail in the coastal area adjoining Peru.

In the far north there are two wet and two dry seasons, similar to Colombian seasons. In most of Ecuador, however, there is a single rainy season, both in the Sierra and in most of the Costa. 

This lasts from November to May in the highlands but from January to May on the coast at 

Guayaquil in the Province of Guayas. Most of the 

Oriente is wet and humid throughout the year, while the Galapagos Islands have little rainfall. 

The climate shows equatorial characteristics in that the seasonal variations in temperature are small, while the daily range may be large. Daily temperatures in Quito, Pichincha, which is situated in the Sierra at an altitude of 9,250 feet, have been known to vary by 40° within 24 hours.

The Land, People, and Cities through the Equator is only 15 miles from Quito, the mean annual temperature in the city is 56° F. 

Much of the Costa is humid and hot, especially in the valley region, which is shut off from the sea by hills. At Guayaquil, the average minimum and maximum temperatures are 72° and 88° 

F. in March and 66° and 84° F. in August. However, temperatures and humidity along the coast are moderated by the influence of the Humboldt Current, which in its northward flow from the 

Antarctic bathes much of the Ecuadoran coast. 

The Santa Elena Peninsula, west of Guayaquil, has the lowest temperatures on the coast and also has light rainfall.

POPULATION 

Ecuador's population at the end of November 1950 was 3,202,757, according to the census of that year. At the end of 1956, the population was estimated at 3,840,261. Since about 1950, the population apparently has been gaining at an annual rate of 3 percent, entirely from natural increase, for the balance of international migration has been negative in most years since World War II.

The population growth in recent years results from a combination of improved sanitary and economic conditions in Ecuador. The death rate has declined sharply during recent decades while the birthrate has fallen only slightly. 

In 1950 the Sierra had 58 percent of the population and the Costa 40.5 percent, but the latter has been growing faster than the Sierra for some time (see table 6).

Table 6.-Area and Population, by Geographic Regions, 1950 Population per square kilometer by the fact that the Province includes considerable piedmont and lowland. 

The most densely populated Provinces of the coast, in persons per square kilometer, are: 

Guayas, 27.3; Los Rios, 25.3; and Manabi, 21.1.

The Oriente has a population density of only 

0.4 per square kilometer. 1 About 90 percent of the rural population of the 

Sierra and 15 percent of the urban Sierra population is Indian or Indiomestizo (mixed bloods following the Indian way of life). The highland 

Indians are Quechuas, most of whom speak one of the Quechua dialects. The 1950 census showed that 172,646 spoke only Quechua and 4,433 spoke other Indian dialects. Most of the Indians are engaged in agricultural pursuits as landless laborers on large estates or as owners of small plots or as members of legally recognized landowning communities. But many of the Indians devote part or full time to handicrafts and produce either for their own needs or for sale. Some of them, such as the weavers of Otavalo, have acquired a measure of prosperity. The weaving of panama hats, made of straw from the torquilla palm, was in more prosperous times a chief source of income in the Provinces of Azuay and Cañar.

There are remnants of Indian tribes on the coast, chiefly in the northern jungles, but the typical citizen of the coast is the montuvio, a fusion of white, Indian, and Negro.

Taking the population as a whole, the relative numerical importance of the racial stocks was estimated in 1942 as follows, in percent: Indian, 39; white, 10; mestizo, 41; Negroes and mulattoes, 5; and other, 5.

Ecuador is predominantly rural. In 1950, 71.5 percent of the population was rural and 28.5 percent urban (see table 7). In the Sierra the proportion of rural dwellers rose to 73.8 percent.

HEALTH 

During the era of Spanish rule (1534–1822) and the first century after independence, the economic development of Ecuador was handicapped by the prevalence of disease in the coastal lowlands. Consequently, there came the concentration of population in, and the relative isolation of, the Sierra. The virtual elimination of yellow fever was the first of a number of conquests in public health.

Substantial financial and technical contributions by the U. S. Government, beginning in 1942, set in motion a notable advance in health and sanitation. Assistance was given initially in planning, remodeling, and equipping hospitals and health centers. Of the 28 hospitals set up under the joint United States-Ecuador program, 26 are now operated by the Ecuadoran authorities and 2 are used as demonstration centers by the health ser vicio of the U. S. Operations Mission (USOM) of the International Cooperation Administration 

(ICA). A national school of nursing has been established. Assistance has been given for the establishment of water systems in 44 communities, and sewerage systems have been established in 22 communities. Improvement of environmental sanitation continues to be of vital importance.

The Eximbank has provided funds for the establishment of waterworks in Quito and Guayaquil.

Great progress has been made in reducing the incidence of malaria, which has long been one of the worst health scourges. The National Anti 

Malaria Service was organized in 1948 to cooperate with the U. S. Institute of Inter-American 

Affairs. The World Health Organization 

(WHO) and the Organization of American

States (OAS) are cooperating in a campaign to eliminate malaria. Yaws control work has reduced the incidence of this disease to less than 1 percent. Owing to the high death rate from tuberculosis, especially at Guayaquil, a nationwide vaccination campaign was carried out by WHO. 

There are two national antituberculosis organizations; one concentrates on prevention and the other on treatment.

Infant mortality has been reduced through improved sanitation and water supply as well as through health education among mothers. The 

United Nations Children's Emergency Fund 

(UNICEF) came to Ecuador following the serious 1949 earthquake and took charge of the feeding of children in the earthquake area. Later,

UNICEF extended its activities, and it contributed $160,000 toward the erection of a pasteurization plant in Quito.

EDUCATION 

The 1950 census revealed that 43.7 percent of the population 10 years of age or above could not read or write. Of the total population, 1,076,038 persons were under 10 years of age and 2,126,719 were 10 years or older.

In 1955-56, public schools numbered 3,962 and private schools, 666. The number of students enrolled in public schools was 414,854 and in private schools, 105,063. The public schools had a total of 11,617 teachers, and the private schools, 4,226 teachers. The Central Government maintains most of the schools. 

Approximately one-third of Ecuadoran children from 6 to 12 years of age do not attend 10

The Land, People, and Cities primary school. The lowest percentage of nonattendance among this age group is in the Provinces of El Oro and Pichincha. The highest percentage is in the Provinces of Manabi, Los Rios, Napo-Pastaza, Cotopaxi, and Cañar.

The higher institutions of learning include state-supported universities in Quito and Guayaquil as well as in Loja, Loja; Cuenca, Azuay; and 

Portoviejo, Manabi.

Since 1946 the United States Government has given technical and financial assistance through an education servicio in cooperation with the Ecuadoran Government. This has included teacher training and the development of both teaching materials and school communities. 

Sanitary facilities and drinking water supply systems have been installed as demonstration projects. Emphasis in urban education has shifted to vocational training. Assistance has been given to the agricultural and medical faculties of the 

University of Quito. Through the exchange-ofpersons programs, training in the United States has been provided for key personnel in various fields. A manual arts servicio has operated in

Despite the limited headway made by popular education in Ecuador, the well-to-do elite are normally very culture-conscious and are versed in literature and art. They have facility in at least one language other than Spanish (in the past, usually French, but now, English). Ecuador has produced a number of outstanding poets, novelists, and journalists.

CHIEF CITIES 

The climatic and geographical differences between the Costa and the Sierra (see table 9) have been accentuated by historical circumstances, and are reflected in the racial, psychological, and institutional divergencies. 

The Sierra has always had the chief concentration of population and been the center of Government. Owing to its comparative isolation, the 

Sierra has persisted for centuries in a traditional organization of society, although the effect of new influences is now evident.

The coastal cities, and especially Guayaquil, the chief port and largest city, have shown a greater spirit of commercial enterprise and have been more open to foreign influences. While a large part of the laborers in the Sierra are tied to the soil and receive most of their remuneration in kind, laborers on the coast are paid in cash and are more mobile and have more initiative. With improving highway connections, more workers from the Sierra are coming down to the piedmont 69 and valleys to work seasonally. Some remain as settlers. 71 75 1 Macuchi has characteristics of a Costa community although the Province in which it is located, Cotopaxi, is classified as a Sierra Province. 

2 Not available.

Economico del Ecuador, Mexico, 1954, pages 39-40. Based on E. Ferdon,

Studies on Ecuadorian Geography, School of American Research and University of Southern California, 1950. 

Guayaquil is the chief commercial and industrial center of Ecuador. About 95 percent of

Ecuador's imports and 45 percent of the exports pass through Guayaquil. Located about 33 miles inland on the Guayas River, it is connected by air and highway with the chief cities of the Sierra. 11

The terminus for the Guayaquil-Quito railway is at Duran (Eloy Alfaro), Guayas, across the river from Guayaquil. A substantial part of the cargo shipped out from Guayaquil is received by barge, launch, and canoe on the Guayas River. Owing to silt in the river, large ocean steamers do not tie up at Guayaquil docks but transfer their passengers and cargo at Puna Island.

Guayaquil has the largest private banks in 

Ecuador. In 1955, credit extended by Guayaquil private banks totaled 1,114 million sucres out of 1,751 million sucres extended by all private banks of the country.

Although manufacturing industries had their early development chiefly in the Sierra, Guayaquil has definitely taken first place in recent years.

Încome tax data for 1953 showed that the industrial enterprises in the Province of Guayas had 50 percent of the total assets of income-tax-paying, industrial enterprises in the country. Industrial establishments in the Province of Guayas accounted for 53.2 percent of the gross value of

Ecuador's industrial production in 1955, according to the Industrial Census of 1956. In

The appearance of Guayaquil has changed in recent years with the construction of hotels, public and private office buildings, banks, clubs, factories, apartment houses, and suburban residences. 

1955 a total of 1,274 buildings were erected in Guayaquil. A new university city is being built on the edge of the city as money becomes available from a special tax. A new water supply system and a sewerage system are being installed. 

The largest city in the Province of Guayas, after Guayaquil, is Salinas, at the point of Santa 

Elena Peninsula, 95 miles from Guayaquil. It takes its name from the salt deposits found there. 

It is the largest of Ecuador's ocean resorts, with beaches and with both sport and commercial fishing. 

Another beach and fishing resort is Playas, Guayas, on the Gulf of Guayaquil, 60 miles from

Guayaquil, with a modern hotel and diversified recreational facilities. 

Also on Santa Elena Peninsula are La Libertad, Guayas, near Salinas, the port from which crude petroleum is shipped, and Ancon, site of the principal oilfields. In 1948, the Government began production in a superphosphate plant at La 

Libertad, near the local phosphate rock deposits.

Paved roads connect Salinas and other points on 

Santa Elena Peninsula with Guayaquil. 

Manta is the second largest city of the Costa and traditionally the number 2 port of the country, although in recent years it has lost its place to both Puerto Bolivar in El Oro Province and 

Esmeraldas in total value of exports. Manta remains in second place in value of imports. In 

1956, however, exports through Manta greatly exceeded those through Puerto Bolivar and Esmeraldas, largely as the result of heavy coffee shipments. Manta is the chief port for exports of coffee; it also exports tagua nuts, cottonseed and copra cake, cacao, castor beans, shrimp, kapok, and toquilla straw hats. 

Manufacturing industries in Manta include a small fish-freezing and fish-packing plant and two important vegetable oil mills. A Roqueforttype cheese is made in this region. Montecristi and Jipijapa, traditional centers of the panama hat industry, are in the Province of Manabi, not far from Manta.

Late in 1956 a highway was completed from Manta to Quevedo, in the Province of Los Rios, where connection is made with the Guayaquil- 

Latacunga-Quito highway. This road passes through Portoviejo, capital of the Province of Manabi. 

Another port in the Province of Manabi is

Bahia de Caraquez, from which exports of cacao, coffee, castor beans, and tagua are made. A railway connects Bahia de Caraquez with Calceta and Chone, both in Manabi Province, but consideration is being given to discontinuing the rail service and taking up the track as soon as the parallel highway now under construction is completed-probably within a year or two.

In the Provinces of El Oro and Esmeraldas, 

Puerto Bolivar and Esmeraldas are benefiting from increased banana shipments. The recent completion of the railway from Quito to the new port of San Lorenzo, Esmeraldas, is expected to stimulate new developments in this northwestern corner of Ecuador.

Quito is the political and cultural capital of 

Ecuador. Although second to Guayaquil in size and general commercial importance, Quito maintains financial predominance owing to its controls over the purse strings of the nation. It is the headquarters of the Central Bank and the National Development Bank. It is also an important industrial center.

The present city was established in 1534 by the Spanish conquerors on the ruins of the Inca capital. It has retained the Spanish colonial atmosphere to a greater extent than any other capital in the Americas. At the same time, new residential areas have been developed northward along the highland valley. 

The third largest city of Ecuador is Cuenca, capital of the Province of Azuay and site of a university. The branch of the Guayaquil-Quito railway southward from Sibambe, Chimborazo, has never been completed all the way to Cuenca, but Cuenca is connected by highway with the Costa and the principal cities of the Sierra. Subsistence agriculture and handicrafts have been traditionally the chief activities. The larger part of the toquilla strawhats are woven in Cuenca or in the surrounding region, and the chief exporters are 12

H H H H H U H 

Business Street in Guayaquil

Sibambe, Chimborazo, Railway Station

The Land, People, and Cities located at Cuenca. Following the decline in the hat industry, the Government issued decree law of 

April 28, 1952, that set up the Institute of Economic Recuperation for the Provinces of Azuay and Cañar (Instituto de Recuperacion Economica de Azuay y Cañar). With the assistance of the

USOM, some progress has been made in improving meat and poultry supplies. Cuenca has a brewery and a textile mill. It also produces handwoven shawls and articles of marble, jasper, and alabaster. 

Ecuador's fourth and fifth cities, respectively,

Ambato and Riobamba, are located in the highland on the Guayaquil-Quito railway. Riobamba, capital of the Province of Chimborazo, is an overnight stop on the railway. It is at an elevation of 

9,000 feet, in a rich agricultural valley. Here four snowcapped peaks mark the beginning of the Avenue of Volcanoes. Near Riobamba the railway ascends to the highest point on the line, 11,841 feet, at Urbino Pass. 

Ambato, elevation 8,431 feet, is 208 miles from 

Guayaquil and 73 miles from Quito. It is a center of vegetable and fruit production and has flour mills, canneries, textile mills, and rubber factories. Ambato suffered considerably in the 

1949 earthquake, but there has been general reconstruction, and new buildings have been erected. 

Latacunga, capital of the Province of Cotopaxi, is dominated by the towering cone of Cotopaxi 

Volcano. There are also several other snowcapped peaks in the vicinity. Latacunga is on the Guayaquil-Quito railway and is also linked with highland and coastal centers by highway.

It is the site of a recently completed pulp and paper mill. 13

CHAPTER III The Government and the Investment Climate 

The Ecuadoran Government generally welcomes capital for all forms of investments which would help develop the local economy. Very little foreign capital has thus far entered Ecuador despite some incentives, such as notable stability of currency since World War II, a minimum of exchange controls, and taxation that is nondiscriminatory and that amounts to no more than in most 

Latin American countries.

The principal deterrents seem to be the following: Lack of important mineral discoveries; a small domestic market, which limits the size of most enterprises and inhibits any large capital flow; shortage of technical and entrepreneurial skills; and lack of continuity in and implementation of official investment policies. 

This chapter describes the Ecuadoran Govern ment, the economic planning that the Government has embarked upon, the role of the Government in business, the nature of Government regulation of business, and the status of foreigners and their investments in the country.

GOVERNMENT 

Ecuador is a constitutional republic known officially as the Republica del Ecuador. The present Constitution became effective on December 31, 1946. Executive power is exercised by a

President who is elected by direct popular vote for a term of 4 years. He may not be reelected until 4 years after the end of the presidential term for which he was elected. The legislative body, the National Congress, consists of two chambers, the Senate and the Chamber of Deputies. 

The Senate has 45 members, of whom 33 are elected by direct popular vote-2 for each of the Provinces of the Sierra and the Costa, 1 for each of the Provinces of the Oriente, and 1 for the Galapagos Islands. There are 12 functional

Senators I each for public and private education, 1 for journalism and the scientific and literary academies, 1 each for agriculture, commerce, workers, and industries of the coastal region, 1 each for agriculture, commerce, workers, and industries of the Sierra, and 1 for the Armed Forces. 

Members of the Chamber of Deputies are apportioned to each Province on the basis of population, with 1 deputy for each 50,000 persons or major fraction thereof. Every Province is represented by at least two deputies. 

The judicial branch of the Government consists of the Supreme Court, superior courts, and primary courts. 

Every Ecuadoran, male or female, who is over 

18 years of age and can read and write may vote.

Voting in popular elections is compulsory for men and optional for women.

With the exception of Pichincha,¹ each Province has a Governor who is appointed and removable by the President without restriction. The

Governors are agents of the Executive in their Provinces and report to him through the members of his Cabinet. A municipality is an association of the inhabitants of a territorial division established by law as a canton. Municipal government is exercised by a cantonal council and, in addition, in the capital cities of the Provinces, by an alcalde (mayor). Relations between the municipalities and the Central Government are conducted through the Ministry of Government 

(Ministerio de Gobierno). 

ECONOMIC DEVELOPMENT PLANS 

Ecuador cannot be said to have established the machinery for overall programing for economic development until the creation, in 1954, of its Na tional Board of Economic Planning and Coordi nation. During World War II, the first steps 

1 The President of the Republic, resident in Quito, the national capital, is the chief executive of the Province of Pichincha. 15

were taken toward the establishment of such a body. In 1942 the Ecuadoran Development Cor poration (Corporacion Ecuatoriana de Fomento) 2 was established and this was followed 2 years later by the creation of the National Development Bank (Banco Nacional de Fomento) and Provincial development banks. The Corporation was charged with ". developing and initiating the production of the natural resources of the Republic of Ecuador, and the development and improvement of agriculture, mining, industry and transport, as well as the construction of public works or any other kind of useful or advantageous projects ." The development banks, financed initially with the blocked assets of Axis nationals, were to bulwark the efforts of the Corporation by providing credit facilities for developing national industry and agriculture. 

Having achieved only limited success with the Corporation, the Government, in 1947, empowered the Ministry of Economy (Ministerio de Economia) to take over its assets. 5 

In December 1946, the National Economic Coun cil (Consejo Nacional de Economia) was established by the Constitution and invested with responsibility for the study of the nation's economic problems and for guidance in financial matters. The Constitution requires the President of the Republic to consult with the National Economic Council before issuing emergency decree laws of an economic character. 

Ecuador's next step in the direction of overall economic planning was anticipated by the exchange control legislation of late 1947, when a surcharge on foreign exchange for certain imports was instituted. Soon afterwards, 90 percent of the proceeds of this surcharge was earmarked for a development fund. This fund remained ineffective until early in 1949, when the Government enacted a Law for the Development of Production 

(Ley de Fomento de la Produccion).10 Under the terms of this law a Development Corporation (Corporacion de Fomento de la Produccion) was created. It was an autonomous Government agency to utilize the development fund for purposes quite similar to those of the earlier Corporation but with less emphasis on public works projects and with more emphasis on the encouragement of foreign-exchange-producing in-

Production also authorized the continuance of the 

National Development Bank and the 15 subsidiary

Provincial development banks as the suppliers of medium- and long-term credits for the development of agriculture and industry.

The Central Bank of Ecuador (Banco Central del Ecuador), organized as a private institution in 1927, became an organ of the state in 1948, operating under the control of a Monetary Board (Junta Monetaria).11 It was charged with maintaining the monetary conditions necessary to the economic development of the country. 

To integrate the activities of the development banks and the Development Corporation, the Law for the Development of Production created a planning body called the Development Institute 

(Instituto de Fomento). Both the Monetary 

Board and the National Economic Council were represented on the Board of Directors of the De velopment Institute. 

Within 9 months of its creation, the Development Corporation was provided with almost 70 million sucres (approximately $4.5 million) which it quickly proceeded to invest in a variety of new and existing corporations engaged in such things as mechanization of agriculture, improvement of cacao cultivation, cattle raising, milk plants, cement and other building materials plants, electric power facilities, and hotels. 

However, in December 1949, the financial allotments to the Development Corporation were cut drastically as a result of the fiscal crisis brought on by the earthquake that occurred earlier in the year. In late 1952, when even its small remaining allotment was taken away, the Development Corporation's usefulness as a source of development capital was destroyed. The situation was capped in July 1953 by the issuance of Executive 

Decree 25 12 that abolished both the Development

Corporation and the Development Institute. The National Development Bank and Provincial de velopment banks remained to stimulate agricultural and industrial development and animal husbandry. The National Development Bank assumed the rights and duties of the Development 

Institute and the Development Corporation as well as the properties of the latter.

Late in 1953, the Ecuadoran Government again began an appraisal of development needs.13 Note was made of the beneficial work of the defunct 

Development Corporation and of the many studies carried on in the country by a variety of private and Government-sponsored technical missions on such subjects as roads, irrigation, industrialization, improvement of agriculture and

The Executive Highway Committee of the Province of Guayas, (Comite Ejecutivo de Vialidad de la Provincia del Guayas), a Provincial agency to develop roads and the port of Guayaquil, was noted as the only entity with a concrete developmental plan. 

The then Minister of Economy went on to record: 

It is certain that there now exists in the country information which we consider sufficient for the formulation, not only of specific [development] projects, but, more importantly, of a general plan. For the same reasons, it is a criterion of the present Government that we must now enter upon the planning stage, as an essential prerequisite necessary to execute projects, general or special, and subject to a strict priority, which are believed indispensable for the general development of the economy and of certain areas or activities in particular.14

In May 1954, the National Board of Economic 

Planning and Coordination was created.15 This new planning board was empowered to draw upon all available national and foreign technical assistance in drawing up and coordinating economic development plans. It was conceived as the agency for channeling independent and often conflicting local and regional efforts into an overall national program, but this conception has not yet been achieved. Some success has been attained in such things as a road network, but, for the most part, the Board has been limited to the preparation of further economic studies.

GOVERNMENT IN BUSINESS 

Ecuador may be classified among those Latin

American countries that have avoided large-scale excursions into what are normally viewed as fields for private investment. The Government's major activities in such fields may, for the most part, be grouped under three headings: (1) Transportation and communications; (2) historical Government monopolies, which were usually born of earlier revenue needs; and (3) investments in industry and agriculture, which have not been found to be attractive to private investors but which have been considered necessary for the economic development of the country.

In the field of transportation, the Government ownership of the nation's railroads is perhaps most prominent. The railroads are operated by the State Railways Company (Empresa de los Ferrocarriles del Estado), a Government corporation with independent financial status and with its board chairman appointed by the Minister of 

Public Works and Communications.16 In the last days of 1956, the Minister of Public Works and 

Registro Oficial, Quito, May 29, 1954, page 4500. 

15 Decree 19 of May 28, 1954 (Registro Oficial, Quito, May 29, 1954). 

16 Decree law 39 of August 3, 1955 (Registro Oficial, Quito, August 3, 1955) and decree 2 of November 9, 1956 (Registro Oficial, Quito, January 3, 1957).

Communications was empowered "7 to set up a private corporation that would take over the railroads. In the corporation, the Government would be a shareholder. The Government hoped that private national and foreign capital might thereby be attracted for the repair and improvement of the system. 

The minority stock ownership (20 percent) by the Government in the joint Colombian-Ecuadoran international steamship company, Flota Mercante Grancolombiana, S. A., is held by the 

National Development Bank.18 The Govern ment's participation in the field of aviation consists of the ownership of all of the country's major commercial airports. These are operated by the Bureau of Civil Aviation (Dirección General de Aviacion Civil) under the Ministry of Public Works and Communications (Ministerio de Obras 

Publicas y Communicaciones).19 

Like many of her sister Republics of Latin 

America, Ecuador has invested heavily in the communications field. The telephone systems of both Quito and Guayaquil are owned by Gov ernment entities (Empresa de Telefonos Automa ticos de Quito 20 and Empressa de Telefonos 

Automaticos de Guayaquil) under the Ministry of Public Works and Communications. The in ternal landline telegraph system is operated by the Ministry, and the Government owns facilities for both national and international 22 radiotelegraph and radiotelephone operations. Ten of the 

Republic's radio stations are owned by the National and municipal governments. 

Ecuador's Spanish colonial heritage is reflected in the state monopolies in alcohol, tobacco, and salt. All of these monopolies are administered 28 by a division of the Ministry of the Treasury (Ministerio del Tesoro). Only the alcohol monopoly may import, export, and distribute sugarcane distillates. Others may distill sugarcane juice or import foreign alcoholic beverages only through monopoly permit. The tobacco monopoly does not manufacture tobacco products, but it licenses their production by local private manufacturers. Tobacco products of foreign manufacture may be imported and distributed only by the monopoly. Salt deposits not worked by the salt monopoly may be worked by private entrepreneurs under permit from the monopoly. Salt distribution is effected by the monopoly.

18 Decree 914 of May 27, 1946 (Registro Oficial, Quito, May 28, 1946).

23 The legislation now governing the operation of these monopolies is decree law 483 of October 19, 1935 (Registro Oficial, Quito, October 29, 1935), as amended. 17

Until recently, matches were manufactured and wholesaled by the Government monopoly. Because of the financial losses incurred, however, the Government abolished the monopoly in June 1957 and initiated steps to attract private capital for match manufacturing.24

Prices of all monopoly products are fixed by the Government.

The monopolies have been maintained as a source of Government revenue. In recent years, however, the monopolies have operated so inefficiently that they have immobilized important Government funds (as in the case of the importation of foreign cigarettes) or they have drained rather than added to Government revenues. In 

1956, the Minister of the Treasury recommended 25 that the Government withdraw from many of the monopoly operations and turn them over to private capital. 

Almost all of the remaining investments of the 

Ecuadoran Government resulted from the planning activities of the former Development Corporation. These investments were made on the theory that they were necessary to the development of the country and that Government funds would have to be used since private capital was not available for either all or part of the projects. The more substantial Government projects include a cement plant, a tile factory, electric powerplants, hotels, a rice mill, and agricultural machinery pools.

GOVERNMENT REGULATION 

The most important of the Government's regulations applicable to business in general are foreign-exchange controls and the attendant controls on commodity imports. (Details of those regulations are to be found later in this chapter and in chapter XII.)

Another phase of Government regulation of wide applicability concerns labor. The Labor

Code and its ancillary legislation guarantee to labor many benefits which accrue to labor in other countries only through collective bargaining (see chapter IV). 

No Government entity 28 is charged generally with the supervision of the operation of corporations 27 or other forms of business organization. 

24 Decree law 13 of June 5, 1957 (Registro Oficial, Quito, June 

10, 1957). 

23 Ministro del Tesoro, Informe a la Nacion, 1956, Quito, Imprenta del Servicio de Suministros, page 110. 

26 In early 1955, the Minister of Economy took note of the great increase in the number of new corporations and urged that a Government body be set up to supervise the operations of commercial and industrial corporations. Ministro de Economia, Informe a la Nacion, 1954-1955, page XLII. As of late 1957, no action had been taken on this proposal.

Little use seems to be made of article 322 of the Code of 

Commerce as amended by decree 160 of March 24, 1936 (Registro Oficial, Quito, March 25, 1936), which provides that 

Judges of courts of commerce shall oversee the operations of corporations, either at their own or a stockholder's initiative. 

Nor is there anything in Ecuadoran legislation resembling a blue-sky law. Corporations are organized by satisfying the Court of Commerce that all requirements of the Code of Commerce, the ruling law in this field, have been observed (see chapter XIII). A corporation must be approved by the legislature if it is organized to construct public roads, navigable channels, railroads, or other undertakings affected with the public interest.28 Companies engaged in a few activitiesfor example, mining-are, additionally, subject to Government supervision. Banks are established with the approval of the Superintendency of 

Banks (Superintendencia de Bancos) who supervises their operations under the terms of the 

Banking Law.29 The Superintendency of Banks also controls the establishment and operation of insurance companies.80

The Ministry of Public Works and Communi cations manages the Government's investments in transportation and communications and regulates private investments in those fields. There are no Ecuadoran statutes that establish benchmarks for utility and transportation ratemaking. 

Rates are decreed by the Ministry after ad hoc negotiations with the utility or transportation company. The Ministry licenses all forms of radio communications under regulations issued in 1952.31 The regulation of civil aviation is also effected by the Ministry under the terms of legislation enacted in 1953.32 

A large reservoir of regulatory power over business is lodged in the Ministry of Economy. 

Ecuador follows the civil law concept that minerals are the property of the state regardless of surface ownership. The Ministry of Economy issues concessions to individuals and companies to work such deposits and supervises concessionaire operations under the terms of the General Mining 

Law, the Petroleum Law, and the Placer Gold 

Law.33 Patent and trademark registrations are effected through the Ministry of Economy under the terms of legislation enacted in 1928 and the subsequent amendments. The purchase by in-

Existing price controls are, for the most part, closely linked to Government imports of basic commodities which are not available in Ecuador or to products subsidized by the Government.

as 'Ecuadoran legislation generally makes no distinction between domestic and foreign nationals and companies on the right to engage in business. 

Aliens and foreign companies qualified to do business in Ecuador may obtain the right to explore for and develop mineral properties. However, the regulations to the Alien Law provide that, "Without authorization specially granted by law, foreigners may not acquire or possess, under any kind of title, directly or indirectly, whether as individuals or companies, lands, waters, mines, and combustibles within 50 kilometers of the frontiers.. 99 37

The captains of such vessels must be Ecuadorans.

Foreign banks and insurance companies may qualify branches to carry on their business in

The Code of Commerce does not require that companies established in Ecuador have nationals as directors or officers. However, as a general rule, firms employing more than 5 persons are required to employ Ecuadorans to the extent of 

80 percent of the personnel." Foreign technicians may be hired in excess of this limitation where it can be shown that qualified Ecuadorans are not available. 

Article 183 of the Ecuadoran Constitution provides that no person may be deprived of the ownership or possession of his property except by court order or by expropriation for reasons of public use. In 1944, without any reference to a similar provision 45 of the Constitution then in effect, the Government nationalized 46 the Guayaquil and Quito Railway Co., a Delaware corporation whose bonds were guaranteed by the Government and held mainly in Great Britain. 

Nationalization of this most important railroad in the country, the only noteworthy instance in recent years, does not now give pause to potential foreign investors although, at the time it occurred, it was one of the factors present in Ecuador which served to discourage the entry of foreign capital.

Under this arrangement, new American investments approved by the Ecuadoran Government may be eligible for such a guaranty from the ICA.

The March 1955 notes also provide the basis for the extension of guaranties, 48 by the ICA, against the nonconvertibility of the principal and earnings of United States investments made in Ecuador with Ecuador's approval.

Under the exchange control legislation now in effect in Ecuador, 49 foreign investment capital may enter through either the official or the free exchange market. The official market operates at a par value of 15 sucres per dollar (15 sucres buying and 15.15 sucres selling) while the free market rate was 16.60 sucres to the dollar in December 1957. Investments may be made through the official market only after registration at the 

Central Bank. At the time of registration, the 

Central Bank will contract with the investor on the percentages of profits, dividends, interests, and capital which may be withdrawn each year at the official rate of exchange. The percentages may not be fixed at less than 12 percent per year. 

Remittances in excess of the fixed percentages may be effected through the free market, as may remittances on investments which entered Ecuador through the free market.

Ecuador has not entered into a Treaty of 

Friendship, Commerce and Navigation with the 

United States. 

Foreign investments have not been subject to discriminatory taxation in Ecuador. While the

Ecuadoran tax system is characterized by a multiplicity of different levies, the generally applicable burden of income taxation is among the lowest in Latin America. Ecuador does not offer income tax exemptions to new foreign investment. Aliens, as well as Ecuadorans, may, however, obtain full Further 

47 Section 413 (b) (4) (B) (H) of the act as amended. information about such a guaranty and the guaranty against inconvertibility may be obtained from the Investment Guaranties

48 Mutual Security Act of 1954, as amended, section 413 (b) (4) (B) (1). 

Foreign Exchange Law (Ley Sobre Cambios Internacionales), codification published in the Registro Oficial, Quito, June 4, 1954. or partial exemptions from other taxes under the terms of the Industrial Encouragement Law 50 

(see appendix A). Under the terms of this law, new and existing manufacturing industries and the expansion of existing manufacturing industries that are deemed to be contributing to the economic development of the country are eligible for the following tax exemptions: Up to 100 percent of the customs duties normally applicable to imports of machinery, spare parts, and raw materials, and up to 50 percent of the consular fees normally applicable to such imports; up to 66.7 percent of the tax on capital 1 and the tax on sales 62 for a period not to exceed 5 years; 100 percent of the stamp tax on corporate shares; and 

50 percent of the corporation registration tax and of the stamp tax applicable to the corporation's constitutive document (escritura de constitucion). 52 

The Industrial Encouragement Law, in addi tion to offering tax exemptions, allows approved industries to defer payment of the tax on capital and of the stamp tax applicable to the corporation's constitutive document, to amortize their machinery and equipment for income tax purposes over as short a period as 5 years, and to amortize still more rapidly those increases in plant and equipment effected through reinvestment of earnings or through borrowings. Approved manufacturing industries can be granted tariff protection against competitive imports.

A new industry that is enjoying partial exemption from the capital and sales taxes may have the period of its exemption shortened by as much as 2 years if the Ministry of Economy determines that the new industry is not "efficient." Investments that qualify under the Industrial Encouragement Law must contribute a sum, equal to 3 percent of the tax exemptions they receive, to defray the costs of the Government agencies administering and enforcing the law. 

Ecuador has not entered into a tax convention with the United States.

CHAPTER IV Labor and Labor Legislation I EMPLOYMENT AND LABOR CONDITIONS Working Population 

The national census of 1950 disclosed provisionally that 1,236,590 persons, or approximately 39 percent of the total population, were employed in some form of economic activity (see table 10). The working population comprised 

886,940 men and 349,650 women. Only slightly more than 1 percent of the economically active were employers, 54 percent were paid employees, 33 percent were self-employed, and 8 percent were unpaid family workers. The status of the remaining 4 percent was undetermined.

Industrial employment is overstated in the census by an estimated 124,200 persons, who should more properly be classified under "agriculture". Information is not available, however, on their occupational status, whether as employers, workers, or self-employed. The adjusted figure for persons engaged in manufacturing activities, 170,494, doubtless includes a substantial proportion of artisans working for their own account and workers employed in workshops of a predominantly handicraft type rather than in factories using mechanical power and modern machinery.

The number of workers and employees covered by social security increased from 103,432 in 1950 

to 133,855 in 1955.¹ The Social Security Fund 

(Caja del Seguro), covering workers in private employment other than banks and insurance, rose in membership from 70,306 to 92,779. Member ship in the Pension Fund (Caja de Pensiones), which covers bank, insurance, and public workers, increased from 33,126 to 41,076.2 

Labor Conditions in Costa and Sierra

Historically, there has been a sharp contrast between labor conditions in the Costa and in the Sierra. In the latter region, a surplus of unskilled labor and very low wages have been common. In the rural areas of the Sierra, most of the workers are huasipungueros, or persons who are tied by tradition generation after generation to an estate where they occupy a hut and have the use of a small plot of land in return for, usually, 4 days' work per week on the owner's farm. There are also many "free" laborers, or persons who work part time as artisans and part time as farmers. In the cities and towns, most of the workers are mestizos or are Indians who have adopted the mestizos' way of life.

In the Costa, scarcity of labor has tended to restrict the expansion of agriculture. In 1950 only 37 percent of the working population were located in the Costa as compared with 60 percent in the Sierra. The remaining 3 percent were in the Oriente and on the Galapagos Islands. There is considerable mobility of labor in the Costa, and remuneration is in cash. A substantially larger part of the population is literate than in the 

Sierra. For example, illiteracy of persons 10 years of age or over averages 28 percent in the 1 Provisional data for 1956 show a total of 140,314 covered workers and employees: Caja del Seguro, 98,300; Caja de Pensiones, 42,014. 

2 See section "Social Security, Workmen's Compensation" for administration of social security funds.

21

Provinces of Guayas and El Oro, but 60 per cent in Cotopaxi and Chimborazo. 

Little movement of workers took place in the past between the Sierra and the Costa, and such movement as did occur was largely seasonal. 

In recent years, however, internal migration has gained momentum owing to improvement of roads, greater competition for workers as the result of expanding agriculture and industry, opening of Government lands to settlement, and the general stimulus given to the desire for personal and family improvement by wartime and postwar developments.

Professional and Technical Skills 

The number of professional and technical personnel in Ecuador is very limited. Teachers, lawyers, clergymen, doctors, and engineers comprise the bulk of the professional class. There are few trained economists, agronomists, and industrial technicians. Trained office workers and skilled mechanics also are in short supply. As a result, industry has operated at a low technical level except in a few enterprises (e. g., brewing, pharmaceuticals, and flour products) owned or established by foreign interests. Ecuadoran schools do not provide training in advanced techniques, and as a rule new enterprises have to provide training for their staffs. With the assistance of the United States Operations Mission and other experts, some steps are now being taken to raise the level of occupational and technical training. Wages

According to statistics compiled by the Social 

Security Fund, the monthly pay of members in 1956 averaged 791 sucres, or an amount equivalent to approximately $52 at the official rate of exchange of 15.15 sucres per $1. Comparable data for 1955 show that members of the Pension Fund had an average monthly wage of 894 sucres ($59) while those in the Social Security Fund averaged 

657 sucres ($43) per month. Average wages in employment covered by social security, however, apply to only a small part of the total working population; they do not include the large number of low-paid workers in agriculture, domestic service, and the many small business enterprises that have not yet complied with social security regulations.

Wages are substantially higher in the Costa than in the Sierra. The average monthly pay in 

1956 for members of the Social Security Fund amounted to 973 sucres ($64) in the Costa as compared with 594 sucres ($39) in the Sierra. The average in Guayas (seat of Guayaquil) was 1,044 sucres ($69), the highest in the country; the average in Pichincha (seat of Quito) was 702 sucres ($46), the highest in the Sierra.

At the bottom of the wage scale are the huasipungueros, who in many cases receive no cash wages at all; when they do, they seldom are paid more than 2 or 3 sucres a day. A notch above them in the scale are agricultural hands, common laborers, and domestic workers. In 1955, agricultural workers for hire in the Sierra were commonly available for 5 to 8 sucres a day, unskilled laborers for 8 to 10 sucres a day, and domestic servants for 150 to 350 sucres a month.

Salaries and wages in public service, commerce, and industry also are low. Heads of Government departments in early 1955 reportedly averaged about 2,000 sucres a month, and Government white-collar staff workers earned an average of 

600 to 700 sucres a month. Accountants in commercial establishments in Quito commanded minimum monthly wage of 900 sucres and secretaries, a minimum of 600 sucres. Actual wages paid well-trained accountants and secretaries were appreciably above these minima. Most industrial workers in early 1955 probably averaged 300 to 

400 sucres a month, while workers with special skills averaged much more. Samples of wages paid, by type of industry and type of work, in the 

Sierra in the early months of 1955 were:

15 to 21 10 25 9.60 25 17 15.60 300 3,650 The labor movement started in Ecuador over 30 years ago, and, in large part, developed along political lines with sharp divisions in ideologies. 

In general, the rank and file have been apathetic toward their unions, and union leaders have not been able to exercise effective control over their organizations. However, judging by the apparent cohesion during primary and sympathy strikes in the petroleum and textile industries, some of the unions appear to have gained in strength and influence.

Tentative data from official sources for 1949-54 indicate that there were 1,315 legally recognized unions in Ecuador. Nearly three-fifths of the total, or 771 unions, were in the Provinces of 

Pichincha and Guayas.

The dominant labor organization is the CTE (Ecuadoran Confederation of Workers), which 22

Labor and Labor Legislation her is said to have a nominal membership of about 

2 125,000. However, probably not more than 

70,000 members actually pay dues. The most imhportant affiliates of the CTE are the FTP (Pichincha Province Federation of Workers) and the FTG (Guayas Province Federation of Workers). The CTE affiliates also include the National Petroleum Workers Federation, which has shown aggressive and effective strength in the past few years. The CTE is controlled largely by leftwing socialists with procommunist leanings, and is affiliated with the communist controlled WFTU (World Federation of Trade 

Unions).

Second in size among the labor organizations is CEDOC (Ecuadoran Catholic Workers Con federation), a strongly anticommunist but loosely knit organization with an estimated membership 

1 of about 15,000. Until recently, CEDOC allegedly did not provide the needed leadership in promoting the welfare of its members. In the past year or two the organization reportedly has 

Egained strength at the expense of the CTE.

Among the other labor organizations, the two most important are the Pichincha Chauffeurs'

Union and the Artisans' Movement, both of which are subsidized by the Government and are oriented toward the political right. Each is well organized, especially the Pichincha Chauffeurs' Union, which is one of the strongest unions in Ecuador.

Other organizations include COG (Workers Confederation of Guayas), a small, firmly anticommunist body which is the only affiliate of the 

International Confederation of Free Trade

Unions (ICFTU) in Ecuador, and the FNT (National Federation of Labor), a small organization supported by the militant and extreme-rightist polítical party ARNE (National Ecuadoran 

Revolutionary Action). Neither of these or ganizations at present appears to wield much influence.

Despite some unfavorable tendencies, industrial relations have been generally calm in recent years. 

Some demands for wage increases have been accompanied by strikes and, in a few instances, supported by sympathy strikes, but the strikes have been of short duration and have generally been settled on reasonable terms, frequently through the intervention of the Government.2 The most serious strike in recent years, the railroad strike of 1955, which was launched against the Government for nonpayment of wages, lasted only about 

2 weeks and was summarily ended by military measures.

The workers of the Manabi Exploration Company, a private 

United States-owned enterprise, went on strike for 2 months in protest against company measures to curtail operations. The strike was terminated by Government intervention in November 1957, but definitive settlement of the dispute between the company and its workers still awaited in early 1958 the decision of a special arbitration committee.

LABOR LEGISLATION The Labor Code enacted in 1938, the Constitu tion of the Republic as revised in 1946, and the Law of Alienship decreed in 1947 regulate the labor field in Ecuador. Most labor matters fall within the jurisdiction of the Department of Labor of the Ministry of Social Welfare and Labor (Min isterio de Prevision Social y Trabajo), although in certain cases appeals can be taken to the regular courts of justice. The laws give the Government a strong role over labor-management relations, and the National Government, as distinguished from municipal and other branches of government, has exclusive jurisdiction over all subjects of labor. Social security is regulated by independent laws, and the system is administered by the National Welfare Institute (Instituto Na cional de Prevision), a semiautonomous agency of the Government.*

The rights and duties of the employer and the worker are determined by the contracts made between them. However, the Labor Code established precise conditions for contracts and stated the definite minimum rights and benefits that cannot be contracted away by the worker."

The Labor Code, as amended, plus the Law of Alienship and the social security laws apply to all workers, particularly to those engaged in commerce and industry. The Labor Code makes some distinctions regarding workers engaged in domestic service, homework, and transportation, and agriculture, as well as artisans. For limited purposes, the code also distinguishes between an employee and a laborer, an employee being a worker whose work is intellectual and who works for a salary rather than for daily wages. To avoid confusion, the term "worker" is used here when both employees and laborers are covered by the particular labor provision being described. 

Alien Labor 

In general, foreigners may work freely in 

Ecuador. However, firms employing more than 

5 persons are obliged to employ Ecuadorans to the extent of at least 80 percent of the personnel complement. Exceptions to this requirement

Duties of Labor and Management The Labor Code enumerates a number of duties and obligations of labor and management. A few special duties of management are: 

1. Provide dining halls in any work establishment employing more than 200 laborers when the establishment is situated more than 2 kilometers from the nearest town. 

2. Maintain schools for workers' children, if the work establishment is located more than 2 kilometers from town and if workers' children of school age in the plant number at least 20. 

3. Supply labor organizations with an office, if the work establishment is located outside a town. 

4. Withhold legal union dues from the workers' pay if requested by the union. 

5. Present each worker, upon leaving the job, with a certificate showing the time, type of work, and salary received while on the job.

Labor Contracts 

All persons over 18 years of age may enter into labor contracts.10 With special permission from their guardians and from the labor authority, minors, also, may contract for work. 

Individual labor contracts may be expressed (written or oral) or implied. Work relationships between employer and workers will be deemed to be implied contracts of employment when no conditions of employment have been stipulated. In general, implied contracts have the same legal effect as expressed contracts and will be deemed to carry conditions of employment equivalent to those prevalent in the community for the same type of work. The following must be in writing: (1) Contracts made between employees and employers (actually many such contracts are not in writing), (2) all contracts related to work requiring technical knowledge, (3) all contracts stipulating work for 1 year or more, and (4) all contracts involving more than 1,000 sucres. 

Terms of remuneration in a labor contract may be fixed on the basis of salary or daily wages or profit sharing, or they may be mixed. Contracts may be made for a fixed period of time, for an indefinite period, for the completion of a specified task or job, or for piecework. Wages must be fixed on a daily basis, if the work is of a temporary character, or by the week, month, or year if the work is of a stable or continuous character. 

Contracts may not be based on hourly wages. All workers are entitled to receive their pay at least once a week." 

Either party breaching a labor contract that was made for a definite term must pay the other an indemnity to cover the unexpired time-the 

Labor Code, articles 39 through 42. 

10 Ibid., articles 8 through 38, 45, 46, and 55. 

11 Ibid., articles 45 and 46. employer pays 25 percent of the worker's wages and the worker 15 percent of his wage." A term contract must be made for a minimum period of 1 year." 18 Most work contracts in Ecuador run for an indefinite period but contain a 3 months' probation clause. During the probation period either side can terminate the contract without incurring liability. By law, however, an indefinite-term contract is deemed to run for a period of 9 additional months if the 3 months' probationary period is satisfactory to both parties." Upon completion of the 1-year period, an indefinite-term contract may be terminated by advance notice of 30 days by the employer or 15 days by the worker." Payment of salary or wage for the corresponding period may be substituted for the advance-notice requirement. 

The simultaneous dismissal of more than 2 workers from an establishment employing not more than 20 workers, or of more than 5 workers in a larger undertaking, is unlawful. Should the employer dismiss a member of the governing board of a workers' organization, except for specified reasons, he must pay the member 1 year's wages as compensation.

When collective labor contracts are in effect, individual labor contracts may be entered into only under the conditions and form prescribed in the former.16 

Collective Bargaining, Strikes, Lockouts ¹7

Labor contract.-The collective labor contract and the labor union are recognized. The closed shop is not mentioned in the code.

Workers negotiate collective agreements with the employer through a worker's shop committee of the individual plant. A shop committee may be established by workers in every enterprise employing more than 15 persons, provided more than 50 percent of the laborers in the plant participate in the organization of the committee. All laborers in the establishment are, without distinction, entitled to join the shop committee. If several unions are represented in a particular business establishment, the various trade unions may negotiate contracts together or separately with the employer.

The collective contract becomes obligatory on all workers and employers in a particular industry within a Province after the collective contract The wage or salary paid as indemnity includes overtime, commissions, profit sharing, and value of lodgings or living allowance normally paid the worker.
