Labor and Labor Legislation 

- has been accepted by two-thirds of the workers and employers of the industry and when approved by governmental decree.

According to the Labor Code the collective contract must be in writing and entered into in the presence of the labor authority. The contract may run for an indefinite or fixed period or for the life of an enterprise, or a particular project or job; it may cover matters relating to hours of work, wages, speed and quality of work, rest periods, and other matters specified by the parties. 

Every collective contract is subject to revision upon its termination, or, if of indefinite duration, every 2 years, upon the request of employers or workers, who are required to give 60 days' prior notice. If the revision is requested by a labor organization, at least 50 percent of the workers affected must concur in the request. Employers' requests for revision are honored only if such employers employ more than half of the workers affected by the contract. 

Strikes.-Workers are given the legal right to strike, but disputes between workers and employees must first be submitted to conciliation and arbitration. Upon any disagreement with the employer, the workers' organization presents a petition in writing to the labor authority. This petition is transmitted by the labor authority to the employer within 24 hours, and the employer is given 3 days in which to submit a reply. 

Upon receipt of the workers' petition, the labor authority organizes a conciliation and arbitration tribunal. The tribunal consists of 5 members, of whom 2 are appointed by the workers and 2 by management. A representative of the labor authority acts as the fifth member and is chairman.

Both management and labor have 48 hours within which to appoint their members. If either side fails to appoint members, appointments are made by the labor authority.

If the parties do not come to agreement in 8 days of conciliation, the tribunal makes its award within the following 3 days. The parties may appeal to the labor authority within 2 days after the award. However, workers' appeals must be on the basis of majority vote in a secret ballot of the workers involved. The appeal is decided within 3 days.

A strike is legal if the employer refuses to comply with the conciliation and arbitration procedure or if during mediation the employer discharges one or more workers without just cause or if there is a failure to organize the concilation and arbitration tribunal without fault on the part of the workers' representatives. 

Strikers may remain in their places of work under police protection, and employers may not resume work during a strike with substitute workers. When a legal strike is ended, strikers are entitled to full pay for the time lost during the 470866-59- -3 strike unless the conciliation and arbitration tribunal decides otherwise by unanimous vote or rejects completely the petition of grievances of the workers or if the strike is continued by the workers after an arbitration award. At the termination of the strike, workers must be reemployed and may not be discharged, except under special conditions, for a period of 1 year, unless the workers have continued the strike after an arbitration award.

The law also recognizes sympathy strikes if the strike being backed is conducted legally. 

However, the employer is not obligated to pay for time lost in connection with sympathy strikes. 

Workers in public service enterprises and those in health and welfare service in general, including food supply, are prohibited from collective stoppage of work unless 10 days' advance notice is given. Strikes of public employees are illegal.

Lockouts.-Lockouts or work stoppages on the part of employers are recognized but only for specific reasons and subject to prior conciliation and arbitration. An employer may not shut down operations, unless it is done to save himself from imminent bankruptcy or because of unforeseeable shortages of materials that can be obtained only from abroad. Employers who stop operations without legal cause may be punished under the Penal Code and may be required to reopen business, to pay workers for time lost, and to pay dismissal indemnities. 

An employer must communicate his decision to stop work and request permission for his action from the labor authority. Within 4 days a conciliation and arbitration tribunal is organized. 

This tribunal renders a decision on authorizing the stoppage and its duration. 

Hours of Work and Overtime Pay 

18 The regulation workday is 8 hours and the workweek, 512 days. By mutual consent and with permission of the labor authority, work may be extended by a maximum of 4 hours per day and 12 hours per week. Saturday afternoon and

Sunday are compulsory rest periods, but with the approval of the labor authority other days during the week may be substituted. For miners, actual underground work may not be more than 6 hours per day, and the total workday may not exceed 7 hours.19

In general, minors under 14 years of age are prohibited from working. Minors between the ages of 15 and 18 may work no more than 7 hours per day and 38 hours per week. Minors and women ordinarily may not work at night or in unhealthful places. Women may not be dismissed

because of pregnancy; they receive full pay during a 6-week period of maternity leave, and when they return to work they may take the necessary time during the workday to nurse their children." 

Nightwork wages are 25 percent higher than day wages. All workers are paid for the weekend compulsory rest period, and overtime pay is at 50 percent above normal if the overtime is performed before midnight and 100 percent above normal if performed between 12 midnight and 6 a. m. or on 

Saturday afternoon and Sunday. 

Vacations, Holidays, Sick Leave 

All workers are entitled to 1 month's sick leave a year but at half pay." 21 All workers receive full pay for 11 official holidays per year: January 1, May 1, May 24, July 24, August 10, October 9, October 12, November 2, November 3, December 25, and Good Friday. In certain Provinces, the number of official holidays is slightly higher.' 29 

All workers are also entitled to 15 consecutive days of vacation per year at full pay, i. e., at the average pay for the year including overtime.23 

Minimum Wages 24 The Constitution provides that "the state shall establish minimum wages in the various fields of work and shall tend toward the establishment of the 'family salary"."" The Labor Code authorizes the Ministry of Social Welfare and Labor to create, in each Province and other places necessary, minimum wage commissions with the responsibility of establishing minimum wages and salaries. 

Each commission is to be composed of 1 delegate representing the labor authority, 1 physician des ignated by the Social Security Fund, 1 delegate representing the municipality, and 1 representative each for employers and workers in the field of work under consideration.

Minimum wages and salaries are to be established every 2 years, or more often if special circumstances require, and the following factors are to be taken into account: (1) Cost of normal necessities of life of a worker as head of a family in keeping with the economic and social conditions

On various occasions the Government has estab lished minimum wages and has ordered general wage increases to improve the living conditions of the worker or to compensate for increases in the cost of living. In 1942, wages of all workers were increased on a sliding scale ranging from 10 percent for workers earning between 300 and 500 sucres per month to 25 percent for laborers earning up to 100 sucres per month or 3 sucres per day.26 In 1943, another increase ranged from 5 to 50 percent, and it was established that the of an industrial laborer in no case could be wages less than 4 sucres per day.27 Another significant wage increase was decreed in 1953. For the 

Guayaquil coastal area, increases amounted to 40 percent for employees earning up to 200 sucres per month and for laborers earning up to 7 sucres per day. The increases ranged to 15 percent for employees earning between 400 and 500 sucres per month and for laborers earning between 14 and 

17 sucres per day. For Quito and the rest of the mountain area, the wage increases were slightly less.28 

Social Security, Workmen's Compensation 

Social security in Ecuador is administered through the National Welfare Institute by the Social Security Fund, covering workers in private employment; and the Pension Fund, covering bank, insurance, and public workers.

The basic law on this subject is the Law of 

Compulsory Social Insurance promulgated July 14, 1942. Social security through the Social Secu rity Fund is compulsory for workers in private employment except workers engaged in domestic or agricultural services. Excluded also are foreigners who have come, or who may come, to the country under private work contracts for a period not to exceed 1 year, or, if for more than 1 year, who are protected by insurance which provides benefits at least equivalent to those established by 

Ecuadoran laws.

The fund is maintained principally by contributions of employer and worker. Contributions amount to 6 percent for the employer and 5 percent for the worker. The contributions are computed on the worker's basic wage.

Benefits under the law are the following: 29 

1. Non-service-connected illness. After 6 months of coverage, the worker receives all medical care and, after the seventh day of illness, compensation at the rate of 50 percent of the average wage for the first 4 weeks and 40 percent for the next 21 weeks. 

2. Maternity care. After 6 months of coverage, the worker receives all medical care and compensation for 7 weeks at 75 percent of average pay. 

3. Invalidity pension. This pension is effective 

I only after 4 years of coverage. Payment amounts to 30 percent of the average wage during the last 

4 years, plus 1.25 percent additional for every year of coverage after the first 4 years. 

4. Old-age pension. Payment amounts to 30 percent of average wage during the last 4 years, plus 1.25 percent additional for every year of coverage after the first 4 years. The worker is eligible for retirement only after meeting certain minima of covered employment, ranging from 10 years for retirement at age 70 to 30 years of coverage for retirement at age 55. 

5. Survivors' pensions. Payment amounts to 

27 to 33 percent of the insured's pension, depending on status of survivors. 

6. Funeral expenses. Payment amounts to 

350 to 1,500 sucres.

Benefits accruing to workers for injuries and disability resulting from industrial accidents and occupational diseases are provided for in the 

Labor Code.30 These workmen's compensation provisions are not unlike those existing in the United States. The incorporation of workmen's compensation benefits into the social security system is intended, but until recently, at least, the employer has had to meet these benefits with his own funds or by adequate liability insurance. 

The total cost of this insurance to the employer probably is equivalent to 1 percent of the workers' wages.

Severance Pay and Pensions ³¹

The severance pay system apparently is designed to serve both pension and unemployment compensation functions. The system is called 

Fondo de Reserva y Jubilación (Reserve Fund and Retirement).

The Labor Code specifies that each employer shall pay into the Social Security Fund 1 month's wages for each worker for every year the worker is in his employ. These monies are considered a reserve fund, or "capitalized work."

If the worker is separated after he has been on the job at least 1 year, he is entitled to receive whatever monies are in his reserve fund. If the worker dies, his heirs, within limits, are entitled to the fund.

The worker not covered by social security and who serves 1 employer 25 or more years may elect to withdraw his reserve fund or use it as a basis for his retirement. If the worker decides to retire, the employer may have to supply additional funds for this purpose. By law, however, the worker's pension cannot exceed 800 sucres per month.

The worker covered by social security must retire through that system. In these cases, the reserve fund is returned to the employer. 

Profit Sharing $2

By law, all employers except artisans must distribute 7 percent of their net profits among all their workers. This sum is distributed among workers proportionately in accordance with the remuneration of each worker. Remuneration received in the form of profit sharing is free from income tax in the case of workers earning wages less than 25 sucres per day. 33

PART The Economy

CHAPTER V Agriculture, Livestock, and Colonization

Agriculture, including animal husbandry, is the mainstay of the Ecuadoran economy. It is the sole or principal occupation of three of every five workers. Farming is far more important than livestock raising. Agricultural crops provide virtually all of the nation's exports, and crop production and income decisively influence the development of all other sectors of the economy. 

Agriculture occupies only a small part of the national territory, however, and large areas of arable land in the Costa and the Oriente have not been brought into production. The Ecuadoran

Government has become increasingly interested in the colonization and cultivation of idle public lands that are located principally in the Costa. 

The Government has offered for sale large sections of cultivable lands in that region and has tried to encourage the excess agricultural population in the Sierra to resettle in the Costa. Sales of public lands to private ownership in 1956 greatly exceeded sales in preceding years.

LAND AND ITS UTILIZATION 

Domestic agriculture supplies most of the nation's food requirements, but considerable imports of foodstuffs, the most important of which is wheat, are needed. The value of crop production has grown rapidly in recent years, principally because of the dynamic expansion in the production of bananas, the country's leading export. 

Two other export crops, coffee and cacao, have also increased significantly in volume and value. 

Coffee has become the nation's second most important export, and cacao has made a substantial recovery from its lean years. Expansion has also taken place in the production of some subsistence crops, such as corn, wheat, and potatoes. The in- 

1 See chapter I. crease in crop production has been achieved largely by cultivating more land rather than by improving cultural methods. 

Climate and Soils 

Although Ecuador is situated astride the equator, the Humboldt Current and lofty mountains have combined to give the country a diversified climate that permits the growth of a wide variety of agricultural products of the Tropical and Temperate Zones. Within a short distance, as measured in miles, the humid heat of the northern and central sections of the Costa gives way to the cool, crisp air of the Sierra plateau and the frigid temperatures of the Sierra's towering mountains. Within the Costa itself, there are great differences. In the Costa, the arid and semiarid lands in the southwestern section and in some parts of the southern section provide a sharp contrast to the lush vegetation found in other sections.

Within the Sierra there also are distinct differences. The northern and north central sections enjoy the most favorable climatic conditions for agriculture. Rainfall diminishes toward the south, and the southernmost reaches are semiarid. 

The greater part of the Sierra is a region of inadequate rainfall.2

The most fertile lands under cultivation are in the Costa, particularly in the valleys and along the slopes of the western foothills of the Andes.3

Within this belt soil types vary, but they are uniformly good, having excellent friability, water absorption, moisture-holding capacity, and resistance to erosion. They consist in general of a brown, mellow loam 6 to 12 inches deep. The

See chapter II for a full description of the country's climate.

Agriculture, Livestock, and Colonization humus supply is good to an unusual depth, and the plant-food components compare favorably with the better agricultural lands in the United 

States. Cacao, rice, sugarcane, and bananas and other tropical fruits thrive on these lands.

West of this fertile region, alluvial bottom lands separated by areas of stiff, brown clay follow the streams. During the dry season the clay soil dries rapidly and sometimes cracks to a depth of 5 or more feet.

A drier belt of alluvial soils ranging in fertility from good to poor lies farther to the west. In the northern part of this region are areas of dark brown loam and fine, sandy loam overlying clay subsoils. In the south, on the flats bordering the 

Guayas River, are gray and black clays of the gumbo type. Within the Guayas River Basin the soils, in general, are good although highly variable. Cultivation in this area at present is devoted principally to rice during the rainy season, and little agricultural use is made of the land during the dry season. Utilization of the basin's underground and surface water supplies for irrigation could convert this area into year-round cropland.

In the humid regions of the Pacific coast, brown clay soils predominate. The desert plain around 

Manta consists of a fine, sandy loam with a high lime content. In the Esmeraldas area the predominant type of soil is yellow clay, and in the drainage basin of the Esmeraldas River there are areas of fertile dark clays, friable and rich in vegetable matter.

Dark, sandy loam characterizes much of the land in the northern Sierra, the most fertile section of the region. To the south, the prevalent soil type is fine, sandy loam overlying light, clay subsoil. 

Throughout the region, continual use for many generations has drained the arable lands of their fertility, and erosion is far advanced. In the higher altitudes of the Sierra, particularly in the northern part, there are large tracts of broken or steep land covered with a thin layer of volcanic soils. Some of this land has been brought into production. 

Use and Distribution of Land

A very small part of the national territory has been brought under cultivation-only 4.5 percent, or approximately 1,361,000 hectares, in 1951.* 

Another 4 percent was pastureland. Threefourths of the remaining land was forested, and the rest was classified as unsuitable for cultivation or not in cultivation because of the lack of irrigation or transportation connections or because of the effects of erosion. Virtually all of the land 

United Nations Economic and Social Council, Economic Com mission for Latin America, El Desarrollo Economico del Ecuador, vol. I, 1953, page 59, table 21. 470366-594 in cultivation and in pasture was in the Sierra and the Costa. The Costa accounted for 49 percent of the cultivated land and the Sierra, 47 percent. Most of the pastureland, however, was in the Sierra-the Sierra contained 65 percent and the Costa 31 percent.

Data developed by the Agricultural Census of 

1954 (see table 11) reveal that 25 percent of the combined area of the Sierra and the Costa was under cultivation or in pasture. Woodlands, underbrush, and barren land constituted 20 percent of the area surveyed. The remaining 55 percent of the land area, which was excluded from the census, consisted of public lands and forests and lands not in agricultural use.

Large areas of idle land in the Costa can be brought into agricultural production if roads are built to provide access to markets and ports. In the Sierra, however, there is little suitable land for further expansion, and increased production depends primarily upon improved agricultural practices and methods.

Land Tenure and Agrarian System 

The ownership of land is very unevenly distributed. According to official estimates, 1,140 estates, or about 1 percent of all rural properties, in 1945 accounted for 39 percent of all land values. By contrast, 92 percent of the rural properties contained only 32 percent of the land by value.

The Agricultural Census of 1954 revealed that, of an estimated 344,234 agricultural properties, 287,936 holdings, or 84 percent of the total, contained less than 10 hectares (about 25 acres) each and represented approximately 12 percent of the land area surveyed. On the other hand, holdings of 1,000 or more hectares, numbering only 705

estates, or 0.2 percent of the total number of properties, contained 37 percent of the land area.

Sierra (see table 12). Approximately 90 percent of the Sierra properties were of less than 10 hectares, as compared with 63 percent in the Costa; and the average size of the properties was 

11.6 hectares in the Sierra and 35.2 hectares in the Costa.

The predominant form of land tenure is private, individual ownership. In the Sierra there are some properties held in traditional village communities, and in both the Sierra and the Costa there are large estates owned by combines and by Government institutions. Proprietors of the great estates are in most cases absentee landlords, and the administration of their properties is usually handled by agents. Parcels of these estates are frequently leased to farmers, usually on a share basis, a practice more common in the 

Sierra than in the Costa.

The very small size of the great majority of landholdings, particularly in the Sierra, has been insufficient to sustain family units in many cases, despite intense cultivation of the land. In the

Sierra, for example, holdings of less than 10 hectares contained only 16 percent of the land area covered by the census but accounted for 46 percent of the land in cultivation. In contrast, the large estates of 1,000 or more hectares contained 

41 percent of the land area but only 10 percent of the land in cultivation. As the result of this condition, a large class of landless peasants has evolved over the generations in the Sierra. The peasants provide an ample but technically unskilled supply of labor for working the lands of the large estates, usually in return for the use of a small plot of land or for produce or grazing and watering rights. In the Costa, where individual holdings are substantially larger, on the average, and more productive than in the Sierra, the situation is different. There, farm labor is in short supply and is generally paid in cash. 

Agricultural Methods and Practices 

In certain areas of activity, particularly in the cultivation of cacao, bananas, sugarcane, coffee, and wheat, and in dairy farming, some of the large plantations and farms have adopted modern techniques and practices, at least on a limited scale. Likewise, some small farmers have been converted to improved farming practices by the agricultural extension services of the Government and by other technical assistance agencies. Since 

World War II, and especially since 1950, there has been a significant increase in the introduction of breeding stock and in the importation and use of agricultural machinery, fertilizers, insecticides, disinfectants, veterinary products, and feeds. For example, the value of fertilizer consumed increased from slightly over 1 million sucres in 1950 to nearly 10.7 million sucres in 1955, after which it declined to 7.4 million sucres in 1956. The use of insecticides and disinfectants increased even more in value, rising from 719,000 sucres in 1950 to 11 million sucres in 1956.

The adoption of modern tools and techniques has been limited to a relatively few Ecuadoran farmers. Most of the farmers, particularly in the 

Sierra, continue to employ archaic and rudimentary methods and practices. They are subsistence farmers who earn a meager living by disposing of their surpluses in nearby markets. In general, they are largely unacquainted with the benefits of crop rotation and chemical fertilizers. Few have altered traditional cultural practices, which have led to soil exhaustion and erosion, a very serious problem in the Sierra. Very limited use has been made of improved varieties of seed, and, aside from recent efforts launched mainly to protect important export crops, such as bananas and cacao, pest control is largely nonexistent.

Irrigation is little used, and mechanization is slight, with only about 1,300 tractors in agricultural use in 1956 throughout the country, according to the Ecuadoran Department of Agriculture.

These factors have kept productivity low, especially in the Sierra. Crop yields, in general, are low compared with those of other South American countries. For example, yields of corn, 34

Agriculture, Livestock, and Colonization wheat, and cacao per hectare (1 hectare equals 

2.471 acres) in Ecuador are only about 50 percent as large as in Colombia, and the unit yield in barley is only about 40 percent as large. Yields in potatoes, beans, and other domestic food crops are also substantially lower in Ecuador than in 

Colombia. Significant exceptions are coffee and bananas. The coffee yield in Ecuador is about the same as in Colombia, while the yield in bananas is considerably higher. 

The increase that has taken place in crop production has been brought about largely by bringing previously idle lands in the Costa into cultivation rather than by increasing unit yields through improved farming methods. The gen- 

Ierally low yields obtained and the very large amount of hand labor employed have resulted in high production costs.

Agricultural Assistance Programs 

Over the years, many agricultural research organizations and assistance programs have been established, some on a national and some on a local basis. But because of the lack of coordinated planning, there have been considerable duplication and waste of effort and funds; many agencies and programs have been terminated after a short period of time. Through mid-1956, for example, only 37 of 93 agricultural experimental stations (and demonstration farms) that had been established were still in operation, and 

10 of 45 agricultural schools had been closed." The principal agencies engaged in agricultural research and technical assistance are the Department of Agriculture of the Ministry of Production (Ministerio de Fomento), a recently organized Ministry which has been invested with all responsibilities relating to agriculture and animal husbandry formerly exercised by the Ministry of 

Economy; the Inter-American Agricultural Co operative Service (Servicio Cooperativo Inter- 

Americano de Agricultura, or SCIA), which is sponsored by the United States International

Cooperation Administration and the Ecuadoran 

Government;

and the Food and Agricultural 

Organization of the United Nations (FAO). 

Several semiautonomous organizations have been formed to foster the cultivation and marketing of specific crops, such as the National Wheat Commission (Comision Nacional del Trigo), the 

National Association of Banana Growers (Asoci acion Nacional Bananeros del Ecuador), the

Junta Nacional de Planificacion y Coordinacion Economica, 

Informe Annual 1955-1956, page 56.

Although the source states "experimental stations," the vast majority were so-called demonstration farms. There are only 5 experimental stations in operation, according to Servicio Cooper ativo Inter-Americano de Agricultura (SCIA). 

The Ministry of Production was created by Executive Emergency Decree 3, signed on December 30, 1957. The decree took effect on January 1, 1958, after which the Ministry was organized. 

Ecuadoran Coffee Institute (Instituto Ecuatori ano del Cafe), the Cacao Renovation Enterprise (Empresa para Renovacion del Cacao), and the National Cotton Commission (Comision Nacional del Algodon).

The Ecuadoran Department of Agriculture and 

SCIA operate agricultural extension services throughout the country. They maintain a combined total of 32 field offices through which their agents work in close collaboration with local organizations in improving agricultural practices. The SCIA operates 19 of the field offices. 

Agents of SCIA in 1956 carried out nearly 3,000 field demonstrations; visited some 4,400 farms; distributed at cost over 48,000 coffee, cacao, and fruit plants; treated more than 9,000 head of cattle; and circulated 65,000 bulletins among farmers. The extension program of the Department of Agriculture in 1956 encompassed nearly 

2,500 field demonstrations and some 3,300 farm visits. The extension services have fostered the development of 4-H clubs, and more than 90 clubs have been organized.

In addition to the extension services, the Department of Agriculture operates a vocational agricultural school in Ambato and two experimental stations located in Tumbaco in the Province of Pichincha and in Ibarra. SCIA operates two vocational agricultural schools, one in Daule, Guayas, and the other in Latacunga, and two experimental stations situated in Pichilingue, near 

Quevedo, Los Rios, and in Portoviejo. A third experimental station, formerly operated by SCIA in Isobamba, Pichincha, is now operated by the National Wheat Commission. SCIA also has a livestock demonstration center near Guayaquil and two machinery pools, one in Guayaquil and one in Cuenca.

The SCIA experimental station at Pichilingue has worked principally on research in tropical export crops, such as cacao, coffee, and bananas. It has produced important results, especially in cacao research, in developing improved cultural methods and more diseaseresistant plant varieties. The station has developed an economical control against Monilia pod rot, a serious cacao disease. The control, which involves the application of wet sulfur, has proved highly successful and is now in wide use in Ecuador. In collaboration with the National 

Association of Banana Growers, SCIA experts at Pichilingue have also developed an economical control against Sigatoka, a disease that has attacked large areas of land under banana cultivation. The Sigatoka control employs an oil spray that may be applied by lightweight equipment carried like a knapsack or by helicopter. Since the control was introduced in 1957, it has been adopted by many private operators and contractors in Ecuador. 35

At its Portoviejo experimental station, SCIA 

has done much work in corn research and mechanized cultivation. The Tumbaco and Ibarra experimental stations of the Department of Agriculture have done considerable research in a variety of products, including citrus fruits, potatoes, barley, oats, corn, and coffee. At Isobamba, the National Wheat Commission, with SCIA assistance, has concentrated in developing improved varieties of wheat for local cultivation.

The FAO mission, which functions as a part of the Ministry of Production to facilitate close collaboration between its international experts and Ecuadoran administrative and technical personnel, operates programs in animal husbandry and crop improvement. It has taken an important role in assisting Ecuadoran farmers to combat livestock diseases and has provided technical aid in improving livestock management methods, particularly in sheepraising. In addition, the 

FAO has done valuable work in crop analysis, soil surveying and mapping, and in colonization planning. As a part of its program, the FAO mission also maintains agricultural training centers and schools in several parts of the country. 

A national irrigation program was launched in 1945 by the National Irrigation Board (Caja 

Nacional de Riego), an autonomous body supported by Central Government and social security funds, but lack of adequate financing has sharply restricted the scope and success of the program. 

Up to the present, canals have been constructed to irrigate some 22,200 hectares in the Provinces of Azuay, Manabi, Chimborazo, Guayas, Pichincha, and El Oro. Because of inaccessibility, however, only about 5,500 hectares of the irrigated areas have beeen brought into production. 

Crop Production and Consumption 

Ecuador produces a wide variety of tropical and temperate crops, but a relatively few constitute the major part of production (see table 13). 

Table 13.-Estimated Production of Principal Crops, 1954-56 

[Quantity in thousand quintals; area in thousand hectares] Crop 1954 1955 1956 The agricultural economies of the two producing regions, the Costa and the Sierra, are basically dissimilar. The Costa concentrates primarily_on producing commercial crops for export. The 

Sierra, on the other hand, is devoted almost exclusively to raising subsistence crops for domestic use.

Export Crops 

The principal export crops are bananas, cacao, and coffee. The combined exports of these products accounted for 90 percent of the value of all exports in 1954-56. Rice, formerly a leading export, is now primarily a domestic crop, although significant quantities are sold abroad from time to time. Small quantities of citrus fruit and some pyrethrum are also exported. Bananas 

Exports of bananas.-Bananas are the leading export, and Ecuador is the world's foremost supplier. Production and exports have increased phenomenally in a short period of time. Between 

1951 and 1956, for example, the area devoted to banana production was expanded from approximately 45,000 to an estimated 70,200 hectares, and the quantity of exports increased by 135 percent (see table 14). In 1947 banana shipments represented only 4 percent of the value of all exports. 

By 1951 the proportion had risen to 20 percent, and by 1955 it had reached 42 percent. In 1956 banana shipments accounted for 39 percent of the value of all exports, slightly below the 1955 peak but well above any other components of the trade. 

Exports normally take about 30 percent of all bananas produced.

Development of banana industry. The rapid development of the banana industry can be attributed to a combination of factors. The reopening of world markets in the postwar years, particularly in the United States and later in Europe, coupled with attractive prices, spurred production. In addition, reduced production in some 

Central American countries increased demands upon Ecuadoran suppliers. Internally, expansion 36

Agriculture, Livestock, and Colonization of the industry has been fostered by the extensive availability of suitable lands for cultivation and by the minimal requirements of capital and technical skills. To promote the industry's development, both the Government and private exporters have extended credits, and the Government has pursued a foreign exchange policy intended to encourage production for export. The good quality of the Ecuadoran Gros Michel bananas in itself has contributed fundamentally to the expansion of foreign sales, and increasing local consumption also has played a significant part in 

Estimulating production. 

Banana-producing Provinces.-Bananas are grown in all of the Provinces in the Costa and in most of the Provinces in the Sierra (see table 15).

put and contains approximately four-fifths of the area under cultivation. The Province of Guayas is by far the largest producer, accounting for 29 percent of national production and 34 percent of 

Costa production. The Province of Pichincha is the principal producer in the Sierra, providing 

29 percent of the region's output. Production in the Costa is intended largely for export, while in the Sierra the crop is largely consumed locally, with the important exception of Pichincha, which annually produces a sizable volume of bananas for export.

Producers' landholdings.-The bulk of the banana crop is produced on many small landholdings by independent farmers. Generally, bananas occupy only a part of the landholding; other crops, such as cacao, coffee, and rice, are planted or livestock is raised. This practice is especially true of most of the older plantations along the riverbanks in the Costa, although in both the Provinces of Guayas and Esmeraldas there are some plantations that are devoted entirely to the cultivation of bananas. Similarly, on many of the newer plantations situated in the valleys and on the slopes of the Andes, production is concentrated exclusively in bananas.

The average size of that part of the individual landholdings planted to bananas in the Costa in 1954 was approximately 5.5 hectares; in the Sierra, slightly less than 1.5 hectares. Over 50 percent of the plantations in the Costa contained less than 10 hectares, and more than 80 percent contained less than 50 hectares. The largest of the plantations in production are owned by ex port firms. The Compania Bananera del Ecua dor, the Ecuadoran branch of the United Fruit

Co., owns and operates a plantation of some 3,000 hectares at Tenguel, located in southern Guayas near the El Oro boundary. The Swedish export firm, Compania Frutera de Astral, has 3 plantations in Esmeraldas with a combined area of about 3,000 hectares, as well as a large plantation in Pichincha. Each of these companies owns additional land that has not been brought into production. Other large plantations include a 

1,500-hectare plantation near Puna owned by the

Chilean Compania Frutera Sudamericana and the 

Swedish-owned 1,000-hectare Hacienda Clementina near Babahoyo. The output of the large plantations, however, constitutes only a minor part of total exports, probably not more than 20 percent, and the trade depends upon the small producers for supplies.

Production methods.-The methods employed by the great majority of small farmers in cultivating, harvesting, and marketing bananas are rudimentary and wasteful. Fertilizers are not used, and no attempt is made to improve the strain of the product. In some areas, particularly the Prov- 

8 Direccion General de Estadistica y Censos, Primer Censo 

Agropecuario Nacional, 1954, pages 153 and 164. 37

ince of Esmeraldas, the crop has been seriously attacked by disease. As production has declined in some sections swept by disease, other lands far removed from economical access to market outlets have been brought into production. The carelessness and transportation delays in handling harvests and shipping them to market have combined to take a heavy toll of production. As a result, rejects among stems offered for sale to exporters are high, ranging from 20 to 40 percent.

Exporters make their purchases at the port of shipment and usually limit their selection to stems of eight or more hands. This limitation results in many more rejects.

Some of the larger producers, especially the leading export firms that operate plantations, have taken steps to improve production methods. and safeguard their crops against disease. On some plantations nitrogenous fertilizers have been introduced, with consequent increases in yields up to 90 percent. Memories of the disaster caused by cacao diseases in the twenties have served to stimulate a growing awareness of the dangers that now threaten banana production. In Guayas and

El Oro the Compania Bananera del Ecuador has launched a program reportedly involving the expenditure of $1 million to combat panama disease (mal de panama) and sigatoka. Another constructive outcome of this concern has been the organization of the Asociacion Nacional de Bananeros del Ecuador (ANBE), which was created by Government decree in July 1955 for the purpose, among other objectives, of devising controls against the diseases and plagues that threaten the industry. In September 1957 exporters joined the campaign against sigatoka by signing an agreement with the Government providing for a 

1.5-sucre increase per stem in the purchase price of bananas to raise additional funds to fight the disease. Prior development of the sigatoka control, with its economy in application, induced the exporters to sign the agreement.

Many new plantations have been established in that area, particularly along the Duran-Tambo and Daule-Quevedo highways. Production for export in the hinterland of the port of Bahia is not highly developed.

1954-56, the United States took 71 percent of all shipments and Western Germany, 14 percent.

New and expanding markets in Europe have accounted for much of the increase in foreign sales. 

Between 1952 and 1955, United States purchases declined from 89 to 71 percent of total exports, while European purchases increased from 7 to 26 percent. In the past few years, exporters have stressed the importance of cultivating European markets in the belief that the United States offers limited possibilities of further appreciable expansion in view of the resurgence of production by long-established Central American suppliers. 

Principal exporters.-Many exporters are engaged in the banana trade, but over four-fifths of the shipments are handled by five companies-two United States firms, Compania Bananera del 

Ecuador and the Standard Fruit and Steamship 

Co., the Chilean Compania Frutera Sudamericana, the Swedish Compania Frutera de Astral, and the Western German Corporacion Ecuatori- 38

Agriculture, Livestock, and Colonization ana y Europea.

The largest of these exporters is the Compania Frutera Sudamericana. 

Neither the Standard Fruit and Steamship Co. 

4 nor the Corporacion Ecuatoriana y Europea owns or operates plantations in Ecuador. Both purchase their bananas through agents. The three other companies produce some of their supplies on their own plantations and purchase the rest from independent producers. In 1954, for example, the Compania Bananera del Ecuador shipped approximately 4 million stems, of which 1.25 million were produced by the company and the remaining 2.75 million were purchased. Exporters are required by law to purchase at least 

150 percent of the bananas that they ship.

Financial assistance to producers.-Under the à stimulus of rising export prices, which increased from an average of 9.5 sucres per stem in 1948 to 

24.3 sucres in 1956, Government and private banking facilities, as well as some of the export firms, have extended financial assistance to producers. 

The bulk of these credits have been provided by the Government-owned development banks (bancos de fomento), which in 1954-56 granted an average of 28.9 million sucres in loans each year. In 1956, development bank loans to the banana industry reached a new high of 32.3 million sucres. 

Favorable conversion rates for foreign exchange receipts have also encouraged the industry.10

Development of banana industry. The orderly development of the banana industry has been impeded by many factors, including the proliferation of very small and inefficient plantations, the lack or deficiency of transportation facilities, and the disparity of local taxes. Growers in the Santo 

Domingo-Quininde zone, for example, have long been penalized by the lack of adequate roads over which to ship their output. Local taxes have encouraged production in some areas and discouraged it in others. For example, taxes on bananas in Manabi Province at the close of 1954 amounted to 4.75 sucres per stem as compared with 2.37 sucres in Guayas Province.

In order to promote the development of the industry along planned lines beneficial to both the producers and the country, the National Association of Banana Growers was organized by the Government in 1955. The structure of the ANBE provides for a National Directive Council, a General Assembly, and Provincial and cantonal councils. Membership is open to all registered producers. The Minister of Economy, or his appointed delegate, serves as chairman of the National Directive Council, the most important body in the Association, and the Department of 

Agriculture and the National Development Bank 

See chapter XI for discussion of development banks. 

10 Particulars regarding the Government's policy and regulations concerning foreign exchange are given in chapter XII. are also represented. The Council is authorized to maintain a technical staff to collaborate with the technicians in the Department of Agriculture. 

Funds for the operations of the Association are derived from a tax of 0.15 sucre per stem of bananas shipped out of the country. That tax is assessed against the exporters.

The basic program of action endorsed by the Association calls for planned expansion of production in areas previously proved to be suitable for cultivation, the formulation and application of disease controls, and the construction of feeder roads to producers. The program also includes among its aims the development of credit facilities to meet the needs of producers and the protection of internal and external market prices through intervention in purchase-sale operations.

Since its inception, the Association has taken an important part in developing new export markets and has achieved a large measure of success in improving the marketing position of its members. Cacao 

During the 19th century, Ecuador was the world's leading exporter of cacao, providing more than 20 percent of the shipments to world markets. In the early years of the 20th century, the emergence of Brazil and of the Gold Coast in the trade dislodged Ecuador from world leadership.

Later, the outbreak of fungal diseases, particularly witches'-broom and, to a lesser extent, monilia pod rot, sharply reduced national production and exports, and Ecuador became a minor world supplier. Between 1895 and 1940, the country's share of world exports fell from 20.9 percent to 1.7.

Exports of cacao.-Cacao production in Ecuador reached a peak in 1915-19, when exports (which are almost equivalent to production, as local consumption currently accounts for only about 500 tons a year) averaged 41,595 tons annually. In the following years, the ravages of monilia pod rot, first observed in Los Rios about 

1914, and witches'-broom, first discovered in

Guayas in 1921, took a heavy toll. Exports dropped precipitously, falling to an annual average of 15,951 tons in 1930-34 and to a low point of 

14,266 tons in 1940-44. 

Since the end of World War II and especially since 1949, there has been an appreciable improvement in the cacao industry, largely in response to the stimulus of rising prices and the extension of technical and financial assistance. In 1952-56, exports averaged 25,846 tons a year (see table 18), 80 percent above the annual average for 1940-44, and Ecuador increased its share of the world market to an estimated 3.6 percent. Spurred by record-high prices, production and shipments in 1954 were the largest since 1925. 39

While cacao no longer dominates the country's export trade as it did in former years, it has never ceased to be one of the most important products in the trade. In the lean year 1944, for example, when the value of cacao shipments amounted to only 11 percent of the value of all exports, the product was third in value behind rice and panama hats. Over the 10-year period 1947-56, cacao accounted for 26 percent of the value of all exports and was never less than third in value among all commodities each year. In four of those years it was first in value, and in the most recent year in which it led the trade, 1954, it accounted for 34 percent of the value of all exports. Lower market prices in 1955 and 1956 reduced the proportionate value of cacao shipments to 22 and 19 percent, respectively. Cacao exports in 1955 and 1956 were exceeded in value by coffee. 

Producing Provinces.-Cacao grows throughout much of the Costa, and a small amount is also found in some parts of the Sierra (see table 15).

The Province of Los Rios is the principal producing area, followed by the Provinces of Guayas, Manabi, and El Oro. Commercial production is concentrated principally in sections of these Provinces near the western foothills of the Andes. The producing zones are designated by the commercial name of the cacao grown in each zone. 

The best quality cacao, know as cacao arriba, is grown in the Arriba region, which comprises the cultivated areas of Los Rios and Guayas in the Guayas River Basin. The other important producing zones are the Bahia region in Manabi, the Machala region in El Oro, and the Balao region, which comprises the southern part of Guayas, including the producing center Naranjal, although cacao from the latter source is sometimes designated by the name of the center.

The number of hectares under cultivation in 1955 was about 24 percent greater than in 1947. 

Estimates vary greatly regarding the number of cacao trees. But, according to agricultural census data, Ecuador in 1954 had a total of 141.1 million cacao trees, of which 114.8 million were in production. The Costa contained about 96 percent of the trees, and the two Costa Provinces Total number trees in proof trees duction

Source: Direccion General de Estadistica y Censos, Primer Censo Agro pecuario Nacional, 1954, Quito, pages 170 through 174. 

Producers' landholdings.-Nearly three-fifths of the cacao plantations in the Costa have less than 20 hectares, but the major part of the cacao crop is produced by plantations of 50 or more hectares. 

In 1954, plantations containing 50 or more hectares constituted only one-fifth of the plantations in the Costa but contained over two-thirds of the trees in production. Cacao plantings flourish in damp, alluvial soils, and consequently plantations are located along rivers. Some plantings have also been established in irrigated areas in the Bahia de Caraquez and Machala regions. On the large landholdings about 10 percent of the land is planted to cacao; the percentage is substantially higher on smaller holdings. The trees begin to produce fruit in quantity about 4 years after planting and reach productivity from 6 to 10 years after planting.

Production methods.-Many different varieties of trees are cultivated, but all varieties are included in two groups-cacao nacional, which consists of indigenous varieties; and cacao venezuela, which comprises foreign varieties that were first introduced from Trinidad, Venezuela, and a few other countries in the closing years of the 19th century. About three-fourths of the trees in

Ecuador are of the native variety, which yields a much higher quality product than the foreign varieties but appears to be more susceptible to some kinds of diseases.

On most of the larger private holdings, cacao cultivation is usually carried on by individuals who work small areas of the land under contract with the owner. With few exceptions, all work is done by hand and little consistent attention is paid to pruning, spraying, and other measures of safeguarding the trees. Modern techniques in cultivation and disease control have been adopted by some of the newer plantations, particularly plantations operated by export firms, and in the past few years important progress in technical assistance to individual planters has resulted from the research and field program of SCIA and the 40

Agriculture, Livestock, and Colonization 

J 1 Cacao Renovation Enterprise, but the majority of the plantations are still administered and operated as in the past.

Ecuador produces three separate annual cacao crops the navidad, or Christmas, crop of December and January; the summer crop of March -through May; and the season crop of June through September. After being harvested, the cacao beans are manually removed from the pods and collected in piles to ferment. After fermenting, they are spread out on platforms to dry in the sun. They are then bagged and shipped to dealers or exporters. Frequently the cacao beans require further drying before export, and in Guayaquil, the center of the export trade, it is a common sight to see court areas and streets covered with cacao beans drying in the sun during the shipping season.

Cacao nacional constitutes about 90 percent of national production, and the superior commercial grade cacao arriba makes up probably 60 percent of the shipments. The Machala region, in which there is about an equal proportion of cacao nacional and cacao venezuela, is second to the Arriba region in production.

Cacao ports, markets, exporters.-Approximately 97 percent of all cacao export shipments are routed through the port of Guayaquil. Bahia de Caraquez handles the bulk of the remaining shipments. Many large companies, including the 

American firm Grace and Co., are engaged in the export trade, which has its principal market in the United States (see table 19). In 1954-56, the United States took 54 percent of all shipments by volume and 53 percent by value.

Table 19.-Exports of Cacao, by Principal Destinations, 1954-56 [Value in thousands of dollars; quantity in metric tons] 

The Enterprise now operates several propagation centers and has distributed thousands of plants for cultivation. The development banks have also provided valuable support. Between 1947 and 

1956, they increased their loans to the cacao industry from 1.5 million sucres to 14.6 million.

Also of primary importance have been the fieldwork and research of SCIA. Coffee Exports of coffee.-Coffee has long been one of 

Ecuador's most important export crops, but it assumed added importance during the 1950's, when world market prices increased rapidly. From 

1950 to 1956 coffee exports annually averaged approximately 27 percent of the value of all exports. 

Coffee was the leading export by value in 1950 and second in value in all other years except 1954, when it was third. In 1954, cacao and bananas ranked first and second. 

In 1950-54, coffee production averaged 25,400 tons a year, or 111 percent above the yearly average for the preceding 5-year period. In 1955, output dropped to some 22,600 tons, a decline of approximately 36 percent from 1954, but in 1956 it recovered to an estimated 29,400 tons.

Coffee is produced primarily for export, for the local market consumes a very small percentage of the crop. Over the 10-year period 1947-56, exports increased 136 percent in volume and 671 percent in value (see table 20). Shipments in 1956 were the highest in volume and value in the country's history.

Producing Provinces. The principal areas of production are the Costa Provinces of Manabi, Guayas, and El Oro and the Sierra Province of 

Loja (see table 15). In 1955, these 4 Provinces contained 86 percent of the total estimated area under cultivation and accounted for 88 percent of national production. The number of coffee trees in production was estimated at 83.4 million in 

1954. Manabi contained 60 percent of the trees in the Costa and 52 percent of the trees in the country. Loja accounted for approximately two- 41

Source: Direccion General de Estadistica y Censos, Primer Censo Agro pecuario Nacional, 1954, op. cit., pages 170 through 174.

The vast majority of the coffee trees are of the 

Coffea Arabica variety. There are some plantings of Liberia and Robusta varieties, and in the past few years there has been some experimentation with high-yielding and disease-resistant strains brought from Colombia, Central America, and other coffee-producing areas. Many of the trees are old and of little productive value. Since 1950, however, many new plantings have been made, and the number of young trees has been substantially increased. From the beginning of 1954, annual imports of Arabica coffee seeds from Central 

America approximated 1,200 pounds. In the 1956 rainy season, over 1.2 million coffee plants of 

Arabica varieties were planted.

Producers' landholdings.-The bulk of the coffee is grown on small landholdings ranging from about 1 to 3 hectares in size. Trade sources estimate that approximately 90 percent of the annual crop is produced by small farmers who grow no more than 25 bags (of 100 pounds) each. 

Production methods.-Most growers plant plantain or banana rhizomes and shade-tree cuttings or seeds simultaneously with the coffee seedlings and usually intersperse among these plantings, particularly during the first 2 or 3 years, some beans, corn, squash, and root crops. Cultivation is generally limited to weeding. Few farmers prune the trees and fewer use fertilizers. 

The main coffee crop is harvested between June and August, although deliveries from producing centers continue throughout the year. Harvesting methods usually consist of stripping the branches of the coffee trees, with frequent injury to twigs and leaves, although in recent years an increasing number of growers have insisted upon more careful and selective picking.

While cultural methods continue to be largely rudimentary and antiquated, there has been considerable improvement in processing methods. In contrast to only a few years ago when export shipments consisted of unwashed coffee, most of the country's production now is processed by the depulping or washing method, and washed coffee, which commands a higher price than the unwashed product, now constitutes most of the export shipments. Between 1955 and 1956, unwashed coffee declined from 72 to 37 percent of total shipments, while washed coffee increased from 28 to 63 percent.

Coffee ports and markets.-The export trade in recent years has shifted from Guayaquil to Manta.

Manta greatly increased its shipments when washed coffee became more abundant. It now normally handles about two-thirds of all export shipments. Guayaquil remains the center of unwashed coffee shipments, and some cargoes are shipped through Bahia de Caraquez. The United

States, which is the principal export market, in 

1954-56 took approximately 65 percent of the volume of all shipments (see table 21). There has

At present Ecuador supplies about 1 percent of the world's export trade. The growing finan cial benefits of the coffee trade, particularly in recent years, have stimulated public and Government interest in its development and expansion. 

Development of the coffee industry. Both of ficial and private agencies have been established to help the coffee industry. In 1940 the coffee growers organized the Coffee Growers' Production and Credit Cooperative (Sociedad Coopera tiva Cafetera de Produccion y Credito) in Jipijapa. The cooperative grants short-term loans to its members and purchases equipment for distribution at reduced prices. In 1949 the Government established the Ecuadoran Coffee Institute as a semiautonomous entity to promote and improve national coffee production. The Institute owns and operates an experimental farm at Camposano, 42

Agriculture, Livestock, and Colonization not far from Jipijapa, where it has carried out of valuable research and trials on new strains of coffee plants. When it was established, the Institute was also intended to serve as an agency of credit for the industry, but it has never had adequate funds to perform this function. Increased activities of the development banks have filled at least a part of the gap. Between 1947 and 1956, loans of the development banks to the coffee industry rose from 1.4 million to 21.7 million sucres. Rice

Rice has been raised as a subsistence crop in Ecuador since colonial times, but it did not become an important commercial crop until the decline in the cacao industry in the 1920's. The first rice mills were established in those years, and the first exports were shipped to neighboring countries. By 1940, exports of rice were second only to bananas in tonnage and were exceeded in value -only by cacao and coffee. In 1946 rice exports reached a peak of 67,100 tons valued at $15.2 million and were first in value among all exports. 

Since then, the trend has been downward (see table 22).

Production and production methods.-Produc tion is heavily concentrated in the Costa Provinces of Guayas and, to a lesser extent, Los Rios (see table 15). In 1955 Guayas accounted for 

65 percent of the recorded national crop and Los 

Rios, 27 percent. An indeterminate but appreciable quantity of rice is grown for home consumption and never enters commercial channels. Two crops are produced, the winter crop and the summer crop. More than 80 percent of production is supplied by the winter crop, which is usually planted in December and January and harvested in May and June. The summer crop, also known as the Vega or lowlands crop, is planted in May and June, after the recession of waters in areas flooded by seasonal rains, and harvested in Sep tember and October. 

Ecuador produces several varieties of rice that may be classified under four general headings: 

Three-month, or short-season; Canilla Largo; 

Fortuna; and imported varieties. The most popular variety of short-season rice is Canilla Chato, which is of poor quality but is popular among many farmers because of its short vegetative period, ease of hand threshing, and thick hull that protects against fermentation during slow and poor transportation to the mills. Canilla Largo is a fine, translucent, long-grain rice that requires a growing period of about 5 months. It is highly acceptable both for domestic consumption and for export, and it constitutes the bulk of export shipments. Fortuna, a large-grain, creamy, translucent variety, is highly regarded in the domestic market, but it is not well adapted for the export trade because of its thin hull and consequent fermentation in transit. Several varieties of imported rice, all of which are marketed under the names of Nira or Rexoro, have been introduced. 

The most popular of these are the Nira strains 

Bluebonnet and Sunbonnet-and the Rexoro strains-Patna, Century Patna, Rexark, and Rex oro proper.

Most of the work in preparing the ground and planting, cultivating, and harvesting rice is done by hand in Ecuador. The majority of rice growers in the Costa are day laborers who rent a cuadra or two (1 cuadra equals approximately 

1.7 acres) from large landholders, usually at a rental of 2 quintals per cuadra. The grower retains part of the output for his own use and markets the rest. Operations include felling and removing timber, clearing and burning brush, preparing seed, transplanting where necessary, weeding two or three times, protecting crops from marauding flocks of birds when the crop is in the milk stage, harvesting by sickle, and transporting the harvest to the rice mills. Very little machinery or power equipment is used, although abortive efforts were made a few years ago to introduce mechanized methods." There has been very limited use of irrigation, and, insofar as crop diseases are concerned, the great majority of growers are unacquainted with means or methods of combating such pests as chinchorros and falsa langosta, which take a heavy toll of production. 

Because of the hand methods in use, the small units of operation, and the dependence on natural rainfall, production costs are high, and even though prices are supported by the Government, there is little profit for the growers. The crop is delivered to the mills and sold to the Government at stipulated prices. In May 1957 a minimum purchase price of 70 sucres per quintal of 

1 In 1950 the Empresa de Servicios Agricolas Mecanizadas, S. A. (ESAM), an enterprise of the International Basic Economy 

Corporation, began operations in Ecuador to mechanize and expand rice production by renting equipment and technical services to growers. ESAM terminated its operations in 1952 and transferred its equipment to the National Development Bank. 43

milled rice was announced. The minimum prices per quintal had been 85 sucres in 1955 and 75 sucres in 1956, and reportedly reflected official determination to discourage surplus production which could not be exported. Under present market conditions, costs are said to be too high to permit regular production for export. 

Exports of rice.-Over the years Ecuadoran exports of rice have reached their highest levels in periods of world shortages and high prices. Shipments in 1946-48, after World War II and before production was fully restored in the Far East, were the largest in the country's history, annually averaging 64,100 tons in volume and $14.3 million in value. With the resumption of trade by the more efficient Far Eastern producers, Ecuadoran rice was unable to compete in price and quality.

In 1954-56, exports averaged 17,300 tons valued at $2.5 million. One of the principal problems now confronting the Government is to find markets for Ecuadoran rice in the face of high world production. To stimulate the export trade, the Monetary Board 12 in March 1957 authorized the conversion of all foreign exchange proceeds from export sales of rice at the free market rate of exchange.

At present, the principal export markets are Colombia and some of the Central American countries, particularly Costa Rica, Panama, and El Salvador.

Other Export Crops 

Ecuador produces many kinds of fruit, but only a few kinds are exported. The most important fruits in the export trade, aside from bananas, are oranges and pineapples. 

Oranges. Oranges are cultivated in areas ranging in altitude from sea level up to more than 7,000 feet. The Agricultural Census of 1954 estimated the total number of orange trees in Ecuador at 837,300 of which 442,300 were in the Sierra and 395,000 were in the Costa. The Sierra Province of Bolivar accounted for over 25 percent of the trees. The other leading Provinces were Pichincha, Los Rios, Guayas, and El Oro, in descending order. Both the sweet orange (Citrus 

Sinensis L.) and the tangerine type (Novilis Delicious) are produced. Reliable data regarding the volume of production are unavailable, but the crop probably approximates between 700 and 800 million oranges a year. Exports of oranges take only a small part of production. Shipments in 1955 and 1956, virtually all of which were sent to Peru, amounted to 13.7 million and 16.5 million units, respectively. 

Pineapples.-Pineapples are grown in all the

Costa Provinces and in some areas of the Sierra 

13 See chapter XI for description of Monetary Board. 

Provinces of Imbabura, Bolivar, Loja, and Pichin cha. The principal producing area is the Milagro region, east of Guayaquil, in the Province of Guayas, which accounts for between 40 and 45 percent of national production. In 1955 an esti mated 432 hectares were under pineapple cultiva tion throughout Ecuador, and the crop amounted to some 2,806,000 pineapples, according to the

Ecuadoran Department of Agriculture. The vol ume of exports is small and practically all shipments are to Peru or Chile. Inclusive of minor quantities of mangoes and other fresh fruits, shipments in 1954-56 annually averaged 1,600 tons valued at approximately $61,000.

Other fruit.-Apart from very limited exports of mangoes and limes, other Costa fruits, such as papayas, avocados, lemons, and grapefruit, are not exported. Some Sierra fruits, such as peaches, apples, plums, and pears, which are grown in abundance in the vicinity of Ambato and Cuenca, occasionally are exported in small quantities to Colombia. Sporadic efforts have been made to promote the exportation of a fruit known as naranjilla, which is a member of the Solanaceae plant family and closely resembles the tomato in its inside pulp and seed. The fruit is grown in the Sierra, principally in the Banos region southeast of Ambato, and its juice is very popular as a beverage among Ecuadorans. Naranjilla was introduced to the United States market at the World's Fair in New York in 1939.

Subsequent efforts to secure a foothold in the mar ket never developed, however, largely because of the difficulty and expense of preserving the fruit over long shipping distances. 

Pyrethrum.-Pyrethrum is one of the Sierra's few exports. A small plant that looks very much like a daisy, pyrethrum produces a flower with a high content of an effective insecticide ingredient. 

The plant was first introduced in Ecuador in 1944, when seedlings were brought in from Kenya, Africa, by the United States Government which, in cooperation with the Ecuadoran Government, started a nursery program. Pyrethrum was found to flourish on the high slopes of the Andes, at elevations of 10,000 feet and above, and two United States companies, S. B. Penick Co. and 

U. S. Industrial Chemicals Co. (now, Food Ma chinery and Chemical Corporation), started operations in Ecuador to produce and export pyrethrum. The latter company is associated with Compania Ecuatoriana Americana del Piretro, a local organization that provides seedlings and buys the grown flowers. S. B. Penick Co. operates its own plantations.

Although still of small-scale importance, the industry has made encouraging progress. Export shipments, by weight and value, in 1954, were 

60,232 kilograms at $30,325; in 1955, 93,543 kilograms at $47,435; and in 1956, 149,253 kilograms at $70,651. 44

Agriculture, Livestock, and Colonization 

L 1 

Domestic Crops 

Crops raised for domestic consumption, apart from the domestic supplies of so-called export crops, include corn, barley, wheat, potatoes, kidney beans, horse beans, peas, lentils, and sugar.

With the exception of sugar, these are essentially 

Sierra crops (see table 23). Since colonial times they have been cultivated on small plots by rudimentary methods, largely as subsistence crops. 

Production for commercial markets has developed on an increasing scale in recent years and has been accompanied by some organized efforts to improve cultural methods and yields.

Table 23.-Estimated Production of Principal Domestic Crops, by Regions, 1955 [Thousands of quintals; number of hectares] because of the inadequacy of local production, imports of wheat for local processing have been greatly increased.

In response to Government support, production increased about 64 percent in volume between 1952 and 1955. The rise in domestic consumption, however, has more than kept pace with the increase in production and has necessitated continued imports in large volume. In 1954-56, imports of wheat averaged approximately $5 million a year, equivalent to more than 5 percent of the value of all imports. Imports in 1956 amounted to $5.5 million, or slightly less than 7 percent of the total value of imports.

In contrast with the foregoing crops, sugarcane is fairly evenly distributed between the Sierra and the Costa. Agricultural census data in 1954 indicated that, of a total of 41,300 hectares in sugarcane production, 19,900 hectares were in the Sierra and 21,400 in the Costa.

The primary inedible domestic crop, in addition to certain forestry products discussed in chapter VI, is cotton. In 1954 the land under cotton cultivation approximated 5,600 hectares, located largely in the Province of Manabi. 

Wheat. The consumption of wheat has increased rapidly in Ecuador in recent years, the apparent consumption of wheat flour having risen from 29,098 tons in 1946 to some 68,500 tons in 

1955. Local production has never been able to fill domestic demands, and until a few years ago imports were predominantly in the form of wheat flour (see table 24). Under the policies instituted by the National Wheat Commission,13 imports of wheat flour have since been sharply reduced, but, 13 The Commission was established in 1949 and reports to the

More than half of the domestic wheat crop is produced in the two Sierra Provinces of Carchi and Pichincha. Other leading producing Provinces are Chimborazo, Bolivar, and Tungurahua.

It has been estimated that up to 90,000 hectares of land in the Sierra are or could be made suitable for wheat production. The estimated area in wheat cultivation in 1956 was somewhat less than 

67,000 hectares. 

Nearly all of the wheat produced in Ecuador is of the type similar to spring wheat in the

United States. The harder varieties are descended from old colonial Spanish types and are more suitable for spaghetti and cookies. The semihard and soft varieties are suitable for bread. 

In 1955, the National Wheat Commission recommended the planting of 3 specific types of wheat: Frontana for altitudes above 9,300 feet, and Bola and type 150 for altitudes up to a maximum of about 10,000 feet.

Crop yields are low. In 1954, wheat production averaged 1,300 pounds per hectare in Ecuador, compared with 2,135 pounds in Columbia.¹4

There is virtually no covered or protected storage, and unthreshed wheat is stored in stacks or piles, with resultant damage from high humidity. 

About 80 percent of the wheat crop is threshed by machine and the remaining 20 percent by ancient methods involving the use of animals. 

14 Junta Nacional de Planificacion y Coordinacion Economica, 

Informe Anual 1955–1956, page 96. 45

Wheat is the only Sierra crop that enjoys a guaranteed price to growers. Prices to wheat producers are fixed and supported by the Government, which obligates the domestic milling industry to purchase the entire commercial crop. 

In May 1957, the minimum wheat prices to be paid producers by the millers were set at 94 to 

104 sucres per quintal, depending upon the moisture content.

Corn.-Corn is the most important cereal crop. 

It is the principal food of the Indians in the Sierra. Corn is produced in all parts of the country, but the leading producing areas are the 

Sierra Provinces of Bolivar, Tungurahua, Pichincha, and Azuay and the Costa Province of Manabi. In 1955, an estimated 151,000 hectares of land were planted to corn, an area much larger than that planted to any other crop except cacao. 

Yields are low, although the introduction of improved seeds in some areas, particularly in Manabi, has substantially increased production. 

The bulk of the crop is used for human consumption, both in the milled and unground state. 

Small quantities are consumed in livestock feed, and in the Sierra an appreciable amount is used in making a popular alcoholic drink called chicha. The increase in the use of corn for poultry and livestock feed has led to occasional and limited imports of corn for this purpose. 

Barley. Another important basic food crop in the Sierra is barley. The crop is grown throughout the Sierra and in parts of the Costa Province of El Oro, but the principal areas of production are in the Sierra Provinces of Chimborazo, Cotopaxi, Pichincha, and Tungurahua. Barley cultivation in 1955 occupied some 110,000 hectares of land. Production has tended to decline, and small amounts have regularly been imported. In 195456, imports of barley averaged approximately 

1,000 tons a year. Improved varieties have been introduced on a limited scale, and larger quantities of the national crop are now being consumed by the domestic beer industry. 

Rye and oats. The other significant grain crops are rye and oats, both Sierra products. Azuay is the leading rye-producing Province, and Pichincha and Imbabura produce most of the oats. 

Slightly less than 2,700 hectares were planted to rye in 1954, and slightly more than 1,500 hectares were planted to oats. Production in 1955 was estimated at 3,800 tons of rye and 1,600 tons of oats. 

The domestic rye crop is adequate to fill local demands, but appreciable imports of oats are required. Imports of oats in 1954-56 annually averaged over 5,000 tons. 

Vegetables.-Potatoes are the most important vegetable crop. Aside from a small quantity grown in El Oro, in the Costa, potatoes are a Sierra product, and the Provinces of Cotopaxi, Chimborazo, Pichincha, and Tungurahua account for the major part of the crop. The ease of cultivation plus the improvements in seeds have substantially boosted production, and the principal factors impeding further expansion are the sharp fluctuations in internal market prices and the difficulty of shipping the crop to centers of consump tion. The lack of storage facilities has prevented controlled distribution, which would meet consumers' needs and stabilize market prices.

A wide variety of other vegetable crops are cultivated, among the more important of which are kidney beans, horse beans, peas, lentils, quinoa, sweet potatoes, yucca, and peanuts. Yucca is one of the leading basic foods in the rural areas and is grown in both the Sierra and the Costa. Peanuts are a Costa product grown principally in the Province of Manabi, and a large part of the crop is used in the industrial preparation of peanut oil. 

The production of peanuts in 1954 was estimated at 108,000 quintals. The other vegetables are grown in the Sierra. With the exception of peanuts, all of these crops are basically subsistence crops. Only limited amounts enter commercial markets, although improved communications have started to promote increased trade in these commodities.

Sugar. Sugarcane is grown in both the Costa and the Sierra, but the Costa produces the major part of the crop. Guayas is by far the most important producing Province, generally accounting for about 40 percent of the acreage in cultivation and over 50 percent of the national crop.

The production of sugarcane dates back to colonial times, and for many years the country exported sugar. Because of depressed business conditions throughout the world, foreign sales of sugar came to an end in 1932 and Ecuadoran growers reduced their acreages. Subsequently, local production fell below domestic requirements, and Ecuador had to start importing sugar. In the late 1930's, the growers launched a drive to increase production, and in 1943 the Government instituted strict controls over sugar importation, permitting imports only by Executive Decree.

Since then, the area of cane grown for sugar has doubled and domestic production has increased significantly, more than doubling in 1947-56 (see table 25). But consumption has also increased, rising from 11.9 kilograms per capita in 1947 to an
