Agriculture, Livestock, and Colonization estimated 24 kilograms in 1955, and imports have continued to be required from time to time. Occasionally, sugar is available for export. In the first quarter of 1957, for example, Ecuador exported to Rumania 9,634 tons of sugar valued at $1.3 million.

In 1956, some 50,800 hectares of cane were in 'cultivation. Approximately 24 percent of the area was devoted to cane for sugar production and 76 percent to cane for the production of panela, aguardiente, and syrups.15 In general, cane for the production of white sugar and syrups is planted in the Costa, while cane for panela and aguardiente is planted in the Sierra. Cane is grown by more than 20,500 operators, and there is a great variation in the size of landholdings. 

The large sugar mills, for example, have holdings of more than 5,000 hectares, not all of which are in cultivation. In 1957, 2 of the largest mills increased their cultivation by over 2,000 hectares. 

Some of the small sugar mills have from 1,000 to 3,000 hectares, while the average holdings of the individual sugar growers range from about 80 to 

250 hectares. On the other hand, the landholdings of growers who produce cane for panela and aguardiente are very small, in general ranging between 5 and 10 hectares and seldom exceeding 20 or 25 hectares.

Sugarcane can be planted in Ecuador at any time of the year. Ordinarily it is allowed to grow at least 11 months before cutting. The harvesting season in the Costa, the principal producing area, extends from July to January, but rains generally limit the cutting and grinding season to 4 or 5 months. By contrast, in neighboring Peru the grinding season covers a 10-month period.

Cultural and production methods on the small I landholdings, especially in the Sierra, are traditional and rudimentary. No irrigation or fertilizer is used. All weeding is done by machete. 

After the harvest, the leaves and other refuse are burned in the fields. Small operators plant the same plot of land to cane over a long period of years, and seldom practice crop rotation to enrich the soil. Little attention is paid to diseases, the most serious of which is mosaic, although it is said that damage from disease is not extensive.

The large plantations in the Costa use tractors and other machinery for planting and cultivating sugarcane. The large sugar mills have experimented with new varieties of cane for cultivation on their plantations, and have introduced improved varieties with greater yields. In the past decade, many of the larger plantations have adopted the use of fertilizers and have made appreciable progress in reducing production costs, which are still high in Ecuador as compared with other sugar-producing countries. 

15 See chapter VIII for a discussion of these products. 

Cotton.-Cotton is cultivated in the Costa Provinces of Manabi (which accounts for about fourfifths of national production), Guayas, and Los 

Rios, and in the Sierra Province of Imbabura.

Because of unfavorable natural conditions, cotton cultivation involves a high degree of risk. Frequently, heavy rains and/or prolonged droughts ruin the crop. Owing to weather vagaries in 1951, for example, the harvest was reduced to 1,442 tons of ginned cotton as compared with 3,153 tons in the preceding year. As a consequence, the area devoted to cotton has been reduced rather than expanded in recent years. According to the Ecuadoran Department of Agriculture, the area planted to cotton declined from some 39,000 hectares in 1949 to an estimated 23,000 hectares in 1954.

The production of ginned cotton in 1952-56 averaged 2,213 tons per year, and ranged from a low of 1,128 tons in 1952 to a high of 2,710 tons in 1956. Production has followed an upward trend in the past few years, but has not recovered to 1950 levels. Imports are required to meet domestic demands. Imports of ginned cotton in 1952-56 averaged 1,427 tons per year, but declined from a high of 2,687 tons in 1953 to 596 tons in 1956. 

The cotton growers devote only small plots of land to cotton. The plots are generally interplanted with other crops, such as corn or yucca. 

Little mechanization is used because of the small areas cultivated. The machete and a few other handtools are employed for clearing the land, planting, and weeding. The planting is done at the beginning of the rainy season, in December and January, and the crop is harvested in late August and September. Yields are low, the national average in 1956 amounting to only 6.9 quintals of seed cotton per hectare. In some parts of 

Manabi, as well as in Imbabura, irrigation is used on a limited scale, but the overwhelming bulk of the crop depends entirely upon a favorable combination of natural weather conditions. 

The principal varieties of cotton plants in cultivation are native plants. Owing to their long vegetative period, they are highly vulnerable to damage by weather and insects, the most destructive of which are the cotton stainer, the pink bollworm, and the stem borer. Some imported varieties of plants are also in cultivation. About 10 percent of the Manabi crop is produced from imported plants. Since 1955, the National Cotton 

Commission has experimented with selected improved varieties and reportedly has achieved some success with such types as Coker W-100 and Delta Pine-15. Greatly increased yields have been obtained on experimental plots. Ecuadoran cotton is, in the main, classified as good middling. 

In general, it is medium-rough, short-staple cotton. 47

8.SA H. R. 

The crop is sold to local buyers who, in turn, sell to ginners. Cotton growers do not own or operate gins for processing their harvests. In the ginning process, the cotton crop, on the average, yields about one-third fiber and one-half seed. 

Shrinkage and foreign materials account for the remainder.

Ecuador is determined to become self-sufficient in cotton. To encourage production, imports are allowed only by special permission of the Minister of Economy, and the local textile industry is obliged to purchase the national crop at prices fixed by the Government. Minimum prices set by the Ministry of Economy in 1957 were as follows, in sucres per quintal: First-quality cotton, 190 for Criollo type and 160 for Acala type; secondquality cotton, 160 for Criollo and 140 for Acala. 

To promote improvement and expansion in cotton production, the National Cotton Commission was created by Government decree in July 1955. 

The Commission is composed of seven membersthe Minister of Economy or his representative, the 

Director of the Technical Bureau of the Department of Agriculture of the Ministry of Economy, two representatives of the Association of Textile 

Industries of Ecuador, two representatives of the cotton growers, and a representative of the cotton ginners. The Commission operates an experimental farm near Bahia de Caraquez and provides technical assistance to growers throughout the country. The Commission also serves the interests of the ginning and manufacturing industries. 

Tobacco. For several years prior to 1949, tobacco production was controlled by the Tobacco Monopoly (Estanco del Tabaco), which purchased the entire crop at fixed prices. Allegedly, the prices paid by the state discouraged production and caused many growers to turn to more profitable crops. Since 1949, growers have been free to sell their crops to private buyers at prices fixed by the state, but production has been depressed by heavy imports of tobacco products by the state monopoly.1

The type of tobacco grown in Ecuador is predominantly black tobacco of the Brazilian or 

Cuban variety. The Costa Provinces of Guayas and El Oro are the principal producing areas. 

Plantings are generally made in late April or early May, and the crop is usually harvested in the period beginning in October and extending through January. Very little care is exercised in seed selection, and the crop is usually an indefinite mixture of relatively poor quality. Few of the growers have drying sheds. The crops are dried in the sun. Some efforts have been made to develop improved strains of tobacco in Esmeraldas, but the efforts have been discontinued because of the expense and limited financial returns. 

16 See chapter VIII. 

Reliable data regarding production are not available, but unofficial estimates for the crop year 

1954-55 placed production at between 20,000 and 

25,000 quintals.

ANIMAL HUSBANDRY 

Livestock products account for about 13 percent of the value of agricultural-pastoral production."

In recent years, appreciable progress has been made in improving dairy herds in the Sierra, but, in general, the livestock industry is poorly developed. The raising of meat animals on a commercial basis is largely restricted to cattle, while the raising of other stock, such as sheep and hogs, is largely carried out by small farmers and landless Indians, principally to satisfy their own needs. 

Data on the numbers of livestock (see table 26) indicate that some significant reductions took place between 1941-54, but the early estimates are highly questionable.

242 and 270. Cattle Breeds of cattle.-The most important sector of the livestock industry is cattle raising. The early 

Spanish colonists introduced the first cattle to Ecuador, and the descendants of those animals, known as criollo, constitute most of the presentday cattle. The criollo breed has developed two principal varieties-a Costa type that is predominantly gray or yellowish gray in color and a Sierra type that is predominantly black in color. 

The criollo type has been crossbred with other types, especially with Holstein Friesian in the Sierra and with Zebu in the Costa. Other breeds found in Ecuador are Brown Swiss and Short horns. In recent years, increasing interest has been displayed in improving the stock. SCIA 

17 Based on 1954 calculations of the Junta Nacional de Planifi cacion y Coordinacion Economica, published in the Informe Anual 

1955-1956, Quito, page 117, table 2. 48

Agriculture, Livestock, and Colonization operates a demonstration farm, Hacienda Florencia,18 situated on an island in the Guayas River near Guayaquil, where it has imported purebred 

Brown Swiss and Zebu stock for breeding purposes. These breeds, especially the Zebu, have proved highly adaptable.

Cattle-raising Provinces.-Among the estimated 

1,216,000 cattle in Ecuador in 1954 were 132,000 work oxen. Excluding work stock, about 61 percent of the cattle were located in the Sierra and 

39 percent in the Costa. The principal cattleraising Provinces in the Sierra were Pichincha, Loja, and Azuay; and in the Costa, Manabi and Guayas.

There are few quality beef cattle, as Government price controls on beef and the system of marketing have provided little incentive for quality production. Most of the beef comes from dairy cattle no longer considered useful for milk production, breeding purposes, or draft power. In the past decade growing attention has been given to the development of dairy herds. Some good breeding stock has been imported and some high-quality herds have been established, particularly in the Sierra, in the region between Ibarra and Ambato. 

Cattle diseases.-Considerable success has been achieved in combating and controlling cattle diseases, largely through the efforts of SCIA and the FAO. The incidence of tuberculosis is low, and encouraging progress has been made in fighting 

Bang's disease. Aftosa has never gained a foothold in Ecuador. Nevertheless, losses from disease and other causes are substantial. Approximately 9 percent of all calves under 1 year of age died of disease or accident in 1954. Moreover, many cows, particularly in the Costa, produce as few as 5 or 6 calves in a lifetime as the result of debilitation from ticks, various diseases, including internal parasites, and inadequate feed.

Cattle feed.-Adequate feed is one of the most difficult problems confronting the livestock industry. Apart from small amounts of alfalfa, oats, and wheat screenings fed to dairy cattle in the 

Sierra, cattle throughout Ecuador are turned out to pasture for their fodder. Pastures in the Costa yield little forage during the dry season, and there are no silos for storing feed from periods of plenty. In addition, many of the pastures are 

Overgrown with thick underbrush that renders the land unsuitable for grazing. In the Sierra the principal problem is lack of adequate space. The land available for grazing is greatly limited by other agricultural demands. In both the Costa and the Sierra, some improvements have been accomplished through sowing pasturage feeds, such as rye grass and cowpeas, and through the use of 18 This farm is owned by the Banco Nacional de Fomento and was formerly operated with little success by the Cattle Repopu lation Enterprise (Empresa para Repoblacion Ganadera, C. A.), the largest of three bovine cattle improvement and breeding enterprises financed by the now defunct Corporacion de Fomento de la Produccion. chemical fertilizers, but such practices have been employed on a very limited scale. 

Yield per carcass.-Cattle sold for beef are shipped to market by truck or boat, or are herded overland. They receive little care along the way and are not fattened before slaughter. As a result, yields are low, averaging from about 80 to 170 kilograms of processed meat per animal, depending upon the breed, age,19 and condition of the animal, and the area from which it comes. 

The fragmentary data available suggest that

Pichincha and Guayas produce the cattle with the highest yield. 

Milk production. Milk production has improved appreciably in both quantity and quality in recent years. Agricultural census data for 1954 indicated that the number of cows being milked was 220,200 and that the total daily milk production was 718,400 liters. Government estimates for 

1956 were 257,500 milk cows with a daily yield of 860,200 liters.20 The Sierra contains about 60 percent of the dairy cattle and accounts for about 70 percent of milk production. The principal dairy 

Province is Pichincha. 

Sheep

The Sierra contains over 99 percent of the sheep in the country. The sheep are well distributed throughout the region, but are most heavily concentrated in the three Provinces of Cotopaxi, Chimborazo, and Pichincha, which combined account for about 40 percent of the total. 

The vast majority of sheep are raised by Indians, who have small flocks ranging from a few up to 50 head. Some of the large estates maintain flocks numbering as many as 500 or more head, largely for the purpose of clearing and fertilizing the land. Landless Indians are usually allowed by landowners to graze their flocks on idle lands before the planting season. Most sheep, however, are pastured at elevations of over 9,000 feet, and forage on land regarded as unsuitable for culti- vation.

Little attention has been paid to improving the flocks. The predominant type of sheep is criollo, descended from stock brought to Ecuador from Spain in colonial days. Some Corriedales have been introduced, but in very limited numbers. As a part of its program, the FAO has attempted to help the sheepraisers. It was instrumental in bringing about the organization in 1956 of the 

National Association of Sheepraisers (Asociacion 

Nacional de Criadores de Ovejas) for the purpose of improving the stock and management methods. 

10 It is illegal to slaughter cattle less than 2 years of age.

Reportedly, however, a large percentage of male calves are slaughtered a few days after birth because of the low profit margin in the meat industry. 

20 Banco Central del Ecuador, Memoria 1956, Quito, page 24. 49

Inasmuch as probably 85 percent of the sheep are raised by Indians who employ the most rudimentary methods, there is little commercial organization and scientific control. In most herds, there are a disproportionately large number of rams and a wasteful slaughter of ewes and lambs. Yields are low in both meat and wool. The average amount of dressed meat amounts to only about 15 kilograms per animal. The wool produced varies greatly in color, length, and diameter of fiber; and the average yield, on the basis of 1954 census data, amounts to only about 2 pounds per animal, an exceptionally low figure that may be due in part to the fact that all shearing is done by hand and as frequently as the owner may have immediate need for wool. Hogs

Hogs are raised for meat and lard in all parts of the country, and nearly every rural dweller has one or more to meet his own requirements. About 

63 percent of the hog population is in the Sierra, and the Provinces of Loja, Pichincha, and Bolivar contain approximately 32 percent of the total. 

Few of the large estates raise hogs on a commercial scale. The small farmers produce the bulk of commercial supplies, which they market after satisfying their own requirements. In some areas, corn and rice polishings are sometimes fed to hogs, but usually hogs are tethered or left to root for themselves in the fields. Yields in meat and lard are said to be satisfactory, although low by comparison with yields in most other countries. Lard production, however, is far below national requirements. The hogs are criollo descendants of stock introduced from Spain. Goats

Goats are raised largely by Indians in the Sierra. 

The Sierra Provinces have about four-fifths of all the goats in the country, and the Province of Loja has over half of the national total. The number of goats in Ecuador appears to have declined substantially in recent years (see table 26).

The animals are used primarily to supply meat, milk, and skins. Only limited amounts of goat meat and goatskins enter commercial markets.

Goats are grazed on some of the large Sierra estates to fertilize the land.

Poultry 

Since the introduction in 1956 of modern techniques in poultry raising, under the aegis of the ICA, there has been a substantial increase in local efforts to improve the quality of the stock and to organize commercial enterprises to supply domestic markets with poultry and eggs. Considerable progress has been made in the past 2 years. The largest poultry installation now has some 11,000 birds on hand and produces about 100 broilers & day. There are now many flocks with more than 

1,000 birds. Cuenca has become an important poultry and egg supply center for the Guayaquil market, and in Guayaquil 4 commercial feed and feed-mixing plants have initiated operations within the past 2 years to accommodate the feed requirements of the poultry industry.

The com- bined output of the 4 plants in 1957 was in excess of 5,000 tons of commercially prepared poultry feed.

In 1956, the latest year for which information is available, there were some 3.7 million poultry (roosters, hens, and chickens) in Ecuador.21 The number of eggs produced approximated 918,300 per day. There are no reliable estimates of the production of dressed poultry for consumption. 

AGRICULTURAL LAND SETTLEMENT 

About half of the land in Ecuador is owned by the state, and one of the most important problems facing the Central Government is bringing this land into production under a planned program that will benefit and enhance the balanced development of the national economy.

Land Law 

Matters pertaining to the sale, colonization, and use of public lands are regulated by the Public 

Lands and Colonization Law (Ley de Tierras 

Baldias y Colonizacion) of May 12, 1936, and its amendments. Under this legislation, the responsible Government agency, the Bureau of Public 

Lands and Colonization (Direccion de Tierras 

Baldias y Colonizacion) of the Ministry of Econ omy is empowered to sell public lands for previ ously approved specific purposes.22 Under the law, the purchaser of public land for agricultural purposes is required to cultivate at least one-fourth of the land within a period of 5 years or the land reverts to the state, and payments already made by the dispossessed purchaser are regarded as rent and therefore not subject to refund. In justifiable cases, the Bureau may grant an extension of up to 

2 years for the purchaser to cultivate the required one-fourth of his property. In cases in which the landholder has not cultivated one-fourth of his land but does have more than 10 hectares in culti vation, the Bureau may grant permanent ownership of the cultivated land plus an area 3 times a Banco Central del Ecuador, Memoria 1956, Quito, page 24. 

23 Sales of public lands through which the Quito-San Lorenso Railway has been built (see chapter IX) are to be handled by the Autonomous Board of the Quinto-San Lorenzo Railway (Junts 

Autonoma del Ferrocarril Quito-San Lorenzo) with offices in Quito. 50

Agriculture, Livestock, and Colonization as large in uncultivated lands adjacent to the cultivated portion, and the remainder of the original plot reverts to the state.

Public lands containing known deposits of salt, minerals, coal, hydrocarbons, or thermal, mineral, or medicinal springs may not be sold. The state must always retain the ownership of such properties, but may negotiate special contracts to lease them to private interests. 

Generally, individuals may purchase a maxi Imum of 50 hectares of public land, but in exceptional cases the maximum may be increased to 200 hectares. Sales involving larger areas require congressional authorization. Aliens are not permitted to own land within 50 kilometers of the country's borders. Land available to United 

States citizens, therefore, would be at least 30 miles from the seacoast. Ecuadoran citizens, or enterprises sponsored by Ecuadorans and aliens, are eligible to purchase land wherever it is offered for sale.

Sale of Public Lands 

Public lands now offered for sale by the Bureau of Public Lands and Colonization are located principally in the general vicinity of Esmeraldas, Quininde, Santo Domingo,23 and Quevedo in the "Unsold public lands in the Santo Domingo zone were set aside in June 1957 for the development of an immigration and colonization project to be financed by a $2 million loan from the United 

States, and the National Colonization Institute (Instituto Na cional de Colonizacion). was organized for the immediate purpose of planning and carrying out the project. Reportedly, the project contemplates the resettling of some 500 families, most of whom would be landless Ecuadorans and the others, European refugees. 

Costa. Lands are also available in the Oriente and in limited areas of the south central section of the country. Sales prices for these lands in 

1956 ranged from 5 to 40 sucres per hectare, depending upon the accessibility of the land to established overland transportation facilities. Most of the available land is far removed from transportation outlets and is heavily wooded and mountainous. Land clearance costs, according to the 

Ecuadoran Department of Agriculture, range from about 800 to 1,000 sucres per hectare.

All sales are regarded as provisional until the purchaser has fulfilled his obligation to cultivate the required portion of his land. Upon evidence of this, the state provides the buyer with a definitive title or title of permanent ownership. Because of the confusion regarding the actual ownership of land assumed to be part of the public domain, some colonists in the past have experienced great difficulty in establishing their title and property rights. Accordingly, it is incumbent upon prospective buyers to determine the legal status of the property. 

In the past 3 fiscal years, mid-1954 through mid- 1957, the Bureau of Public Lands and Colonization issued provisional titles covering 50,038 hectares of land and definitive titles covering 47,068 hectares. The program was greatly accelerated in fiscal 1957, when the Bureau set a new record by negotiating sales contracts covering 66,791 hectares, of which 32,817 hectares were transferred under definitive, or permanent, titles and 33,974 were transferred under provisional titles. 51

CHAPTER VI Forestry and Fisheries 

Timber of many kinds in rich stands is found in the Costa and Oriente regions of Ecuador, but has been exploited commercially in only small amounts, with the principal exception of balsa, because of the lack of transportation facilities and the resultant high cost of operations. 

Readily accessible stands of commercial value in the Costa have largely been logged. Exploitation of the less accessible areas of the Costa and virtually all of the Oriente has been discouraged by the difficulties of removal and shipment. Logging methods, in general, are primitive and wasteful, and, apart from balsa, the lumber industry is rudimentary.

The country's fisheries along the Pacific coast are a source of wealth that has been officially recognized only in recent years. The Government has granted operating concessions to several fishing companies established in Ecuador, and annually collects sizable receipts in registration and license fees from foreign fishing enterprises working the tuna grounds in territorial waters. Development of the domestic fishing industry is still in the formative stage. There are few processing facilities, and the overwhelming bulk of production is exported in a frozen state with little delay. 1 

FOREST PRODUCTS Principal Species 

The forests of Ecuador contain many valuable species, ¹ but commercial development of the forest resources has been retarded by the expense and difficulty of extraction. Lumbering has been limited largely to readily accessible areas in the 

Costa. Some 2,240 species of wood and timber have been identified and cataloged. This figure does not include all species that may be found in 1 Some of the principal commercial woods in Ecuador, according to the Ministerio de Economia, Departmento Forestal, and other sources, are: Mangle, guayacan, colorado, laurel, moral, balsamo, amarillo, chanul, seca, cascol, caoba, roble de esmeraldas, roble dé guayaquil, maria, tilio, figueroa, jigua, fernan sanchez, balsa, guadua, cedro de castilla, ebano, canelo, sisin, aliso, nogal, eucalipto, and encinillo. 2 the Oriente, where botanical research has been carried out in only limited areas. At present, however, only a relatively few species of timber are of commercial importance in the local market and export trade. For example, of some 40 arborescent species found on an estate in Los Rios, only 12 were marketable and half of those were suitable only for use in temporary construction. intermixture of marketable and nonmarketable species in some areas poses serious and expensive logging problems; sometimes, the cost of delivering salable timber to market is more than the timber can be sold for. The

Extensive forests are situated in the Costa and the Oriente. The Oriente is covered with virgin stands that have not been exploited because of their inaccessibility, although there has been some intermittent exploitation of cinchona bark and rubber. The Sierra is largely unforested, the bulk of its timber having been felled long ago, but throughout the region are eucalyptus plantations that were begun in the 1860's and now supply the 

Sierra with fuel and construction material. 

Major Commercial Products

The forests of the Costa are the present sources of supply for the internal and export lumber market, and they include a diversity of types of flora. 

Mangrove.-Dense mangrove forests grow along sections of the Pacific coast, particularly in 

El Oro, southern Guayas, and Esmeraldas. About 

3,000 tons of mangrove bark are used annually by the Ecuadoran tanneries, chiefly in the Costa, for the Sierra tanneries mostly use a certain grade of encinilla bark that is obtained on the western slopes of the Andes. Mangrove wood is a popular construction material in the Costa. About fourfifths of the present production of mangrove lum- 

"La Explotacion Forestal y la Industria Maderera en el Ecuador," Dr. M. Acosta Solis, Boletin Industrial Ecuatoriano, 

January 1957, Quito, page 11. "Forest Resources of Western Ecuador," Eugene F. Horn,

Agriculture in the Americas, United States Department of Agriculture, Washington, D. C., March 1947, vol. VII, No. 3, page 48. 53

ber comes from the estuary of the Santiago River in the Province of Esmeraldas. 

Balsa and guadua.-Balsa and guadua are found in abundance in the Costa. The balsa trees, which are widely distributed throughout the wet, tropical vegetation that covers much of the region, proliferate freely and grow to lumber size in less than 

10 years. The best grade of balsa comes from the

Province of Esmeraldas. Most of the production is exported; only a small amount is used locally, principally for such purposes as lining refrigerators and manufacturing toys. In 1954-56, annual exports of balsa averaged approximately 3,900 tons valued at $990,000.

Guadua, a species of bamboo, occurs along riverbanks and in wet lowlands. It is a giant member of the grass family, reaching about 50 feet in height and as much as 6 inches in diameter. Large quantities of guadua are used in the Costa, particularly in rural areas, for construction. Splits of the stems provide durable boards for floors and walls, while the intact stems are used for timbers and for making scaffolds and fences. At present, guadua is considered a rudimentary building material. 

Hardwoods. Several species of hardwoods that are valued in the furniture-making and carpentry trades are found, including balsamo, amarillo, laurel, caoba, seca, and moral. Balsamo, a richappearing, reddish-brown wood, grows abundantly in the forests of Manabi and Esmeraldas. 

The best variety of laurel, which has a beautiful coffee-color grain, comes from the Island of Puna and the Santa Elena Peninsula in Guayas. Amarillo, a brilliant orange in color with little tendency to warp, occurs in the Guayaquil area of Guayas, where it is now being cultivated, and also in Esmeraldas, where the largest species is produced. 

Caoba, which is of the genus that includes mahogany and resembles mahogany in appearance, comes 'from the northwestern section of Esmeraldas. It is a very hard wood and difficult to work. Seca, which is found in Manabi, is a bright red wood with dark stripes. It is used for cabinetwork as well as for construction. Moral grows throughout the wet tropical forests of the Costa, but is especially plentiful in Esmeraldas. It is a bright yellow wood that is easily worked. 

Other hardwoods of commercial value include guayacan, cascol, and roble, all of which are essentially construction materials. Guayacan, a heavy, highly durable, olive-brown wood, occurs in the dry tropical zone of southwestern Ecuador, chiefly in Manabi, Guayas, and El Oro. It is commonly used for house supports, frames, posts, and crossties. Cascol, also a dry tropical forest product and the heaviest of Ecuadoran woods, weighing about 80 pounds per cubic foot, is used principally for posts and articles of turnery. It is a dark reddish wood difficult to work. Roble, a species of oak, is pale brown in color and has excellent working properties. It is used for struc tural purposes, floors, and interior finish.

Cedro, or Spanish cedar, occurs in the wet tropical forests of the Costa, where the largest specimens reach a height of 125 feet. It is a durable and attractive wood, easy to work. Ebano, which closely resembles the ebony family, is a product of the savannas in western Ecuador. It is a very strong wood suitable for heavy and durable construction. Another Costa hardwood is maria, a light but strong and attractive wood commonly employed in general construction, furniture, and interior woodwork.

Semihard woods.-The leading semihard woods produced in the Costa are figueroa and fernan sanchez. Figueroa, a dark reddish wood somewhat like cedar in appearance, is found principally in Esmeraldas and is used for a variety of purposes, including furniture and cabinetwork as well as general construction. Fernan sanchez, which is used for making boxes, cheap furniture, and low-cost paneling, is related to the cedro family and grows abundantlyin the flood plains of the Costa. Many species of jigua, another semihard wood valued for both carpentry and construction, are found in commercial quantities in Los Rios and Guayas.

Other woods. On the western slopes of the 

Andes, canelo and sisin are found. Canelo, a species of laurel, provides high-quality lumber for carpentry. Some of the better grades are used in furniture. Sisin, the country's only native conifer, yields a soft, clear wood used for carpentry and cabinetwork. In the upper reaches of the Andean forest are aliso and nogal. Aliso, & member of the birch family, is a reddish wood used in flooring, furniture, and cabinetwork. It is cultivated around some farms in the Sierra. 

Nogal, a native species of walnut resembling black walnut, is valued also for furniture and cabinetwork. Plantations of nogal have been developed in some parts of the Sierra. 

Rubber, cinchona, kapok, and other products. 

In addition to many kinds of wood, the Ecuadoran forests also yield many other products, including rubber, cinchona bark, kapok, tagua nuts (vege table-ivory nuts), tannin dyes, castor beans, palm oil, and toquilla straw. The floras that now supply these products are found largely in the wet tropical forests and lowlands of the Costa, with the major exception of the ceibo tree, the source of kapok, which grows in the dry tropical zone of southwestern Ecuador. These products cultivated, but are exploited in their wild state by primitive means. Some 500 tons of kapok and about the same amount of rubber are obtained each year. Export shipments of tagua and castor beans, both of which are harvested for export, annually averaged 6,800 and 7,000 tons, respectively, in 1954-56. are not 54

Forestry and Fisheries CONSERVATION AND REFORESTATION The Forestry Department of the Ministry of 

Economy in recent years has given much attention to promoting conservation of the nation's forest resources and to reforesting sections of the Sierra, and has enlisted the technical assistance of the FAO. In 1955, the Ministry drafted a proposed forest law designed to encourage commercial exploitation consistent with conservation requirements. No congressional action has yet been taken on the proposed law. In the Sierra, the "Ministry has cooperated with local groups in developing plantations of eucalyptus, pine, cyprus, acacia, and other species to supply the region with some of its requirements. At present, the Ministry maintains nurseries in the Sierra at Tulcan, Ibarra, Quito, Ambato, Riobamba, Guaranda, and

Cuenca. The nurseries supply the public with trees for transplanting at reasonable prices.

The Ministry conducts research in tree culture for reforestation at its nurseries and at its experimental station in Conocoto, Pichincha. At Conocoto, foreign species of euclayptus, conifers, acacia, and poplars are under study.

LUMBER INDUSTRY 

The Guayaquil area of Ecuador became an important shipbuilding and lumber center in early colonial times, and a substantial export trade in lumber was developed with neighboring Peru. 

Logging, since the early days, has been carried out in areas accessible to rivers, and, later and to a lesser extent, in areas accessible to roads and railways. Some of these areas have been logged several times, and valuable timber stands are no longer within easy reach. As a result, lumber has become relatively scarce and expensive in Ecuadoran cities. At present, logging is centered principally in Costa areas linked by river or land communications with Guayquil, which is still the center of the lumber trade. Logging is carried on also in the upstream regions of the Esmeraldas and Santiago Rivers in the Province of Esmeraldas, whence the logs are floated downstream to coastal centers.

Logging methods are primitive. The trees are felled by hand and roughly squared or peeled to facilitate handling. Most hardwood logs are square hewn in the forest; balsa logs are peeled immediately after being felled. Logs are generally skidded, or dragged, out of the forest by oxen or mules, or sometimes by men. Tractors are now being used increasingly. Cutting is usually restricted to areas close to a stream or roadside because of the difficulty in skidding the timber, and logs that are considered too large to drag or too small to warrant the expense of dragging are left to decay. For transportation down the rivers, the logs are generally made up into rafts. The largest rafts contain from 80 to 100 logs. In most cases, the rafts are used to carry agricultural products downstream.

Sawmill operators have rarely combined logging and sawmilling, but have depended upon independents, contractors, or middlemen for logs. 

The principal sawmills are located chiefly in 

Guayaquil and in the Province of Esmeraldas. 

The leading lumber centers in Esmeraldas are 

San Lorenzo, Borbon, and Limones, all situated in the estuary of the Santiago River, and the port city of Esmeraldas, located at the mouth of the Esmeraldas River. Other centers, though less important, include Manta and Bahía, both in the Province of Manabi, and some small communities in the Provinces of Los Rios and El Oro. 

Most of the sawmills are small, old, and poorly equipped. The major exceptions are some of those sawmills that process balsa logs. The soaring demand for balsa during World War II led to the installation of modern machinery and drying facilities in some mills. Apart from balsa, which is at present the only important lumber export, most of the lumber is prepared by rudimentary methods and is not dried before being marketed. The leading lumber firm in the export trade is the Compania Ecuatoriana de Balsa,

Guayaquil, which processes and exports balsa exclusively. The company has a modern, wellequipped sawmill. Many other Costa mills were actively engaged in the balsa trade during World War II, but, with the sharp decrease in the world demand for balsa in the postwar period, they shifted to processing hardwoods for the domestic market, which is a small one.

One of the largest firms in the domestic trade is Bruno y Compania. It operates a lumber company under the name Aserradero El Pailon; has sawmills in Guayaquil and Limones, Esmeraldas; and is the only firm that processes mangrove lumber on a commercial basis. None of the lumber companies are equipped to produce veneers and plywood or to treat and preserve wood. 

Domestic demands for lumber (see table 27) are filled by local production except for Douglas fir and yellow pine, both of which are imported occasionally and in small amounts. Requirements

OCEAN FISHERIES 

Some of the richest fishing grounds in the world are off the Pacific coast of Ecuador. But seafood is seldom eaten in most Ecuadoran households outside the Costa. Most of the catch that is landed in Ecuador is marketed for immediate consumption because of the lack of refrigeration and the high cost of air transportation, the only feasible means of shipping fresh fish within the country. 

Consequently, fish is a scarce and expensive food in interior communities.

Fishing Industry 

The fishing industry is largely a family enterprise employing primitive and inefficient methods. 

According to the census of the fishing industry taken in 1954,5 a total of 4,067 families in coastal areas of Ecuador were engaged in fishing. Sixtytwo percent of the heads of the families depended exclusively on fishing for their livelihood; the rest were occasional fishermen. 

In recent years, large-scale commercial exploitation of the Ecuadoran fishing grounds has been initiated and carried on by foreign fishing fleets and by fishing companies established in Ecuador primarily to export their catch to foreign markets. 

Ecuadoran exports of fish rose from 501 tons in 1950 to 3,443 tons in 1955, but declined to 2,356 tons in 1956. In 1954-56, the annual value of fish exports averaged $286,400, with peak receipts of $321,900 in 1955.

Vessels fishing the territorial waters and belonging to foreign companies located outside Ecuador do not land their catch in Ecuador. They freeze or can their catch aboard or on mother or factory ships and land and distribute their catch in foreign markets.

The domestic market depends upon imports of fish for many of its needs. Fish imports, consisting mostly of canned fish, particularly sardines, averaged 1,045 tons valued at $318,000 per year in 1954-56. 

Some principal Ecuadoran marine species of commercial value are: Alewife, amarillo, anchovy, barracuda, black marlin, broad sole, brotulid eels, bumper, cavalla, jack cavalla, crab, crawfish, croaker, dolphin, goatfish, grunt, grouper, halfbeak, horse mackerel, hogfish, mackerel, marine catfish, moonfish, mullet, oyster, Pacific sailfish, Peruvian hammerhead, robalo, sea bass, sea bream, shad, shark, shrimp, skipjack, snapper, Spanish mackerel, spiny lobster, threadfin, thread-herring, triggerfish, white seabass, wrasse, and yellow-finned tuna.

Source: Direccion General de Estadistica y Censos, Primer 

Censo Nacional de Pescadores, Quito, 1954.

Direccion General de Estadistica y Censos, Primer Censo Na cional de Pescadores, Quito, 1954, page 2.

Registration of Fishing Vessels 

Ecuador claims territorial jurisdiction over its coastal waters for a distance of 200 miles from the point of lowest tide along its shores, but it permits foreign-flag vessels belonging to fishing companies established outside Ecuador to exploit its territorial waters upon payment of registration and license fees.

The fees vary in accordance with the type of fishing to be carried on. In the case of tuna, the principal commercial catch, the registration fee is $200. The registration is valid for the calendar year. The license fee is $12 per net register vessel ton, and the license is valid for 1 voyage. 

Foreign-flag vessels operated by fishing companies established in Ecuador receive the same treatment as national-flag vessels, and are required to pay an annual fee of only 50 sucres ($3.30) per register ton. This concession is authorized for only 1 year, upon the conclusion of which the fishing companies in Ecuador are obligated to transfer to Ecuadoran registry whatever foreign-flag equipment they intend to continue using in 

Ecuadoran waters.

Fees paid by foreign fleets in 1954 amounted to $488,538, and in 1955 to $160,018. The Government is empowered to allocate 60 percent of the fees to the Ministry of Economy for the development of the national fishing industry." 

Fishing Companies in Ecuador

The fishing companies in Ecuador have Government concessions that provide duty, tax, and other benefits and that obligate the companies to establish processing and freezing facilities in the country. In early 1957, five companies, or concessionaires, were in business. Three were Amer ican owned or American controlled: Industria

Ecuatoriana Productora de Alimentos, C. A. 

(INEPACA); Pesca Ecuatoriana Santaelenense, C. A. (PESCA); and Productos del Pacifico 

Ecuatoriano, S. A. The other two concessionaires were the Sociedad Nacional de Galapagos, C. A., owned by a Spaniard long resident in Ecuador, and the Productos Maritimos Ecuatorianos, S. A. 

(PROMESA), owned by Guayaquil interests. These companies export virtually all of their catch to foreign markets, with the principal and limited exception of INEPACA, which sells a small part of its catch in the domestic market. 

Tuna and shrimp are the principal catch of the concessionaires. Shrimp fishing has become an

The annual catch of this species approximates 1.8 million pounds. Other species of shrimp, plus crawfish and lobsters, also are items of growing importance in the export trade.

Controls on Fish Exports 

The concessionaires, especially those concentrating in the shrimp industry, have been confronted with some difficult problems, for the Government in 1956 altered some of the conditions under which they are permitted to operate. Formerly, companies exporting frozen shrimp were exempted from the payment of export taxes; now, they are required to pay taxes of 40 centavos per kilogram, an amount equivalent to about 1 cent per pound. 

In June 1956, the Government issued emergency decree law 20, which provided that exporters of fish would have to deliver their full foreign exchange earnings from exports or that portion of their earnings that the Monetary Board might stipulate to the Central Bank at the official rate of exchange. Before, exporters had been required to surrender only $100 per ton, regardless of the type and price of export, and had been permitted to retain the remainder of the exchange or convert it through the free market. In August 1956, the 

Monetary Board established a rate of $300 per ton on exports of shrimp as the amount fishing companies would be required to convert at the official rate of exchange.

The export tax on shrimp was imposed by the Government because it considered that the fishing companies were not performing the industrial operations in Ecuador required under their concessions. In April 1957, an Executive decree prohibited the granting of new concessions to companies to fish in Ecuadoran waters unless the companies had already installed shore facilities to process the fish. 57 470366-59——5

CHAPTER VII Minerals and Power 

Mining accounts for only about 2 percent of the gross internal product and less than 2 percent of the national income in Ecuador. With the exception of petroleum and, to a lesser extent, gold and salt, there is little mineral production of commercial importance, although from colonial times there has been some rudimentary and intermittent small-scale extraction of other minerals, such as silver, copper, coal, sulfur, gypsum, and lead.

Geological characteristics of the country suggest the presence of many types of minerals, and specimens of a wide variety have been found, including manganese, iron, zinc, antimony, and titanium. 

Energy is derived from domestic and imported petroleum products and from waterpower. Charcoal and wood are widely used but their use is limited essentially to fuel for household purposes. 

Coal and coke are of very little importance, either as a household or an industrial fuel.

PETROLEUM AND OTHER MINERALS Petroleum 

Petroleum has been produced in Ecuador since 1917, but the industry did not become commercially important until 1923, when the Ancon field was discovered on the Santa Elena Peninsula. All productive wells now in operation are located on this peninsula, where some 283,000 acres of land were under production in March 1957.

Extensive concessions have been granted by the Government for exploration and development in other parts of the country.

1950 production. Commercial gasoline, fuel oil, and diesel oil in 1956 accounted for 88 percent of the total output of refined products. The extraction of gasoline from natural gas increased 29 percent between 1952, when production began, and 1955, but in 1956 declined slightly. 

National production includes most types of refined products with the major exceptions of highoctane aviation gasoline and high-quality lubricating oils and greases. However, in 1955 the largest refinery in the country produced some jet plane fuel, which was actually kerosene of a narrow boiling range.

Local refining facilities are inadequate to process the volume of local crude production and to satisfy domestic requirements for refined products. 

In 1954-56, the volume of local crude refined in 

Ecuador amounted to 250.5 million gallons,¹ or 

59 percent of the crude produced during the period. 

Annual averages in the 3-year period were 141.4 million gallons of crude produced and 83.5 million gallons refined. Consequently, a large part of the domestic crude is exported. 

Exports. In 1954-56, exports of crude amounted to 155.8 million gallons, an average of slightly over 51.9 million gallons per year, or 37 percent of production.2 In 1955, exports totaled 61.9 million gallons, the largest volume since 1948; but in 

1956, export shipments declined by 40 percent to 37 million gallons as foreign sales fell off and local production dropped by about 3 percent. 

Exports of crude in 1954-56 averaged $1.4 million a year, and for many years have represented a significant source of foreign exchange to the producers. The Ecuadoran Government also exports crude which it receives as royalty from the two principal producers, the Anglo-Ecuadorian 

Oilfields, Ltd., and the Manabi Exploration Co.³

Domestic consumption.-Domestic requirements have increased greatly in recent years (see table 30), necessitating substantial imports above local refining capacity. 

Between 1950 and 1956, the consumption of gasoline rose 106 percent while production increased 25 percent. In the case of diesel oil and residual fuel oil, however, production increased at a faster rate than consumption. In 1950-56, the production of diesel oil rose 522 percent as against a rise of 115 percent in consumption. In

Imports.-Imports of petroleum and derivatives increased 800 percent in volume (from 10,000 to 91,000 tons) between 1950 and 1956, and the value of imports in 1956 amounted to $4.6 million. 

Gasoline is the principal petroleum import, followed by lubricants, fuel oil, and diesel oil. In 

1954-56, imports of gasoline averaged 49,000 tons a year and over $2 million in foreign exchange. 

Some 57,000 tons valued at $2.4 million were imported in 1956. All requirements of aviation gasoline are imported. The International Petroleum Co., a subsidiary of the Standard Oil Co. of New Jersey, maintains offices in Guayaquil and imports aviation gasoline from its plant in Talara, Peru, for distribution to airlines operating in

Ecuador. Imports of ordinary gasoline are authorized by the Government and up to the present, at least, have been handled and distributed by the 

Anglo-Ecuadorian Oilfields, Ltd. 

Lubricants imported in 1954-56 averaged 6,600 tons a year valued at $1 million. Imports in 1956 amounted to 4,800 tons with a value of $1.1 million. Diesel and fuel oil imports in 1954-56 aver aged 15,700 tons a year valued at $342,300, and in 1956 totaled 21,000 tons with a value of $533,000.

By far the largest of the producers, Anglo- 

Ecuadorian Oilfields, Ltd., is controlled by British and United States interests, with 70 percent of the company's stock equally divided between the 

British-United States firm, Lobitos Oilfields, Ltd., and the United States firm, the South American 

Gold and Platinum Co. Anglo-Ecuadorian Oil fields, Ltd., operates at La Libertad a refinery with a daily maximum capacity of about 5,600 barrels of crude, 4 small units for subsidiary products (lubricating oils, heavy oils, and asphalt) with a combined handling capacity of 2,500 gallons, and 3 plants for extracting gasoline from natural gas with an output in 1955 of 6.5 million gallons of natural gasoline.

Under a new concession contract, which the company negotiated with the Ecuadoran Government in January 1956, Anglo-Ecuadorian Oilfields, Ltd., agreed to build a new refinery within 

30 months. The refinery, which will be an atmospheric distillation plant, will be constructed near the present plant in La Libertad and will have an initial capacity per day of 6,000 barrels with provision for capacity expansion to 10,000 barrels. 

The company expects that the new plant will enable the national industry to meet all domestic requirements for refined products except aviation gasoline and high-quality lubricants, but anticipates that imports of crude will be necessary for plant operations.

In 1955, Anglo-Ecuadorian Oilfields, Ltd., had 

1,303 wells in production, and the average daily output per well was 5.27 barrels as compared with an average of 5.68 barrels in 1954. The company placed 140 new wells in production in 1955 and drilled 18 exploratory wells of which 8 turned out to be dry. 

Second in size among the producers, the Manabi 

Exploration Co. is owned by United States interests and controlled by the Santiago Petroleum

Corp. Manabi operates at its Tigre field, Guayas 

Province, a topping plant with a daily capacity of 450 barrels of crude and an extraction plant which in 1955 produced 437,000 gallons of gasoline from natural gas. The company had some 125 wells in production at the close of 1955, and during that year made 2 important strikes with initial daily flows of 180 and 260 barrels. Toward the close of 1956, the company started exploratory drilling near Manta, where it holds a concession. 

Up to mid-1957, production by Manabi had come entirely from its holdings on the Santa Elena Peninsula. Manabi owns a distributing company, Manaoil Distribuidora del Ecuador, which it organized in 1955 to handle its own products and 

Texaco lubricants.

The three other producers are small companies. 

Two are subsidiaries of Anglo-Ecuadorian Oilfields, Ltd. They are Carolina Oil Co. and Petropolis Oil Co. The third company, Concepcion 

Ecuadorian Oilfields, Ltd., is a privately owned 

Ecuadoran enterprise. None of these three companies are equipped with refining facilities. In 

1955, the Carolina Oil Co. installed an extraction plant, which produced 369,000 gallons of gasoline from natural gas during the year.

In addition to the producing companies, there was one other petroleum company in active operation in Ecuador in mid-1957, the California- 

Ecuador Petroleum Co., a subsidiary of the Standard Oil Company of California. This company in 1953 was granted large concessions in the Provinces of El Oro, Guayas, Manabi, and Esmeraldas, and while it has not started to produce petroleum, it has large-scale exploration under way. 

All but two of the other concessions in April 1957 were in the process of being approved by the Government. The largest concession is that of the Leonard Exploration Company of Ecuador, which is Canadian owned. The Leonard concession includes a very large part of the Oriente. 

Preceding concessions in the Oriente were granted to the Shell Oil Co. in 1938 and jointly to Shell and the Esso Standard Oil Co. in 1948, but explorations conducted by these companies uncovered no petroleum deposits of commercial value.

Their explorations were abandoned. Some of the petroleum concessions include offshore lands along the full length of the Ecuadoran coast line. The 

California-Ecuador Petroleum Co. holds concessions in the Morro Channel, off the southern shore of the Santa Elena Peninsula, which it reportedly regards as promising. 

Distribution of petroleum products.-Petroleum products are distributed internally by ocean tankers in combination with highway tank trucks, railway tank cars, and/or pipeline. All of the producing companies are equipped with field pipelines 

The Santiago Petroleum Corp. is owned by a United_States group which controls the Pan-Israel Oil Co. and Israel-Mediterranean Petroleum, Inc. 61

to deliver crude to the refineries. Anglo-Ecuadorian Oilfields, Ltd., operates a fleet of ocean tankers and highway tank trucks to transport petroleum products and also maintains a fleet of railway tank cars that are operated over the state railways. The company maintains storage plants in all of the major cities and operates retail gasoline stations. 

Manabi Exploration Co. operates an ocean tanker and through its wholly owned subsidiary, Manaoil, has organized a distributive system for marketing its products. 

In October 1954, a 6-inch pipeline was placed in service between Bucay, Guayas, and Palmira,

Chimborazo. Built at a cost of approximately 27 million sucres ($1.8 million) by a U. S. firm, J. A. Jones Construction Co., the 45-mile-long pipeline carries gasoline and can also transport diesel oil. Shipments from Guayaquil to the

Sierra now move by rail to Bucay, by pipeline to 

Palmira, and by rail beyond. The Government plans to extend the existing pipeline to connect 

Guayaquil and Quito.

Petroleum industry in Ecuadoran economy. The petroleum industry is significant to the Ecuadoran economy. In 1955, public receipts from taxes on the industry amounted to over 107 million sucres (more than $7 million), and some 2,400 persons were employed by the 5 producing companies. In the absence of important discoveries of new reserves, however, the industry has grown little in recent years; producers have been reluctant to invest in additional refining and operating facilities in the face of the possibility of a decline in petroleum production. Moreover, the industry reportedly is discouraged by the allegedly nonremunerative level of Government-fixed prices on petroleum products. Sales of imported gasoline, for example, have long been fixed at prices below the international market price, and the oil companies have had to absorb the loss with earnings from crude exports. 

Other Minerals 

Other minerals produced on a commercial scale in Ecuador include gold, sulfur, and salt. Silver, copper, and lead are byproducts of gold mining (see table 32). 

Gold.-Gold is mined by the Compania Industrial Minera Asociada (CIMA), at Portovelo,

El Oro. Between 1895 and 1950, a U. S. firm, 

South American Development Co., worked the

Portovelo gold mines but finally abandoned the operation because of reduced yields and rising costs. The Ecuadoran Government purchased the company's equipment and sponsored the formation of CIMA to continue working the mines. 

CIMA was legally organized in January 1952, and shares of the company were divided among the municipality of Zaruma, El Oro (35 percent), the management group composed of private individuals (35 percent), and the miners and workers (30 percent). The miners' and workers' share was donated by the Central Government. 

The long-worked Portovelo mines are becoming exhausted, and gold production has declined sharply since the 1940's. In 1955, CIMA proc essed 54,000 tons of quartz with an average yield per ton of 0.2725 ounces of gold of which 91.84 percent was recovered. At the close of 1955, ore reserves were estimated at 96,500 tons. The company is undertaking exploratory work in adjoining areas, but to date no deposits of commercial importance have been found.

A small amount of gold also is produced by prospectors in placer mining. Production in 1955 and 1956, however, amounted to only 1,449 and 

491 ounces, respectively.

CIMA accounts for virtually the entire national output of silver, copper, and lead, which are sub-

Gold and allied metals have been found in other parts of Ecuador-in the Provinces of Esmeraldas, Chimborazo, Cotopaxi, Cañar, Azuay, and in the Oriente but to date no mining enterprises of importance have been established to work the deposits.

Sulfur. Sulfur was produced until recently by the American-owned Ecuadoran Mining Co., S. A

The company was granted a 20-year concession in 1951 to work the Shucos sulfur deposit near 

Tixan, Chimborazo, and started operations in 1952. The company operated only intermittently, however, and suffered financial reverses because of high operating costs and attendant difficulties in competing in the world market.

Success of such an undertaking depends on the world market, for domestic demands are insufficient to support the enterprise. It finally ceased operations altogether, and the Government revoked the company's concession in 1957. 

Other sulfur deposits exist in Ecuador, particularly in the Galapagos Islands and in the Prov inces of Carchi, Cotopaxi, and Chimborazo, but 62

Minerals and Power their geologic characteristics and economic possibilities have not been fully determined. 

Salt. Salt production is controlled by the Salt

Monoply (Estanco de Sal) of the Bureau of 

Monopolies (Direccion General de Estancos), which purchases the entire output, processes it, and distributes it at fixed prices. The important salt deposits are located on the seashore in the Province of Guayas, and the principal pits are in the community of Salinas, where the Salt Monopoly operates a refinery. In 1956, the production of refined salt amounted to 2,200 tons. Iodized and highly refined salt are imported. Imports of salt in 1955 and 1956, however, aggregated only 

19 and 17 tons, respectively. 

Minor minerals. Other mineral production is of little importance. In the Province of Guayas, the Canteras Nacionales, S. A., an affiliate of La

Cemento Nacional, C. A., and a subsidiary of the 

Ecuadorian Corporation, Ltd., produces some gypsum in its quarrying operations. Small amounts of gypsum also are produced in the Province of Azuay. Kaolin (china clay) and lignite are produced in small quantities on an intermittent basis in the Provinces of Chimborazo and Cañar, respectively, and a small amount of iron oxide is extracted in the Province of Guayas. 

Deposits of a wide variety of other minerals are known to exist. Near Biblian, Cañar, coal deposits with an estimated yield of 1 million tons have been explored, and some preliminary steps have been taken to form an enterprise to extract the coal. 

In 1956, a rich deposit of coal reportedly was discovered in the Province of Esmeraldas. Manganese deposits are found in the Province of El Oro, and a Chilean company, Compania Salitrera de 

Tarapaca y Antofogasta, is surveying the deposits with a view to establishing mining operations. 

Iron ore deposits have been found in the Provinces of Guayas and Manabi, and copper in the Provinces of Pichincha and Cotopaxi and in other areas. The Minas Nacionales, C. A., is now exploring the La Plata zone in the Province of Pichincha, where important minerals with a reportedly high copper content have been discovered.

Other minerals that have been located and that might be found to exist in significant quantities through further exploration include the following, by Province and/or region: Magnesium (Chimborazo), cinnabar (Pichincha), mica (Tungurahua), platinum (Esmeraldas and the Oriente), quartz crystal (Imbabura and Loja), zinc (Pi chincha and the Oriente), titanium (the Costa), antimony (Loja), beryl (Azuay), graphite (Tungurahua, Cañar, and Oriente), silver (Pichincha), and lead (Manabi, Guayas, and other Provinces).

Reports of deposits of radioactive minerals, principally uranium, led to the establishment in December 1949 of a national land reserve of about 5,000 square kilometers in an area largely within the Province of Cotopaxi. However, no significant finds of radioactive minerals have been made.

Ecuador suffers from a serious shortage of electric power, even though generating capacity has increased by over 40 percent since 1950. As of

Power production in 1955 amounted to 240.6 million kilowatt-hours (kw.-hr.). In terms of per capita averages, there were 17.8 kw. installed and

Plant Capacity 

Thermal units in 1955 represented 60.5 percent of the total plant capacity as compared with 39.5 percent for hydroelectic units and accounted for 

51.4 percent of total production as against 48.6 percent for hydroelectric units. About 95 percent of the installed thermal capacity was in the Costa, and about the same proportion of the installed hydroelectric capacity was in the Sierra. 

Total installed capacity in the Costa amounted to 25.6 kw. per inhabitant as against 12.5 kw. in the Sierra, and production in the Costa averaged 

5 Junta Nacional de Planificacion y Coordinacion Economica, 

Informe Anual, 1955-1956, Quito, page 142; Banco Central del 

Ecuador, Memoria, 1955, Quito, page 25. 63

kw.-hr. per capita as compared with 57.7 kw.hr. in the Sierra.

The Provinces of Guayas and Pichincha accounted for 67 percent of the total installed capacity and 78 percent of the total power generated. 

However, only 19 percent of the plants were located in these 2 Provinces, a fact indicative of the sizable power installations in Guayaquil and

Quito as compared with the rest of the country. 

The average size of the powerplants in the Provinces of Guayas and Pichincha was 351 kw. per plant as against 42 kw. for all other installations in Ecuador.

Electric Service 

6

Only about 15 percent of the population is serviced by electricity. All of the principal cities and many of the towns are equipped with electricpower systems, but in the rural areas there are no public facilities. Most of the equipment in public service is old and in poor condition, and transmission systems are generally inadequate and overloaded. In Quito, for example, overloading caused one of the largest generators to burn out in March 1957, temporarily depriving the city of about 20 percent of its power supply. Voltages are subject to sharp variations, and service, in general, is unreliable. In most of the towns, service is limited largely to the night hours and current is supplied for only a few hours during the day. In the cities, service is normally provided on a 24-hour basis, although rationing is sometimes necessitated during plant failures. For example, power was rationed in Quito in March 

1957. Alternating current, at 110/220 volts, 3 phase, 60 cycles, is distributed by the public-service plants in most leading cities. Guayaquil is supplied with both alternating current (single phase) and direct current (220 volts), while Quito is supplied with alternating current (single phase and 3 phase) at 110 volts. 

Residential and commercial use and public lighting accounted for 49 percent of the electric power consumed in 1955. Other community requirements and Government needs took 3 percent, and about 18 percent was lost or illegally diverted in transmission. The remaining 30 percent (71.8 million kw.-hr.) was consumed by industry. Public service plants provided 62 percent of the power for industrial consumption, and 38 percent was supplied by private plants. The lack or insufficiency of public power has handicapped industrial development, and many industries have had to install their own generating plants. At the end of 1955, powerplants of 100 kw. or over that were operated for private use represented 23 percent of the total installed capacity in Ecuador.

Junta Nacional de Planificacion y Coordinacion Economica, 

Informe Anual, 1955-1956, Quito, page 266.

Electric Power Production and Rates 

Production. Despite the national shortage in generating capacity, electric power production has increased substantially as new or improved facilities have been placed in service. In 1950-56, the annual rate of increase was 19.1 percent, and output rose from 123.2 million kw.-hr. in 1950 to an estimated 264.4 million kw.-hr. in 1956. The increase in production has been unevenly distributed; the five principal cities account for over four-fifths of the electric power generated in 

Ecuador (see table 34).

Production of electric power in the 5 cities increased 115 percent between 1950 and 1956; however, Guayaquil and Quito accounted for 93 percent of the increase for the group. Guayaquil boosted its output 158 percent, and Quito raised its production 93 percent. Increases in the 3 other cities were as follows: Ambato, 107 percent; Riobamba, 56 percent; and Cuenca, 30 percent.

The largest electric-power company in the country is the Ecuadoran subsidiary of the American and Foreign Power Company, Inc., the Empresa Electrica del Ecuador, Inc., which serves 

Guayaquil and Riobamba. The company's Guay aquil facilities total 23,735 kw. and include a new steam-electric station containing two 5,000-kw. units, the first of which was placed in service in 

1955 and the second, in January 1957. An additional 10,000-kw. unit is on order and is expected to be in operation in 1959. In Riobamba, the company operates 2 hydroelectric stations with a combined installed capacity of 1,130 kw. 

Company sales of electricity rose from 76.6 million kw.-hr. in 1955 to 85 million kw.-hr. in 1956, and gross operating revenues increased from 48.6 million to 55.1 million sucres ($3.2 million to $3.6 million). The number of customers served increased from 37,103 at the end of 1955 to 40,839 at the end of 1956."

Second in size and importance is the Empresa 

Electrica Quito, S. A., a municipal enterprise that 

7 American and Foreign Power Company, Inc., Thirty-Third 

Annual Report-1956, New York, N. Y., page 18. 64

Minerals and Power de is the sole supplier of electric power in the Quito area. In early 1956, the company operated 3 hydroelectric stations with a combined capacity of 12,080 kw. In March 1956, the company was granted a loan of $5 million by the World Bank to help finance hydroelectric and thermal power projects originally designed to add 18,980 kw. to the company's system. A U. S. engineering firm, R. J. Tipton Associated Engineers, Denver, Colorado, was awarded a contract in 1956 to supervise installation of a 14,480-kw. hydroelectric plant at Cunucyacu, Pichincha, on the San Pedro River, about 6 miles southeast of Quito. Plans also contemplated installation of a 3,000-kw. thermal plant in Quito, replacement of the older of the 2 distribution systems, and enlargement of substation capacity. Increasing costs, however, together with important technical revisions, - brought about significant changes in the initially approved plans. The project as finally approved calls for a 20,000-kw. plant at Cumbaya, Pichincha, a small town situated a few miles northeast of Quito, with construction scheduled to begin in early 1958. The approved project also contemplates the possibility of installing an additional 

20,000 kw. in the future. Meanwhile, two 500-kw. units have been installed in Quito, and in June 1957, 6 diesel generators with a combined capacity of 1,400 kw. were placed in operation as a partial replacement for the 2,000-kw. generator that burned out in March 1957.

The facilities of the Empresa Electrica del 

Ecuador, Inc., and the Empresa Electrica Quito, S. A., account for over 60 percent of the installed capacity of all public-service power stations in operation. Among the other installations, the largest and the newest are the following hydroelectric plants located in or near the indicated cities: Miraflores (Cuenca), 2,500 kw.; Alao (Riobamba), 5,000 kw.; and Illuchi (Latacunga), 1,400 kw. Both the Miraflores and Alao plants are intended to provide regional service embracing several communities and are to be expanded. 

The Illuchi plant is designed to serve the community and industrial requirements of the city of Latacunga and its environs.

Most of the other public-service plants vary in capacity from 100 to 600 kw. Two of the most efficient of these plants, although far from adequate to meet requirements, are the installations in Ambato and Ibarra. Each is equipped with only 600 kw. and urgently needs to expand its generating capacity and to improve its transmission system. In the great majority of the other population centers, existing power facilities are adequate to supply only a small part of the publiclighting requirements. 

Rates.-Electric-power rates vary greatly throughout the country. In 1955, the average rate per kilowatt-hour ranged from a low of 0.16 sucre 470366-59- -6 (1.06 cents) in the Province of Cotopaxi to a high of 1.91 sucres (12.6 cents) in the Province of Los

Rios. In general, rates are higher in the Costa than in the Sierra, amounting in 1955 to an average of about 0.66 sucre (4.4 cents) per kilowatthour in the former as compared with 0.37 sucre (2.4 cents) in the latter. 

Investment in Electric Power

Because of the generally low rate schedules in force in relation to operating costs, private investment in the electric-power industry has not been stimulated. There is only one important privately owned power company in the country-the Empresa Electrica del Ecuador, Inc. The public powerplants, with a few exceptions, are municipally owned and operated. In recent years, some mixed companies composed of municipalities and public or semipublic entities, such as the Public 

Assistance Board (Junta de Asistencia Publica), National Development Bank, or the National Wel fare Institute, have been organized and now own and operate such plants as the Miraflores in Cuenca, the Zamora in Loja, and the Empresa Electrica in Quito. The formation of mixed companies, with their larger resources, is regarded as an important step in the development of electric power and industrialization in Ecuador. The pooling of funds by two or more municipalities, together with the contributions of Government agencies, is envisaged as a means of halting the proliferation of isolated, small, high-cost community installations and of developing power production and distribution on a regional and interregional basis. At the same time, the limitations of this approach are widely recognized, and the importance of making the power industry more attractive to private investment has been pointed out by the National 

Board of Economic Planning and Coordination.8 National Electrification Plan 

Government planning for power development is based largely on a national electrification plan drawn up in 1953 by the United Nations. Under this plan, the installation of some 62,000 kw. is projected over 5 years, to be followed by installations totaling some 75,000 kw. over a second 5-year period. The Board of Economic Planning and 

Coordination, which, it is reasonable to expect, will also play an important part in official planning, recommends the installation of some 

83,400 kw. in 1956-60. The proportions of these plans render them improbable of fulfillment with- 

8 Junta Nacional de Planificacion y Coordinacion Economica, 

Informe Anual, 1955-1956, Quito, 1956, page 155. 65

out large foreign credits. In 1950-54, public investments in electric power averaged the equivalent of approximately $580,000 per year. A large part of this outlay has been allocated to the development of hydroelectric power facilities in the Sierra.

Greater use of waterpower resources is contemplated in all developmental plans. Present dependence on small thermal units entails an annual outlay of about $1 million a year for fuels, which in increasing amounts must be imported to meet domestic requirements. The rivers and streams of Ecuador have been estimated to supply a hydroelectric potential of 1,300,000 horsepower, equivalent to 969,150 kw. Only a small part of this estimated potential has been harnessed.

CHAPTER VIII 

Manufacturing and

Construction 

Ecuador has a predominantly agricultural economy, but in recent years efforts have been intensified to expand existing industrial plants and to introduce new industries. In 1950-56, the value of manufacturing production, at current prices, increased 59 percent while national income rose 

56 percent. Industry accounted for 16.5 percent of the national income in 1956, and its average yearly participation in 1950-56 was 16.2 percent.¹

The industrial directory of Ecuador for 1955 listed 18,982 establishments, which had fixed assets of 1,025 million sucres ($67.7 million), 60,369 operatives, and 4,805 salaried employees in December 1955.2 The directory reported the value of production in two columns. The first column included the "large" firms that had bookkeeping records and covered the gross value of production for the year 

1955; this column totaled 1,391.8 million sucres ($91.9 million). The second column gave the value of output of "small" firms, firms that lacked adequate records, for the month of December 1955; this column amounted to 39.3 million sucres ($2.6 million). Assuming that the average rate of production by the small firms was the same during the other months, the gross value of industrial production in all plants in 1955 amounted to 1,863 million sucres ($123 million).

Most of the establishments listed in the industrial directory are not factories in the correct sense of the word, but cottage or handicraft units. 

From the 18,982 establishments included in the industrial directory, 955 were selected and were sent questionnaires that sought information for the first industrial census. The criteria used in selecting the plants were: (1) 5 or more persons engaged; (2) 1955 production valued at 100,000 sucres ($6,600) or more; and (3) at least 200,000 sucres ($13,200) of fixed capital. The selected plants accounted for only 5 percent of the establishments in the industrial directory, but for 90 percent of the declared value of production. 

The industrial census did not include construction enterprises or electric powerplants. When, in addition, the extractive phase of the petroleum industry and quarries are eliminated, the main

Foodstuffs accounted for 42 percent of the total gross value of production and for 30 percent of the total value added in production. Other leading industries were textiles, beverages, petroleum derivatives (refining), and chemicals and pharmaceuticals. Combined, these 4 industries accounted for 39 percent of the total gross value of production and 52 percent of the total value added in production.

Productivity 

The technical level of much of Ecuador's industry is low, but there are some modern and efficient plants. A report of the United Nations published in 1951 stated that "at present 6.11 times as many man-hours per kilogram of yarn and 18.56 times as many man-hours per kilogram of fabric are employed as would be necessary if the mills satisfied required standards of modernity, size, organization and quality of cotton, in order to obtain the highest possible yield for the workers." In recent years, however, a considerable number of automatic looms have been imported, factory organization improved, and the quality of the goods raised. 3

Industry in Five Latin American Countries, New York, 1951, page 67.
