Most of the capital in industry is Ecuadoran, but a number of outstanding enterprises have been established by foreign interests, such as the leading brewery, malt works, cement plant, publishing establishment, and cracker factory. There is also foreign capital in a rice mill, ice cream plant, textile mills, and drug plants. Immigrants were active in setting up many of these enterprises. 

Most industrial enterprises, including those controlled by Ecuadoran capital, employ foreign production management. 

Location of Industry 

Manufacturing industries are heavily concentrated in 3 Provinces, Guayas, Pichincha, and 

Manabi, which account for more than 85 percent of the total industrial output of the country. The 

Province of Guayas accounts for about 50 per cent and the Province of Pichincha for about 25 percent. Among the other Provinces, the most highly industralized are Tungurahua, Chimbo razo, and Imbabura. According to the Central

Bank, 78 to 80 percent of new industrial investments in 1955, estimated at some 100 million sucres ($6.6 million), were in the Province of Guayas; 

15 percent were in the Provinces of Azuay and Cañar (following the granting of special tax concessions to new enterprises in those depressed 

Unpublished memorandum, El Primer Censo Industrial de 

Ecuador, by Cesar Molestina O., table 1, page 3. The source does not include fixed assets employed in production by the petroleum industry.

Unpublished memorandum, El Primer Censo Industrial del 

Ecuador, by Cesar Molestina O., page 5. 68

Manufacturing and Construction 

Provinces); and the remainder were in such centers as Quito, Ambato, Riobamba, and Latacunga.

The most important of the industrial centers is Guayaquil. It has had some industry since colonial times, when it became a shipbuilding center for Spanish settlements on the west coast, owing to the availability of timber, a suitable natural port, and waterways leading to the required raw materials. 

In more recent times, new industries have been established to process local materials (rice- and coffee-cleaning mills and sugar grinders) or to supply the local market (bakeries, printshops).

As the principal port, as well as one of the largest markets in the country, Guayaquil was favored as the site for industries using bulky imported raw materials (wheat flour mill) or dependent on large amounts of fuel or power (cement plant). The rapid expansion of Guayaquil's installed electricpower facilities and the progress in highway construction in the Province of Guayas have contributed to Guayaquil's industrial leadership. The spirit of business enterprise for which the city has long been known has also been significant in its industrial development.

In the Province of Manabi, industries are located chiefly around the port of Manta, except such handicrafts as hatmaking, which is centered around Montecristi and Jipijapa, or cheesemaking, which is carried on in many rural communities. 

The industries in Manta process local vegetable 

Foils, rice, and fish.

The Sierra has been the traditional center of the textile industry and of the flour-milling industry. 

Tanning, shoe manufacturing, brewing, cigarette manufacturing, and woodworking have been divided between the Costa and the Sierra. The majority of the factories in the Sierra are located in Quito. Other Sierra centers of industrial importance include Ambato, Riobamba, Cuenca, and Latacunga.

Encouragement to Industrialization 

Ecuador has encouraged industrialization by both domestic and foreign capital. In 1921 there was enacted an industrial protection law which granted tax and customs duties exemptions to approved industries for specified periods. The 

Ecuadoran Government has recently adopted an investment law designed to replace the industrial protection law and to stimulate greater investments compatible with the interests of the country. To facilitate industrial development, the ICA and the Ecuadoran Government early in 1957 entered into tentative arrangements for the establishment of an industrial development board to give assistance to industrial activities. 

Banco Central del Ecuador, Memoria 1955, Quito, page 23. "See appendix A.

In general, industrialization in Ecuador has evolved in the pattern described and endorsed by the National Board of Economic Planning and Coordination:

Industrial development should be considered at the present time as a complementary activity, depending strictly on agricultural-livestock development. For some time its growth must come from an increase in the productivity of agriculture, from an increase in internal demand, and through the processing of agricultural products both for the domestic and foreign markets. In the light of the present economic situation it would be premature and even dangerous to give industrialization an accelerated rhythm. Industrial development will come through the vigorous expansion of the agricultural-livestock sector.

LEADING INDUSTRIES 

Food and Beverages

The food and beverage industry includes a large number and a wide range of enterprises. In some fields of activity-for example, brewing and bottling, flour milling and flour products, the processing of edible fats and oils, and sugar milling-many modern plants are in operation. Most of the establishments, however, are small and rudimentary, and employ a high proportion of manual operational methods. Nevertheless, with the major exceptions of preserved foods and some types of alcoholic beverages, the local industry is able to satisfy most market requirements.

Meat products.-Ecuadorans are not large consumers of meat. According to estimates of the

National Board of Economic Planning and Coor dination, per capita consumption in 1955 amounted to 23.7 pounds, of which 16.3 pounds were beef, 5.1 pounds were pork, and 2.3 pounds were mutton and goat meat. Although prices are controlled, only relatively well-to-do persons can afford to purchase meat through the commercial outlets. In rural areas, many homesteads raise some livestock, particularly hogs, for food, but in the urban centers large numbers of people eat little or no meat because of their inadequate purchasing power.

Most slaughtering performed under official controls is carried on in municipally owned and operated abattoirs. In general, facilities are poor and methods are primitive, with resultant waste of many of the valuable byproducts, such as dried blood and offal. The slaughterhouses have no refrigerated storage facilities, and, consequently, operate on a day-to-day basis, distributing their products on the same day that they are processed. 

This situation gives rise to fluctuating periods of abundance and scarcity. Meat is usually consumed within 24 hours after slaughter. 

8 Junta Nacional de Planificacion y Coordinacion Economica, 

Informe Anual, 1955-1956, Quito, 1956, page 47. 69

In addition to the controlled slaughterhouses, there are many small unregulated slaughterhouses operating throughout the country. They serve their immediate market, and they operate with a minimum of equipment. Their output, added to that of clandestine and farm slaughter, constitutes a substantial part of national meat production, probably as much as 20 or 25 percent. 

Estimates of livestock slaughter, inclusive of unregistered and clandestine operations, for 1953-55, indicate an annual kill of about 732,000 animals, of which approximately 70 percent were equally divided between hogs and sheep, 25 percent were cattle, and 5 percent were goats."

Slaughter data for 1955, based on the best available estimates, show a kill of 775,561 animals distributed as follows: Cattle, 188,768; sheep, 267,946; hogs, 280,847; and goats, 38,000.

Meat production, based on yields deduced from slaughter estimates, is shown in table 37 for 1953-55.

Little high-quality meat, especially beef, is available. Pork is obtainable in adequate amounts throughout most of the country, but most of the beef and virtually all of the mutton are produced in the Sierra. 

There is no meat-canning industry in Ecuador. 

Sausage-type meats and cured and smoked meats are produced in limited quantities in a few small commercial establishments, the largest of which are located in Quito, Ambato, and Guayaquil, but the bulk of production is carried on in small markets and on farms, and the quality of the products is generally poor. National production in 1955 is believed to have approximated 190,000 pounds. Imports of bacon, ham, salt pork, sausages of all kinds, tinned meat, and meat extracts in 1955 were valued at slightly over $19,300. 

Poultry and eggs.-The production and marketing of poultry and eggs on a commercial scale is not well developed, although considerable progress is being made on a broiler industry. The Veterinary Department of the University of Guayaquil, in conjunction with a mission from the University of Idaho, initiated in 1956 a project to give practical instruction to local farmers and students in raising broilers, and the program has proved to be successful. • Estimates of livestock slaughter calculated by the Agricultural 

Attache, American Embassy, Quito, Ecuador.

The first commercial enterprise of importance in the poultry business, Empresa Avicola Guayas, C. A., was organized in Guayaquil in 1949 with the financial aid of the former Development Corporation (Corporacion de Fomento de la Produccion). To date, however, poultry in the commercial market has been scarce, poor in quality, and very high in price.

Dairy products.-There are 3 milk-pasteurizing plants in Ecuador, 2 in Guayaquil, and 1 in Quito, with a combined daily capacity of not more than 

35,000 liters (1 liter equals 1.0567 liquid quarts) of milk. The plant in Quito has a daily capacity of about 10,000 liters (equal to about one-sixth of the city's average daily milk supply), but in early 1957 was operating at only 60 percent of capacity. 

A new pasteurizing plant, with a maximum capac ity of 45,000 liters in an 8-hour run, was under construction in Quito in mid-1957 with completion expected before the end of the year.

The pasteurizing plants produce plain and chocolate milk and some butter. Neither evaporated nor condensed milk is produced in Ecuador. An estimated 50,000 to 60,000 liters of milk per day are used for manufacturing purposes in the Sierra, an amount equivalent to about 7 percent of the total national output of fluid milk. About half of the milk used in manufacturing is consumed in the preparation of whole milk powder, and the other half is used in making butter and cheese. The most important and only milk-powder producer of substantial size in the country is the Miraflores plant in Cayambe, which was completed in 1955 and has an annual capacity of 1.5 million liters of powdered milk. In early 1957 the plant was oper ating at only about one-fifth of capacity, principally because of the competition of imported powdered milk and regionally produced fluid milk in the Costa market. Powdered milk that is manufactured in the Sierra and marketed in the Costa is as expensive as fresh milk. 

The bulk of the butter and all of the cheese are farm produced. Raw milk is used in making these products. The principal areas of produc tion are the Cayambe and Machachi Valleys, located north and south, respectively, of Quito. 

Many of the farms specialize in certain types of cheeses, with European types and cooked cheese of the cottage cheese variety constituting the bulk of production. A small amount of the Roquefort type is produced, particularly in the Province of 

Manabi in the Costa. 

Factory production of ice cream is centered principally in Guayaquil, where there are three or four small manufacturing plants, one of which is owned by a United States citizen. Quito also has one or two small ice cream plants. In the rest of the country there is no important factory produc tion, and hotels, restaurants, and other purveyors prepare their own supplies, mostly with imported 70

Manufacturing and Construction ingredients. Guayaquil is also the site of the largest commercial ice plant in the country, the Compania Frigorifica del Guayas, owned by the 

Ecuadorian Corporation, Ltd.

To overcome the deficiency in nationally produced dairy products, Ecuador in 1950-56 imported an average of more than 3 million pounds of dairy products annually, of which about 80 percent was powered milk from the United States. 

Preserved foods. Although Ecuador produces a wide variety of Temperate Zone and tropical fruits and vegetables, the preparation of preserves on a commercial scale is not well developed. Four or five small plants, located principally in Quito and Guayaquil, produce a limited and assorted volume of jams, jellies, fruit juices, tomato catsup, and a few other items. One of the largest of these plants is the Compania General de Frutas, in 

Quito, which also bottles some imported wines and liquors. The annual average output of this plant approximates about 300,000 sucres ($19,800) in value.

Canned foodstuffs are preponderantly an im- I port item that is retailed at high prices and consumed by well-to-do persons. Imports of canned fruits, fruit juices, jellies, marmalade, fruit pulp and paste, canned vegetables, vegetable juices, and soups in 1955 amounted to slightly over 650 tons = valued at $175,800.

Flour.-Twenty mills produce wheat flour and, in some cases, also process corn, barley, peas, and :beans. All but two of the mills are located in the Sierra, but the largest and newest of the mills, Harinas del Ecuador, C. A., which was opened in 1952, is situated in Guayaquil. The 20 mills have a total daily milling capacity of 506 tons of wheat flour; the Sierra mills have a capacity of 

336 tons and the 2 Costa mills, 170 tons. Harinas del Ecuador, C. A., is reported to have an annual capacity of 22,000 tons, equivalent to approximately 30 percent of the nation's capacity.

Until recent years, the mills operated at well below capacity because of (1) insufficient supplies of domestic wheat and (2) heavy imports of Canadian and United States wheat flour, principally for Costa consumption. Beginning in 

1953, the Government took steps to protect and promote the domestic wheat-milling and wheatraising industries. Through a succession of Government and industry agreements, millers agreed to purchase national wheat at prices and in quantities fixed by the Government. In January 

1954, the import duty on wheat flour was substantially increased, and imports were restricted to those authorized by special permits. As a result of the latter measure, imports of wheat flour declined from 28,260 tons in 1952 to 79 tons in 1955. 

On the other hand, estimated national wheatflour production increased from 24,200 tons in 1952 to 68,399 tons in 1955, according to data of the Central Bank of Ecuador. 

Increased milling activity has necessitated increased imports of wheat, for domestic production presently satisfies only about 40 percent of requirements. Between 1952 and 1955, wheat imports increased from 15,491 to 59,897 tons.

Imported wheat is blended with the domestic product, and, according to many commercial consumers, the flour produced is below the quality of imported flour. Most of the imported wheat is milled in the Costa, where no wheat is grown. 

Reportedly, little has been done to develop a market for the byproducts of the milling operations. 

Rice milling is also an important industry.

The mills are concentrated in the Costa Provinces of Guayas, Los Rios, and Manabi, where 95 percent of the rice is produced, as well as in the 

Provinces of Cañar and El Oro. The industry comprises 46 public mills, which purchase and process deliveries from all producers, and some 304 private mills. Most of the mills are small, old, and inefficient.

The public mills handle about 95 percent of the crop, and the largest of these mills are located in the Province of Guayas. The foremost of these is the Piladora Modelo, which is situated in Guayaquil and has a monthly milling capacity of 2,070 tons. The Piladora Modelo, which was opened in 1936 under private ownership, has been owned and operated by the Provincial Development Bank (Banco Provincial de Fomento) since 1950.

Next to the Piladora Modelo, the largest mills in the country are the privately owned Piladora Guayaquil and Piladora Ecuador, both located in Guayaquil, and the Piladora Sante Fe, located in Tenguel, Guayas, and owned by the United Fruit 

Co.'s subsidiary, Compania Bananera del Ecuador.

Each of these mills is engaged in public milling and has a monthly capacity of 1,380 tons.

In 1952-56, hulled rice produced by the mills for commercial distribution averaged 69,000 tons a year. An appreciable but indeterminate quantity of rice is processed by handmills and never enters commercial channels. Production has consistently exceeded domestic requirements, which in 1952-56 averaged about 41,500 tons per year, providing an annual surplus for export. During and directly after World War II, and later during the conflict in Korea, the Ecuadoran ricegrowers and rice millers enjoyed a prosperous export trade. With the recovery of Far Eastern sources of supply, however, Ecuador has been unable to compete successfully in price and quality in world rice markets, and the export trade has declined. The decline has followed an irregular pattern, however, and in 1957 exports were the largest in 4 years. 

Flour products.-Ecuadorans bake their own bread and cakes, which are made largely of barley and corn flours in the Sierra and of rice flour in 71

the Costa. The commercial production of wheatflour products is limited to the leading cities. 

In addition to many small bakeries that supply urban neighborhoods, 3 factory-type bakeries have some commercial importance-1 in Quito and 

2 in Guayaquil. One of these establishments is the Guayaquil firm, Panificadora Automatico de

Nascer, S. A., which produces bread and rolls. 

The plant is equipped with up-to-date machinery. 

Crackers are a popular bakery product, especially in the Costa, where, because of the humid climate, it is difficult to preserve bread. Guayaquil is the center of commercial production of this item, and the principal manufacturer is the Fabrica La Universal, which produces a quality line of both crackers and cookies. The company uses flour milled from imported wheat by Harinas del Ecuador, and imports its requirements of glucose and fats.

One of the leading manufacturing establishments in the country, Fabrica La Universal, was founded by Italians and has capital and reserves in excess of 22 million sucres (nearly $1.5 million). In addition to crackers and cookies, the plant produces flour pastes for spaghetti, macaroni, and noodles; it makes hard candies, chocolates, cocoa, and cocoa butter. Fabrica La

Universal has a modern plant, parts of which are air conditioned. The various departments of the plant are well equipped with machinery of British, Italian, German, and American manufacture. 

In 1954, sales of the company amounted to about 

36 million sucres ($2.4 million), and its market was fairly evenly divided between the Guayaquil area and the rest of the country. The company probably accounts for about 70 percent of the domestic sales of candies, chocolates, cocoa, cookies, and crackers. In addition, it sells about 

20 percent of the flour pastes.

Flour pastes are produced by many other establishments besides the Fabrica La Universal. In general, however, they are small enterprises. The total commercial output of flour pastes is not large in relation to apparent market potential. 

Sugar. The production of white sugar has been substantially increased to meet rapidly rising demands. In 1956, sugar production amounted to 

73,700 tons, the highest in history; and in 1952-56, annual production averaged 61,100 tons, an amount nearly 3 times the average oputput in the years immediately preceding World War II.

Two mills process 85 to 90 percent of the refined sugar, the Sociedad Agricola e Industrial, C. A., and the Compania Azucarera Valdez, S. A., both of which are located in the Province of 

Guayas about 30 or 35 miles east of Guayaquil.

The former mill alone produces about 60 percent of the national output. Several small mills are located in the Provinces of Guayas and Los Rios. 

All of the mills both raise and purchase sugarcane and, in most cases, own large parcels of uncultivated land that can be placed in production. 

Although the majority of the mills are rudimentary installations, the two leading refineries are well equipped and well operated. With the help of a loan from the Eximbank, a loan of $310,000, the Sociedad Agricola e Industrial has modernized its plant and installed automatic machinery of the latest type. Similarly, the 

Azucarera Valdez has carried out important improvements; in 1953, it invested some 8.3 million sucres ($548,000) on machinery, construction, and repairs. Each of these mills maintains its own network of distribution facilities and wholesale outlets throughout the country.

The combined refining capacity of the existing mills is considered adequate to meet domestic requirements, but the industry has been harassed by crop and market uncertainties. From time to time crop disasters or unexpected increases in local market demands have reduced or exceeded mill production, and sugar imports have been required. 

Because of disastrous weather conditions in 1953 and 1954, for example, substantial imports of sugar were needed in 1954 and 1955. At the end of the 1956 grinding season, however, the mills held surplus stocks that enabled the Government to grant permits for the exportation of 1,840 tons of sugar to Chile. 

Ecuadoran sugar is not refined to United States standards. It is a comparatively coarse-grained product known locally as plantation white and in quality is slightly below the standard of refined sugar marketed in the United States. It is produced by the centrifugal process and bleached by sulphitation. In the main, Ecuadorans prefer the locally produced sugar to the more highly refined imported sugar. Because of its present quality characteristics and the high cost of production, however, Ecuadoran sugar, when available for export, has not been able to compete in foreign markets. 

White sugar is a rare or unknown commodity to many Ecuadorans, particularly in the Sierra. 

They use raw sugar, known locally as panela or raspadura, a primitively processed brown sugar.

Panela is produced in many handmills throughout the country, and annual production in recent years has amounted to about 24,000 tons. 

Confectionaries.-The confectionary industry is composed preponderantly of small shops that produce a limited variety of candies for distribution within a very restricted area. There are only two modern, factory-type establishments of significant industrial importance: Fabrica La 

Universal, the principal producer, and Fabrica Bios, a Quito firm. The former, which probably accounts for 60 percent of the sales of nationally made candies, is large, well-equipped, and efficient, It produces a wide variety of hard candies and 72

Manufacturing and Construction chocolates, which are attractively wrapped and packaged. Fabrica Bios is a small establishment with a market largely limited to Quito and the northern Sierra. It produces both high- and medium-quality chocolates and hard candies. 

Imported flavorings and glucose are used by the leading manufacturers. 

Locally made candies share the internal market r with imports, for which there is normally a brisk demand in the so-called quality trade. Imported sweets in 1955 were valued at slightly more than $188,000.

Beverages. Carbonated mineral water and flavored carbonated soft drinks are very popular in Ecuador, particularly in view of the generally unsafe supplies of drinking water. Current data on consumption of these products are not available, but in 1955 annual sales of mineral water were believed to approximate 11.5 million liters. 

Until recent years, at least, mineral water has led flavored soft drinks in sales. The bulk of production is supplied by 18 plants located principally in or near Guayaquil, Quito, and Cuenca.

By far the most important of the mineral-water bottling plants is the Tesalia Springs Co., S. A., which probably produces at least 95 percent of the total commercial output. The firm markets a popular mineral water with natural effervescence under the trade name "Guitig," which is distributed throughout Ecuador. It also produces and markets liquid carbon dioxide and dry ice.

The company operates plants 10lo- cated in Machachi, Pichincha, about 25 miles south of Quito, where there are many mineral springs, and in Guayaquil.

A large number of plants, many of them very small and poorly equipped, manufacture soft drinks with imported syrups, essences, and colorings. Three plants in Guayaquil produce popular American beverages under franchise: Compania Embotelladora del Guayaquil (Coca Cola), Bebidas Gaseosas, S. A. (Pepsi Cola), and the 

Embotelladora Canada Dry del Ecuador (ginger ale and other drinks). Each of these plants is a modern, mechanized mechanized installation. The last named, and the newest, was opened in 1953. 

The products of these three plants account for most of the sales of soft drinks. The output of the other manufacturers of soft drinks consists overwhelmingly of fruit-flavored carbonated drinks which, for the most part, are highly local in appeal and distribution. Domestic production is adequate to meet demands. Imports of nonalcoholic beverages, exclusive of fruit and vegetable juices, amounted to only $1,557 in 1955.

Ecuador has a well-established beer-brewing industry. Production in 1954-56 averaged 56.8 million bottles per year.

There are seven breweries, located in Guayaquil, Quito, Cuenca, and Riobamba. Two of them account for 99 percent of the total output: 

Compania de Cervezas Nacionales, in Guayaquil, and the Cerveceria y Malteria La Victoria, in Quito. The first-named company supplies probably 75 percent of the domestic market, including a large share of the demand in the Sierra. 

The Compania de Cervezas Nacionales is one of the largest and most efficient manufacturing enterprises in Ecuador. It is a subsidiary of the 

Ecuadorian Corporation, Ltd., a holding company organized under the laws of the Bahamas in 1937, that has manifold interests in Ecuador, including the Compania Frigorifica del Guayas. Cervezas 

Nacionales, as it is commonly called, is equipped with the latest type of automatic machinery, including 2 bottling machines with a combined rated capacity of 650 bottles per minute. Until recently, production was based entirely upon the use of imported malt, which produced a very good quality beverage, one that is unobtainable with domestic malt. The company now obtains a part of its requirements of malt from a maltery built in Quito by the parent company. The maltery, known as the Compania Ecuatoriana de Maltas y Cervezas, S. A., was completed in 1955, enlarged in 1957, and is intended ultimately to provide the malt requirements of Cervezas Nacionales. The maltery processes locally grown barley cultivated from imported Chilean barley seed that it has experimented successfully with and that it has distributed to farmers with a guaranty to purchase the crop at a fixed price. 

The Cerveceria y Malteria La Victoria also is a well-equipped and modern installation. Until recently, at least, the brewery was under the direction of a German braumeister, and from time to time it has employed the services of German and United States technicians. To improve the quality of its output, the company has developed its own production of barley based on Danish barley seed. By the end of 1955, its own production satisfied about half of the company's requirements of malt. In early 1957, the company announced plans to build a maltery in 

Latacunga to process Ecuadoran barley. The brewery markets a high-quality beer that is popular in the Sierra. La Victoria also produces, as a sideline, mineral water, an orange-flavored soft drink, and some liquid carbon dioxide. 

National brewing capacity and production are more than adequate to supply the domestic market, and imports of beer are limited to negligible amounts. The industry has to import its supplies of yeast and hops. Some of its requirements of barley and malt must also be imported, although local production and processing have substantially reduced dependence upon imports and are expected to fulfill the industry's demands in the near future. 73

The manufacture of alcohol and alcoholic beverages other than beer is a Government-controlled industry administered by the Bureau of Monopolies. In actual practice, the Alcohol Monopoly (Estanco del Alcohol) produces only a part of national output in its own distilleries. Alcohol and alcoholic beverages are also produced by many privately owned distilleries, which are required by law to sell their total output to the Alcohol Monopoly.

The production of distilled alcoholic beverages is based principally on the distillation of aguardiente, a low-grade rum from fermented cane juice, and potable alcohol from molasses. Until recently, at least, about 65 percent of the aguardiente was produced in small distilleries on or near the farms where the sugarcane was grown. On the other hand, all but a very small part of the potable alcohol is produced in distilleries owned by the Alcohol Monopoly. In 1954-56, the production of aguardiente, including a small annual volume of refined aguardiente tinctured with anise, averaged 7 million liters per year; and the production of potable alcohol averaged 1.5 million liters a year. 

Aguardiente is itself an alcoholic beverage and is simply mixed with water and marketed as aguardiente de cana, or puro. The best grades of puro, however, are obtained from potable alcohol mixed with water. Refined aguardiente is puro with anise flavoring. In addition to the two basic distillates, there is some production of aguardiente in the form of brandy from grape juice and honey. Many kinds of alcoholic beverages, including so-called whisky, gin, wines, and liqueurs, are derived from the two basic distillates with admixtures of water and imported flavorings, extracts, and essential oils. The quality of these synthetic products is poor, and they find a limited acceptance in the domestic market.

The Alcohol Monopoly operates three distilleries: Las Penas, Duran, and La Zulay. The first-named, and the oldest, is located near Babahoyo; the second, in Duran; and the third, in 

Puyo, Napo-Pastaza. The three distilleries are devoted chiefly to the production of potable alcohol, but they also produce some aguardiente and synthetic beverages. None of the distilleries are equipped with bottling facilities; all their products are distributed in bulk. 

Largest and best-equipped of the Alcohol Monopoly's distilleries is the Duran installation, situated on the bank of the Guayas River opposite the city of Guayaquil. The Duran distillery was built in 1946 at a cost of 22 million sucres. For several years it was inactive, but, after an overhauling, it resumed operations in 1953. The plant has a maximum capacity, on a 24-hour basis, of 

20,000 liters of 190-proof alcohol, 5,000 liters of aldehyde alcohol, and about 250 liters of fusel oil.

Production is based on the distillation of molasses. 

The Duran distillery has consistently operated at well below capacity and has proved to be a distinct disappointment to the Alcohol Monopoly.

The distillery was expected to be an important earner of foreign exchange and a promoter of allied subsidiary industries. Instead, it has been unable to gain a foothold in the export market because of its high production costs, which are attributed to equipment obsolescence and labor deficiencies. As a result, the Director of the Alcohol 

Monopoly in his annual report for 1955 to the Minister of the Treasury strongly urged that the distillery be sold to private industry. 

The Congress in the closing days of its 1956 session passed a resolution recommending gradual elimination of the Alcohol Monopoly. As of early 

1958, however, no further action had been taken. 

The privately owned distilleries are small and inefficient. They produce both distillates and synthetic liquors. They are under the control of the Alcohol Monopoly, which purchases the total output of distillates and then allots the supplies of aguardiente and potable alcohol for the manufacture of end products. In the past few years, the Alcohol Monopoly has moved toward concentrating a larger share of production, especially of basic distillates, in its own distilleries, for the purpose of curbing overproduction and exercising more effective control over production standards and sales.

Aguardiente is the principal liquor marketed and consumed. In 1956, for example, the quantity sold by the Alcohol Monopoly, which controls all sales, amounted to 6.8 million liters that were worth 69.6 million sucres ($4.6 million). According to the Alcohol Monopoly, however, contraband sales of aguardiente greatly exceed legal sales, accounting for about 9 million liters a year, or nearly 60 percent of total sales. 

Among many of the poorer people, particularly the Indians in the Sierra, aguardiente is an unobtainable luxury. They drink chicha, an undistilled fermentation of corn, rice, barley, or the juice of the century plant. The stronger grades of chicha are fermented in the presence of brown pan sugar.

Unlike most other Latin nations, the Ecuadorans do not drink very much wine. Local consumption has been discouraged by the generally poor quality of national products and by high market prices as compared with other alcoholic beverages. The industry consists of some 20 wineries located in or near Guayaquil, Quito, Ambato, Cuenca, and Riobamba. Two of the largest establishments are the Destileria de Eladio Mz. de Espronceda, in Guayaquil, and the Fabrica de Licores El Progreso, in Quito. In 1953, the former was reported to produce, per year, about 74

Manufacturing and Construction 

54 4 را 

55,000 liters of wine and 13,000 liters of brandy; and the latter, approximately 30,000 liters of wine and 2,000 liters of brandy. Most of the wineries are equipped to produce only a few thousand liters of wine and brandy each year. Synthetic wine, fruit wine, and honey wine constitute the bulk of production, but raisin wine and grape wine, including vermouth, are also produced. 

Demands for quality wines and liquors are normally filled by imports, although a small distillery in Cuenca is now producing a British brand of gin under license. Imports, which are controlled by the Alcohol Monopoly, amounted to $438,700 in 1955.

Edible fats and oils.-The commercial production of edible fats and oils is a comparatively new industry. The first company to enter the field, Industrias Ales, C. A., was organized in 1943. 

Since then the industry has grown rapidly; now there are seven producers, all located in Guayaquil and Manta. 

Producers. Average monthly production of edible fats and oils, by firms, in early 1957, was, in thousands of pounds: La Favorita, S. A., in 

Guayaquil, 480; Industrias Ales, C. A., in Manta, 360; Phidagesa, S. A., in Guayaquil, 160; Inalca, in Manta, 150; Oleica, S. A., in Guayaquil, 140; Teneria La Iberia, S. A., in Guayaquil, 110; and 

Cilca, C. A., in Guayaquil, 50.10

The manufacturing plants are well equipped. 

Manufacturers were given Government protection until September 1956, and were permitted to import machinery, equipment, and some raw materials free of duty. One of the best-equipped plants is that of Industrias Ales, C. A., which produces both edible and inedible fats and oils. Its facilities include 2 gins, 2 oil-extraction plants of the latest type, a refinery, and equipment for manufacturing end products. The company's plant capacity for the production of vegetable lard and edible oils amounts to about 600,000 pounds a month. 

Commercial output of edible oils and vegetable lard increased from less than 1,200 tons in 1949 to more than 6,500 tons in 1956. Vegetable lard constituted 80 percent of total production in 1949–56.

National production of hog lard, on the other hand, declined sharply in 1956 from 1954–55. Production, 1949-56, by type of product was, in tons: 1949. 1950. 1951 1952. 1953. 1954. 1955 1956 Vegetable lard 872

Domestic production of vegetable oils in 1956 amounted to approximately 20 percent of national consumption; the production of vegetable lard accounted for about 85 percent of the amount consumed. The vegetable oils include a small quantity of castor oil and linseed oil plus oil from soybean, cottonseed, olive, palm, palm kernel, palm seed, almond, and other nuts. 

Because local production of oilseeds is insufficient for local demand, the Ecuadoran manufacturers of fats and oils have had to rely upon imported raw materials to the extent of about twothirds of their requirements. To stimulate domestic agricultural production, the Ministry of 

Economy and four producers (Ales, La Favorita, La Iberia, and Inalca) in January 1955 signed an agreement under which the manufacturers promised to buy the entire national output of oilseeds, particularly peanuts and sesame seeds, at remunerative prices fixed by the Ministry, and the Ministry agreed to provide increased credit facilities through the National Development Bank. In 

April 1957, the Government took another step in the direction of helping the growers of oilseeds by requiring all manufacturers of fats and oils to use national raw materials in the amount of at least half of their total product. Until recently, at least, the major factor handicapping the development of the industry has been the difficulty in obtaining adequate supplies of raw materials. 11 Fish products.-The fishing industry was largely neglected until about 1950, but since then it has undergone substantial commercial development. Exports of fish increased 587 percent in volume between 1950 and 1955. 

The commercial fishing industry is concerned almost exclusively with catching, dressing, and freezing fish and shellfish for export. Little attention has been given to developing the domestic market, although the largest producer, Industria

Ecuatoriana Productora de Alimentos, C. A. 

(INEPACA), does maintain sales outlets in coastal cities and in Quito. Local processing facilities operated by the commercial producers consist of a fish-freezing and -packing plant in 

Manta, owned by INEPACA, and a modern freezing plant for shrimp in Posorja, Guayas, owned by the Pesca Ecuatoriana Santaelenense, C. A. (PESCA). A small pilot plant for freezing fish and manufacturing fish flour, financed by the Government and completed under the direction of a United Nations technician, was opened in Manta in November 1956. 

INEPACA produces a good-quality canned tuna, which is sold throughout Ecuador. Other local requirements of canned fish are filled by imports, the most important of which are sardines. 

The market for imports is limited to well-to-do persons, and consumption is small. "The fishing industry is discussed in detail in chapter VI. 75

Tobacco Products Production of tobacco products.-The manufac ture of cigars and cigarettes is controlled by the

Bureau of Monopolies. Production is carried on in privately owned factories, which are required to sell their output to the Tobacco Monopoly (Estanco del Tobaco). In turn, the Tobacco Monopoly resells the stock to the manufacturers and sets the price at which the products may be sold in the wholesale and retail markets.

Cigarettes constitute the bulk of production. In 

1952-56, annual cigarette production averaged 52 million packages.12 In the same period, the number of cigars produced averaged 8.4 million per year. Guayaquil is the principal manufacturing center and the site of the largest factory, Fabrica El Progreso, which normally produces over fourfifths of the total output of cigarettes and a large share of the cigars. Cigarette factories also are located in Quito and Sangolqui, Pichincha, about 

10 miles southeast of Quito, and a few small cigar factories are located in Portoviejo and one small factory in Quito.

The local industry has been affected by a combination of adverse factors, and production has seriously declined. Cigarette production, which reached a peak of 55.9 million packages in 1951, fell to 49.2 million packages in 1956. Cigar production has followed a downward trend since 1937, and the output of 4.2 million units in 1956 was only 

7 percent of 1937 production and less than 60 per- cent of 1955 production. The decline in cigar production is ascribed, in part, to changes in smoking habits, but the cigar, as well as the cigarette, industry is affected primarily by the poor quality of domestic tobaccos. As a consequence, there has been a great increase in the smuggling of better quality foreign-made cigarettes, especially those of United States and Colombian manufacture. 

Imports of cigarettes.-Imports have also impaired the domestic industry. The Tobacco Monopoly imports foreign-made cigarettes, preponderantly from the United States, and sells them to wholesalers and retailers who, in turn, market them at fixed prices. The income from this trade is one of the most important sources of revenue for the Bureau of Monoplies. Consumption of foreign-made cigarettes distributed by the Tobacco Monopoly amounted in 1956 to 15.4 million packages, largely of the 20-cigarette pack, valued at 72.1 million sucres ($4.8 million). The Tobacco 

Monopoly has promoted the sale of imported cigarettes through a pricing policy designed to discourage contraband traffic and to protect and to increase its own revenues.

Position of domestic tobacco industry.-As the result of these competitive factors, the domestic 

1 Domestic brands of cigarettes are marketed in packages containing 14, 18, or 20 cigarettes, depending upon the brand. industry has been placed in a difficult position, and from time to time some of the cigarette and cigar factories have been idle. The Government reportedly is considering divesting itself of its tobacco monopoly and opening the field to free enterprise. In 1957, at least one major United 

States manufacturer approached the Ecuadoran authorities with a proposal to build a cigarette factory in Ecuador and to use local tobacco of the

Virginia burley type, which would be cultivated in the Province of Esmeraldas. The Fabrica El 

Progreso meanwhile has been trying to improve its local supplies. Since 1954, it has been working toward developing tobacco of the Virginia burley type and improving its own curing facilities. 

Textiles and Related Industries 

The textile industry is the most highly developed and best organized in Ecuador. It employs more workers in mechanized operations and contributes more to the total value of national industrial production than any other single industry, when the components of the food industry are considered separately. 

Number of spindles and looms.-The textile industry has made notable progress in recent years. 

At the beginning of 1950, for example, the member companies of the Association of Textile Indus tries of Ecuador (Asociacion de Industriales Tex tiles del Ecuador), to which all the important companies belong, were equipped with 38,535 spindles and 1,462 looms. At the close of 1955, the member companies were equipped with 89,102 spindles and 

2,103 looms, distributed by types as follows: 

Spindles-85,000 cotton and 4,102 wool; looms- 

1,636 cotton, 91 wool, and 376 artificial fibers. 

Production of textiles.-Between 1950 and 1955, the production of fabrics increased from 12,093 to 21,200 meters, or 75 percent. The manufacture of other types of textile products increased from 775 to 4,320 tons, or 457 percent.13 The composition of production for 1952-54 is shown in table 38.

In 1950-54, textile production increased from 

114.3 million sucres ($7.5 million) to 169.1 million ($11.2 million), or 48 percent. Sales of textiles rose from 117.5 million sucres ($7.8 million) to 

186.2 million ($12.3 million), or 58 percent.

Nationally made textile products satisfy only a part of market demands, however, and foreignmade yarns, fabrics, and other textiles normally constitute one of the country's leading imports. 

In fact, as local production has increased, imports have also increased to meet expanding requirements for quality textiles that are not produced locally. Imports are also preferred to most do-
