11.1 percent in 1951 to 11.7 percent in 1955. In 

1951-55, cotton textiles accounted for 48 percent of all textile imports by value; woolen textiles, for 

23 percent; and other textiles, for 29 percent. 

Another competitive factor affecting the domestic industry is the contraband traffic in textiles from neighboring Colombia. In early 1956, several textile mills in Ecuador reportedly were forced to curtail production, in part because of the large-scale smuggling of better and cheaper textiles and textile products from Colombia.

Location of textile plants.-The textile industry is concentrated in the Sierra. Only one factory is located in the Costa, Textiles San Antonio, a new cotton mill on the outskirts of Guayaquil. It was completed in early 1955. There are some 25 plants of industrial importance in the Sierra Provinces- 

Pichincha has 18, Imbabura 3, Tungurahua 2, Chimborazo 1, and Azuay 1. In addition, there are many small establishments and households that produce an indeterminate volume of rugs, shawls, ponchos, and other textile products. Both spinning and weaving are highly advanced handicrafts in Ecuador.

Quito is the principal textile center, with some 16 mills. Other leading centers are Ambato, Otavalo, Riobamba, and Cuenca. Many of the mills produce a variety of textiles and, accordingly, are difficult to classify by type of operations. Classified by principal output, however, there are 12 mills in cotton textiles, 2 in woolen textiles, 6 in knitwear, and 5 in rayon fabrics. 

Cotton Textiles

The oldest and largest of the textile mills are those that make cotton products. Only two of them, however, were in operation before World War I. The oldest mill in the country, Fabricas 

Chillo Jijon, which is located outside Quito and is devoted to the production of both cotton and woolen textiles, was established in 1841. Most of the mills date from the period between the two world wars, and three-Textil San Pedro and 

Textiles Nacionales, both in Quito, and Textiles 

San Antonio, in Guayaquil—were founded after

World War II. 

Investment in cotton textiles.-With the major exception of Textiles Nacionales, which is owned by American and Ecuadoran interests, there is little foreign capital investment in the cotton textile industry. The largest of the companies and the largest producer of textiles in Ecuador, the Quito firm La Internacional, has a capital investment of some 50 million sucres ($3.3 million).

Second in size and importance is La Industrial 

Algodonera, with a capital investment of between 

16 million sucres ($1.1 million) and 20 million sucres ($1.3 million). La Industrial Algodonera has a plant in Atuntaqui, Imbabura, and another in Ambato. Investments in the other companies are relatively small, the largest of them probably not exceeding 6 million or 7 million sucres ($396,000 or $462,000).

Plant and equipment in cotton textiles.-Only 2 of the companies were equipped with more than 9,000 spindles at the end of 1953, the latest year for which company data are available. La Internacional had 12,964 spindles and La Industrial 

Algodonera, 9,940.14 The other spinning mills had an average of about 2,000 spindles, ranging from a minimum of 900 to a maximum of 7,000. 

La Internacional had 450 looms and La Industrial 

Algodonera, 300. Most of the other weaving mills had fewer than 100 looms, the majority being equipped with between 20 and 50 looms.

Much of this equipment is old, but important amounts of new machinery have been imported and installed in the past few years. Imports of textile machinery through the port of Guayaquil in 1953-55 amounted to $5 million. To be sure, a part of this machinery is for the manufacture of rayon or woolen textiles, but the installation of new factories for the production of cotton products clearly indicates substantial imports of cotton spinning and weaving equipment. Between 1949 and 1954, the cotton mills installed 693 automatic looms.

One of the most important recent additions is La Internacional's new plant, which represents an investment of some 30 million sucres ($2 million).

The plant, located on the south side of Quito, was completed in 1953 but was not in full operation in 1956. The plant is equipped with Swiss-made 

14 Asociacion de Industriales Textiles del Ecuador, unpublished data, Quito, January 1955. 77

spindles and looms. Original plans called for the installation of 7,000 spindles and 200 looms.

Cotton textile products.-The cotton mills manufacture largely low-price materials because they are all that most consumers can afford to buy. 

The mills are required to use domestic cotton, a low-grade, short-staple product which seriously impairs the quality of manufactures. Supplies of domestic cotton are insufficient to meet industrial demands, and, accordingly, cotton is imported and blended with the local product. 

The two leading cotton textile manufacturers are La Internacional and La Industrial Al godonera. In 1953, the combined value of production of these companies amounted to approximately 74 million sucres ($4.9 million), or about 

45 percent of the value of all textile production.

The output of La Internacional was valued at 45 million sucres ($3 million) and that of La Industrial Algodonera at 29 million sucres ($1.9 million).

Handicaps to cotton textile industry.-Through the Association of Textile Industries of Ecuador, the cotton textile manufacturers have taken steps to improve the quality of domestic cotton and reduce dependence upon imports. In 1954 they contributed funds to form the Sociedad de Fo mento Algodonero Nacional, C. A. (FANCA). 

Since its inception, FANCA has contracted for the technical assistance of the Food and Agricultural Organization of the United Nations and has established relations with the Instituto de Fo mento Algodonero de Colombia. FANCA has established in Bahia de Caraquez, near the principal cotton-growing areas, an experimental station to conduct research in cotton culture. 

Another handicap to the cotton textile industry, as well as the woolen and rayon textile industries, is the generally poor dyeing and finishing facilities in most of the mills. Each mill does its own dyeing and finishing, using imported materials, and, for the most part, the products are uneven in color and they lack fastness. Moreover, there is little originality in color and design in comparison with the imported fabrics from the United States, United Kingdom, and Japan, the principal foreign sources of supply. 

Woolen Textiles

The manufacture of woolen textiles on a factory scale is relatively new in Ecuador. Two factories produce woolens solely or principally. Fabrica 

San Pedro, in Otavalo, produces woolen yarn, and 

Industria Textilana, in Quito, manufactures woolen fabrics, including cashmere and flannel, and some rayon fabrics. Industria Textilana was established in 1954 and is equipped with the latest type automatic machinery, mainly of Belgian manufacture, including 20 looms for woolens, 2 wool-carding machines, 2 spinning mules, and 2 warping frames. The plant also has 10 rayon looms, which were imported from Japan. Fabrica 

San Pedro is also equipped with much new and efficient machinery which was installed within the past 2 years.

In addition to these two plants, several other mills produce woolen textiles. Among them are the Ambato branch of La Industrial Algodonera, which produces woolen yarn and textiles along with a much larger output of cotton textiles; Fabricas Chillo Jijon, which manufactures both cotton and woolen fabrics; and Fabrica Inca-Sedalana, in Quito, whose output includes woolen and cotton fabrics and knitwear and nylon stockings. 

As in the case of the cotton textile industry, the producers of woolen textiles are confronted with a serious problem in the matter of domestic prime material. Ecuadoran wool is deficient in both quantity and quality. Local production reportedly supplies about 10 percent of total consump tion. Local wool is said to be inferior to imported short-fiber scrap wool. Domestic manufactures are based upon the use of blended Ecuadoran and foreign wool, and, as a result, no worsteds are produced. Imports of dirty or washed, restored, and carded or combed wool in 1955 amounted to approximately 33 tons worth $50,600.

Synthetic Textiles 

The production of rayon textiles is a new and expanding industry. Some of the cotton textile mills have diversified operations to include rayons. 

In Quito, five mills manufacture rayon textiles principally. The newest and largest of these mills is Textiles San Vicente, in which some 25 million sucres ($1.7 million) has been invested. The plant is equipped with approximately 170 automatic looms and 9 spinning machines, in addition to warp-preparation and pressing and drying machinery. In late 1954 monthly production averaged 180,000 yards of rayon fabrics in 14 varieties.

The company has constructed a cotton spinning and weaving mill on adjoining ground. 

Among the other rayon textile mills, Textil La Union recently installed new machinery valued at 2 million sucres ($132,000) to expand its production, which in 1954 averaged about 30,000 yards a month. A few manufacturers of rayon goods also produce silk fabrics. Perhaps the most important of these manufacturers is La Europea which produces about 70,000 yards of rayon and silk textiles each month. La Europea, in which about 8 million sucres ($528,000) has been invested, is owned in part by American and Swiss interests who hold approximately 25 percent of the capital stock.

The combined output of the domestic rayon textile industry reportedly satisfies between one-half 78

Manufacturing and Construction and two-thirds of national consumption. Imports of synthetic thread, yarn, and fabrics, including mixtures of artificial and natural materials, authorized in 1955, totaled nearly $2.5 million. Rayon yarn is not produced in Ecuador.

Within the past few years, at least one company, Fabrica Inca-Sedalana, has started the manufacture of nylon hosiery, in addition to its older line of knitted garments of cotton and wool. 

In 1954 the plant was equipped with 12 automatic full-fashioned hosiery machines, which it planned to double by new installations, and production averaged between 600 and 700 dozen pairs of hosiery per month. The nylon thread is imported, for none is produced in Ecuador. Clothing

The manufacture of clothing is largely a shop or home handicraft. There is no large-scale factory production of heavy outerwear. Coats and suits are generally made to order. In addition to the knitwear mills, which produce underwear, knitted outerwear, and a few other finished items, about 10 plants, located principally in Quito, Ambato, and Guayaquil, manufacture shirts, pajamas, underwear, neckties, and other articles of apparel. One of the largest of these plants is 

Patex, a Quito firm, which produces about 10,000 pieces of all types per month, an output equal to approximately 20 percent of total national production. Domestic manufacturers produce most of the shirts and pajamas marketed in Ecuador, but the quality of production is, in general, inferior to that of imports.

Some small establishments produce work clothes. Confeccion de Overalls Chiriboga, in

Quito, is one of the leaders in this field. The company manufactures overalls, work shirts, and related products; it uses both domestic and imported materials. The factory is equipped with up-to-date cutting and sewing machinery imported from the United States; it produces about 

100 units per day, a volume below its productive capacity. Reportedly, the industry has suffered from the competition of duty-free imports that are permitted Government agencies and some large private institutions.

The manufacture of felt hats is centered principally in Quito. The largest manufacturer is

Fabrica de Sombreros Fielson, which produces a variety of felt headwear and hat bodies. The factory uses about 60 percent domestic and 40 percent imported wool for its wool-felt products, and it uses rabbit fur for the manufacture of furfelt products. Daily production in 1954 amounted to about 1,300 units, well below the plant's capacity of 2,000 units. While the factory is equipped to produce high-quality hats, its principal product is low-price hats for the Sierra

Indians, who buy about 80 percent of the plant's output. Most of those consumers who can afford to buy better quality and more expensive headwear prefer to purchase imported hats.

Panama Hats 

The production and export of panama hats has long been one of Ecuador's most important industries and one of the country's leading dollar earners. During and directly after World War 

II, when competition from the Far East and from Italy was eliminated in the United States and South American markets, the Ecuadoran panama hat industry enjoyed its best years. Exports annually averaged $5,369,000 in 1944-46 and reached a peak of $6,117,000 in 1946. Since then the industry has declined as the result of changing customs and tastes in the United States market, upon which Ecuadoran exporters have depended for around 90 percent of their sales, and as a result of the resumption of United States imports of cheaper straw hats from the Philippines, Japan, and Italy. In 1955, exports of panama hats amounted in value to only $992,400, the lowest figure since 1940, and represented only 1.1 percent of the value of all exports, as compared with an annual average of over 19 percent in 1944-46. The export trade recovered to some extent in 1956, however, with shipments valued at $1.5 million. 

Panama hats are woven of toquilla straw by thousands of Indians and others in the Provinces of Manabi, Azuay, and Cañar. It is a handicraft and a home industry that is generally carried on as a sideline to subsistence farming. The toquilla straw is found in the Costa, principally near the 

Guayas coastal town of Manglar Alto, and the best quality hats are produced in Montecristi and Jipijapa. For many years, however, the chief area of production has been the Sierra Provinces of Azuay and Cañar, where the industry was introduced by the Government during the 19th century. About 80 percent of all panama hats exported from Ecuador are produced in Azuay and Cañar.

Cuenca, the capital of Azuay, is the principal center of the panama hat trade. In Cuenca are some 20 exporters who purchase the bulk of the output, mainly through field agents. The exporters undertake the final stages of manufacture; they shape and bleach the hats and finish the hat brims. The hats are then classified by quality standards and shipped by air to Guayaquil for export. The decline in exports in recent years has prompted the exporting firms to try to develop new hat styles that will be less expensive and have a wider sales appeal in foreign markets. 79

Bagging and Other Fiber Products 

Bagging fabric is not made commercially in Ecuador, despite the availability of local raw materials and the good domestic market. Illustrative of the size of the market, permits granted in 1955 for the importation of jute burlap for bagging amounted to slightly over $297,700. In early 

1957, a small factory to manufacture bags from locally grown cabuya fiber was in the planning stage for construction in Montecristi. The project is under the direction of the ICA and is intended to provide employment for weavers in the depressed panama hat industry.

Cordage is another important fiber product that is obtained abroad. Imports of cables, cord, and rope made of hemp, flax, jute, abaca, agave, or sisal authorized in 1955 aggregated approximately $139,500.

There is some small-scale production of mattresses in which locally grown kapok is used as a filler. This is essentially a shop-type industry generally operated by one or two individuals and equipped to handle only a custom trade. Some establishments manufacture brooms and similar items made of domestic broom straw, but the effective demand for these items is small and limited usually to the few large cities in the country.

Chemicals and Allied Products 

The most highly developed of the chemical industries in Ecuador are pharmaceuticals and soap, and to a lesser degree, matches and plastics. 

Insecticides and fertilizers are produced in very limited amounts, and a few simple industrial chemicals, principally denatured alcohol, are also made.

Industrial chemicals.-The Alcohol Monopoly produces a small quantity of denatured alcohol in its distilleries. Production in 1953-55 annually averaged a little over 267,000 liters. Domestic production is supplemented by small quantities of imports to meet the limited local requirements.

Liquid carbon dioxide is produced as a byproduct of the mineral water bottling industry, and some of the bottling companies, most notably, the 

Tesalia Springs Co., produce dry ice.

Caustic soda is produced by the Military Chemical Service in a plant outside Quito. It is made by the electrolytic process, and the output approximates 50 tons a year. National requirements are many times this amount and, of necessity, are filled by imports, which in 1955 amounted to 1,161 tons valued at more than $77,000.

No alkalis other than caustic soda are produced in Ecuador.

No acids are produced. In 1952, an Americanowned company, the Ecuadorian Mining Corporation, S. A., was reported to be considering the construction of a sulfuric acid plant at Tixan, where the company was mining sulfur. The acid plant was never built, however, for the company discontinued its mining activities because of high production costs and finally went out of business in 1956.

There has been little incentive for either private or public capital to undertake the production of acids or other basic chemicals for industrial use because of the limited demands of the local economy. Imports of acids and organic anhy drides in 1955 totaled only 2,735 tons worth $277,720. 

Paints. Paints are produced by nine manufac turers situated in Guayaquil, Quito, Cayambe, and Pasaje. Products include ready-mixed paints, oil paints, water paints, rubber-base paints, varnishes, enamels, lacquers, stains, and fillers. Production satisfies only a part of requirements, which approximate 1,400 to 1,800 tons per year. Imports of paints in 1955 amounted to 1,355 tons valued at $499,470, and in 1956, to 

694 tons worth $436,960.

The market for paints has been limited largely to urban communities, and, apart from industrial application, the use of paints has been confined chiefly to external trimmings and internal surfaces, woodwork, and furniture. The principal item of consumption is whitewash, which is used as a coating on the stone, cement, and stucco buildings that constitute most urban construction. them

Some of the necessary raw materials for producing paints are available locally, among industrial alcohol, casein, and some iron oxide and natural resins. Many of the ingredients, however, especially alkyd resins and chemical pigments, must be imported.

American capital has been invested in the paint industry in Ecuador. W. R. Grace and Co. and the Glidden Co. are owners of the country's new est paint factory, PINTEC (Pinturas Ecuto rianas), which was built in Guayaquil at a cost of some $200,000. The factory was opened in December 1957. At present, PINTEĊ employs & force of 30 workers and is operating at a rate equivalent to the production of 240 tons of paints per year. The company expects to increase its output to an annual rate of 300 tons by the end of the first year. Its products include rubber-base paints and cement paints, in addition to enamels, varnishes, semigloss paints, oil paints, and industrial paints. 

Soap. There are several soap-manufacturing plants, located chiefly in Guayaquil, Manta, Quito, Cuenca, and Ambato. The Guayaquil firm, 80

Manufacturing and Construction 

Jaboneria Nacional, S. A., which also owns the vegetable-oil plant La Favorita, is the largest producer, followed in importance by Industrias 

Ales, S. A., in Manta, and Briz Sanches Hnos. y

Cia., in Quito. Data regarding the industry's output are not available, but the combined annual production of the 3 leading plants probably amounts to about 4,000 tons and accounts for about four-fifths of total commercial production. 

Laundry soap in cakes is the principal product.

Toilet soap in cakes is also produced in appreciable quantities. Small amounts of soap powders and soap flakes are made, but there is no production of detergents. In general, the domestic products are of poor quality, although the glycerin content is relatively high. There is no commercial recovery of glycerin in the manufacturing process.

Local manufactures, however, satisfy the bulk of market requirements, and imports are limited essentially to quality products. In 1955, imports of soap and other cleaning products amounted to 324 tons valued at $157,500. Practically all of these imports came from the United States. 

Locally available fats and oils produced by the oilseed crushing plants are used by the soap industry. Jaboneria Nacional, for example, obtains a large part of its requirements from La Favorita, while Ales produces and refines its own raw materials. Tallow, of course, is also used. Domestic supplies of these materials are insufficient, however, and imports are required. Essential oils and chemicals for soap also have to be imported. 

Toilet preparations.-Toiletries and cosmetics, in the main, are produced on a small scale in many drugstores and a few laboratories in Quito and 

I Guayaquil. One of the most important local manufactures is eau de cologne, which is prepared by some of the laboratories and by the Alcohol Monopoly, which operates a plant in Quito. In 

1955, the Alcohol Monopoly's plant produced 

26,457 liters of eau de cologne valued at 645,432 sucres ($42,599). Production in 1956 declined to 

21,045 liters valued at 553,666 sucres ($36,542).

Other leading manufactures include dentifrices, talcum powder, and a few items made from imported materials. The output of all these items is limited, as the market has a strong preference for imported products. In 1955, imports of perfumes, cosmetics, dentifrices, and other toiletries except soap were valued at $506,400. 

Drugs and pharmaceuticals.-The manufacture of drugs and pharmaceuticals is one of the principal industries in Ecuador, even though the bulk of prime materials have to be imported. In both 

Quito and Guayaquil, many drugstores prepare simple proprietary compounds, and several sizable establishments are devoted to the manufacture of medicinals and pharmaceuticals for the wholesale trade. The industry has developed rapidly since the early years of World War II, and pharmaceutical products now represent one of the country's principal exports, shipments having amounted in value to $960,700 in 1954 and $839,000 in 1955.

The largest and most important of the manu facturers, Laboratorios LIFE, S. A., was estab lished in Quito by Italian technicians in 1940.

The Central Public Assistance Board (Junta 

Central de Asistencia Publica), Quito, is a shareholder in the company, which has a paid-in capitalization of 18 million sucres. LIFE is an efficient organization employing some 500 wellpaid workers, including a few Italian and 

German supervisory personnel. The company operates a well-equipped plant with excellent laboratory facilities. 

It produces a wide range of manufactures, among which are vitamins, vaccines, antitoxins, glandular products, tonics, digestive preparations, sedatives, analgesics, disinfectants, laxatives, serums, and a long list of proprietaries and specialties. The output also includes some foreign patent medicines and specialties, which LIFE produces under license. LIFE does not manufacture antibiotics. A few years ago the company experimented with the production of penicillin but found the costs too high to produce a competitive product. The annual value of the company's production in the past few years has exceeded 30 million sucres ($2 million).

LIFE maintains branches in Guayaquil and 

Portoviejo and agencies in Ambato, Cuenca, Loja, and Tulcan. Domestic sales, however, have been limited by the long-established market preferences for imported drugs and pharmaceuticals, which average about $3 million a year, and the company has concentrated on developing an export trade by establishing sales branches in the capital cities of 

Bolivia, Colombia, Costa Rica, Cuba, and El

Salvador. The company also has agents and distributors in Guatemala, Haiti, Panama, Peru, and

Venezuela. Export sales now constitute about two-thirds of the firm's trade, and virtually all of the pharmaceuticals exported from Ecuador are produced by LIFE. 

Among the other manufacturing establishments, perhaps the most important is Compania Anonima 

Comercial, Industrial, Farmaceutical-CIF, a 

Quito firm organized in 1950. CIF produces some 

200 different types of products. Between 1951 and 1954 the company's sales in the domestic market increased in value from 446,000 sucres ($29,436) to more than 2 million sucres ($132,000). 

In June 1956, the Sterling Drug Co., a U. S. firm, opened a small plant in Guayaquil to produce drugs. The plant makes and markets "Mejoral," a trademark for a product similar to aspirin. Production is based on raw materials imported from the United States. 81

At present, the local industry probably does not account for more than 25 percent of the total volume of drugs and pharmaceuticals consumed in

Ecuador, but the steady growth of the industry indicates an increasing displacement of imports by local manufactures. 

Insecticides and pesticides.-A small quantity of insect and pest control products, based largely upon imported DDT, is produced in a few plants in Guayaquil. Some insecticidal oil is produced by the Anglo-Ecuadorian Oilfields, Ltd., the country's leading petroleum company. Apart from the last-named product, which has been on the market only since 1954, the other local manufactures have not been able to satisfy quality and quantity requirements. 

The cultivation of pyrethrum, a daisylike flower which is the base of an effective insecticide, has been undertaken on a commercial scale. 

The insecticide is produced locally only in small quantities, however, and the domestic industry is limited essentially to cultivating, harvesting, drying, and exporting the flowers. Exports amounted to 93.5 tons valued at $47,400 in 1955 and 149.3 tons valued at $70,700 in 1956. Two U. S. firms, the

Fairfield Chemical Division of the Food Ma chinery and Chemical Corp. and S. B. Penick and Co., are financially interested in this industry 

(see chapter V). 

Matches. Prior to June 1957 the manufacture of matches was a Government monopoly. The 

Bureau of Monopolies operated two match factories, Chimbacalle and El Batan, both located in 

Quito. Because of the poor quality of the national product, contraband matches, principally from 

Colombia and Peru, found a ready market in Ecuador, and domestic match production declined. Between 1954 and 1956, the combined output of Chimbacalle and El Batan dropped from 62.2 million to 40.1 million boxes of 50 matches each. Operating losses of the 2 factories in 1955 reportedly exceeded 662,000 sucres (nearly $44,000), and in 1956 some 12 million boxes of nationally made matches remained in factory warehouses because of the decrease in sales.

In 1956, the Chimbacalle factory was closed. 

In June 1957, the Government abolished the state match monopoly, closed the El Batan factory, and invited private enterprise to take over the match industry. To date, the Government reportedly has approved three separate proposals from prívate interests in Colombia, Venezuela, and Yugoslavia to establish match factories in Ecuador.

Up to the present, however, no further action has been taken. Meanwhile, the domestic market must depend upon Government imports of matches, which are distributed at fixed prices. Under a contract awarded in May 1957, Ecuador has imported 30 million boxes of matches from Yugoslavia. The Ecuadoran Government, in early 

1958, invited bids for the supply of 60 million boxes of matches for the nation's consumers. 

Fertilizers.-Two fertilizer plants were in service in early 1957, the Government-owned Fabrica Na cional de Abonos in La Libertad, Guayas, and the privately owned Sociedad Industrial Ecuatoriana, C. A., in Quito. The Fabrica Nacional de Abonos, which has been in operation since 1948, produces between 1,500 and 2,000 tons of lime and guano phosphate per year. The Quito plant, opened in 1954, is geared to produce about 2,000 tons of fertilizer per year, utilizing industrial and vegetable waste and various chemicals. 

A third plant, owned by the Government and financed primarily by the National Development

Bank, was constructed on the outskirts of Quito in 1957. The plant is designed to produce about 25 tons of fertilizer per day, using garbage and other waste materials treated with chemicals. 

In recent years the use of fertilizers has increased greatly as the result of the programs to expand and improve agricultural production carried on by the Ministry of Economy with the technical assistance of the United Nations and the United States. Imports of fertilizers of all types amounted to $535,000 in 1954 and $647,000 in 1955. 

Plastics.-Plastic products are manufactured from imported materials in two factories, one in 

Quito and one in Guayaquil. The larger of the two manufacturers, Plasticos Dalmau, located in Quito, produces a wide range of items, including combs, toothbrushes, tableware, jugs, gift boxes, inkstands, ashtrays, and tiles. Its annual rate of production in late 1954 was estimated at 2 million sucres ($132,000). The plant is equipped to produce thermoplastics by the injection method, and the management reportedly plans to install extrusion machinery to manufacture new products, such as tubing and covered wire.

Imports of plastic materials and manufactures increased 1,266 percent in volume and 914 percent in value between 1953 and 1955, mounting from 74 tons valued at $117,000 in 1953 to 1,011 tons valued at $1.2 million in 1955. The great increase in imports was accounted for by raw materials for the local plastics industry and by polyethylene sheeting used as a protective wrapping for bananas exported from Ecuador. Payments authorized for imports of the sheeting exceeded $700,000 in 1955. Imports of plastic manufactures are limited largely to items not produced locally. 

Wood Products 

National requirements of wood products are filled by local industry, with limited exceptions. 

In 1955, imports of barrels and casks, finished products for construction purposes (doors, frames, sashes, etc.), and unspecified manufac- 82

Manufacturing and Construction tures of wood, exclusive of furniture, amounted to $43,700. Apart from a few minor items, imports of furniture are prohibited, and the domestic market depends entirely upon native production. 

Wooden furniture imports in 1955 amounted to only $1,464.

The furniture industry, in general, is a handicraft carried on in many small shops throughout the country. For the most part, the items produced are of poor quality, as the wood is not properly seasoned or cured for lack of wooddrying kilns. In Quito and Guayaquil, a few relatively large manufacturers of furniture also produce a variety of other items, such as doors, window frames, and flooring. 

Perhaps the largest of these establishments is 

Industria Maderera Robalino, a Quito firm that in recent years has increased the value of its annual production from about 300,000 to nearly 1 million sucres ($19,800 to nearly $66,000). The company recently completed a new factory equipped with machinery designed to double production. The principal wood used by the company is eucalyptus obtained from its own logging and sawmill operations about 40 miles from Quito, but some imported cedar and other special types of wood also are used.

In Ambato, one firm, La Nacion, produces wooden lasts for the manufacture of footwear. 

The plant's output is small and is consumed largely by the shoe factories in the Ambato area. 

Paper and Paper Products

Aside from a small amount of kraft paper, paper and paper products are imported. Domestic manufactures of paper and paperboard are produced from imported materials and are limited to only a few items, the most important of which are bags and paperboard boxes and containers. 

Imports of paper, paperboard, and cardboard, and their manufactures in 1953-55 annually averaged $2.6 million. Newsprint is the principal import.

The country's only pulp and paper mill, owned by the Industria Papelera Ecuatoriana, C. A., 

(IPECA). and situated in Latacunga, was completed in 1957. The mill cost approximately $1 million, which was provided by the National Development Bank and private subscription, including some Italian capital. Equipped with German and Italian machinery, the mill can produce up to about 10 tons of paper per day, with margin for greater output if conditions warrant. At present, however, production approximates 1 ton of kraft paper per day, but the mill management intends to expand and diversify production to include bond, printing, and waterproof papers, and bristol board. No production of newsprint is contemplated in the near future. 

The mill processes banana stalks, which have to be transported from the Costa or from the foothills of the Sierra region. Consideration has also been given to using bagasse and paramo grass for raw materials. Abundant supplies of paramo grass are available near the mill. The use of bagasse and paramo grass, however, would require the installation of additional machinery. 

Several Quito firms manufacture paper bags. 

Two of the leading manufacturers are Fabrica 

Omega and Fabrica Ecuador. Fabrica Omega produces chiefly ordinary-size paper bags for store use and supplies a large part of the Sierra market. 

Fabrica Ecuador, which has been in production only since January 1955, makes large-size bags for industry and agriculture. The plant, built at a reported cost of 3 million sucres ($198,000), is equipped to produce up to 60,000 bags in an 8-hour day. Shortly after the plant opened, it obtained an order from the Cemento Nacional Rocafuerte, in Guayaquil, for 2 million bags for packing cement. 

Until recently, at least, Guayaquil and other

Costa consuming centers always imported their requirements of paper bags. Industrial census data show only one Costa manufacturer of paper bags, a small firm located in Pasaje. 

Paper and paperboard containers are produced principally in Quito and Guayaquil. Two of the leading Quito manufacturers, Miltex and Noe 

Mucarzel A., produce candy and gift containers. 

One Guayaquil firm, Industrias de Envases de

Carton, produces paperboard containers for butter, vegetable lard, and ice cream. Its annual output is about 80,000 containers. 

Rubber Products

About a dozen firms in Guayaquil, Quito, Ambato, and in one or two other localities produce a variety of rubber goods, including overshoes, tennis shoes, soles and heels, belting and matting, banding, rubberized cloth, raincoats, ballons, and nipples. Tires are not manufactured in Ecuador, but tire retreading is well developed, particularly in Quito, where there are several shops. Manufactures are made from used rubber, imported camelback, and native rubber, the annual production of which approximates 500 tons. The output of the domestic industry satisfies some of the ordinary requirements, but all tires and tubes and all specialities, particularly specialities of soft rubber, have to be imported. In 1955 imports of rubber and rubber products were valued at $2.6 million, of which tires and tubes accounted for more than 80 percent. 83

Principal manufacturers.-Two of the largest and most important manufacturers of rubber products are Fabrica Venus, in Ambato, and 

Fabrica Diana Industrializadora del Caucho,

C. A., in Guayaquil. Both plants are devoted principally to the production of tennis shoes and rubber shoes and heels; they account for the bulk of output of those items.

The larger of the two factories, Fabrica Venus, operates a modern plant that is equipped with machinery made in the United States. The plant has about 70 workers. The plant uses native rubber, which is treated with imported chemicals. In manufacturing tennis shoes, the plant uses domestically produced canvas for uppers. In early 

1955, when the factory was operating at a rate far below capacity because of limited market demands, output of the principal product, tennis shoes, averaged about 1,300 pairs per week. Annual production in 1954, according to the management, was 70,000 pairs. Fabrica Venus also produces rubber boots, garden hose, and some automobile accessories.

Fabrica Diana produces about 20,000 pairs of tennis shoes per year. The firm also manufactures bathing shoes, rubber balls, and a few other items. 

Its market is limited to the Guayaquil area. 

A third company, the Compania Industrial

Caucho y Plastico Ecuatoriano, S. A., in July 1956 

opened a large, modern factory in Guayaquil to produce canvas and rubber footwear. The company is associated with the United States firm 

RoSearch, Inc., of Waynesville, N. C.

Tire plant.-A private company known as the 

Ecuadorian Rubber Co. was organized in 1955 to build and operate a tire factory in Cuenca. The company is capitalized at 24 million sucres ($1.6 million) and is financed by Ecuadoran and United 

States capital, with Ecuadoran interests holding about 60 percent of the stock. The principal stockholders are the owner of the Fabrica Venus and the U. S. firm, General Tire Co. The latter has a 30-percent interest in the enterprise.

The company signed an industrial protection contract with the Ministry of Economy in Febru ary 1956, agreeing to build a plant employing at least 100 workers and eventually producing up to 

400 tires a day. In June 1957, some of the machinery required for the plant had already arrived in Cuenca, and construction of the factory building was expected to start within a short time. 

Reportedly, the factory will produce tires of 6 sizes, ranging from 6: 50 x 15 to 9:00 x 20. These sizes comprise approximately four-fifths of Ecuadoran tire imports at present. All tires will be made of six or more plies. About 75 percent of production will be for heavy trucks and pickup trucks, and the remaining 25 percent for passenger cars. 

Leather and Leather Products

Supply of hides and skins.-Leather is made commercially by some 20 tanneries located in 

Guayaquil, Quito, and Ambato, and also in Cuenca and Ibarra. Two of the largest tanneries, which probably account for nearly half of the total national output, processed 69,000 hides in 1954. Traditionally, domestic hides and skins are used, but, in recent years, the volume of local materials, particularly cattle hides, has declined. Some of the tanneries have had difficulty in obtaining adequate supplies. For example, the tanneries processed an estimated 158,000 hides in 1947; in 1954 total domestic production of hides was estimated by trade circles at between 125,000 and 135,000. The decrease in the availability of domestic hides has led to the restriction of exports of hides and the stimulation of leather imports. In 1955, imports of hides, skins, and their manufactures amounted to 53 tons valued at $250,000, the highest in the country's history. 

Kinds of leather produced.-Sole, split, and lining leathers are made. Small quantities of belting leather and some fancy or special leathers, including patent, suede, kid, glove, and bag, are also produced. All the sole leather is produced in the 

Costa; uppers and linings are turned out in both the Costa and the Sierra. Most of the fancy and special leathers are processed by the Sierra tanneries. With minor exceptions, the sole leather is vegetable-tanned, principally with domestic mangrove bark, and the upper and lining leathers are chrome-tanned, the chemicals for which are imported.

The machinery in use in the tanning industry is old and inefficient. Manual methods continue to play a large part in the manufacturing process. 

Tanneries. The largest tannery, Teneria La 

Iberia, in Guayaquil, which produces about 45 percent of all the sole leather and probably 30 or 35 percent of all the upper leather made in Ecuador, in 1954 processed 45,000 hides, of which 65 percent were converted into sole leather. The tannery employs about 100 workers. La Iberia also operates one of the most important vegetable oil plants in the country.

The other tanneries are small enterprises. One of the largest is Teneria ENIC, in Quito, a producer of upper leather and linings. ENIC has about 35 workers and in 1954 processed 24,000 hides. The plant uses high-quality hides obtained from the surrounding Sierra. In late 1954, production was concentrated in calf and a small quantity of suede. 

Leather shoes. An estimated 70 to 75 percent of the leather produced in Ecuador is used in the manufacture of footwear. Most of the remainder is used in a variety of manufactures, including 84

Manufacturing and Construction luggage, handbags, wallets, saddles, and novelties. rod A small amount is exported. 

Several factories and innumerable small shops manufacture footwear. Reportedly, output of the sfactories is substantially exceeded by that of the many individual shops and home-type enterprises, which produce nearly all of the high-grade women's shoes. According to some trade estimates in 

1954, handmade footwear annually accounts for about 60 percent of the more than 1 million pairs of shoes sold in the country. Although the factories have specialized essentially in producing low-priced shoes, the price differential in favor of the factory product has not been great enough to displace the handmade product, which, moreover, is made to order. Sar 194 mater res

Three factories produce the bulk of the machine-made footwear. By far the largest establishment is the Fabrica Nacional de Calzado, C. 

A., in Guayaquil, which produces more than 200,- 

000 pairs of shoes per year. The other two plants are the Fabrica de Calzado Ecuador, C. A., in 

Quito, and the Sociedad Manufacturera del Calzado, S. A., in Riobamba. The equipment in use in these factories is antiquated, for the most part, at and, as a result, productivity is low and operating costs are high. The Fabrica Nacional, for example, was reported in 1954 to be using machinery ed anywhere from 30 to 50 years old. Moreover, the the plant possessed no automatic processing equipestment.

Men's footwear constitutes the larger part of factory production, and the Goodyear welt is chiefly used in the manufacture of these products. 

Women's and children's shoes are generally made by the Mackay, Turn, or Stitchdown methods.

Domestic leathers as well as domestic rubber soles and heels are used by the factories, virtually all of the imported quality leathers entering into the production of custom-made shoes. The industry has no foreign competition in the domestic market, for, with occasional minor exceptions, imports of leather footwear are prohibited. 

Other leather products.-Other leather products are manufactured on a small scale and almost entirely on a handicraft basis. Many of these items, such as wallets, handbags, and cigarette cases, are designed for the tourist as well as the local trade and are produced and sold in small shops in the larger cities. Production consists almost entirely of consumer goods. Industrial items, principally transmission belting and hat linings, are imported. Similarly, some of the items produced locally, such as luggage, are augmented by imports of superior quality, although in only small quantities. Domestic leathers are used in local manufactures; metal accessories are imported. There is little use of artificial leathers. 

Stone, Clay, and Glass Products 

Cement, bricks, tiles, and ceramics are produced.

The cement industry, one of the leading and most efficient industries in the country, is expanding. 

In other lines, some important new manufacturing plants have entered production. Output continues to lag far behind requirements, however, and many kinds of items-most notably, glass manufactures-are not produced commercially. Increasing domestic demands have stimulated both official and private initiative in stone, clay, and glass products.

Cement. There are two cement plants in Ecuador: Cemento Nacional Rocafuerte, which was opened in 1935, and the Cemento Chimborazo, which was opened in 1956.

The Cemento Nacional Rocafuerte, a subsidiary of the holding company, Ecuadorian Corporation, is located in San Eduardo, Guayas, about 5 miles from downtown Guayaquil. The plant produces portland cement from materials that are obtained from the nearby limestone quarries owned and worked by Canteras Nacionales, C. A., another subsidiary of the Ecuadorian Corporation. Between 1935 and 1955, the cement plant increased its output from 13,642 to 146,287 tons. The reported value of 1955 production was 55 million sucres ($3.6 million). The plant has been enlarged in the past few years, and now has an annual capacity of about 200,000 tons. 

Despite the great increase in cement production by the Cemento Nacional Rocafuerte, imports have been required to meet demands, particularly in the Sierra, where scarcities have frequently raised prices to exorbitant levels. Imports of cement landed in Guayaquil in 1955, for example, totaled some 19,320 tons valued at $309,400.

The Cemento Chimborazo, located near Riobamba, was built primarily to supply the needs of the Sierra market. The plant represents an investment of about 47 million sucres ($3.1 million), most of which was provided by the Na tional Development Bank. The municipality of Riobamba, the Social Security Fund, the German firm MIAG, which supplied machinery, and some private individuals also invested in the enterprise. The plant, which started production in 

September 1956, has an annual capacity of 50,000 tons of cement. Extensive limestone deposits are situated near the plant. 

Two more cement plants have been considered for some time. In January 1955, the Industrias 

Guapan, S. A., was organized to develop plans and muster financial support for the construction of a cement plant at Guapan, Cañar, about 20 miles from Cuenca. In and around Guapan are deposits of exceptionally high-quality limestone and clay. Moreover, in nearby Biblian there are 85

large deposits of coal that could be used for fueling the plant. Although no tangible progress has been made on this project, the National Development Bank reportedly has been considering engaging an American engineering firm to make preliminary plans for the construction of the proposed plant.

A fourth cement company, Fabrica de Cementos Cerro Azul, C. A., was organized by private 

Ecuadoran capital in 1947. It was to build a cement plant on a site about 8 miles from Guayaquil. However, the company was unable to raise the requisite funds to carry out its plans and, instead, purchased a lime kiln with a capacity of 10 tons a day. The company still owns or holds options on limestone and shale deposits near the kiln that it operates, and has not abandoned its efforts to interest additional capital in the original project. Reportedly, U. S. capital has shown an interest in this project. 

Many small establishments manufacture cement products, particularly blocks, tiles, and pipes, for the building trade. The largest and leading plant is Industrias de Concreto Roca, in Guayaquil, which produces cement blocks and ready-mix concrete. In 1954 the firm expanded its capacity to include the manufacture of asbestos-cement products. At that time, its output amounted in value to about 50,000 sucres ($3,300) per week. The combined production of the cement-products industry is far below the requirements of the building trade. 

Brick and tile.-Brickyards are found on the outskirts of every community of importance in the Sierra. Nearly all of them make brick and tile by hand, and, in most cases, the quality of the products is not very high. One exception among the brickyards is the recently established Alfareria Nacional, S. A., in Quito, which represents an investment of 2 million sucres ($132,000).

This plant is equipped with 3 kilns and a grinding and pressing machine that can produce up to 40,000 bricks a day, and has ample storage space. In addition to bricks, the plant produces wall and floor tile and other clay manufactures. 

Some refractory brick is made in the new and still uncompleted Fabrica de Ceramica, in Riobamba, but as yet the amount produced is inconsequential in relation to national requirements. 

The Fabrica de Ceramica makes or is to make many other clay manufactures, the most important of which are floor tiles, glazed tiles, mosaics, and electrical and sanitary fixtures. The plant started on a small scale in mid-1954 and through early 1955 had produced only a very small quantity of refractory bricks, floor tiles, and mosaicsall, reportedly, of very good quality. In May 

1956, the plant installed a new kiln that will double the production of tiles from 16,150 to 33,000 square feet a month. Funds for the construction of this plant were provided by the National Development Bank, which invested 3.2 million sucres ($211,200) in the project. 

Many other plants produce tiles. One of the more important is the Cuenca firm Fabrica Rocafuerte, which can produce about 645 square feet of floor tiles per day, mainly for the Azuay, Cañar, and Loja markets. The plant also makes earthenware pipe and produces plaster of paris and ground and powdered marble. The marble and plaster of paris are sold throughout Ecuador.

The tile-manufacturing industry has not been able to satisfy domestic requirements, particularly for quality products. Manufactures have consisted largely of cement and earthenware tiles. 

Porcelain and most glazed tiles have been imported. Presumably, the Fabrica de Ceramica and the Alfareria Nacional, S. A., will increase the variety and improve the quality of production. 

Other manufactures of clay or earthenware are of little industrial importance. The crude production of small potteries supplies the clay kitchenware and tableware of most households. 

Homemade earthenware utensils are also in common use. Better quality crockery and other earthenware household utensils and objects are imported. In 1955, imports of these articles amounted to 783 tons valued at $296,900. The limited demands for porcelain wares are also filled by imports. 

Manufactures of stone.-Around Cuenca there are several marble quarries, and marble objects are made by several small shops in that city. In one or two of the shops the quality of work is exceptionally good, and some intricate items are produced. The objects for the most part, ashtrays, bookends, and similar items are marketed in 

Quito and Guayaquil. 

Glass and glass manufactures.-Flat, structural, and plate glass are not produced in Ecuador.

There is no important commercial production of bottles and other types of glass containers. Reportedly, the few attempts to develop this industry in the past failed because of the unsuitability of local raw materials. In Guayaquil, a few establishments produce mirrors from imported glass, and at least one firm manufactures a small quantity of glassware and household fixtures from imported raw materials.

In 1955, the country spent approximately $1 million on imports of glass manufactures. Imports of bottles alone through the port of Guayaquil in 1955 amounted to 2,296 tons valued at $376,000.

Metal Products 

Ferrous and nonferrous metals, apart from small amounts of gold and silver, required for domestic manufactures must be imported. Ecuador 86

Manufacturing and Construction has no steel mill or rolling mill. There is no production of aluminum, copper, or other nonferrous metals in primary forms for industrial use. Imports of metals and metal products amounted to $2.7 million in 1955 and $2.3 million in 1956. 

Iron and steel products.-Many small foundries and metalworking shops produce iron or steel manufactures to order. În general, they are equipped to undertake only simple jobs. A few comparatively large establishments, which are located in Quito or Guayaquil, produce iron or steel manufactures on a factory scale. Some of the more important items produced are office equipment, kerosene stoves, wire screening, rollup iron doors, nails, and structures for electrical equipment.

The largest manufacturing establishment is the 

Siderurgica Ecuatoriana, S. A., in Quito, which produces many different items, including beds, bedsprings, stoves, window frames, license plates, cabinets, commercial furniture, and a variety of gray-iron castings. The annual value of the firm's output in 1954 was said to average about 

3.6 million sucres ($235,000), and production was reported to be about equivalent to capacity. The company has since constructed an addition to its plant. 

Other important manufacturers of iron and steel products include the Quito firms Industrias 

Metalicas Lanford, S. A. (rollup iron doors); 

Ideal (wire screening); ATU-Articulos de

Acero, S. A. (a variety of products); and Fabrica - de Tapas Corona, S. A. (bottle caps). Guayaquil firms include the Steel Products Corp., which manufactures steel furniture, and the Fabrica de 

Clavos Guayas, S. A., the country's largest manufacturer of nails. The wire for making nails is imported, for Ecuador has no wiredrawing mill.

Such items as structural shapes for construction, hardware, screws, washers, tubes, pipes, and rails are not manufactured in Ecuador. Local production of bottle caps, nails, wire screening, and virtually all other manufactures is greatly increased or exceeded by imports. It is said that the total output of the local iron and steel products industry accounts for probably not more than 

20 percent of domestic sales. 

Nonferrous metal products.—With the major exceptions of aluminum ware, which is produced in commercial quantities, principally by the Fabrica de Aluminio UMCO, S. A., in Quito, and aluminum venetian blinds, produced by another Quito firm, Flexalum, the manufacture of nonferrous metal products is largely a handicraft trade. 

Silver jewelry and other items, such as cigarette cases, ashtrays, and jewel boxes, some of which are worked in delicate filigree, are produced in the 

Sierra and find a popular market, especially among tourists. Some locally produced gold also is used in the manufacture of jewelry. Copper and bronze manufactures are limited to small quantities of lighting and household fixtures.

Machinery and Vehicles 

No machinery, automotive vehicles, or related parts and accessories are manufactured in 

Ecuador. A few small shops in Guayaquil and

Quito repair automobile bodies and occasionally build crude bodies for buses. Automobile sales and maintenance, and automobile parts and accessories, are handled by a large number of importers and dealers, particularly in Quito, where there is a heavy concentration in this trade.

Machinery, vehicles, and instruments (principally medical, surgical, and scientific instruments, none of which are made locally) have consistently led all other Ecuadoran imports in value, and in 1955 reached a new high of $31.1 million. 

Printing and Publishing

Commercial facilities for job printing are found in practically all of the larger cities, but 

Guayaquil and Quito are by far the leading centers. Most newspapers accept job orders. The great majority of the printing shops are very small and poorly equipped; often they are operated in connection with stationery shops. Some of the larger establishments are reasonably well equipped and have machinery for multilithing and assembly. In both Guayaquil and Quito, a few shops do engraving and photoengraving, and a few are equipped for offset and photo-offset. work.

The largest of the printing shops is the 

Imprenta La Reforma, a Guayaquil firm with sales of about 11 million sucres ($726,000) a year. 

In addition to routine job printing, La Reforma also does lithographing and bookbinding, and its output includes commercial forms, labels, books, maps, calendars, and many other items. The firm also imports and sells stationery, office supplies, and printing materials and machinery. 

Among publishers, the leading commercial firm is Reed and Reed, a company established in 

Guayaquil in 1927 by a U. S. citizen. It has developed into an organization with stockholders in many countries. In 1955, the company's capital and reserves exceeded 10.3 million sucres ($680,000). Reed and Reed is the principal publisher of schoolbooks and textbooks. It publishes about 150,000 books a year. The company also imports, wholesales, and retails office sup- 87

plies and equipment, household appliances, radios, paper, and printing supplies. The firm maintains branch offices in Quito, Ambato, Manta, and Ibarra. 

A few small printing shops in Guayaquil, Riobamba, Cuenca, and Quito also issue publications; some of the newspapers occasionally publish books and magazines. Several Governmentowned plants publish official reports, bulletins, and other documents. The Government plants are unable to handle all official jobs, some of which are assigned to commercial shops. Reportedly, existing commercial capacity is unable to meet public demands.

Apart from the work produced by the leading firms, the printing and publishing jobs are poor in quality. The industry depends entirely upon imports for its materials and equipment. 

Construction 

With the growth in urban communities and in related commerce and industry, Ecuador has experienced a building boom in recent years. Expenditures on new construction during the years

To a very large extent, the building boom has been concentrated in Guayaquil, Quito, and Ambato. Ambato has undergone considerable reconstruction since the ruinous earthquake of 1949.

However, public works have accounted for the major part of the investments, and a large number of projects concerned with transportation, irrigation, and community services have been initiated or concluded in many different sections of the country. 

Headquartered in Guayaquil and Quito are several engineering and contracting firms that have played an important part in private and public construction. Among the largest and most important of these are Ingenieros Nacionales Con structores Asociados (INCA), Compania Naci onal Conaca, Compania de Construcciones AT- 

LAS, and Empresa Edificaciones Ecuatorianas. 

Some of the large-scale public projects, however, have been carried out by foreign companies. 

Service Industries 

Most service facilities are located in Guayaquil and Quito. In other cities there are few modern agencies for most types of repairs and maintenance. Both Quito and Guayaquil have some wellequipped and well-stocked shops to service automotive equipment. Most of these shops are owned by vehicle importers and dealers who service the equipment they sell. A few establishments, like 

Siderurgica Ecuatoriana, S. A., in Quito, undertake automobile repairs in their shops as a sideline to their principal business. Vehicle maintenance is a serious problem; only a few shops have the equipment and/or trained mechanics to make major repairs.

These limitations also apply, in general, to other types of maintenance and repair shops. Most of the foundries and machine shops are not well equipped, and most of their equipment is old. The best-equipped shops belong to some of the large companies, such as Compania Bananera del Ecuador, in Guayaquil, and to the Government-owned railway at Duran. These private and Government-owned shops have modern welding equipment. Services to shipping include a few small repair yards in Guayaquil, the largest of which can handle craft up to 200 gross tons on slipways. 

Both Guayaquil and Quito have several laundries and dry-cleaning plants, a few of which, such as Lavanderia y Tintoreria La Quimica in 

Quito and Lavadero Nacional in Guayaquil, are relatively sizable enterprises. Many of these establishments have entered business within the past few years, indicating the increasing demand for the services that they provide. In the main, however, household demands for commercial laundry services are sharply limited by the use of domestics who take care of the family wash. Outside the two leading cities, the demands are negligible.

Hotels have improved considerably in the past few years. The lack of suitable hotel accommodations often has been cited as one of the principal deterrents to the development of the tourist trade, although the Hotel Metropolitano in Guayaquil has long been regarded by many travelers as one of the best in Latin America. After the close of 

World War II, the Estrada family organized & 

firm, La Compania Hostelera de Guayaquil, to build and operate a chain of hotels. The company now has in service three well-designed and well-equipped hotels, the first of which was built in Playas in 1949, the second in Guayaquil in 

1953, and the third in Quito in 1954. New hotels 88

Manufacturing and Construction fe have also been built in Ambato, Cuenca, and Ibarra. In other centers, however, hotel facilities are generally poor.

The small shop is the predominant form of retail sales outlet, and most of the shops handle a very limited quantity and variety of goods. Quito has two so-called supermarkets, and Guayaquil has a few self-service stores. With the exception of a few shops in Quito and Guayaquil that deal in luxury goods, window and counter displays and other merchandising methods have not been highly developed, presumably because much of the shopping for kitchen and domestic necessities has customarily been regarded as a chore suitable only for servants. This attitude has been greatly modified in recent years, however, and greater importance is now attached to physical facilities and merchandising promotion. 470366-59- 89

CHAPTER IX Transportation and Communications 

Ecuador has greatly improved its transportation and communications facilities since the close of World War II. One of the major achieve ments has been the completion and improvement of highways linking the Sierra and the Costa, thus opening up new agricultural areas in the Costa and in piedmont areas of the Sierra. The 

National Government has invested a large part of its resources in highway construction. The Province of Guayas also has carried out a notable highway program.

Commercial aviation facilities and services have also been improved and expanded, appreciably easing the transport situation, particularly of passengers and mail, in a country where the great mountain barrier presents unusual difficulties for surface transportation.

The railway situation is less encouraging. The main railway artery, the Guayaquil-Quito line, has lost much money, and services have not kept pace with requirements. Recent reorganization of the railway administration promises some improvement. The railway system in early 1957 was extended to the Pacific port of San Lorenzo in northwestern Ecuador, but in other areas services have been curtailed and some lines have been or are about to be abandoned. 

Port facilities are acknowledged to be grossly inadequate, and plans have been drawn up to build a new port at Guayaquil and to undertake the installation of modern facilities at other leading ports.

Telecommunication facilities have been improved. New and efficient automatic telephone systems have been installed in Quito and Guayaquil, and some modern radiotelegraph and radiotelephone equipment has been placed in service. 

Throughout the country, however, the telecommunications facilities are insufficient, and most of the equipment in use is old and inefficient. 

RAILWAYS 

The railways are owned by the Government and are operated by the autonomous State Railways Company.

The railway system consists of 5 single-track lines with a total length of 696 miles. The railways, by miles of track and gage, are: 

Guayaquil-Quito-San Lorenzo.

Sibambe-Azogues.. 

Puerto Bolivar-Pasaje. 

Puerto Bolivar-Piedras. 

Bahia de Caraquez-Chone..

Miles of track 

Gage in inches 511 42 72 42 16 42 47 30 50 30 41488 A standard-gage line, 94 miles in length, formerly connected Guayaquil with Salinas, Guayas, but service over this line was abandoned at the end of 1953 because of heavy operating losses resulting from the diversion of traffic to a parallel highway. The tracks of this line have been raised, and some of the rails have been used to relay part of the Guayaquil-Quito line.

Five Railway Lines 

Puerto Bolivar-Pasaje; Puerto Bolivar-Piedras. 

The Puerto Bolivar-Pasaje line and the Puerto 

Bolivar-Piedras line, which are operated as the Consolidated Railways of El Oro, lie entirely within the Province of El Oro and are not connected with any of the other railways. Their combined extension is small and their tracks and equipment are in poor condition. The two lines serve principally the banana plantations in the hinterland of Puerto Bolivar, but they also carry many passengers.

Bahia de Caraquez-Chone.-The Bahia de Cara quez-Chone railway is also isolated and also in poor condition; service at present is limited to the use of autorail cars. The line extends only a short 91

distance from the coast into the interior of the Province of Manabi, and service over the railway is often disrupted by floods and washouts during the rainy season. The line is to be abandoned when a parallel highway now under construction is completed.

Guayaquil-Quito-San Lorenzo.-The Guayaquil- 

Quito railway, which was placed in service in 1908 and now constitutes the southern section of the 

Guayaquil-San Lorenzo railway, is regarded as one of the great engineering accomplishments in railroad history. Between its southern terminal, Duran, and Chimbacalle Station in Quito, the line follows a 280-mile route that traverses tropical lowlands in the Guayas River Delta, ascends steep mountains with gradients of 5.5 percent, reaches peak elevations of 11,841 feet in the Urbina Pass, at the foot of Mount Chimborazo, and 11,653 feet in the Province of Cotopaxi, and finally descends into Quito, which is over 9,200 feet above sea level. In one short stretch of 49 miles between Bucay and Palmira the line climbs 9,651 feet and along the way negotiates a double switchback carved out of solid rock on a mountainside near Alausi known as the 

Devil's Nose (Nariz del Diablo). 

Because of the terrain over which it is built, the railway is highly liable to damage from natural causes. In March 1953, for example, heavy mountain rains flooded and washed out sections of the line, and through service between Guayaquil and Quito was not restored until May. In

March 1956, a mud slide destroyed the railway repair shops at Sibambe and interrupted service on the Sibambe-Azogues branch line for 9 days.

At all times the rugged terrain poses difficult operating problems for the railway and makes for heavy operating costs. On the uphill climb from Guayaquil to Quito, the direction in which two-thirds of the freight traffic moves, payloads are stringently limited by the steep gradients, and running times are slow. The trip for fast freight trains usually takes between 19 and 20 hours. 

The Quito-Ibarra section of the Guayaquil- 

San Lorenzo railway was completed in 1929, and by 1952 the line had been extended beyond Ibarra for a distance of approximately 30 miles to the village of Carchi, Province of Carchi. In March 

1957, the 93-mile line connecting Carchi and San

Lorenzo, a Pacific coast town in the northern part of the Province of Esmeraldas, was completed after 5 years of work that had been frequently delayed by lack of funds. A long-time national ambition to link Quito and the northern interior with the Pacific Ocean by rail was finally ful filled. The Carchi-San Lorenzo section of the 

Quito-San Lorenzo line penetrates hitherto isolated and largely unexplored areas in the Provinces of Carchi and Esmeraldas. It is now open to traffic, but before it can accommodate heavy equipment, leveling and ballasting of the way must be carried out.

The Carchi-San Lorenzo line was constructed by two affiliated French firms, Comptoir Industriel et Agricole de Ventes a l'Etranger (CIAVE) and Entreprises Metropolitaines et Coloniales (EMC), under a contract signed in 1951 with the Autonomous Board of the Quito- 

San Lorenzo Railway (Junta Autonoma del Ferrocarril Quito-San Lorenzo). The contract called for completion of the railroad and installation of a modern port at San Lorenzo at a projected cost of 157.7 million sucres ($10.4 million), but the cost of the project to date has greatly exceeded estimates. Work on the port and harbor of San Lorenzo remains to be done. 

Sibambe-Azogues.-The line connecting Si bambe and Azogues is a branch of the Guayaquil- Quito-San Lorenzo railway. Azogues, the southern terminus of the branch line, is approximately 

19 miles from Cuenca. Cuenca is linked to the railroad by the Pan American Highway over which railway freight is transferred by truck. 

In early 1957, work was started on extending the 

Sibambe-Azogues line into Cuenca. Little prog ress has been made on this project, however, because of the large expense involved. 

Revenue Railway Traffic 

Volume of traffic.-In 1953-55, the most recent 

3-year period for which complete data are available, the railways carried 524,384 tons of revenue freight and 2,255,672 revenue passengers each year, on an average. Traffic movement during the period averaged 71.8 million freight-tonmiles and 68.2 million passenger-miles per year, with an average freight haul of 137 miles and passenger trip of 30 miles.

Composition of freight traffic.-In 1953-55, manufactures accounted for 46 percent of all railway freight tonnage; agricultural products, for 39 percent; and minerals, for 7 percent. The remaining 8 percent consisted of livestock, forest products, and other freight.

In 1955, the freight traffic comprised 263,005 tons of manufactures, 208,226 tons of agricultural products, 42,114 tons of minerals, and 40,473 tons of livestock, forest products, and other items.

Freight, 63 million sucres ($4.1 million); passengers, 12.3 million sucres ($812,000). Freight accounted for 83 percent of the traffic revenues in 

1953-55 and 84 percent in 1955.

The railway tariff system authorizes discriminatory rates to favor domestic manufactures over imports. Nationally produced textiles, for example, in 1955 were subject to a rate of 15.60 sucres ($1.03) as against 19.20 sucres ($1.27) for imported textiles. Some of the other commodities that were given preferential rate treatment over imports were beer, clothing, sugar, cigars and cigarettes, cocoa, matches, cookies and crackers, and malt. 

The Guayaquil-Quito line has to ferry freight between Duran, the southern terminus of the line, and Guayaquil, which are on opposite banks of the Guayas River. For the use of its river equipment, the railway in 1955 charged from 100 to 1,000 sucres ($6.60 to $66) per hour for tugboat service and from 50 to 600 sucres ($3.30 to $39.60) per hour for launch service, the specific rate depending upon the size of the tugboat or launch. 

Operating losses.-The railways have sustained heavy operating losses for several years. In 

1950-56, for example, the annual operating ratio (operating expenses divided by operating revenues) averaged 108.4 percent. In 1954-56, the ratio rose from 102.3 to 112.1 in 1955 and to 128.1 in 1956. Revenues in 1954–56 averaged 76.9 million sucres ($5.1 million) per year, while expenses averaged 87.2 million sucres ($5.8 million) annually. The accumulated operating losses and indebtedness of the railways in early 1957 reportedly exceeded 200 million sucres ($13.2 million). 

In part, the deficits have been caused by requiring the railways to subsidize some sections of the economy. For example, the railways have had to absorb the loss in carrying gasoline from the Costa to the Sierra at an unrealistic charge of 

0.57 sucre ($0.038) per gallon as against an actual cost to the carrier of 1.36 sucres ($0.09) per gallon. Similarly, they have been called upon to carry salt at 8 sucres ($0.528) per quintal as compared with a cost of 15.5 sucres ($1.02).

Operating expenses have been greatly increased by administrative and technical factors. At the close of 1955, there were 5,081 railway employees, and the payroll exceeded 46 million sucres ($3 million), a sum equivalent to more than 60 percent of total freight and passenger receipts. A large part of the motive power and rolling stock has usually been laid up for repairs, and the generally poor condition of the rails and way has retarded the utilization of rolling stock for revenue traffic. 

These two factors have seriously limited the railway's earning capacity.

Railway Administration 

The State Railways Company continues to administer the railways, but since December 1956, the Government has openly invited private enterprise to take charge of the industry. An emergency decree law issued in December 1956 assured Government assistance to any approved commercial enterprise formed to take over the railways. The Government presumably would retain a minority capital interest in such an enterprise. Up to the present, however, there has been no tangible expression of private commercial interest in operating the railways.

Meanwhile, the State Railways Company has attempted to reduce expenses and to improve services. Appreciable reductions in personnel have been made since 1956. Some new equipment, most of it ordered by the preceding railway administration, has been placed in service. The railways are still inadequately equipped to accommodate freight-traffic requirements and inadequately maintained to provide reliable and expeditious service. 

In November 1957, the World Bank authorized a loan of $600,000 to Ecuador for the State Railways Company. The loan is to finance imports of spare parts, tools, materials, and eight petroleum tank cars. In addition, the loan is to pay for the services of experts to assist in improving the administration of the railways.
