93

HIGHWAYS 

The highway system contains few roads that meet modern traffic standards, although the Government, particularly in recent years, has placed great emphasis on road construction and improvements. În 1950-54, for example, public expenditures on highways amounted to 387 million sucres ($25.5 million), a sum equal to approximately 25 percent of the value of all public investments. In 

1955 the Government's projected highway program contemplated the construction or improvement of some 1,300 miles of roads at a total cost of approximately 844 million sucres ($55.7 million), but rising costs and financial problems have since prompted the Government to revise the program and to concentrate on projects of the greatest or most immediate importance. 2 As of mid-1956 there were 6,200 miles of highways and secondary roads in service. Approximately 3,400 miles were open to traffic throughout the year; 2,800 miles were summer roads, open to traffic only in dry weather. Only a small part of the highway system is paved, most of the roads being built of macadam, cobblestone, or gravel and dirt.

Pan American Highway and Tributaries 

The backbone of the highway system is the Pan American Highway, which extends from Rumichacha, Carchi, on the Colombian border just north of Tulcan, to Macara, Loja, on the Peruvian border. In the south the highway divides into two branches, one via Catacocha, Loja, and one via

Cariamanga, Loja, which reunite at Macara. By way of Catacocha, the highway has a total length of 714 miles in Ecuador; by way of Cariamanga, 712 miles. Sections of the two branches of the highway near the Peruvian border are under construction, but transit into Peru is now accommodated by a dirt road over a portion of the Cariamanga-Macara route. The highway links the important centers in the Sierra and interconnects by branch roads with many leading Costa cities, including Guayaquil. 

Throughout its northern extension above the branch road to Riobamba, the Pan American

Highway is largely a narrow cobblestone strip with gravel or dirt shoulders. In the approaches to the major cities and over most of the 55-mile stretch between Quito and Latacunga, the highway is paved. Over its southern extension below the Riobamba connection, the highway is of macadam construction as far as Loja, below which construction work is now in progress. The highway 

2 Ministerio de Obras Publicas y Comunicaciones, Informe a la Nacion, 1955-1956, Quito, page 27. (Later official data are not available.) follows the Andean range and between Azogues in the south and Ibarra in the north parallels the railway. It is a narrow and dangerous highway, and in many sections it is liable to washouts and landslides. Tolls are collected at many points along the highway to finance maintenance work, which is performed with primitive means. Traffic over the highway, particularly between Quito,

Latacunga, and Ambato, has increased substantially in the past 5 years. Because of the high incidence of traffic accidents, the first road signs in Ecuador to promote traffic safety were placed along the highway between Riobamba and Ibarra in 1956. 

Quito-Quininde branch.-The Pan American 

Highway connects with several highways that lead into the Costa. In the north a 133-mile macadam highway links Quito with Quininde. The 

Quito-Santo Domingo section winds precipitously down the side of the Andes, and traffic is allowed to move in only one direction at a time. Between 

Quininde and Esmeraldas work has nearly been completed on a 60-mile highway which will permit through transit between Quito and Esmeraldas. 

Latacunga-Quevedo-Manta branch. Farther south, the Latacunga-Quevedo-Manta highway, 224 miles in length, was inaugurated in September 1956. The highway is paved throughout between Manta and Quevedo, but only a few short stretches of the Quevedo-Latacunga section have yet been paved. 

Ambato-Babahoyo, Gun-Duran, and Cuenca- 

Machala branches.-Three other important Costa tributaries of the Pan American Highway are the 

127-mile Ambato-Babahoyo, 87-mile Gun (Cañar 

Province) -Duran, and 117-mile Cuenca-Machala highways. All of these highways are undergoing further improvements but are open to traffic. 

Both the Ambato-Babahoyo and Cuenca-Machala highways at present contain extensive sections of dirt or gravel construction over which travel is hazardous in wet weather. Guaranda, an intermediate point on the Ambato-Babahoyo highway, is connected to Riobamba by a gravel road. South of Babahoyo a dry-weather road provides access to the Gun-Duran highway and thence to Guayaquil, but generally vehicles are ferried between Babahoyo and Guayaquil, both of which are situated on the Guayas River. A small part of the 

Gun-Duran highway has been paved, and plans are being made to pave the remainder. 

Costa Highways

Within the Costa, the best roads radiate out of Guayaquil, which is connected by paved highways with the Guayas communities of Daule, Progreso, Playas, and Salinas. The Guayaquil-Daule highway is a link in overland connections between 94

Transportation and Communications 

Guayaquil and the central Sierra via Quevedo and 

Latacunga. Traffic must be ferried across the

Daule River, although a bridge will soon be in service. North of Daule, an asphalt highway connects with the new Latacunga-Manta highway. 

Several other highway projects now in progress will greatly improve communications in the Costa and between the Costa and the Sierra. One of the most important of these is the 110-mile Santo Domingo-Chone highway, which will provide a link in the interconnection between the Latacunga- 

Manta and Quito-Esmeraldas highways. Work on this project is tentatively scheduled to be completed in 1960. In the Province of El Oro some preliminary work has been done on clearing the way for a highway designed to traverse the Costa from north to south along a route roughly parallel to the Pan American Highway. As presently envisaged, the highway will extend from Santo Domingo, Pichincha, in the north to Pasaje in the south, and will unite such intermediate points as 

Quevedo and Manuel J. Calle, Cañar. This, however, is a long-range project that will take many years to complete. Other important projects include the Alausi-Sibambe-Bucay highway, 46 miles in length, which is being macadamized. 

Loja-Zamora, Ambato-Puyo Highways 

To the east of the Pan American Highway, a road is now under construction between Loja and Zamora (Santiago-Zamora Province) and farther north some improvements have been carried out on the Ambato-Puyo highway, although traffic over this road was recently interrupted by heavy rains and washouts. For all practical purposes, however, the Pan American Highway marks the eastern limit of the highway system, for there are few roads capable of safely carrying motor-vehicle traffic eastward from the Andes.

Highway Finance and Contracts 

In addition to the work done by the Central Government, an important part of the highway program in the Province of Guayas and adjoining Provinces has been and is being undertaken by the Executive Highway Committee of the Province of Guayas, an autonomous body organized in 1945. Since its inception, the committee has built and/or maintained nearly 1,500 miles of roads, and to the end of 1955 had invested some 106 million sucres ($7 million) in highways. With an $8.5 million credit granted by the World Bank in 

1954, the committee launched a program to build 363 miles of paved roads and several bridges (including the one at Daule) in Guayas and neighboring Provinces. Some of these roads are now up in service, and others are approaching completion. The committee has been largely responsible for giving the Province of Guayas the best roads in the country.

The national highway program carried out by the Central Government has been financed by the Government itself, with the major exception of the Manta-Quevedo highway, which was financed largely by credits from the Eximbank totaling nearly $8 million. Government funds for the program have been derived from gasoline taxes and from budgetary appropriations. To help finance the work, the Government also issued dollar bonds to many contractors a few years ago, but this practice was stopped because of the inflationary effects it produced on construction costs. 

Because of the limited availability of domestic resources, however, the Central Government in September 1957 negotiated a loan of $14.5 million from the World Bank for the highway program. 

An amount of $11 million from the loan is earmarked for necessary imported goods and services in the construction of these highways: Que vedo-Santo Domingo-Aloag (Pichincha Prov ince), 145 miles; Chone-Santo Domingo, 100 miles; Chone-Bahia de Caraquez, 30 miles; and 

Duran-Cochancay (Cañar Province), 55 miles.

The last-named project is mainly reconstruction of a part of the present highway connection between Guayaquil and Cuenca.

Construction of these highways will open up large areas of fertile but undeveloped land. The projects are scheduled for completion in 4 years. 

The remaining $3.5 million of the World Bank loan will be used for new equipment, replacement parts for existing equipment, tools and materials for repair shops, office requirements, engineering fees, and a small part of the cost of a 4-year training program to develop an effective maintenance organization.

Contracts for the construction of the national highways, with few exceptions, have been carried out by Ecuadoran companies. One major exception is the Manta-Quevedo highway, which was recently completed by a United States firm, the J. A. Jones Construction Co. 

Registration and Traffic 

Motor vehicle registrations in 1956 totaled 

22,422, of which 5,151 were passengers cars, 1,145 were jeeps, and 16,126 were trucks. Twentythree percent of the vehicles were 1955 or 1956 models; 43 percent were 5 or more years old. 

Approximately 64 percent of the registrations were in the 2 Provinces of Guayas (8,340) and

Pichincha (5,908). The Provinces with the next largest registrations were Manabi (1,178) and 

For details concerning the dollar bonds, see chapter X. 95

Tungurahua (1,120). Gasoline consumption in 

1956 amounted to 50.3 million gallons, an increase of 103 percent since 1950. Guayaquil and Quito account for between 60 and 65 percent of the gasoline consumed in the country.

The large proportion of buses and trucks in the number of vehicle registrations suggests the importance of highway passenger and freight transportation. Trucks, some of which are used as buses, accounted for 72 percent of all registrations in 1956. Buses accounted for 13 percent of all registrations in 1955, the latest year for which data are available.

There are no data to show the volume of bus and truck services, but traffic movement by highway has steadily increased, largely at the expense of railways in competitive areas. The basic reason for the abandonment of the Guayaquil-Salinas railway, for example, was the diversion of traffic to the highway which paralleled the railway line. The Pan American Highway and the 

Guayaquil-Quevedo-Latacunga highway have diverted traffic from the Guayaquil-Quito railway. 

However, under present conditions, particularly in the Sierra, highway travel is uncomfortable and hazardous, and shipments must be restricted to comparatively light loads.

With the qualified exception of a few bus companies that operate fleets of small station-wagon type equipment in intercity service, the highway transport industry is not well organized. Most of the trucking services employ one or possibly two trucks. National regulations require that all motor vehicles undergo a safety inspection twice a year, but in most areas regulatory enforcement is perfunctory.

Bus lines in interprovincial service are under the jurisdiction of the Central Government, which establishes the regulations concerning rates and equipment. Ocean Shipping 

Ocean shipping carries most of Ecuador's foreign trade. In 1954-56, Ecuadoran ports handled an average of 843,700 tons of export cargoes each year. During the same period, imports through the port of Guayaquil, the only im- z portant port of entry, normally handling at least 90 percent of the volume of import traffic, annually averaged 332,400 tons.

Ocean carriers.-To accommodate this trade, a large number of ocean carriers under many flags z regularly call at Ecuadoran ports. Several carriers offer regular and frequent sailings from t 

United States Atlantic, Gulf, and Pacific ports to Ecuador.

A large part of Ecuadoran port traffic consists of company-owned or company-chartered vessels operated by the fruit-exporting companies, such as Compania Bananera del Ecuador (the Ecuadoran subsidiary of the United Fruit Co.), the 

Standard Fruit and Steamship Co., Compania

Frutera Astral, and Compania Frutera Sud 

Americana. The fruit company fleets generally provide passenger and cargo space on voyages inbound to Ecuador, but offer only limited passenger accommodations on outbound sailings. The fruit companies account for almost all of the traffic in the ports of Esmeraldas, Bahia de Caraquez, and Puerto Bolivar, and for much of the traffic in the port of Guayaquil.

Common-carrier services on a regular basis are offered by some 18 or 19 shipping_companies. 

American-flag carriers are the Grace Line, which operates to U. S. Atlantic and Pacific ports, and the Gulf and South American Steamship Co., which operates to Gulf ports. Other services to the United States are provided by the 

Chilean Line, the Chilean North Pacific Line, the

Knutsen Line, the West Coast Line, and the Flota Mercante Grancolombiana. Frequent European sailings are also maintained out of Guayaquil, and a cargo service is provided to Japan via the Pacific coast of the United States.

Trucking services are not regulated. Trucking is generally performed on an individual basis, the operators contracting to provide their equipment and services for each haul. Rates are determined by negotiation between trucker and shipper and vary considerably with carrier competition. On the average, trucking rates are said to be substantially higher than railway freight rates.

The Ecuadoran-flag merchant fleet, as of June 

30, 1957, comprised only 7 vessels with an individual gross of 1,000 tons or more. The fleet, 96

Transportation and Communications which included 6 freighters and 1 tanker, had a combined gross of 19,000 tons and a combined deadweight of 26,000 tons. In addition, many privately owned small craft are used in both international and domestic coastal trade. The 

Ecuadoran Government is a shareholder in the Flota Mercante Grancolombiana, the shipping line organized by the Governments of Colombia, Venezuela, and Ecuador in 1946. Venezuela withdrew from the enterprise in 1953, and the company was reorganized, with Colombia purchasing 80 percent of the capital stock and 

Ecuador 20 percent. The Flota Mercante Grancolombiana, along with Ecuadoran-flag shipping, receives preferential treatment in Ecuador's import trade; consular invoice fees for shipments on 

Flota vessels are fixed at 8.5 percent of the f. o. b. value in contrast with 9.5 percent for shipments i carried on foreign-flag ships. 

The Ecuadoran Government reportedly is interested in organizing a fleet for the bananaexport trade. Allegedly, the shipping space now available is completely utilized by the established producers and exporters, and further expansion of the trade has been handicapped by lack of shipping. The project under consideration is said to envisage participation by the Flota Mercante 

Grancolombiana and Ecuadoran producers and exporters. At present, Flota provides limited space for banana shipments out of Ecuador. 

Ocean freight rates. Following are a few examples of ocean freight rates applicable to shipments from U.S. Atlantic, Gulf, and Pacific ports to Guayaquil. The rates apply to tons of 2,000 pounds or 40 cubic feet: General cargo, $80; roadbuilding machinery, boxed, $43; automobiles, boxed, $24 from Atlantic and Gulf ports and $30 from Pacific ports. 

The following additional handling charges per ton are in effect in Pacific coast ports: General cargo, $1.90; roadbuilding machinery, $2.30; and automobiles, $2.50. 

Coastal and River Shipping 

Apart from a weekly steamer service from 

Guayaquil to Manta and Esmeraldas, coastal shipping services are operated on an irregular basis, with sailings arranged in accordance with cargoes offered. International voyages generally are undertaken as far as Peruvian ports to the south and Panamanian ports to the north. The trade is centered in Guayaquil and is carried on by several small operators. Domestic coastal shipping, which is also centered in Guayaquil, serves such ports as Puerto Bolivar, Salinas, Manta, Bahia de Caraquez, and Esmeraldas. 

'United States Maritime Administration, Merchant Fleets of the World, as of December 31, 1957, Washington, D. C., page 2. 470366-59- Only national-flag ships are permitted to offer domestic services. Cargo entering and clearing the port of Guayaquil in domestic coastal traffic has approximated 250,000 tons annually in recent years.

The rivers, particularly the Guayas River and its tributaries, also play an important role in domestic transportation. Traffic on the Guayas

River consists mostly of barges and rafts, which carry export commodities, principally bananas, downstream to Guayaquil. In the north, the banana plantations in the hinterland of Esmeraldas depend upon the Esmeraldas River to dispatch their shipments downstream to the ocean port. Throughout much of the Costa, rivers are the only means of transportation during the rainy season. 

Coastal and river freight rates are determined largely by negotiation, for there is no comprehensive system of rate regulation. Charges Auctuate in accordance with the demands for space. 

There is at present, however, a wide rate differential in favor of shipment by water as compared with shipment by truck. Freight charges per ton for shipment by coastal vessel from Manta to 

Guayaquil, for example, average 40 sucres ($2.64) as against an average of 180 sucres ($11.88) by truck. Ports 

None of the Ecuadoran ports have berths for oceangoing vessels, and in all ports ocean shipping must load and discharge by lighter. 

Port of Guayaquil. The port of Guayaquil is situated on the right bank of the Guayas River, about 35 miles from the Pacific Ocean. The river current is very strong at Guayaquil, and silt has long impeded deep-draft navigation. As a result, many of the large oceangoing vessels, including all 

Grace Line ships, do not ascend the river but stop at Puna Island, which is located at the mouth of the Guayas River, where passengers and cargo are transferred to or from launches. 

Within the port of Guayaquil only the Customs 

Wharf is equipped with cargo-handling gear, which consists of two lift cranes. Occasionally, heavy import items are handled direct from ship to railway cars aboard a railway barge in midstream and then dispatched through Duran, the railway terminal on the left bank of the river, without further handling. With very limited exceptions, however, all inbound shipments from abroad are deposited at the Customs Wharf.

Closed storage facilities in customs are grossly inadequate, and incoming shipments are commonly 

Rader and Associates, Report on Port Improvements at Manta, 

Manabi, Ecuador, Miami, Florida, October 1956, page 47. Prepared for the Ministerio de Obras Publicas y Comunicaciones del Ecuador. 97

piled in open yards and in streets behind the wharf. Delays in clearing shipments and the limited mechanical means available to handle cargo cause serious congestion, which is costly to both carriers and shippers.

Import shipments landed in Guayaquil are subject to a tax per ton of 105 sucres ($6.93) of which 

63 sucres ($4.16) is for handling and the remainder for the maintenance of other necessary port services. In periods of heavy traffic, carriers' expenses are further increased by delays resulting from harbor and wharf congestion. 

The inadequacies of the present port are to be eliminated by the construction of a new port, if present intentions materialize. After several years of controversy as to where and how such a port should be built, the Executive Highway Committee of the Province of Guayas, which has assumed responsibility for the undertaking, has decided in favor of a site about 6 miles from Guayaquil located on the Estero Salado, a saltwater inlet navigable from the Pacific Ocean.

The committee in 1956 engaged the services of a 

U. S. firm of consulting engineers to prepare economic and technical studies for the proposed port, and approached the World Bank for a loan to finance the port project. 

Other Ecuadoran ports.-None of the other 

Ecuadoran ports are equipped with mechanical gear for handling cargo, and all are open roadsteads. Esmeraldas and Puerto Bolivar are essentially banana-exporting ports. In 1954-56,

Esmeraldas handled 22 percent of all banana shipments as compared with 57 percent for Guayaquil, and Puerto Bolivar handled 21 percent. Banana exports through Puerto Bolivar have increased greatly in the past few years, shipments in 1956 having amounted to 5.3 million stems as compared with 1.7 million stems in 1953.

Manta is principally a coffee port, normally handling nearly two-thirds of the country's coffee exports, but it also handles a substantial volume of other exports, including ivory nuts, castor beans, frozen fish, kapok, and panama hats.

As in other ports, vessels load and discharge offshore.

Other leading ports include La Libertad and Bahia de Caraquez. La Libertad is an oil port and handles shipments of crude petroleum delivered by pipeline from the fields of the Anglo- 

Ecuadorian Oilfields, Ltd., in nearby Ancon, Guayas. Bahia de Caraquez is a small bananaexporting port. Of potential importance is the new port of San Lorenzo, the terminus of the recently completed railway across northwestern Ecuador. San Lorenzo has a well-protected harbor with deep-water approaches, but at present the port has but a small dock, which is able to accommodate only small craft. The Government plans to install modern facilities at San Lorenzo as funds become available.

Government plans also contemplate improvement of the other ports. To finance such a program, the National Congress in November 1955 established a special tax of 0.5 percent on the f. o. b. value of all imports and exports for the express purpose of constructing port facilities at 

Manta, Puerto Bolivar, Bahia de Caraquez, Esmeraldas, and the river port of Quevedo. Now under Government consideration are construction and improvement plans for the ports of 

Manta, Bahia de Caraquez, and Puerto Bolivar.

The plans were prepared by a United States → engineering firm.

COMMERCIAL AVIATION

Between 1951 and 1956, the volume of passenger traffic increased 71 percent, and the volume of freight carried increased 319 percent. 

Ecuadoran airlines. Several Ecuadoran airlines have been organized, most of them within the past few years. The leading national airline, and one of the oldest, having started services in 

1949, is Aerovias Ecuatorianas C. A. (AREA), which operates C-47's between Quito, Esmeraldas, Bahia de Caraquez, Manta, Portoviejo, Cuenca, and Guayaquil. The company formerly also offered a scheduled international service between 

Guayaquil, Quito, Panama, and Miami, but suspended this service for financial reasons in 1954. 

AREA carries about 50 percent of the domestic passenger and freight traffic.

Six Guayaquil-based carriers provide air services along the coast and through the interior of the Costa: Compania Ecuatoriana de Transportes Aereos (CEDTA), Vias Aereas Nacionales, S. A. 

(VIANSA), Servicios Aereos Nacionales (SAN), Lineas Aereas Nacionales, S. A. (LANSA), Aero lineas Ecuatorianas, S. A. (ALESA), and Com- 98

Transportation and Communications pania Ecuatoriana de Aviacion, C. A. (ECUA- 

VIA). All of these airlines use small aircraft, principally Cessnas, Pipers, and Stinsons, and operate largely on a nonscheduled basis. 

Two carriers, Transportes Aereos Orientales (TAO) and Vias Aereas Internas Orientales, S. A. 

(VIOSA), provide air services out of the Sierra into the Oriente. TAO maintains regular flights between Quito; Pastaza, Napo-Pastaza; Tena, Napo-Pastaza; Macas, Santiago-Zamora; and

Sucua, Santiago-Zamora. TAO also offers charter flights into the jungle. The airline also operates into Ambato and Cuenca in the Sierra and maintains a regular service into Esmeraldas in the 

Costa. TÃO has two aircraft, a Norseman and a Junkers 52.

VIOSA, which was organized in August 1956 and is based in Ambato, operates nonscheduled flights to Pastaza, Macas, Sucua, Tena, and other points in the Oriente. The company has two planes, a Ryan Navion and a Howard. 

Another national-flag carrier, the Compania Ecuatoriana de Aviacion, S. A., which was organized in 1957, recently started international services to Lima and Miami out of Guayaquil and 

Quito. The airline has two C-46's. 

All of the Ecuadoran airlines are owned by private Ecuadoran capital.

Foreign-flag airlines.-Eight foreign-flag airlines operate into Ecuador. Three of the carriers are United States companies: Pan American

Grace Airways (PANAGRA), Braniff Inter national Airways (BNF), and Aerovias Sud 

Americana, Inc. (ASA), a cargo carrier which inaugurated services between St. Petersburg, Fla.; 

Quito; and Guayaquil in February 1956.

The other foreign-flag airlines are the following: Aerovias Nacionales de Colombia, S. A. 

(AVIANCA), Royal Dutch Airlines (KLM), the 

Honduran airline, Transportes Aereos Nacionales,

S. A. (TAN), the Chilean carrier, Sociedad de 

Transportes Aereos Limitada (ALA), and the 

Panamanian company, Aerovias Panamenas (APA). In early 1958, two other foreign airlines, AIR FRANCE and SWISSAIR, were planning to initiate services to Ecuador. 

All of the foreign-flag carriers serve Guayaquil.

PANAGRA, ASA, and AVIANCA also serve 

Quito. International traffic in 1955 totaled 52,340 passengers and 8,556 tons of freight. PANAGRA carried 77 percent of the traffic.

Ecuador is one of the few countries that permit foreign-flag airlines to carry domestic traffic. 

PANAGRA enjoys this privilege and has a network of DC-3 services linking Guayaquil, Quito, Cuenca, Manta, and Esmeraldas. The company's 

DC-6 international flights also carry domestic traffic between Quito and Guayaquil. 

PANAGRA generally handles about 25 percent of the domestic traffic. 

Airports. There are some 45 airports in Ecuador, but the majority are little more than open fields capable of handling small aircraft and usable only in dry weather. All of the Provincial capitals except Guaranda, Azogues, and Zamora are served by airports which, apart from the airport serving Babahoyo, can accommodate DC-3's. 

The two principal airports, the Simon Bolivar 

Airport in Guayaquil and the Mariscal Sucre Airport in Quito, are adequate to handle DC-7's and are the only airports used in scheduled commercial international services. Both of these airports have been improved, paved runways having been laid down, and new terminal buildings are being constructed. Flights into Quito are frequently canceled in periods of bad weather or poor visibility because of the mountains in the approaches to the airport. The Guayaquil airport is the only airport in Ecuador that operates at night, handling a substantial flow of international through-traffic. Other leading airports are those serving Cuenca, Manta, Portoviejo, Esmeraldas, Ambato, and Ibarra. All airports in commercial service are owned by the National Government or the respective municipal government. 

Customs and immigration services are maintained at the airports in Guayaquil, Quito, and Esmeraldas. 

Airport construction and improvement have become prime objectives as traffic has increased. In the period September 1, 1956-June 5, 1957, the 

Bureau of Civil Aviation spent 14.4 million sucres ($950,400) on airport planning, construction, and maintenance. To help finance the improvements at the Quito and Guayaquil airports, the Ecuadoran Government was granted Eximbank credits totaling $3,680,000, of which $2,780,000 was for paving and extending runways and $900,000 was for constructing terminal buildings. Contracts for the work at these airports were let to two U. S. firms, the Smith Engineering and Construction Co. and the Airways Engineering Corp.

Other projects recently completed include a new airport at Salinas and new landing strips at 

Manta, Portoviejo, and Santo Domingo.

COMMUNICATIONS 

Telephones

In 1957, the Ministry of Public Works and 

Communications reported that 16,970 telephones were in service in Ecuador. This represented an average of slightly over 4 instruments for every 

1,000 inhabitants. Most of the telephones in use are of the automatic, or dial, type. 

Cities with telephones.-Telephones, which are limited to the larger cities, have been installed in 

Quito, Guayaquil, Cuenca, Riobamba, Ambato, Latacunga, Manta, Ibarra, and a few other centers. 

Services in many of these cities have been curtailed or interrupted from time to time by faulty equipment and technical failures.

The most modern and efficient telephone systems are in Guayaquil, Quito, and Cuenca. These three cities have dial systems that were installed by the Swedish firm L. M. Ericsson. The first automatic system in the country was installed in Cuenca in 1949, and now has about 1,000 telephones in service. It is municipally owned. 

The Quito and Guayaquil telephone systems are by far the largest in the country. In 1957, the 

Quito system had about 7,800 telephones and the 

Guayaquil system, about 5,800. In both cities, however, demands far exceed present services, and waiting lists for telephones are long. Both systems are owned by the Government and operated under the jurisdiction of the Ministry of Public 

Works and Communications. The automatic sys tem in Quito, operated by the Empresa Telefonos

Automaticos de Quito, has been in service since 1950 and has been steadily expanded. In April 

1956, the Government signed a contract with L. 

M. Ericsson to supply and install 5,000 new telephones in Quito within a period of 42 months at a cost of $2,256,800. 

The automatic telephone system in Guayaquil, which is operated by the Empresa de Telefonos 

Automaticos de Guayaquil, was inaugurated in

October 1955. It replaced the manual system of the Compania Nacional de Telefonos, a subsidiary of the British-owned Cable and Wireless Co.,

Ltd., which had serviced the city. In August 

1956, the Ecuadoran Government approved a contract with L. M. Ericsson to install 1,000 additional telephones in Guayaquil at a cost of $394,000.

Magneto and battery equipment is used by the telephone systems in other cities. The plant and service facilities are old and urgently in need of replacements. The systems are owned and operated by the municipalities. 

Telephone rates.-Monthly rates for local telephone service are low by United States standards. 

In Quito, for example, monthly telephone rates, by type of service, in 1956 were: Residential, 30 sucres ($1.98); professional, 45 sucres ($2.97); and commercial, 75 sucres ($4.95). These were basic rates that covered a monthly maximum of 

150 residential calls, 250 professional calls, and 

400 commercial calls. 

Installation charges for new telephones are high, however. In Guayaquil, they amount to nearly $50, part of which serves as a returnable deposit; and in Quito, they amount to more than $69. 

Radiotelephone.-Long-distance telephone communications are handled by radiotelephone. A 

Government-owned radiotelephone network connects the cities of Quito, Guayaquil, Cuenca,

Manta, Bahia de Caraquez, Esmeraldas, and Loja. 

Domestic radiotelephone facilities are also operated by the railways and are open to public use. 

International radiotelephone facilities are operated by the Government-owned Radio Internacional del Ecuador. It provides direct communication with the United States, where connection is made with the American Telephone and Telegraph Co. 

Telegraph and Cable

The telegraph system is owned by the Government and operated by the Ministry of Public 

Works and Communications. The landline sys tem in 1955 had 8,650 miles of line and connected 262 telegraph offices. Much of the equipment in use is in poor condition.

The landline system is supplemented by the radiotelegraph, which in many areas is the only means of telecommunication. In 1955 there were 

41 radiotelegraph stations. The Marconi Wire less Telegraph Co., Ltd., in early 1958 was installing a modern multichannel radiotelegraph and radiotelephone system connecting Quito with 

Guayaquil, Latacunga, Ambato, Riobamba, and 

Cuenca. International radiotelegraph services are provided by Radio Internacional del Ecuador. 100

Transportation and Communications 

Telegraph rates for domestic service were increased in early 1957 from 0.30 to 0.40 sucre per word. 

All American Cables and Radio, Inc., provides cable service to all parts of the world through its cable connections at Salinas. 

Radio and Television

Radiobroadcasting stations are located in some 

20 cities and towns. About 90 stations are in operation, more than half of which are in Quito and Guayaquil. All of the leading commercial centers have at least one station. Most of the stations have between 100 and 250 watts; only a few have 1,000 watts or more. The most powerful station, HCJB (50,000 watts), is located in

Quito and is operated by the World Radio Mission Fellowship. Aside from about 10 stations owned and operated by the National and municipal governments, the largest of which is the National Government's Radio Casa de la Cultura 

Ecuatoriana (1,000 watts) in Quito, broadcasting facilities are privately owned and operated under Government license.

Radio is not highly developed, although it has become an important medium of advertising. 

There is no network transmission, and the effective range of the commercial stations in operation is limited. Radio programs on commercial stations consist principally of music and news interspersed with advertising. Advertising rates, which vary among stations, are reasonable. 

The number of radio sets in Ecuador is not known. In 1950, the number in use was estimated at 50,000. Additions since 1950 minus old sets that have been discarded probably make the total number in use about 100,000. The receiving sets are concentrated largely in Guayaquil, Quito, Riobamba, Ambato, Cuenca, and a few other urban centers. Guayaquil and Quito account for most of the sets in use. Most of the sets plug into an electric outlet, but battery sets are in common use in rural areas. 

Television has not yet been introduced in Ecuador, but considerable interest has been expressed in establishing transmitting facilities. The 

World Radio Mission Fellowship has petitioned the Government for permission to open a television station in Quito, and a U. S. firm is reported to have indicated its intention to install a television station in Guayaquil. It is generally believed, however, that there is little likelihood of establishing the television industry within the near future.

Publications Daily newspapers are published in several cities, but only the leading Guayaquil and Quito dailies have a circulation in excess of 20,000 copies. The 3 most widely read newspapers, by daily circulation, are: El Universo, Guayaquil, 39,000; El Comercio, Quito, 27,000; and El Telegrafo, Guayaquil, 24,000. In other cities, the maximum circulation of any one newspaper reportedly does not exceed 5,000 copies, and most of the newspapers circulate 1,500 to 2,500 copies.

All newspapers are published in the Spanish language.

The basic rate for advertising in El Universo and El Comercio is $1.35 per inch on weekdays and $1.50 on Sundays.

Only a few magazines are published in Ecuador, and none of them circulate a very large number of copies. The most popular magazines of general interest are imported. 101

CHAPTER X 

Public Finance, Money, and Prices

PUBLIC RECEIPTS AND EXPENDITURES 

The public sector of the Ecuadoran economy (the Central and Provincial Governments, municipalities, and autonomous entities) accounts for a substantial share of the national income and gross national product. Excluding cash balances and external loans, net receipts of the public sector in 1955, the most recent year for which information is available, amounted to 2,154.6 million sucres, equivalent to 23.5 percent of the national income and to 20 percent of the gross national product. Net expenditures in the same year, excluding cash balances but including net external borrowing, totaled 2,269.6 million sucres, an amount equal to 25 percent of the national income and to 21 percent of the gross national product.

The public sector has expanded its role in the general economy in recent years. Between 1950 and 1954 for example, per capita expenditures of the Central Government, in terms of 1950 prices, increased from 181 to 298 sucres, or 65 percent.

In 1950-53, per capita expenditures of the Central and local governments combined, also in terms of 1950 prices, increased from 281 to 340 sucres, or 

21 percent.¹

Receipt and Disbursement of Funds The most striking characteristic of public finance in Ecuador is the manifold dispersion of financial resources. In addition to the Central 

Government, Provincial governments, and municipalities, some 600 separate and autonomous entities share in the receipt and disbursement of public funds. Some of these entities, such as the social security institutes, the National Securities 

United Nations, Economic Survey of Latin America, 1955, United Nations, N. Y., 1956, Document E/CN 12/421/Rev. 1, 1956, page 116, table 8.

Commission (Comision Nacional de Valores), the development banks, the Executive Highway Committee of the Province of Guayas, and the National Economic Council, play an important part in the economy of the country. A very large number, however, are strictly local or narrowly specialized in scope; for example, they engage in the construction of schools and churches and in the beautification of cities; they supply health, welfare, and cultural services; and they administer agricultural centers, universities, irrigation systems, municipal facilities, and sports organizations.

This dispersion of public funds, which has supported a profusion of programs and projects conceived and launched without reference to coordinated planning on a national, regional, or local basis, has long harassed the Central Government. As far back as 1916, for example, 79 percent of the revenues collected by the Central Government was assigned to autonomous entities and special accounts, and the amount reserved to the Central Government was inadequate to meet general administrative expenses.2

Attempts to centralize control of a larger share of the public revenues and expenditures have met with limited success. The Central Government in 1954 created the National Board of Economic Planning and Coordination to establish priorities for national projects and to review and coordinate the investment projects of the National and local governments and autonomous entities. Since its inception, the Board has carried out some valuable studies but has not yet become an effective instrument of coordination.

Local and regional interests have continued to resist the centralization of fiscal resources and fiscal responsibility, and have prevented comprehensive budgeting of national income and the execution of a consistent national fiscal policy.

Prices rose about 17 percent in 1950-55, and in terms of 1950 prices, revenues in 1955 were approximately 57 percent greater than in 1950. During the period, public revenues increased from 

19.5 percent of the national income to 23.5 percent.

Distribution of revenues, by type of authority.- 

The relative share of the Central Government in the revenues averaged 37 percent for the period 1950-55, but increased from 32 percent in 1950 to 40 percent in 1955. The autonomous entities received nearly 45 percent of all revenues in 1950-55, but their share declined from 49 to 42 percent between 1950 and 1953, after which it increased to 

43 percent in 1954 and to 44 percent in 1955. The municipalities received slightly less than 17 percent of all revenues, and the Provinces 2 percent, with narrow fluctuations in the shares of both authorities.

Revenues of the autonomous bodies consist in large part of assigned taxes that are set by law. 

Consequently, the funds available to many independent entities often bear no realistic relationship to requirements, and in some cases entities and the taxes which support them have continued to exist after the expiration of the need for which they were created. Provisional data for 1954 indicate that taxes applied and collected by the autonomous entities and local governments accounted for 75 percent of their ordinary receipts as against 25 percent in the form of subsidies

Import duties are by far the most important single source of public revenue. Consumption taxes, which yield a large part of the receipts from indirect taxes, include excise taxes on articles of mass consumption. Among these articles are the products marketed through the state monopolies salt, tobacco, matches, and alcoholic beverages. The Central Government incurred an annual deficit and accounted for most of the public deficit in 1950-55. On the other hand, the autonomous entities reported a surplus each year, with the exception of 1953, and accumulated a sizable surplus. 

In practice, cash deficits of the Central Government are made up in part by transfers of cash surpluses from autonomous entities. 

Budget of the Central Government 

The budget of the Central Government, which is the Ecuadoran national budget, consists of three different budgets: (1) The operations budget, which covers operating expenditures of the Cen- 

Junta Nacional de Planificacion y Coordinacion Economica, 

Informe Anual, 1956-1957, Quito, 1957, pages 19 through 24. 105

tral Government and is financed by tax revenues and the profits of state enterprises; (2) the capital budget, which covers investment expenditures of the Central Government and some of the state enterprises and is financed by tax revenues, transfers from the other two budgets, and loans; and (3) the annexed budgets of certain state enterprises, which cover operating expenses and are financed by operating income and loans. The finances of the Central Government also include a fourth component. That component is not treated as an integral part of the consolidated budget. It consists of "special accounts" that cover individual and additional commitments and that receive their money from specific taxes. The consolidated budget and the special accounts cover calendar-year operations.

Cash receipts and expenditures.-The distribution of cash receipts and expenditures of the Central Government, by budgets and special accounts for 1952-56, is shown in table 45. These 

Table 45.-Cash Receipts and Expenditures of the Central Government, by Budgets and Special Accounts, 1952-56 

[Millions of sucres]

Particularly in the past few years, the annual budgets have undergone substantial revisions through deficiency appropriations in the form of special allocations and additional interbudgetary transfers. As the result, the effective budgets have been appreciably different from the budgets originally adopted by the Congress. Moreover, the accounting procedures have been changed.

Since 1955, for example, the total budgets of the state monopolies have been included in the budget of the state enterprises.

More than two-thirds of the total expenditures in 1956 and in the first 2 months of 1957 were in 5 areas of activity, as follows, by percent of total expenditures: Defense, 20; public works, 18; education, 11; subsidies, 10; and state monopolies, 9.

A sharp rise in subsidy payments in 1954 and subsequent years resulted from incorporating, in the budget of the Central Government, 36.7 percent of the customs-duty collections allocated by law to specified autonomous entities.

PUBLIC DEBT 

At the close of 1956, the total public debt, with internal obligations converted to dollars at the free exchange rate of 17.40 sucres per $1 for highway bonds and at the official rate of 15.15 sucres per $1 for all other internal obligations, amounted to $90.9 million. This amount was equivalent to almost 15 percent of the estimated 1956 national income of 9,406 million sucres ($620.9 million at the official rate of exchange).

External debts of the Central Government were reduced by 27 percent in 1953 and by 32 percent in 1955. These reductions were really bookkeeping transactions, however, for they were based upon the settlement for liquidation of the longin-default British credits-Guayaquil-Quito railway bonds, Condor Bonds, and Salt Certificates.

In 1953, the Ecuadoran Government and the Council of Bondholders of London reached an agreement reducing Ecuadoran indebtedness on these 

Banco Central del Ecuador, Memoria, 1954, Quito, table 80; 

Memoria, 1955, Quito, tables 97 and 98; and Memoria, 1956, Quito, table 91. bonds and certificates by $11.6 million and providing for the resumption of interest and principal payments in 1954. Technical and legal details necessitated additional negotiations before payments could be made, however, and, by an agreement signed in May 1955, the outstanding debt was further reduced to $7.3 million, exclusive of interest, as compared with $33.3 million in principal and interest carried in the accounts of the Central Government as of the end of 1952. 

External debts of public entities other than the Central Government increased 150 percent in 1950-56 and represented 45 percent of the total foreign debt in 1956 as compared with 18 percent in 1950. Repayment of most of these loans is guaranteed by the Central Government.

Components. The components of the external debt, including principal and interest, at the end of 1956, were, în million dollars: British bondholders, 7.1; Eximbank, 26.2; World Bank, 2.9;

Comptoir International d'Achats et de Venets a l'Etranger (CIAVE), 7; Colombian Federation of Coffee Producers, 3.1; Defense Supplies Cor poration, less than 0.1; L. M. Ericsson, 0.4; Federal Republic of Germany, less than 0.1; and

United States Government, 2.2.6 

Eximbank loans accounted for 53 percent of the total foreign debt at the end of 1956. These loans were applied to several different projects, including the debt consolidation of 1950; construction of the Quevedo-Manta highway; installation of water supply systems in Quito, Guayaquil, Ambato, and Guano (Chimborazo); improvement of the Quito and Guayaquil airports; purchase of railway equipment; and the maintenance of highways. Second in size among the foreign obligations were the British credits. Third was the obligation of CIAVE, the French company responsible for the construction of the new railway to the port of San Lorenzo.

The debt to the World Bank consisted of drawings under the $8.5 million credit authorized for the Executive Highway Committee of the Province of Guayas in 1954 and under the $5 million credit authorized for the Empresa Electrica de

Quito, S. A., in 1956. The United States Govern ment loans comprised a disbursement of $1.7 million of a $3.1 million loan authorized under the 

Mutual Security Act and the Agricultural Trade Development and Assistance Act in 1956 plus a credit of $0.5 million. The other loans included credit granted by the Colombian Federation of

Coffee Producers to the National Development 

Bank for Ecuadoran participation in the capitalization of the Flota Mercante Grancolombiana and credit granted to the cities of Quito and Guayaquil by the Swedish firm L. M. Ericsson for automatic telephone equipment. • Banco Central del Ecuador, Memoria, 1956, Quito, page 122. 107

Servicing. Servicing of the foreign debt, exclusive of suppliers' credits, in 1951-56, amounted to the equivalent of $23.3 million, a sum equal to 

4.6 percent of the dollar value of foreign exchange receipts of the Central Bank during those years. In 1956, servicing cost $4.5 million, or 

4.9 percent of the Central Bank's foreign exchange receipts during the year.

Default. Much of the foreign debt was in default for many years. The largest part of this defaulted debt was the British-held portion. 

Prior to the resettlement of the British-held obligations, service on the obligations had not been effected since 1929.

Two French-held bond issues to finance the construction of a railway in Ecuador have been in default since 1913, when the Ecuadoran Government disclaimed any direct obligation to the bondholders. In 1955, the French Government requested, and the Ecuadoran Government indicated its willingness to consider, settlement of the debt for approximately $140,000. As far as is known, however, settlement has not been made. 

Eximbank and World Bank loans. The published data of the Central Bank on the foreign debt include only disbursed funds and accrued interest. Data regarding Eximbank and World

Suppliers' credits.-Suppliers' credits, a form of external debt, are of recent origin. These credits are advanced to public entities by foreign suppliers, preponderantly European suppliers, to promote the sale of materials, equipment, and/or services. They are usually extended on a mediumor long-term basis at an annual interest of 5 to 

6 percent. Inasmuch as the credits carry repayment obligations in foreign exchange, they directly affect external public indebtedness, and the Central Bank has become concerned about their impact on the country's financial and economic situation. 

Suppliers' credits outstanding at the end of 1954 totaled $3.1 million and increased to $5.7 million by year-end 1955. In 1956, however, there was a sharp decline in new credits, and the balance outstanding at the close of the year was $4.9 million. Redemption of suppliers' credits in

National highway bonds.-The national highway bonds, commonly referred to as dollar bonds, constitute a special class of internal obligations.

In 1954, the Central Government began to issue dollar bonds to finance 50 percent of the cost of the national highway program. The payments were made half in cash and half in 5 percent, 10year bonds repayable in dollars calculated at the exchange rate of 17.40 sucres per $1.

The value of the highway bonds delivered to contractors by the end of 1956, converted at the applicable rate of 17.40 sucres per $1, amounted to $6.4 million. 

Issuance of the dollar bonds, which is really an attempt by the Central Government to force savings out of the private sector of the economy, has given rise to serious concern in some official quarters. On the surface, the bonds, serviced in dollars at an attractive rate of exchange, appear to offer an incentive for private investment. Actually, however, few highway contractors are able to provide long-term credit to the Central Government, and the necessary acceptance of such bonds has induced increases in contract costs to offset anticipated losses in discounting the bonds for cash. Reportedly, this is precisely the situation that has developed, and the bonds have become a serious potential threat to the nation's credit both at home and abroad.

Servicing. Servicing of the internal debt, including highway bond service, in 1956 amounted to 132 million sucres, or 1.4 percent of the estimated national income. 

Highway bond service in 1956 cost 13.9 million sucres, and in 1954-56 totaled 23.8 million sucres.

CURRENCY AND MONEY SUPPLY 

Currency 

The monetary unit of Ecuador is the sucre, which is divided into 100 centavos. The sucre has a par value of 0.0592447 grams of fine gold, which may be changed in accordance with the obligations assumed under the terms of membership in the 

International Monetary Fund. The currency comprises coins and banknotes issued by the Central Bank to which the right of issue is exclusively reserved by law. The coins are of nickel in values of 5, 10, and 20 centavos and 1 sucre.

The banknotes are in denominations of 5, 10, 20, 50, 100, 500, and 1,000 sucres.

A multiple exchange rate system is in effect, consisting of an official rate, a free rate, and mixing rates for certain exports. The official rate, which has been in effect since December 1, 1950, is 

15 sucres per $1 for buying foreign exchange and 15.15 sucres per $1 for selling. The official rate applies to essential imports, to most exports, to registered foreign capital, and to certain other officially approved transactions. The free rate, which averaged 16.38 sucres per $1 (buying rate) and 16.43 sucres per $1 (selling rate) in January 

1958, applies to nonessential imports, to the subsidization of some minor exports, to unregistered capital movements, to many service items, and to mixing arrangements for certain exports, such as bananas and pharmaceuticals.

Until March 1957, there was, in addition to the official and free markets, a regulated free market in which the Central Bank maintained a buying rate of 17.30 sucres per $1 and a selling rate of 

17.40 sucres per $1.

Payments of any kind to be executed in Ecuador must be made in sucres. Obligations that stipulate payment in gold, silver, or foreign currency or exchange shall be settled and paid in sucres on the basis of the legal conversion rate in effect at the time the obligations are assumed. Certain exceptions are permitted. These include international payments, external remuneration for temporary services performed within the country, minor transactions by tourists and other travelers, insurance premiums and indemnities stipulated in contracts if they conform with the regulations of the Monetary Board, bank deposits in foreign currencies or exchange, and bonds issued by the Government or the Central Bank if monetary policy so demands. Transactions in the free exchange market are also excepted. 

Money Supply

Between the end of 1950 and the end of 1956, the money supply, or means of payment, in the hands of the public increased from 915 million sucres to 1,357 million sucres, a rise of 48 percent (see table 49). Internal sources (increased bank credit and public spending) accounted for 42 percent of the money supply at the close of 1950 and 71 percent at the end of 1956. 1950

The money supply declined 6 percent in 1955 from 1954 as the result of the contractive effects of balance-of-payments deficits and the institution by the Monetary Board of advance deposit requirements on some imports. Further contraction continued through the first half of 1956, but in the second half of the year money supply expanded owing to a recovery in foreign reserves combined with increased means of payment from internal sources. 

During 1956 the money supply increased to 

1,363 million sucres in September and October, after which it contracted slightly. Its contraction continued at an accelerated pace through the first 5 months of 1957, principally as the result of reduced means of payment of internal origin, but in June 1957 the trend was reversed and the money supply subsequently rose to 1,335 million sucres in August as compared with the lowest 

1957 level of 1,230 million sucres in May.

Compensatory credit operations of the Central 

Bank expanded to offset in part the contraction of private bank operations in 1955 and development bank credit in 1956. The sharp drop in private bank operations in 1955 was induced by the decline in foreign trade and the deficit in international payments; the contraction in development bank credit in 1956 was attributable to the lack of available loanable funds (see table 50).

The cost of living for workers in 1956 compared with 1955 declined by 4.7 percent in Quito and 1.5 percent in Guayaquil. The principal categories of price decline in Quito were food and various expenditures; the only category of substantial price increase was fuel and light. In

Guayaquil, lower prices for fuels, clothing, and food more than offset price increases for housing, water and light, and miscellaneous requirements. 111

CHAPTER XI Financial Institutions 

Internal credit facilities include the banks, social security agencies, and insurance companies. 

Although they have increased appreciably in recent years, transferable savings are small, and loans and investments are concentrated largely in short-term credit and high-yielding mortgage bonds for which there is a ready demand. 

The private banks, which are the principal source of credit, are essentially commercial banks with small and conservative investment portfolios. 

The development banks, which are undercapitalized and must depend upon Central Bank rediscounts for most of their loanable funds, are forced by lack of resources to follow a short-term lending policy and are unable to meet requirements for medium- and long-term development credit. The social security agencies and, to a lesser extent, the insurance companies are important sources of short-term credit to their members and clients.

The social insurance funds have also provided appreciable credit to private business, but mostly on a short-term basis and against highly liquid collateral.

In the absence of a capital market for longterm development investment, despite Government efforts to organize such a market, retained private earnings are a primary source of private investment.

THE BANKING SYSTEM 

The Ecuadoran banking system comprises the

Central Bank of Ecuador (Banco Central del 

Ecuador), development banks (bancos de fomento), and private banks (bancos privados).

The banking institutions are subject to the control of the Monetary Board, which is responsible for national monetary policy, and the Superintendency of Banks, a technical and autonomous agency charged with the supervision of operations in all public and private banks. A third official entity, the National Development Bank (Banco Nacional de Fomento), is responsible for the control and overall administration of the development banks. 

Central Bank of Ecuador

The Central Bank of Ecuador, organized as a private institution in 1927, became an organ of the state under the Monetary Law of 1948. Ownership of the bank is vested in the National Government and the private banks. Authorized capitalization of the institution is 20 million sucres, of which 15.6 million sucres had been subscribed as of December 31, 1957, as follows: Banks, 13.5 million sucres; National Government, 2.1 million sucres.

Share capital.-Capitalization of the bank is provided by the sale of shares with a value of 100 sucres each. The shares are divided into two series, A and B. Series A shares may be acquired only by banking institutions operating in the country and are freely negotiable among banks. 

Series B shares may be acquired only by the 

National Government and are nonnegotiable.

Banks are required to purchase and hold series A shares of the Central Bank equal in value to at least 5 percent of their capital and reserves. Development banks, however, are excepted from this requirement. (The 

Control by Monetary Board.-The Central Bank operates under the control and direction of the Monetary Board, the highest authority in monetary matters. The Board is composed of nine members, eight of whom represent the National Government, the private banks, and the chambers of agriculture, industry, and commerce. chambers are associations of private enterpreneurs.) The ninth member is elected by the 

Board. Membership of the Board, with the exception of the Minister of Economy, who is an ex officio member, is subject to periodic renewal or replacement. Representatives of private banks and the various business chambers serve 2 years; 

Government representatives serve 2 years in the case of the congressional member and 4 years in the case of the representative of the National Economic Council and the National Welfare Insti- 

1 Monetary Law of 1948 (Ley de Regimen Monetario), decree 

434, March 12, 1948, published in the Registro Oficial 149, Quito, March 13, 1948. 113

tute. The ninth member of the Board serves 1 year.

The Board is charged with the responsibility of adopting the necessary measures to meet and fulfill the objectives of the national monetary policy in conformity with the provisions of the Monetary Law. This involves formulating the rules, regulations, terms, and other pertinent controls on banking and credit operations and initiating appropriate action to safeguard the economy against inflationary or deflationary tendencies of monetary origin.

The Central Bank carries out the policies and directives of the Monetary Board, by which the general manager of the bank is appointed and to which he is responsible for his administration. 

Functions. The bank performs the customary functions associated with a central bank, including the issue and stabilization of currency, the holding and management of official foreign exchange reserves, the conduct of international financial transactions, and the issuance of import and export permits. The bank is the depository of all minimum banking reserves required by law of private banks operating in the country and of official funds. The minimum banking reserves and other bank funds deposited with the bank serve as the basis for a check-clearing system operated under the direction of the bank.

The Central Bank has the authority, invested by the Monetary Law, to engage in a dual system of credit operations. Under normal conditions, the bank may carry out credit transactions-that is, the discount, rediscount, purchase, and sale of bills of exchange, bank acceptances, promissory notes, and other credit instrumentswith other parts of the banking system, the Government, and other official entities. Such transactions are limited to 180 and 270 days for commercial, agricultural, and industrial credits and to 1 year for official credits. In deflationary periods, however, the bank is empowered to increase its credit operations under a policy of stabilization credit, which permits the bank to extend the period for all transactions up to 1 year and to engage in direct credit operations with the public. It was under the latter phase of the stabilization credit policy, to cite an example, that the bank in February 1955 granted 90-day collateral credits to importers for clearing merchandise through customs.

Reserves. As the agent of the Monetary Board, the bank executes the regulations governing the determination of legal reserves and interest rates. 

Under the Monetary Law, minimum banking reserves deposited in the bank ordinarily may not be less than 10 percent or more that 35 percent of the deposits in the individual banks. In times of inflationary tendencies, the minimum requirement may be increased to 50 percent. Reserve requirements have remained unchanged since 1949 at 25 percent against demand deposits and at 15 percent against time deposits. 

Interest rates.-The legal maximum rate of interest on bank credit is 10 percent per year.2 Interest rates on Central Bank rediscounts and advances to private banks are 5 percent and to development banks, 3, 4, and 5 percent for agricultural, industrial, and commercial purposes, respectively. Discounts and advances to the public bear the maximum rate of 10 percent, while loans to the Central Government and other official entities are at 2-percent interest. Central Bank rates on purchases of bank acceptances from private banks and from the public are 5 percent for agricultural and industrial purposes and 6 percent for commercial purposes. Private bank loans, discounts, and advances are fixed at a maximum of 9 percent for agricultural and industrial credits and 10 percent for commercial credits. 

Development bank loans for agricultural and industrial purposes are subject to interest rates of 

8 and 9 percent, respectively. 

Interest payments on time deposits in private banks are 2 percent up to 90 days, 4 percent_up to 180 days, and 6 percent up to 1 year. Demand deposits in savings accounts yield 4 percent. The maximum interest rate on mortgage bonds issued by banks is 10 percent and on mortgage bonds backed by the authorization and acceptance of the Central Bank, with 10-year maturity, 6 percent.

Capital and reserves.-Capital and reserves of the Central Bank as of December 31, 1957, amounted to 41.3 million sucres and assets totaled 

1,819 million sucres. Deposits and obligations amounted to 978.2 million sucres, and loans and rediscounts totaled 848 million sucres.

The rate of interest on short-term loans granted by money. lenders is much higher, reportedly commonly exceeding 18 percent per year. (See El Desarrollo del Ecuador, United Nations, Docu ment E/CN.12/295, 1953, page 149.) 114

Financial Institutions 

In the second half of 1957, the total amount of 

Central Bank credit outstanding rose to a peak of 868.2 million sucres in September and then declined to a balance of 848 million sucres at the year's end. The sharp expansion of credit in 

1953, 1955, and 1956 came as the result of the bank's compensatory policy to reduce the contractive effects of balance-of-payment deficits upon commercial bank operations and the resultant tightness in the money market.

Development Banks 

The development banks constitute a separate system of Government-owned banks intended to supply the need for medium- and long-term credit in agriculture and industry and thereby promote the development of the country's natural resources. The system was organized in 1944, when the Ecuadoran Mortgage Bank (Banco Hipotecario del Ecuador) was taken over, and consists of the National Development Bank and 15 

Provincial banks. 3 The development banks receive their working capital from specifically assigned percentages of import duties and property and profit taxes, and from the Central Bank, which makes its credit facilities available to the development banks. 

National Development Bank. Under the Law of the System of Development Credit, the National Development Bank, which is located in Quito, is charged with the responsibility to control and coordinate the operations of the Provincial banks. 

It acts as the depository of the required banking reserves of the Provincial banks and establishes the rates of interest that the banks may charge. The minimum reserve requirement for 

Provincial banks is 10 percent of deposits; interest rates may not exceed the maximum rates established by the Monetary Board. 

It approves the credit plans throughout the system, authorizes mortgage loans with bonds as proposed by the Provincial banks and exercises exclusive right to issue such bonds, participates in the organization of suitable enterprises to develop the productive capacity of the country, and authorizes the acquisition of large estates for subdivision on the basis of social and economic criteria. In its banking relations with the Provincial banks, the National Development Bank is empowered to discount and rediscount negotiable instruments, provide advances and grant overdrafts in current account, and make mortgage loans through the Provincial banks for 10 years or more solely through the issuance of bonds. 

Collections for the account of the development banks in 

1954-56 amounted to 33.4 million sucres. (See Contraloria 

General, Informe, 1956-57, Quito, 1957, appendix 36.)

Ley del Sistema de Credito de Fomento, published in Registro 

Oficial, Quito, July 23, 1953.

It is controlled and administered by a Board of nine members, presided over by the Minister of Economy and comprising representatives of the executive and legislative branches of the Government, the National Economic Council, the Monetary Board, and the chambers of agriculture and industries in the Costa and in the Sierra. The representatives of the chambers are selected by the President of the Republic. With the exception of the representative of the executive branch of the Government, who is freely appointed and removed by the President, the members of the Board are appointed for 4 years and may be reelected. Members of the Government and the official bodies, however, retain their places on the Board only as long as they continue in their official capacities. 

Immediate responsibility for the administration of the bank is vested in a general manager appointed by the Board. 

Provincial banks.-The Provincial banks, located in the capitals of the 15 Provinces in the Costa and the Sierra, are administered in each case by a Board of 3 members. The boards of the Provincial banks in Quito and Guayaquil are appointed by the President of the Republic but each includes two members from a list of candidates proposed by the respective chambers of agriculture and industries in the two Provinces or cities. 

The boards of the Provincial banks other than in Quito and Guayaquil are appointed by the President and the National Development Bank. The 

President appoints one member of the board of the Provincial bank from a list of recommendations submitted by the legally constituted agricultural organization in each Province. Members of the boards are appointed for 2 years and may succeed themselves. Each bank operates under the immediate control of a manager appointed by the board of the National Development Bank.

The Provincial banks are empowered to receive short-term cash deposits where authorized by the 

National Development Bank; to receive time deposits for periods of not less than 6 months and also to receive savings deposits; to make short-, medium-, and long-term loans for agricultural, livestock, forestry, and industrial development; to grant indorsements and bank acceptances for such development loans; and to issue agricultural and industrial bonds and bonds backed by collateral. In those places where permitted to receive short-term cash deposits, the Provincial banks may grant commercial loans up to 50 percent of the value of such deposits. The Provincial banks may rediscount their commercial paper in either the National Development Bank or the Central Bank.

In addition to the 50-percent limit on commercial loans, the Provincial banks are limited to 80 percent of the value of the collateral on ordinary loans and to 60 percent of the value of the property on mortgage loans. In the case of mort- 115

gage loans granted to farmers of modest means for the purchase of parcels of land in the subdivision of large estates, loans up to the full value of the property are authorized. 

As of August 31, 1957, the latest period for which information is available, the combined capital and reserves of the development banks totaled 381.9 million sucres, and the combined assets amounted to 995.8 million sucres.

Investments and loans of development banks. 

Investments by the development banks are composed in large part of holdings transferred from the former Development Corporation, which had organized several industrial, agricultural, and commercial enterprises between 1949, when it was established, and 1953, when it was dissolved. Although the development bank system is intended to accommodate needs for medium- and long-term credit, it has been forced by lack of adequate resources to place the bulk of its loans on a short-term basis. A large part of the system's resources is frozen in overdue loans, which in mid- 

1957 reportedly amounted to 140 million sucres.

In 1954-56, approximately 82 percent of the value of all loans was granted for a period of up to 1 year, 12 percent was repayable within 1 to 5 years, and only 6 percent was extended for more than 5 years. Because of the demands upon its limited resources, the system was increasingly obliged to follow a short-term lending policy, and the volume of credit granted for a period of up to 

1 year rose from 79.5 percent of the total credit extended in 1954 to 85 percent in 1956. 

Private Banks

Private banks antedate Government-owned banks in Ecuador. To cite a few important examples, the Banco del Pichincha, in Quito, was founded in 1906; the Banco de Prestamos, also in 

Quito, was established in 1909; and La Previsora

Banco Nacional de Credito (popularly known as 

La Previsora), in Guayaquil, was organized in 1920.

Number and location.-Fifteen private banks are in operation. Two of these banks, however, are not, strictly speaking, private banks. The larger of the two, the Banco Popular, is a joint 

Colombian-Ecuadoran enterprise with both private and official capital. The other is the Caja de 

Credito Agricola, a semiofficial institution organized and operating under special legislation. 

Only one of the private banks is foreign owned, the Bank of London and South America (Banco de Londres y America del Sud) in Guayaquil and Quito. 

Eight of the banks are located in Guayaquil, six in Quito, and one in Cuenca. The locations of the main offices and branch offices of the individual banks are: Banco de Abasto, Quito; Banco del 116

Azuay, Cuenca; Caja de Credito Agricola, Quito;

Banco de Descuento, Guayaquil; Banco La Filan tropica, Guayaquil; Banco de Guayaquil, Guayaquil; Banco de Londres y America del Sud, Guayaquil, branch in Quito; Banco Manabita, S. A., Quito, branches in Bahia and Manta; Banco deĺ 

Pichincha, Quito, branch in Ibarra; Banco Popular, Quito, branches in Guayaquil, Cuenca, and

Guayaquil; Banco de Credito Hipotecario, Guayaquil; and Banco Territorial, Guayaquil. 

Size, type, and capitalization. The largest of the private banks are La Previsora Banco 

Nacional de Credito, Banco de Descuento, and the Banco del Pichincha, the combined assets of which, as of May 15, 1957, accounted for 71 percent of all assets held by the 15 banks. La Previsora Banco Nacional de Credito alone held 36 percent of the total assets. With the exception of the Banco de Credito Hipotecario and the 

Banco Territorial, which are mortgage banks, the private banks, exclusive of the Caja de Credito Agricola, which is a special instrument of agricultural credit, are commercial banks. Five of the commercial banks also are mortgage and savings banks, two operate mortgage departments, two operate savings departments, and three restrict their operations to commercial accounts. 

Under Ecuadoran banking regulations, commercial and mortgage banks, or departments of banks, are required to be capitalized at not less than 500,000 sucres in cities of 80,000 or more inhabitants. In smaller localities, commercial banks must be capitalized at not less than 250,000 sucres, and mortgage banks at not less than 300,000 sucres. Savings banks, or departments, are required to have a minimum capital of 200,000 sucres in cities of over 80,000 inhabitants and 

100,000 sucres in other localities. 

Functions of commercial banks.-Commercial banks are authorized to make commercial loans, with or without collateral, up to 1 year, and, subject to certain limitations, mortgage loans not exceeding 5 years. As a part of their multiple functions, the commercial banks may receive deposits on a short- or long-term basis; extend credit in current account up to 180 days; discount and negotiate credit instruments falling due within a year; grant advances guaranteed by official or officially backed bonds and other obligations or by readily marketable securities other than stocks; purchase, hold, or sell bonds and other obligations

Ley General de Bancos, Registro Oficial, Quito, August 9, 1948. of the National Government, municipalities, and officially backed institutions; and, similarly, purchase, hold, or sell mortgage bonds issued by their own mortgage departments and shares in the Central Bank, subject to the restrictions stipulated in the Monetary Law.

Functions of mortgage banks.-Mortgage banks are empowered to grant ordinary mortgage loans up to 10 years and loans of gradual amortization up to 50 years. The amortization loans are financed by mortgage bonds issued by the bank and backed by bank capital and reserves and by the total amount of the bank's current amortization mortgage loans, which must be supported by a first mortgage and may not exceed 50 percent of the assessed value of the mortgaged property. The mortgage banks, subject to certain limitations, also are authorized to grant loans up to 10,000 sucres, against signatures, for a period of not more than 1 year, and loans backed by agricultural or industrial collateral for no more than 5 years.

In common with the commercial and savings banks, mortgage banks may buy, hold, and sell shares of the Central Bank in accordance with the restrictions in the Monetary Law. 

Functions of savings banks.-Savings banks are permitted to accept savings accounts up to a total of 15,000 sucres for individuals and up to 40,000 sucres for organizations. The savings banks are authorized to invest in Government and muncipal bonds and other obligations, bank securities, and other marketable securities except stocks. The banks are authorized to grant mortgage loans up to 5 years, make loans and advances repayable within 180 days, and handle commercial paper with a time limit of 180 or 270 days and acceptable to the Central Bank. 

Balances outstanding.-As of August 31, 1957, the combined capital and reserves of the private banks, including the Caja de Credito Agricola, totaled 234.8 million sucres, and assets amounted to 1,950.2 million sucres. Most of the private bank credit is in the form of commercial and mortgage loans. Investments are small and have varied little in the past few years. The mortgage loans are financed in large part by the issuance of mortgage bonds of which the value outstanding amounted to 617.3 million sucres as of August 31, 1957. Funds for ordinary loans are derived largely from deposits and from the placement of mortgage bonds.

Very limited use is made of Central Bank rediscounts and other credit facilities principally because many attach the suggestion of weakness to a private bank that resorts to the Central Bank. 

As of August 31, 1957, for example, Central Bank credit to private banks amounted to only 23.3 million sucres as compared with 187.2 million sucres to development banks. Consequently, the private banks maintain a high degree of liquidity and customarily retain reserves greatly in excess of minimum requirements. Their self-imposed restrictions upon their capacity to extend credit from time to time accentuate the pressures that force the Central Bank to enter into direct credit operations with the public. 

Loans for commercial purposes account for most credit transactions handled by the private banks (see table 57). In 1954-56, commercial loans represented 86 percent of all credit transactions and 82 percent of all credit granted. 

Private bank credit also has been heavily concentrated in short-term loans. In 1954-56, loans up to 1 year accounted for 99.6 percent of all credit operations and 93 percent of all credit extended. Loans up to 90 days represented 59 percent of the transactions and 49 percent of the credit, and loans repayable within 91 to 180 days constituted 33 percent of the transactions and 41 percent of the credit.
