SOCIAL SECURITY AGENCIES The social security agencies, the Social Security 

Fund and the Pension Fund, are an important source of transferable savings and of financing.

In 1952-56, the agencies had an average annual balance of receipts over expenditures amounting to 147.5 million sucres, and between 1952 and 1956 the favorable balance increased from 110.5 million to 178.9 million sucres.

58). Loans accounted for 60 percent of the investments; and mortgage-secured loans represented 43 percent of all investments and 72 percent of all loans. About two-thirds of the loans were to members and one-third to employers and to public and private establishments." 

Negotiable securities accounted for 27 percent of total investments as of November 30, 1956; and other investments, including real estate, accounted for 13 percent. Forty-nine percent of the investments in securities were in mortgage bonds and

Investments in Government bonds, including bonds of the autonomous entities, declined by 5.2 million sucres in 1956 and represented only 12 percent of total security holdings. Predominantly because of this, the social security agencies acquired approximately one-fifth of the increase of 85 million sucres in Government bonds outstanding between 1950 and 1954.

NATIONAL SECURITIES COMMISSION The National Securities Commission (CNV), an autonomous official agency, was established in 1955 primarily to mobilize local funds to finance public works. The CNV is actually the successor to the former Securities Regulation Fund (Fondo de Regulacion de Valores or FRV), which was organized under the Monetary Law of 1948 to invest in Government securities and to support the price of Government issues through open-market operations. Essentially, the Securities Regulation Fund took up Government issues, which had virtually no market. Assets and assigned revenues of the Fund were transferred to the CNV, which was invested with powers broader than those of the Fund.

The CNV is governed by a Board of seven members comprising the Minister of Economy and the Minister of the Treasury and representatives of the National Legislature, the Central Bank, the National Economic Council, and the chambers of agriculture and industries in the Sierra and in the Costa. With the exception of the Minister of Economy and the Minister of the Treasury, who are permanent members, the Board is reelected or replaced every 2 years. Immediate responsibility for the administration of the CNV is vested in a manager chosen by the Board. The manager serves 4 years and may be reelected.

The CNV is empowered to buy and sell securities of the National Government, of other official entities, and of private institutions with a public purpose. It also may buy and sell mortgage bonds that banks issue for agricultural and industrial development, establish commercial exchanges wherever it deems fit and supervise the exchanges, and contract internal and external loans to carry out its program. In addition to utilizing its own funds, the CNV may channel into the securities market idle official funds; excess reserves of private banks, if the banks so request and the Monetary Board approves; and a part of the reserves of insurance companies.

Ordinary resources of the CNV consist of assigned import tax receipts and earnings on investments. To an increasing extent, however, it has resorted to borrowing. In 1954-56, Central Bank loans accounted for approximately 50 percent of the CNV's total resources and in 1956 amounted to 

74 million sucres as compared with 20 million sucres received from import taxes and 32.9 million sucres from earnings and repayments on investments. As of December 31, 1956, the Commission's capital and reserves totaled 165.5 million sucres.

Government bonds and loans comprise most of the CNV's investments. The Commission took four-fifths of the 85-million-sucre increase in Government bonds outstanding between 1950 and 1954; and in 1950-56 invested 266.4 million sucres in 

Government securities and loans as against 32.9 million sucres in municipal bonds and 34.7 million sucres in other bonds.

INSURANCE COMPANIES 

The insurance business is governed by the In surance Inspection and Control Law (Ley sobre Inspeccion y Control de Seguros) of December 31, 1937, as amended, and is conducted under the control of the Superintendency of Banks. Authorization must be obtained from the Superintendency before an insurance company may be initiated. 

The minimum paid-in capital required by law for national insurance companies is 1 million sucres for life or annuity insurance and 300,000 sucres for other types of insurance. Foreign insurance companies are required to maintain se- 119

curity funds against prospective risks (montos de seguridades) amounting to 1 million sucres for life or annuity insurance and to between 

150,000 and 500,000 sucres for other types of insurance, depending upon the value of annual premiums. These funds are deposited with the 

Central Bank. All life insurance companies have to maintain a reserve that must be approved annually by the Superintendency of Banks. All other insurance companies are required to maintain in Ecuador minimum reserves equivalent to 35 percent of the net premiums received in the preceding year. 

Six national and 22 foreign insurance companies were operating in Ecuador in mid-1957. 

Eight companies sold life insurance and 25, including 5 of the life underwriters, sold other forms of insurance, including transportation, automobile, hull, fidelity, accident, airplane, theft, livestock, fire, and contract guaranty. At the end of 1956, there were in force 16,420 life insurance policies with an aggregate value of 317.3 million sucres. Life insurance premiums in 1956 amounted to 17.5 million sucres, and settlements totaled 973,000 sucres. Other types of insurance totaled 4,716.6 million sucres, and premiums and settlements amounted to 33.8 million and 16 million sucres, respectively.

The insurance business in 1956 was depressed by poor business conditions throughout much of the first half of the year, and policy values and premium income were about 6 percent below 1955 levels.

At the close of 1955, there were 17,292 life insurance policies in force with a value of 348.8 million sucres. The value of other forms of insurance in 1955 amounted to 4,987.7 million sucres. Total 1955 premium income amounted to 54.6 million sucres (life insurance, 17.3 million, and other insurance, 37.3 million). Life insurance settlements amounted to 1.2 million sucres, and payments under other types of insurance policies totaled 16.8 million sucres. 

National companies issue most of the property and risk insurance, but handle only a small part of the life insurance business. In 1955, for example, national insurers accounted for 68 percent of property and risk insurance coverage and received 76 percent of the premiums. On the other hand, they issued only 17 percent of the life insurance and received 19 percent of the premiums. 

It should be noted, however, that the Ecuadoran companies are recent entrants in the field of life insurance. One national company, La Union,

Compañia Nacional de Seguros, S. A., issued 24 

percent of the property and risk insurance; one foreign company, Sud America, Cia. de Seguros sobre La Vida, a Brazilian firm, issued 83 percent of the life insurance. Capital and reserves of the national companies amounted to 23.4 million sucres, as of December 31, 1956; security funds and reserves of the foreign companies amounted to 12.7 million sucres.

National and foreign life insurance companies in Ecuador are required to invest at least 25 per- cent of the annual increase in their mathematical reserves, based on the volume of such reserves as of December 31, 1955, in securities marketed by the CNV. Foreign life insurance companies also must invest in such securities 50 percent of their security funds against prospective risks.

National companies in other fields of insurance are required to invest at least 10 percent of the total of their reserves for current risks and of their reserve funds in securities offered by the CNV; foreign companies must invest in such securities 10 percent of their reserves for current risks and 50 percent of their security funds against prospective risks.

The CNV, however, cannot compel the insurance companies to purchase security offerings that yield less than 8 percent interest per year.

In addition to required purchases of securities marketed by the CNV, the insurance companies are required to invest half of their remaining capital and reserves within the country in the form of loans to insured clients, mortgage loans, mortgage bonds, municipal bonds, real estate acquired in payment of debts or land for their own use, and bank deposits. 

As of December 31, 1956, loans and time deposits of the insurance companies amounted to 

15.8 million and 10.7 million sucres, respectively. 

Securities held by all insurers totaled 42.9 million sucres of which mortgage bonds accounted for 

25.3 million sucres, or 59 percent of the total. 

Other principal holdings were CNV certificates, 7.5 million sucres; corporate stocks, 5.9 million sucres; and municipal bonds, 3.1 million sucres. 

Holdings of Government bonds amounted to 

999,000 sucres. Under the provisions of decree law 8 of March 29, 1956, published in the Registro Oficial, Quito, April 3, 1956. This decree amended the Insurance and Inspection Control Law of December 

31, 1937. 120

Exports of merchandise account for most of Ecuador's receipts on current account. In 195056, commodity exports supplied 90.2 percent of the total receipts from goods and services. Next in importance were miscellaneous services, including export and import commissions, collection charges, technical services, and film rentals, which accounted for 3.1 percent. Third in importance were Government services (diplomatic and consular services, consular fees, and other receipts), which accounted for 2.6 percent. Transportation and insurance receipts, investment earnings, and travel expenditures of foreigners in Ecuador accounted for the remaining 4.1 percent. The lastnamed source of income has increased its contribution considerably in the past few years as the country has made greater efforts to attract tourists, but expenditures by Ecuadorans on foreign travel continue to exceed receipts.

Imports represented 62.2 percent of total payments for goods and services in 1950-56. Payments on foreign investment (estimated earnings and remittances of private foreign investment in 

Ecuador and interest payments on official loans) accounted for 13.3 percent. The remaining payment accounts in order of importance, as percent of total payments for goods and services, were as follows: Transportation, 9.8; miscellaneous payments, 5.9; Government payments not included under other headings, 3.8; expenditures on foreign travel, 3.3; and insurance, 1.7.

Ecuador has succeeded in maintaining a stable currency since 1950 and has attracted increasing private foreign investment, which in 1950-56 averaged $3.6 million per year. 

Commodity Trade

Among the South American Republics, Ecuador is exceeded by all other countries, except Bolivia and Paraguay, in the value of its foreign commodity trade. Nevertheless, Ecuador's trade with other nations has grown rapidly and substantially in value since the 1930's. In 1936-38, the last full 3-year period preceding the outbreak of World War II, imports averaged $11.5 million per year, and exports, $13.9 million. By 1946-48, the annual average had increased to $41.7 million for imports and $45.1 million for exports. In 

1954-56, annual imports averaged $94.6 million and exports, $94.5 million. The value of average yearly imports in 1946-48 was 263 percent above the 1936-38 average, and in 1954-56 was 127 percent above the 1946-48 average. The value of average yearly exports in 1946-48 was 224 percent above the 1936-38 average, and in 1954-56 it was 

110 percent above the 1946-48 average.

Rising prices rather than expanding volume, however, accounted for the great increase in the value of trade during and directly after the war. 

The annual import tonnage in 1946-48 compared with 1936-38 averaged only 13 percent greater while the annual export tonnage averaged 2.5 percent less. But during the 1950's, the volume of trade expanded rapidly as imports were substantially increased by both Government and private spending and exports were raised to record levels, largely through the phenomenal increase in banana shipments. In 1954-56, the average yearly import tonnage was 169 percent greater than in 1946-48, and the annual export tonnage was 134 percent above the 1946-48 average. 

Terms of trade in the present decade improved steadily for Ecuador from 1950 to 1954, after which there was a sharp decline in export prices. 

Based on 1950 prices, the export price index reached a peak of 121 in 1954, and then fell to 110 in 1955 and 105 in 1956. The comparable index for import prices declined from 117 in 1951 to a low of 112 in 1954, and then climbed to 114 in 1955 and to 118 in 1956, according to the International 

Monetary Fund. 

Agricultural products, particularly tropical foodstuffs, represent the bulk of exports. Manufactured goods and materials and some basic foodstuffs that are not produced in sufficient quantities within the country constitute the bulk of imports. 122

Exports. A few commodities make up the country's export trade (see table 61). Agricultural products constitute all but a small part of the trade, having accounted for 95 percent of the value of all exports in 1954–56. Second in importance are manufactured goods, consisting of panama hats and pharmaceuticals, which represented 

2.7 percent of the export trade by value in 195456. Third are minerals, comprising petroleum, mineral earth, gold dust, and concentrates of copper and lead; and fourth are fish and fish products, including shellfish. Minerals accounted for 

2 percent of total exports by value in 1954-56, and fish, 0.3 percent.

Bananas, coffee, and cacao are by far the most important exports. In 1954-56, they accounted for 89 percent of all exports by value, as follows, in percent: Bananas, 36; coffee, 28; and cacao, 25. 

Rice, the country's leading export during World

War II and immediately after the war, has since followed an irregular, downward trend to the point of secondary importance. 

Nonagricultural exports also have tended to decline. Exports of panama hats have trended downward since 1946 as foreign competition has increased. Foreign sales of pharmaceuticals have been affected by unfavorable market conditions in neighboring Colombia in the past few years. Increasing internal requirements have taken a progressively larger share of domestic petroleum, leaving less for export. However, a new and promising entry in the export trade is fish and shellfish, which has recently become an increasingly important commercial item.

Source: Banco Central del Ecuador, Boletin, September-October 1957, 

Quito, page 122.

The port of Guayaquil, however, handles the bulk of the import traffic, having accounted for an average of 90 percent of all imports by value in 1954-56. 

The most striking feature of the import pattern is the large amount of foodstuffs that Ecuador purchases abroad. Between 1950 and 1954, imports of foodstuffs, including beverages and tobacco, increased from $5.3 million to $17.2 million. 

Under the restrictive effects of controls imposed by the Government, purchases declined to $12.9 million in 1955 and to $11.4 million in 1956. In 

1955 and 1956, the only years for which the data are available, imports of foodstuffs, excluding beverages and tobacco, amounted to $10.6 million and $7.9 million, respectively. The principal item in this food bill is wheat.

International Transactions and Commercial Policy for 73 percent of the value of all capital goods imported in 1950-56.

Direction of Trade The Western Hemisphere (the United States, the Latin American Republics, and Canada) and

Europe account for nearly all of Ecuador's foreign trade (see table 65). In 1954-56, those 2 areas took 99 percent of Ecuador's exports and supplied 98 percent of her imports. The Western 

Hemisphere took 74 percent of the exports and provided 65 percent of the imports. 

Prior to World War II, Europe was the leading area of trade, but the postwar trade pattern has consistently been heavily oriented toward the United States, which is Ecuador's principal market and supplier. In 1954-56, the United States supplied 53 percent of Ecuador's imports and took 62 percent of her exports as compared with 

35 and 39 percent, respectively, in 1936–38.

Next to the United States, Western Germany is 

Ecuador's principal trading partner. In 1954-56, Western Germany took 9 percent of Ecuador's exports and supplied 11 percent of her imports. 

Among the other European countries, Belgium-

Among the Latin American countries, Colombia is by far the most important market, followed by Chile and Argentina. Peru is the leading supplier, followed by Chile and Panama.

Favorable trade balances with the United States and Latin America have more than offset deficits in trade with Europe and Canada (see table 66), a fact underlining the fundamental importance of United States and regional trade to the Ecuadoran economy.

TRADE CONTROLS 

The most important instruments of Ecuador's commercial policy in recent years have been the tariff system, the licensing requirements, and the exchange controls, as modified by the commercial agreements. State trading has not attained any major significance. The commercial policy of 

Ecuador is primarily oriented toward balancing its trade by discouraging the importation of less essential articles. 

The tariff schedule, which affects both imports and exports, is utilized primarily for revenue purposes and secondarily for protection of domestic industries and for discouragement of nonessential or luxury imports. Control over exports is exercised mainly through the licensing regulations, which require that proceeds from most exports be returned to Ecuador. In addition, the exchange regulations on the rates at which proceeds must be liquidated provide benefits that accrue incidentally to the licensing regulations. Control over essential and nonessential or luxury imports is exercised mainly through the exchange differential; the licensing of imports insures their proper exchange classification.

Control of import and export licensing and foreign exchange is exercised by the Central Bank under policies established by the Monetary Board 

(see chapter XI). The Monetary Board also ad vises the executive branch of the Government through the National Economic Council on changes in customs duties.

Tariff Import duties.-The present Ecuadoran import tariff system provides for a single-column tariff.¹

Equal treatment, except as otherwise provided in commercial agreements, is accorded all countries. 

Most Ecuadoran tariff items are subject to a com- 1 Matters relating to Ecuadoran import duties are regulated by the Organic Tariff Law of July 11, 1953 (emergency decree law 22), the Regulations of the Organic Tariff Law (decree 2237 of 

December 14, 1953), the Customs Law and Customs Tariff of 

July 11, 1953 (emergency decree law 23), and decree laws 401 

of March 21, 1956, and 601 of April 13, 1956. The last-named decree laws, which became effective March 24, 1956, and April 14, 1956, respectively, represent a complete revision of the tariff schedule and established compound, specific, and ad valorem duties. The classification system established in decree law 23, which became effective January 1, 1954, is maintained in general. 127

pound duty composed of a specific charge plus an ad valorem charge. In some cases duties are assessed entirely on a specific or ad valorem basis. 

A limited number of items are duty free. In addition to the duties assessed according to the tariff schedule, imports are subject to certain other ad valorem taxes.

In most cases, specific duties are assessed on net weight in kilograms. Net weight in the Ecuadoran tariff law includes containers, cartons, cards, wrapping, and packing materials which are normally used in marketing the product. Packing materials used for protection of goods while in transit are not included. Some specific duties are based on the gross weight in kilograms of the goods, and duties on a few items are assessed on a unit basis. Ad valorem duties are assessed on the f. o. b. value, port of exportation. This is the wholesale market value in the country of origin, including packing but excluding maritime freight and insurance, consular fees, and commissions. 

With the exception of the fees for legalization of shipping documents paid to Ecuadoran consulates abroad, all import duties and charges and the consular fees are stated and paid in sucres by the 

Ecuadoran importer.

Export duties.-The Customs Law and Customs 

Tariff of July 11, 1953, imposes export duties on a few items. Coffee beans, cacao beans, and silver articles are dutiable on an ad valorem basis. Cottonseed, domestically produced hides and skins, sole leather, toquilla straw, and old material and scrap of iron and other base metals are subject to specific duties assessed on their gross weight in kilograms; refrigerated or processed shrimp and lobsters pay specific duties on their net weight in kilograms. Specific duties per unit are assessed on horses and stems of bananas.

Taxes on Imports 

Commodity taxes.-A tax of 5 percent of the c. i. f. value of import list I articles was imposed by an emergency decree of December 21, 1956.

The same measure, in effect, levied a tax of 10 percent of the c. i. f. value of import list II articles. 

Formerly, a tax of 10 percent of the f. o. b. value of certain list II items was in effect. 

Special-purpose taxes.-Four special-purpose taxes, instituted by congressional decree, are levied on imports into Ecuador, based on f. o. b. value, port of embarkation: (1) 0.75 percent for port construction, (2) 0.50 percent for public works, (3) 

0.25 percent for electrification and school construction, and (4) 0.50 percent for highway construction. 

A statistical tax of 10 centavos per 100 gross kilograms, with a minimum charge of 1 sucre, is collected on all commercial imports granted free customs entry, except for imports through the postal service, Government imports, and imports with a gross weight of not more than 200 grams or with a value of less than 50 sucres.

The Monetary Board may impose an import surtax on imports from countries with which 

Ecuador has an unfavorable trade balance.

In addition to the regular import duties, alcoholic beverages are subject to a tax based on the quantity in liters.

There are a number of minor internal taxes assessed either on a commodity or a Provincial basis.

Consular fees.-The main consular fee on shipments to Ecuador is paid in sucres to the Central 

Bank by the importer. This fee is 9.5 percent of the f. o. b. vessel value of the shipment, except for a shipment made on an Ecuadoran-flag vessel or on a vessel of the Flota Grancolombiana, when the fee is 8.5 percent. The Ecuadoran importer must pay a fee of 8.5 percent f. o. b. vessel value and make the appropriate advance deposit (see section, Licensing Requirements) before the import license will be issued. The receipt for the payment of this fee is attached to the import license and sent to the shipper for submission to the Ecuadoran consul with the other shipping documents. If the shipment is subject to the 9.5percent fee, the importer pays the additional 1percent fee after the arrival of the goods in Ecuador.

The fees for consular legalization of shipping documents are paid by the shipper to the Ecuadoran consulate abroad in one certified check. 

Port taxes.-The Ecuadoran Government imposes port charges or taxes on loading and unloading operations in addition to the charges for these services by private firms. The basis for the assessment of these taxes is the weight of the merchandise stated in the consular invoice. No deduction or adjustment in these weights is permitted.

Port taxes on unloading imports are assessed at the following rates, per ton, gross weight: Tax in sucres 105 20 20 20 In ports in which Government wharves are available_

Within 30 days after arrival in Ecuador, merchandise may be declared for deposit in customs warehouses for a period of 1 year; this period may be extended for 6 months in exceptional cases. 

Such merchandise is subject to charges of 0.10 sucre per gross kilo for each period of 30 days or fraction thereof. On the expiration of the permitted deposit period, if the goods are not removed, they are considered abandoned and are liable to sale at public auction.

Baggage which remains in customs storage more than 10 days after arrival is subject to a tax of 0.30 sucre per gross kilo for each 30-day period or fraction thereof. 

Taxes on Exports

Commodity taxes. In addition to the duties specified in the current tariff schedule, all exports except rice, manufactures of toquilla straw, including panama hats, vegetable ivory (tagua), castor beans, balsa wood, and cinchona bark are subject to a tax of 0.75 percent of the f. o. b. value, port of embarkation. 

Statistical tax.—A statistical tax is collected on all commercial exports granted duty exemption except exports through the postal service, Government exports, and exports with a gross weight of not more than 200 grams or a value of less than 

50 sucres. The rate of tax is 10 centavos per 100 gross kilograms, with a minimum charge of 1 sucre.

Port taxes.-Port taxes on loading exports, per ton, gross weight, are: 470366-59--10 Там in sucres

Tributarias del Ecuador, Hasta Junio, 1957, Quito, 1957, page 555. Documents

For the clearance through customs of import shipments, an import declaration must be presented to the customs authorities, accompanied by legalized copies of the import permit, the consular invoice, the commercial invoice, and a negotiable copy of the bill of lading. 

The documents required for export transactions are the customhouse permit; the bill of lading; the export license; the phytosanitary certificate, if necessary; and receipt for payment of taxes. 

For documents required by tourists, commercial travelers, and resident aliens, see appendix B. 

Licensing Requirements 

Permits are required for all import and export commodity transactions except those specifically exempted. Exchange licenses are issued for certain transactions in invisibles which have access to the official market. There is no differentiation on a country basis in regard to the licensing requirements. The Exchange Department of the Central 

Bank is the licensing agency for all transactions. 

Import permits. An import permit costing 7.40 

sucres (4 sucres for the legal form and 3.40 sucres for fiscal stamps) is required in all cases except those listed in the following paragraph. A consular fee of 8.5 percent of the f. o. b. value, port of export, must be paid by the importer before the Central Bank will authorize the permit. In addition, many items included in list II require an advance deposit of 100 percent of the c. i. f. value of the goods exclusive of the consular fees. This deposit must be paid to the Central Bank before an import permit is issued. The original copy of the import permit, sent by the importer to the shipper, must be presented at the Ecuadoran consulate before the necessary documents will be visaed.

The following are exempted from the requirement for import permits: Samples without value or valued at $40 or less than $40 or its equivalent in other currencies; articles without commercial value, such as advertisements; replacements for lost or damaged shipments; gifts valued at $40 or 129

less during any 6-month period; equipment and materials destined for the development of national production and imported into Ecuador as nonmonetary foreign investment; articles and personal effects of foreign diplomats accredited in Ecuador and those of Ecuadoran diplomats returning to the country, within the limits established by the Ministry of Foreign Affairs (Ministerio de 

Relaciones Exteriores) and the tariff laws; articles, effects, and raw materials admitted by the customs laws of Ecuador as temporary imports; articles and materials necessary for the operation of foreign companies or for the maintenance of their workers provided that the companies use their own exchange for such imports; imports representing foreign loans contracted by Government or official agencies; and small border transactions.

The Central Bank must act within 3 days on all applications for import permits. The bank may refuse to issue permits or may limit the importation of items that are: (1) Restricted for export in one or more producing countries; (2) produced in Ecuador, and their importation is intended solely to fill the demand not covered by national production; and (3) controlled or restricted for distribution in Ecuador by the Government or by other official entities. The bank may also refuse to issue an import permit when it is necessary to seek commercial equilibrium with countries that have taken special measures with respect to their imports or that have placed special restrictions on their imports; and when a persistently unfavorable balance of trade with certain countries is to be corrected. In the last-mentioned two cases, imports may be limited or prohibited only by resolution of the Monetary Board with previous authorization of the Ministry of Economy. 

Persons who are more than 30 days delinquent in payment on a foreign draft may be denied further import permits. 

Permits covering imports from North America and South America are valid for 120 days; those covering imports from other continents are valid for 180 days.

Special authorization for imports.-In addition to an import permit, special authorization from various Ecuadoran Government entities is required for the importation of certain items, as follows:

Shipments of livestock must be accompanied by a health certificate for each animal, issued by an official veterinary service, together with pictures of each animal. 

Certificates must be legalized by the Ecuadoran consul at the port of shipment. 

Certificates of chemica analysis, purity, quality, age, and healthfulness are required to obtain approval. 

Imports for airlines operating in the country may be exempted from payment of customs duties by the Ministry of the Treasury. Do.

Export permits.-Permits must be obtained from the Exchange Department of the Central Bank for all exports, except rice, mining products as stipulated in the contracts between the mining companies and the Government, articles and souvenirs exported in travelers' baggage, and minor border transactions. If tourists' purchases are not exported as part of their accompanying baggage, an export permit is required. Applications for export permits are processed within 3 days, and the permits are valid for 90 days.

To obtain an export permit, the applicant must also arrange for the delivery to the Central Bank of freely convertible exchange at the applicable rates of conversion or for remittance through a payments agreement account. Exports to be paid for in exchange not freely convertible in the international market are prohibited. 

Exchange Controls 

Exchange system.-Ecuador has a multiple exchange rate system (see chapter X). 

Import controls.-Imports permitted into Ecuador are enumerated in two lists maintained by the 

Monetary Board. List I includes necessary or essential goods; list II contains so-called luxury or nonessential goods. In addition to listed items, gifts valued at $40 or less may be imported, and

Government or other official imports previously approved by the Monetary Board may also be imported. The importation of all other goods is prohibited.

Foreign exchange for imports in list I is supplied at the official selling rate of 15.15 sucres 130

International Transactions and Commercial Policy per $1. Exchange for items in list II must be purchased at the free market rate. Moreover, most items in list II are subject to a 100-percent advance-deposit requirement.

The lists are modified as conditions warrant. 

For example, many items, including various paper products, iron and steel products, wool, and cotton thread and yarn, have been shifted from list 

I to list II. Similarly, some previously prohibited items have been added to the lists. The lists serve as a flexible control that may be adapted as foreign exchange availabilities and the interests of the national economy may dictate. 

Exchange for most imports is released only after the arrival of the goods in an Ecuadoran port. The Central Bank is authorized, however, to issue advance payments and irrevocable letters of credit for some list I items, including agricultural products imported under United States Public Law 480. 

Export controls.-Exchange proceeds from exports are subject to different conversion rates depending upon the commodity involved. Receipts from many exports, including such important commodities as coffee and cacao, are surrendered to the Central Bank at the official buying rate of 15 sucres per $1. On the other hand, foreign exchange received from many exports of secondary importance, such as rice, panama hats, balsa wood, castor beans, mangrove bark, pyrethrum flowers, and fresh fruit other than bananas is sold in the free market. Proceeds from some exports, among them bananas, tuna fish, shrimp, and pharmaceuticals, are converted at mixed rates of exchange. 

Controls on invisibles.-Most transactions in invisibles are made at the free market rate. Certain invisibles have access to the official market, subject to an exchange license from the Central Bank.

The Central Bank issues such licenses for necessary payments and remittances of the Government and official agencies; interest payments, amortization of loans, and other obligations abroad which are registered with the Central Bank; payments of dividends, profits, interest, and amortization on registered foreign investments up to at least 12 percent annually; press services; and expenses of students who register with the Central Bank, provided that the amounts do not exceed passenger fares, travel expenses, tuition, and up to $100 per month for living expenses. Foreign capital registered with the Central Bank is subject to the official rate of exchange; the Central Bank may refuse to register capital if it considers the investment to be contrary to the national interest. Requirements of foreign companies for actual operating costs, foreign official loans, and Government receipts are also liquidated at the official rate. 

The Central Bank in July 1955 withdrew from trading in the free market for invisibles; however, it retains the legal right to enter this market at such time as it may deem proper. 

In general, exchange for invisibles related to imports is available at the rate applicable to the goods themselves. However, exchange for the payment of overdue interest on drafts and for payment of current interest charges with rates in excess of 7 percent must be purchased at the free rate.

All other invisibles have access to the free market rate and are not subject to control by the exchange authorities. Unregistered capital and capital transfers by Ecuadoran residents may enter and leave the country in unlimited quantities through the free market. There is no limit on the export or import of Ecuadoran or foreign currency.

State Trading 

Government participation in international trade is limited to a relatively few commodities. The 

Bureau of Monopolies is a direct importer of cigarettes and matches. On the other hand, alcoholic beverages are imported by private individuals under prior authorization of the Bureau. The 

Office of Subsistence (Direccion de Subsistencias), when conditions demand, imports items of public necessity in short supply, generally foodstuffs and fuel. These imports supplement rather than displace private imports. Many other official agencies are direct importers of supplies and materials required for their operations and programs.

The Government ships crude oil received as royalties from domestic producers. Occasionally, it exports sugar and rice. In general, however, trading is left to private enterprise, and Government intervention is restricted to regulatory functions.

COMMERCIAL AGREEMENTS 

Ecuador has trade agreements and, in a number of cases, payments agreements with several countries. At present there is no commercial agreement in force between Ecuador and the United

States, the trade agreement between the two countries, which dated back to October 1938, having been terminated by the United States in July 1956. 

The trade agreements in general provide for most-favored-nation treatment, with the exception of concessions granted to border countries, accorded under customs unions, or provided in certain other arrangements. Lists of products or duty concessions on specific items are incorporated in some of the trade agreements. The payments agreements provide for settlement in dollars. 131

In addition to its bilateral commercial agreements, Ecuador is a signer of the Carta de Quito (Quito Charter) along with the Governments of 

Colombia, Panama, and Venezuela. The charter, which was signed in Quito on August 9, 1948, calls for the establishment of an economic and customs union among the four countries. Ecuador has ratified the charter, which still remains inoperative pending formal approval by the other signatories.

Agreements, by Country 

Argentina.-Trade and payments agreements between Argentina and Ecuador became effective on September 21, 1953. The agreements were for 

3 years, subject to renewal for a similar period at the express wish of the contracting parties or for annual periods by tacit consent, unless terminated by either party on 60 days' notice. Since 1956, the agreements have been renewed for annual periods.

The trade agreement provides for general mostfavored-nation treatment with exceptions for: (1)

Special advantages granted to bordering countries or under customs unions, (2) Argentine concessions accorded under the Act of Santiago, and (3) Ecuadoran concessions that may be granted under the Quito Charter. National treatment, as well as most-favored-nation treatment, is provided in cases of (1) internal taxation and regulations and (2) capital investment by either country in the other. Provision was also made for the establishment of a permanent mixed commission of Argentines and Ecuadorans, with offices in Buenos 

Aires and Quito, to exercise advisory and supervisory responsibilities in the implementation of the agreement.

In addition to the general provisions of the trade agreement, both countries agree to facilitate the importation of products set forth in lists attached to the agreement. Principal Ecuadoran exports are coffee, cacao, bananas, petroleum and sulfur (to be compensated with Argentine wheat), natural rubber, forms for hats, pharmaceutical products, various forest and agricultural products, and various manufactures characteristic of 

Ecuador. Principal Argentine exports are wheat (to be compensated with Ecuadoran sulfur and petroleum); wheat flour; cotton fiber, yarn, and textiles; wool, wool yarn, and wool textiles; edible oils; oats; malt; livestock; electrical apparatus; agricultural and industrial machinery; chemical and pharmaceutical products; and a variety of other products. The lists are not restrictive or exclusive; the two Governments agree to facilitate trade between themselves in other products. The lists are valid for 1 year from the effective date of the agreement. New lists are supposed to be issued during the 90 days before the expiration date, but this has not been done and the original lists are still in effect.

The payments agreement provides for the maintenance in the respective central banks of noninterest-bearing accounts in dollars. These accounts are to be used for trade and authorized nontrade transactions. Merchandise paid through the payments agreement may not be reexported without the consent of the originating country. A swing credit of $1 million was established in the original payments agreement; this credit was increased to $2 million by a protocol of November 1, 1954. No settlement of any balance will be required unless the balance exceeds this amount. 

In case of a balance greater than $2 million, the excess will be paid promptly by the debtor country in free dollars. 

Canada. A commercial modus vivendi between

Ecuador and Canada has been in force since December 1, 1950. It is automatically renewable each year unless denounced by either Government on (1) 3 months' notice, or (2) on 60 days' notice if settlement of any claim has not been effected in 60 days. The agreement provides for general most-favored-nation treatment with the exception of special advantages granted to bordering countries and/or granted under customs unions; Canadian concessions accorded the British 

Commonwealth, its dependent territories, and the 

Irish Republic; and any Ecuadoran concessions made under the Quito Charter. 

Chile. Trade and payments agreements between Chile and Ecuador became effective on January 1, 1958, for 1 year. They are tacitly renewable for annual periods. Both agreements may be terminated by either party on 90 days' notice.

The trade agreement provides for general mostfavored-nation treatment except for special concessions granted to bordering countries or under customs unions. Capital invested by either country in the other is to be treated the same as national capital and to receive most-favored-nation treatment on profits, taxes, and restrictions. Provision is made for the establishment of a permanent mixed Chilean-Ecuadoran commission as an advisory body.

In addition to the general provisions of the trade agreement, the two Governments also grant specific duty concessions. Ecuadoran articles granted duty-free entry into Chile include alkaloids; bananas; palm nuts for buttons; printed books, magazines, and newspapers; balsa wood; peanuts; pips and seeds of cotton, palm, tagua nuts, and castor oil plants; pineapples; and panama hats of all kinds. In addition, duty reductions are authorized for such Ecuadoran products as castor oil, cacao, coffee, and crude rubber. 132

International Transactions and Commercial Policy 

Chilean articles granted duty-free entry into 

Ecuador include oats, husked or not; cherries; printed books, periodicals, and other publications; apples; sodium and potassium saltpeter; copper sulfate; and fresh grapes. Duty reductions of from 8 to 30 percent are authorized for such Chilean products as copper, in bars and wire, polished, tin plated, nickel plated, plated with common metals or worked in another manner; dried plums or prunes; dynamite for mining; chickpeas; tertiated wood, counter-veneered; mining lamps; tubes and pipes of copper of various kinds; raisins; and vermouth.

The agreement also provides that Ecuadoran sugar exported to Chile will be subject to special arrangements and in no case will the sugar be given less favorable treatment than that of other countries. This refers to the special agreement establishing a Chilean-Ecuadoran sugar consortium.

The payments agreement provides for the maintenance of special accounts in dollars in the central banks of Ecuador and Chile. The accounts are to be balanced annually and settled in dollars. 

If an imbalance in excess of $500,000 occurs, the excess is to be paid direct by the debtor nation or through a third country that has a payments agreement with both Ecuador and Chile. The payments agreement is not applicable to Chilean exports of crude copper and nitrate, which must be paid for in dollars. 

On August 24, 1955, the Chilean and Ecuadoran

Governments signed an economic cooperation agreement in Santiago. At the same time, they signed a special agreement for a sugar development project that would be established in Ecuador and that would be capitalized at $10 million, with each country contributing equal amounts. The project envisaged an annual production of 50,000 tons of sugar, of which Chile agreed to purchase three-fourths. No action, however, has been taken to implement this agreement. 

Colombia.-A trade agreement between Ecuador and Colombia, signed on July 6, 1942, and modified on October 14, 1943, entered into force April 

4, 1945, on the exchange of instruments of ratification. This agreement, which is still in force, takes precedence over the Treaty of Friendship, Commerce and Navigation, which was signed by the two countries on August 10, 1905.

The agreement provides for general mostfavored-nation treatment with the exception of special advantages granted to bordering countries or extended under customs unions. The specific concessions granted under the agreement are considered special advantages granted to bordering countries and as such are not extended to many other countries having most-favored-nation agreements with Ecuador. National and most-favorednation treatment is provided on internal taxes and charges and on capital investment for industrial development.

In addition to the general most-favored-nation treatment, certain products in trade between the two countries are free of duty. Ecuador grants duty-free entry for Colombian exports of medicinal oils; toiletries; articles of porcelain, flint, and glass; anise; sweet carbonated drinks; leather bags; cement, asbestos-cement, and cement manufactures; cigars and cigarettes, provided the imports are authorized by the Bureau of Monopolies; hides and skins; tin cans; pharmaceutical specialties; medicinal syrups; books and magazines; vegetable lard; agricultural and industrial machinery; cotton and silk stockings; metal furniture; woolen cloth; coal; crackers; confectionery; cosmetics; metal doors and windows; nails; sisal sacks; caustic soda; sera and vaccines; raw and prepared tobacco (trade controlled by Ecuadoran monopoly); bottle caps; printed cotton cloth; and table services and household utensils of iron and aluminum. 

Ecuadoran exports granted free entry into Colombia are mineral water; cotton; rice; pearl barley and barley in grain; sugar; vegetable ivory manufactures; cacao; pickled or jerked meat; woolen cloth and shawls; cement; beans and lentils; fresh fruit; carbonic gas; gasoline; dry ice; cotton and woolen yarn; yeast cake or powder; books and magazines; butter; salt; wheat; candles; chalk; calcium chloride for industrial use and pharmaceutical specialties; and cotton cloth (crude and dyed sheetings and drills). 

On July 31, 1955, a new commercial agreement between Ecuador and Colombia was signed. This new agreement provides for an overall reduction in customs tariffs of 20 percent and a special reduction of 80 percent on pharmaceutical specialties and products, insecticides, and veterinary products. This treaty, however, has not been ratified.

A payments agreement between the two countries became effective on April 1, 1949. The agreement is renewable for annual periods by tacit consent unless denounced by either country on 3 months' notice. Accounts are maintained in the Bank of the Republic of Colombia and in the 

Central Bank of Ecuador for trade and authorized nontrade transactions. Items traded under this agreement may not be reexported. The accounts are balanced twice yearly, on June 30 and December 31, with payments made in dollars within 30 days at the request of the creditor country. 

Finland. A commercial modus vivendi between 

Ecuador and Finland went into effect on December 11, 1957, for an initial period of 1 year. It is automatically renewable each year unless denounced by either government on 90 days' notice.

Commodity lists, which may be modified by mutual agreement each year, are an integral part 133

of the modus vivendi. Ecuadoran commodities include cacao and coffee beans, rice, balsa wood, oleaginous seeds, edible vegetable oils and fats, oranges, bananas, pineapples, rugs, manufactures of toquilla straw, leather manufactures, tagua nut buttons, cocoa butter, wild animal skins, ethyl alcohol, and bark and bark extracts for tanning. 

Finnish commodities include plywood, paper and cardboard, industrial and agricultural machinery, iron, porcelain, earthenware, and glass products.

The agreement establishes two local committees, one in Quito and the other in Helsinki, to develop trade between the two countries. 

France. A trade agreement and a payments agreement between Ecuador and France have been in force since October 25, 1949. The agreements are tacitly renewable each year unless terminated by either country on 4 months' notice. They may also be terminated at the end of 30 days if satisfactory settlement of any claims has not been effected within 30 days. France gave notice of termination in 1956, but later withdrew the notice and the agreements are still in force. 

The trade agreement specifies that all French colonies, possessions, and territories and the French customs unions with Monaco and the Saar are included in the agreement. The agreement provides for general most-favored-nation treatment except for special privileges enjoyed among the French territories and for advantages granted by either party to bordering countries or for advantages under customs unions. National treatment, as well as most-favored-nation treatment, is provided on internal taxes, consular fees, and capital invested by either country in the other for the development of trade and basic industries. 

Merchandise imported into either country under this agreement may not be reexported. 

Principal Ecuadoran exports to France, and the only transactions negotiable through the payments agreement, are bananas; cacao; coffee; rice; palm nuts; balsa wood; rubber; hardwoods, including railroad ties; hats of toquilla straw; cinchona bark; mangrove bark; quinine; cocoa butter; wild animal skins; leather for shoe soles; annatto; oleaginous seeds and fruits; vegetable oils and greases; vegetable fibers, wool, and sponges; sharks' liver oil; and typical Eucadoran handicrafts. Ecuadoran imports from France which are negotiable through the payments agreement include all merchandise permitted entry into 

Ecuador. Luxury items may amount to 30 percent of total French shipments. The payments agreement provides for a swing credit of $500,000. 

Mixed advisory commissions are established in

Paris and Quito to supervise the functioning of the agreements and to suggest modifications when advisable. 

Federal Republic of Germany.-Ecuador and the

Federal Republic of Germany have a trade treaty and a trade agreement, both of which have been in effect since October 15, 1954. The treaty and the agreement are renewable for annual periods by tacit consent or may be denounced by either Government on 3 months' notice. The two countries formerly had a payments agreement, which was terminated by Germany on January 1, 1956.

The trade treaty provides for most-favorednation treatment except for special advantages accorded to bordering countries and/or granted under customs unions or economic or defense unions. Prerogatives derived from the 1952 treaty between Germany and the Allied Powers and concessions granted by Ecuador under the Quito Charter are also exempt from most-favored-nation treatment. In matters of capital investment, taxation, and exchange and other commercial controls, the application of mostfavored-nation treatment is stipulated. 

Under the trade agreement, the contracting parties agree to facilitate trade transactions and to provide necessary documentation freely. No specific German products are mentioned, but the German Republic agrees to grant special facility to trade in the following Ecuadoran products: 

Bananas; banana flour; cacao beans; cocoa butter; rice; corn; pineapples; jellies, juices, and syrups of naranjilla, banana, and pineapple; molle seed; tuna; animal forage, except cottonseed cake; coffee; tobacco; hardwood; balsa wood; castor beans; castor bean oil; huantuc seeds (Datura red); atropine from chamico (Datura Stramonio); huarango pods; musk; cinchona bark; mangrove bark; annatto; pinenut seed; fertilizer cakes from pinenut and castor beans; rubber; sulfur; hats of toquilla straw; vegetable ivory; kapok; wild animal skins; wool carpets; and fiber of manila hemp, sisal, or maguey.

This list is not limiting in any sense. The trade agreement also provided for the establishment of a mixed commission, with offices in both capitals, for supervisory and administrative purposes.

Italy. A trade and payments agreement has been in force between Ecuador and Italy since May 7, 1952. The agreement is automatically renewed for annual periods unless denounced by either party on 3 months' notice.

The trade agreement provides for unconditional and unlimited most-favored-nation treatment except for special advantages granted to bordering countries or to countries under actual or provisional customs unions. Exceptions are also made of special concessions granted by Italy to San Marino, the Vatican, and other internationally recognized juridic territories. The contracting parties agree to facilitate trade between the two countries, especially in products listed in the agreement. Trade is not limited to the articles mentioned. 134

International Transactions and Commercial Policy 

Ecuadoran exports mentioned in the agreement are coffee, cacao, oleaginous seeds, hardwoods, vegetable ivory, balsa wood, rubber, quinine, and raw hides not suitable for leather making. 

Among the long list of Italian exports are: 

Preserved foodstuffs; dried fruit; wines; edible olive oil; medicated cotton; thread, cloth, and textile manufactures of linen, cotton, hemp, jute, wool, silk, and artificial fibers; insulated copper wire and cable; cutlery; nails and bolts.

The list of Italian exports also includes metal covers; manufactures of aluminum and its alloys; industrial and agricultural machinery; electric equipment; automobiles, motorcycles, and velocipedes; railroad equipment; office equipment; agricultural tools; scientific and surgical instruments; lenses and spectacles; precision instruments in general; glass and crystal; rubber manufactures; citric and tartaric acid; antimony salts; glue; disinfectants; phosphorus; insecticides; pharmaceutical and medicinal preparations and specialties; saccharin; chemical products; musical instruments; hats; buttons; notions; games; artists' brushes; motion picture films; handicrafts; fruits and fruit juices; licorice; cheese; liquors; almond oil; tomato paste and peeled tomatoes; marble and alabaster; sulfur; ceramic manufactures; and leather manufactures.

The payments agreement provides facilities for all trade between the two countries. It provides for the maintenance of accounts in dollars in the 

Central Bank of Ecuador and the Italian Office of Exchange. The accounts are balanced every 

6 months. If the balance exceeds $150,000, the debtor nation must pay the excess in dollars within 30 days; meanwhile, transactions under the agreement are suspended. 

Norway.-Norway and Ecuador have a commercial modus vivendi, which entered into effect 

February 1, 1951. It is automatically renewed for annual periods unless terminated by either country on 60 days' notice. The agreement provides for general most-favored-nation treatment, subject to the usual exception of special advantages granted to bordering countries or to countries under customs unions. Exemption is also made of concessions that may be granted by Ecuador under the Quito Charter. 

Spain. Trade and payments agreements between Ecuador and Spain have been in force since 

August 24, 1955. They are automatically renewed for annual periods until denounced by either party on 3 months' notice.

The trade agreement provides for unconditional and unlimited most-favored-nation treatment except for special advantages granted by either party to bordering countries or to countries under customs unions. Exemption is also extended to special customs concessions granted by Spain to Spanish Morocco and Spanish territories in Africa, and any Ecuadoran concessions under the 

Quito Charter. Most-favored-nation treatment is also granted on internal taxes.

The contracting parties agree to facilitate trade, especially of products listed in the agreement. 

Ecuadoran exports listed are: Alcohol; annatto; sulfur; cacao beans; cocoa butter; coffee; cinchona bark; rubber; manila hemp, sisal, and maguey fiber; hats and other manufactures of toquilla straw; handicrafts and other typical manufactures; kapok; balsa wood; castorseeds; tobacco; vegetable ivory; and totaquine.

Spanish exports listed are chemical fertilizers; olive oil; essential oils; handicrafts characteristic of the country; metalware, hardware, cutlery, and watermeters; firearms and cartridges; cork; crystal and worked glass; tools for farmers and craftsmen; books and educational printed matter; sanitary and domestic earthenware and artistic earthenware; rubber tires and tubes; sewing machines; agricultural and industrial machinery, apparatus, and tools; mercury; electric equipment for heating and lighting; electric motors and pumps; scientific precision equipment and scales; printing material and stationery; cigarette paper; printed motion picture films; phonograph records; woolen textiles; and paints, colors, and coloring earths.

Trade under the agreement is not limited to the articles listed, and the lists may be enlarged at any time. Merchandise paid for under the payments agreement may not be reexported. 

The payments agreement provides for the maintenance of special accounts in dollars in the Central Bank of Ecuador and the Spanish Institute of Foreign Money and for the establishment of a swing credit of $500,000. The accounts are balanced at the end of each month. If the balance exceeds $500,000, the creditor country may request immediate payment of the excess in dollars and meanwhile suspend new transactions under the agreement. The agreement provides that upon its expiration there is to be a period of 6 months in which to liquidate transactions under the agreement, after which the debtor country will pay the remaining balance in dollars. 

Switzerland.-Switzerland and Ecuador signed a commercial agreement in Quito on October 8, 1957. It will not become effective until instruments of ratification are exchanged. 

The agreement runs for an initial period of 1 year, with renewal by tacit consent for annual periods, unless denounced by either country on at least 3 months' prior notice. The agreement provides for most-favored-nation treatment with the exception of privileges granted neighboring countries, advantages accorded under a customs union or in a free-exchange zone, and any concessions extended by Ecuador under the Quito Charter.

Capital investment by either country in the other 135

for commercial and industrial development will be accorded national and most-favored-nation treatment.

An important trade concession to Ecuador in the agreement is a reduction in the Swiss import duty on Ecuadoran bananas. The duty is lowered from 40 to 25 Swiss francs per 100 kilograms. 

Uruguay.-Trade and payments agreements between Ecuador and Uruguay were signed on June 4, 1955. They will become effective on the exchange of instruments of ratification. They will be valid for 3 years and will be renewable expressly for a like period or tacitly for 1 year until denounced by either party on 60 days' notice.

The trade agreement provides for unconditional and unlimited most-favored-nation treatment except for special advantages granted to bordering countries or to countries under customs unions. 

Exemption extends also to Uruguayan concessions to Bolivia and Paraguay and to Ecuadoran concessions under the Quito Charter. National treatment, as well as most-favored-nation treatment, is granted on internal taxes. A mixed commission, with offices in both capitals, will be established as a supervisory and advisory body.

The contracting parties agree to facilitate trade between the two countries, especially of the goods listed.

Ecuadoran exports listed are castor bean seeds and oil; annatto; articles characteristic of the country; sulfur; bananas; cacao beans; cocoa butter; coffee; cinchona bark; rubber; manila hemp, sisal, and maguey fiber; hats and other manufactures of toquilla straw; banana flour; kapok; balsa wood; hardwoods; syrups, juices, saps, and jellies of naranjilla, banana, and pineapple; crude petroleum; wild animal skins; pineapples; pyrethrum flowers; pharmaceutical products and specialties; blood plasma substitutes; pinenut seeds; tobacco; vegetable ivory; and totaquine. 

Uruguayan exports are linseed oil; glass bottles and containers; buttons, except those of tagua; dried meat; preserved foodstuffs; leather transmission belts and bands; cured hides and skins, except shoe leather; stearine; glycerin; wheat flour; weaving thread or worsted; wool; tires; hog lard; pressed wood; pharmaceutical products and specialties; tallow; felt hats and hat forms; woolen textiles; wheat; glass; wine; and nontranslucent tableware.

Trade is not limited to the articles listed. Merchandise traded through the payments agreement may not be reexported without the approval of the contracting parties.

The payments agreement provides for the maintenance of an account in dollars in the Central 

Bank of Ecuador in the name of the Bank of the Republic of Uruguay and the establishment of a swing credit of $200,00. The account is balanced at the end of every month. If the balance is in excess of $200,000, the debtor nation is obligated to pay the excess in dollars. The provisions of the payments agreement are not applicable to Ecuadoran exports of petroleum. 136

PART Business and Tax Laws

CHAPTER XIII Business Organization Law FORMS OF ORGANIZATION 

Foreign persons may conduct a direct investment in Ecuador through an Ecuadoran sole proprietorship, corporation, or partnership, by themselves or in company with Ecuadorans. Foreign corporations have the option of establishing a branch or agency of the parent corporation or of organizing a subsidiary corporation in Eucuador. 

To be eligible to engage in business, all foreign individuals or entities must comply with ordinary registration requirements, must join a local chamber of commerce, industry, or agriculture, and must show that they are of good moral character and have not been convicted of fraud or illegal bankruptcy.2

Sole Proprietorship 

The one-owner type of business in Ecuador is usually limited to modest endeavors. The individual starting in business by himself, whether he be foreign or Ecuadoran, in most cases simply obtains the necessary permits and licenses and sets up shop, taking title to property, renting, buying, and selling in his own name, very much as he would do in the United States.

Every merchant operating with capital in excess of 1,000 sucres must list his business in the official list of merchants (Matrícula de Comercio) and register with the local court and the Registrar of 

Public Documents (Oficina de Inscripciones). 

He must submit in writing the following information: (1) Type of business, and whether it is wholesale or retail, (2) place of business, (3) name of the firm and sample of signature to be used, and (4) the amount of capital to be used in the business. The merchant must also register 

1 However, firms engaged in land exploitation must be domiciled in Ecuador if Eucadorans own more than 10 percent of the firm decree of April 22, 1936, published in Registro Oficial, Quito, April 30, 1986). 

Decree 5, January 5, 1938; decree 51, published in Registro Oficial, Quito, August 21, 1936; decree 24, published in 

Registro Oficial, Quito, February 10, 1987; decree of September 

7, 1953, published in Registro Oficial, Quito, October 22, 1953. 

Code of Commerce, articles 21-34; decree 228, May 15, 1936; decree 177, May 12, 1937. copies of any circulars announcing the establishment, alterations, or discontinuance of the business. 

Foreigners who have entered Ecuador as tourists cannot engage in business.* 

Corporation 5

The corporation is the most popular type of business organization in Ecuador. It is favored in most cases because of its features facilitating continuity of existence and limited liability and its maneuverability in capital recruitment, organization, and management. 

In Ecuador the counterpart of the United

States corporation is called Compañía Anónima. 

Its capital consists of shares subscribed by shareholders whose liability is limited to the extent of their shareholdings. It is a juridical person with its own name, domicile, and property. 

Foreigners may organize a corporation by themselves or in company with Ecuadorans. There are no minimum capital requirements. Directors and stockholders may be foreigners, and the meetings of the board of directors and stockholders may be held abroad, if the corporation's articles of incorporation or bylaws so provide. Local judges have authority to protect minority stockholders but closely held corporations are possible. 

Insofar as Ecuadoran law is concerned, a foreign corporation may participate in the formation of an Ecuadoran corporation. Only two parties are required to organize a corporation, and one of the parties for all practical purposes may be a "straw." A foreign corporation may also buy a minority or majority interest in an existing corporation. 

To participate in the formation of an Ecuadoran corporation, the foreign corporation must act through its properly authorized agent. Ordi- 

Law of November 4, 1948, article 10. 

Code of Commerce, articles 21-34, 261-266, 285-356; Civil 

Code, articles 2076-2138; decree 130, March 10, 1927; Code of 

Commerce, amendment of October 28, 1929, published in Registro 

Oficial, October 30, 1929; decree 160, March 24, 1936; decree 230, published in Registro Oficial, Quito, May 1, 1936; decree 228, May 15, 1936; decree 177, May 12, 1937. 139

narily the agent is supplied with copies of the foreign corporation's constitutive documents, a resolution of the board of directors authorizing the agency and stating its purpose, and a power of attorney, all authenticated by the Ecuadoran consul. Armed with these documents, the agent may then join two other parties and proceed to incorporate.

The corporation is formed by means of a written contract by which the parties agree to contribute capital or services (industria) to engage in mercantile operations and to divide the profits therefrom. The Commercial Code does not specify the required contents of the contract but by implication various provisions of the code seem to indicate that the principal points to be covered are the following: (1) Name of the proposed business, (2) names of the stockholders and the capital contributed and the shares to be held by each, (3) object or objects of the business, (4) declaration that the capital has been paid in, or a statement of the date and method of payment of the capital contribution, (5) name and nationality of each of the managers or directors and officers, and (6) proposed location of the business. 6

The corporation's contract and bylaws are formalized by an Ecuadoran notary and then presented to the local court for approval. The corporation must be approved by legislative authority if it is organized for the purpose of constructing public roads, navigable channels, railroads, or other undertakings affected with the public interest. In effect, however, such approval consists of a negotiated contract with the National Government. Once approval is obtained, the notarial document must then be recorded with the office of the Registrar of Public Documents and published in a newspaper, at least once, together with the certificate of approval.

The Commercial Code is silent as to many matters usually covered in corporation statutes. Some of the more important provisions of the code are given below:

Corporate name.-The name of the corporation should designate the object of its business. All invoices, advertisements, publications, and other printed matter issued by a corporation must show the name of the corporation preceded or followed immediately by the words "Compañía Anónima" and a statement of the corporation's capital. 

Promoters. Promoters are personally liable for obligations they may incur in organizing the corporation but in turn they may have a cross action against the corporation in certain cases. Promoters may not reserve for themselves any special Ecuadoran law does not require that a corporation have directors or a board of directors. Management may be lodged in the corporate officers or managers. However, it is customary to provide in the bylaws for a board with at least three directors. gain or advantage from the capital of the corporation or in the form of shares. However, they may make arrangements to compensate themselves from part of the profits of the corporation for a fixed period of time. Actual costs in forming the corporation are reimbursable. 

Management.-The corporation is managed by its directors, officers, or managers, who serve for terms specified in the corporation's constitutive documents or at the pleasure of the shareholders. 

Directors need not be shareholders and they may be compensated for their services. However, the office of director cannot be held by the corporation's bankers, constructors,' lessees, or suppliers of materials.

Directors may not delegate their duties of administration but they may appoint agents to undertake a specific business transaction. Directors are personally liable to third parties in connec tion with: (1) Execution of legal formalities per taining to the corporation, (2) accuracy of the books of the corporation, and (3) the existence of funds to cover declared dividends. Directors in office at the time of organization are also held responsible as to the truth of statements on capital subscription and paid-in capital.

Capital. A corporation is not definitely organized unless its entire capital has been subscribed. 

Shares may be issued for money, raw materials, or other values which may be used in the type of business in which the company proposes to engage provided contributions made in a form other than money are appraised by experts and such appraisal is accepted by the stockholders. scribers are personally responsible for the full payment of the shares to which they have subscribed, whether or not they subsequently assign such shares.

Sub- If capital is depleted to one-third of the original amount, the corporation must be liquidated unless the stockholders prefer to refinance or make other adjustments.

All corporations are required to build a reserve fund amounting to not less than 10 percent of the capital. Five percent of profits must be set aside each year until reserve fund requirements are met. 

Shares.-No-par-value shares are not authorized. Both registered and bearer shares may be issued, but bearer shares must be fully paid up. 

Registered shares not fully paid up may be issued only in provisional certificates, and such certificates may not be negotiated as commercial paper until one-fourth of the shares is paid up. Bearer shares are negotiated by mere delivery, but registered shares must be recorded in the books of the corporation. The law permits the conversion of shares from bearer to registered and vice versa. 

7 Presumably, contractors constructing buildings for the use of the corporation. 140

Business Organization Law 

There are no limitations or restrictions as to the number of shares which may be issued or the minimum and maximum value of each share. It is assumed that the corporation must have at least two shareholders, but there is no limitation as to the number of shares any one or group of shareholders may own.

Shareholders' meetings and voting.-Annual shareholders' meetings are compulsory, and on this occasion the balance sheet of the corporation must be presented for approval. Shareholders must appoint auditors to inspect, every 6 months, management statements on inventory, profit and loss, and the balance sheet.

Shareholders as auditors.-Every shareholder has the right within a period of 8 days prior to the annual meeting to inspect the list of the corporation's shareholders and to obtain a copy of the annual balance sheet and a copy of the report of the auditors.

Shareholders representing one-third of the total shares of the corporation are entitled to call a general shareholders' meeting.

The decisions reached in a general meeting of shareholders, within the limits of its authority as determined by the corporation's bylaws, are binding on all shareholders. However, a minority representing no less than 25 percent of the shares may appeal a decision of the majority to the Superior Court (Corte Superior), and the decision of this court in turn may be appealed to the Supreme Court (Corte Suprema) by either of the parties.8 

A shareholders' meeting has a quorum when at least one-half of the capital invested in the corporation is represented. When the percentage of capital represented is lower, a second meeting may be held, upon an announcement at least 8 days in advance, outlining the purpose of the meeting.

The second meeting may transact business regardless of how much capital is represented. 

Shareholders may be represented at meetings by proxy; directors, however, may not act as proxies, nor may directors vote in questions dealing with approval of the corporation's balance sheet or in deliberation respecting the obligations and responsibilities of directors. 

Dividends.-Dividends are declared at the dis cretion of the board of directors. No dividends may be declared except out of earned profits. 

However, in case of corporations requiring a certain period of time to begin operations on the proposed basis, interest may be paid out of capital, but only for a determined period of time, and provided rates do not exceed those generally accepted in business. 

8 Decree 153 of 1936, published in Registro Oficial, Quito, March 

18, 1936. 9

Partnerships and Other Forms 

In addition to the sole proprietorship and the corporation, Ecuadoran law recognizes the general partnership and the limited partnership, both of which are considered juridical persons. Partnerships which engage in business in Ecuador must maintain a legally appointed agent in the country to accept process and to answer for the firm in legal matters, according to legislative decree of October 21, 1929, published in Registro 

Oficial, October 30, 1929.

The law also recognizes a business organization for noncommercial purposes, which is called Sociedad Civil and is regulated by the Civil Code, unless it be organized as a corporation, in which case the provisions of the Commercial Code apply. 

Another form of business association which is authorized by articles 357 through 362 of the Com mercial Code is called Cuentas en Participación. 

This is an ad hoc arrangement with characteristics of the joint venture form sometimes used in the 

United States.

When the partnership is general, i. e., when all partners are jointly and severally liable, it is called Compañía en Nombre Colectivo; and when some of the partners are liable only to the extent of their investment in the business, the partnership is called Compañía en Comandita and is roughly comparable with the limited partnership in the United States. The Compañía en Coman dita may issue shares, in which case it is known as Compañía en Comandita Por Acciones and in some respects resembles the corporation except that, in the former, at least one of the partners must be a general partner who remains liable without limitation.10

A partnership contract must be in writing and must be processed as a notarial document, and an extract of the contract must be registered with the office of the Registrar of Public Documents and published in a newspaper. 

A fair number of sizable firms still use the partnership form in Ecuador. As in the United

States, the main drawback to the Ecuadoran partnership is the problem which arises if partners die or have disagreements.

Branch of Foreign Corporation 

A foreign corporation wishing to establish a branch or direct agency in Ecuador must first obtain permission from the Ecuadoran local court. • Code of Commerce, articles 21 through 34, 261 through 284, 288 through 356; Civil Code, articles 2076 through 2138; decree 

130. March 10, 1927; Code of Commerce amendment of May 5, 1928; Code of Commerce amendment of October 28, 1929, published in Registro Oficial, October 30, 1929; Code of Commerce amendment of May 15, 1936, published in Registro Oficial, May 19, 1936; decree 160 of March 24, 1936; decree 228, May 15, 1936; decree 177, May 12, 1937. 

10 Code of Commerce, article 282. 

141

For this purpose, the foreign corporation must give its agent a power of attorney, supported by a resolution of the board of directors, authorizing him to establish a branch in Ecuador. He must be supplied with a certified copy of the articles of incorporation and bylaws of the foreign corporation showing that the corporation is duly constituted and authorized in the country of its domicile and that it is permitted to do business abroad.

These documents must be authenticated by the Ecuadoran consul and officially translated into Spanish. In Ecuador the documents are then formalized and entered into the permanent record of an Ecuadoran notary, published in a local newspaper at least once, and registered in the office of the Registrar of Public Documents. The corpora tion must also join a local chamber of commerce, industry, or agriculture, and it must register in the official list of merchants for which purpose it must supply, among other data, the amount of capital with which the branch is to operate and the name and signature of the person who is to act for the company.

The foreign corporation is required to maintain in Ecuador an agent or representative empowered to answer for it legally." There is no requirement as to the nationality of such agent. 

Often, income tax factors weigh heavily in making the choice between the branch operation or the Ecuadoran subsidiary as the method of conducting an investment in Ecuador (see chapter 

XIV). One legal factor which sometimes tends to discourage the use of the branch is the possibility that foreign legal judgments against the branch may be asserted against the parent organization. Occasionally, however, the nature of the business or the structure of the parent corporation is such that the use of one or the other method of operation is dictated despite the tax consequences.

All individual merchants, partnerships, and corporations must maintain in Spanish a daily journal, an accounts ledger, a balance ledger, an inventory ledger, and a correspondence book. 

Retail merchants may carry only one book, but they must record daily total cash sales and each individual sale made on credit, and must make a business inventory when commencing business and at the end of each business year.
