ORGANIZATION COSTS 

Legal fees in the establishment of a business organization will, of course, depend upon the professional standing of the attorney and the complexity of the problem presented in each situation. 

Legal fees in Ecuador are no higher than those in the United States.

The charges incurred for notarial fees, stamp taxes, and registration fees vary in accordance with the number of official papers to be filed and the capital of the business. Ordinarily, costs are higher for corporations. Excluding attorneys' fees, organization costs for a corporation with a capital of 100,000 sucres ($6,600) would approximate 3,600 sucres ($238); including attorneys' fees, the costs would approximate 10,000 sucres ($660).13 Stamp taxes, which must be paid on the constitutive documents and stock certificates, are imposed on a graduated basis and in accordance with the capital of the business organization or the value of the stock certificate. Stamp taxes on capitalization of 1 million sucres ($66,000), for example, amount to 7,000 sucres ($462).

Registration charges are made for compulsory membership in one of the chambers of commerce, industry, or agriculture. The Chamber of Commerce of Guayaquil, for example, assesses an initiation fee ranging from 125 to 375 sucres ($8.75 to $24.75), a library fee ranging from 65 to 500 sucres ($4.29 to $33), and regular monthly dues ranging from 15 to 250 sucres ($1 to $16.50), in accordance with the capitalization of the firm being admitted." 14 Commercial firms are also subject to the payment of a franchise tax (patente) assessed by the municipalities. In Guayaquil, the tax is assessed on the basis of the firm's capital and varies from

INDUSTRIAL PROPERTY PROTECTION 

Patents

The Ley de Patentes de Exclusiva y Explota cion de Inventos (the Patent Law of July 17, 1928), as amended, governs the issuance of patents.17 Under this law, patents for improvements of methods or for discoveries which are the origin of new industries or new applications of old industries will be granted only if the improve- .ments or discoveries (or inventions) bring about a substantial change and improve the existing methods and applications. Patents may also be granted for imports of machinery or apparatus which is to be used in the manufacture or preparation of articles not previously produced in Ecuador, or which represents a significant change in the methods of manufacture or production. 

Application. An application for a patent must be presented to the Ministry of Economy, accompanied by a detailed description of the invention, method, improvement, machine, or process for which exclusive rights are requested. Documentation must be in duplicate. The application must include a certificate showing payment of onefourth of the fee for the patent applied for. If the patent is denied, the payment is not refunded. 

If the patent applied for concerns an imported article, the application must include an agreement to consign to the Government 5 percent of the value of the industry. This is a deposit to serve as guaranty of installation, and it is refunded after 

1 year of operation of the imported equipment.

The deposit is not returned if the patent is forfeited. 

15 Ordinance of the city of Guayaquil, December 21, 1944. 

16 Decree of September 13, 1943. 

17 The text of the law, with amendments, was printed in the 

Registro Oficial, Quito, May 3, 1937. In 1957, the Ministry of

Economy proposed the enactment of a new law covering patents and trademarks. Action on the proposal has been deferred, and the specific provisions of the proposal have not been publicized. 

The applicant may be a national or a foreigner, a natural person or a company with juridical personality, or a commercial corporation established in accordance with the Commercial Code and other laws of Ecuador or of the country to which the applicant belongs. The inventor or his assignee may apply for the patent.

If an application is filed in accordance with the legal provisions, the Ministry of Economy orders publication of the application once in the Registro Oficial and 3 times in 2 important newspapers of the country within 8 days after the presentation of the application. If within 90 days following publication, there is no opposition to granting the patent, 3 experts are appointed by the Ministry to report on the application. If there is opposition, the Ministry will rule on the matter before the appointment of the experts, unless their opinion is needed. The report of the experts on the application, in turn, is studied by a committee composed of the Under Secretary of the Ministry of Economy, the Chief of the Patent Office, and the three experts. If the decision of the committee is favorable, a decree granting the patent is issued. The decree is signed by the President of the Republic, the Minister of Economy, and other officials, and is recorded in the appropriate register. One copy of the decree, constituting the patent title, is returned to the applicant with the duplicates of the documents which accompanied his application. The Ministry retains the application and the original documents. 

Publication. When the patent is granted, the application, a description of the invention or imported article, drawings, any other documents, and the respective decree are published in the Registro Oficial. In addition, the patent must be published in 2 of the country's principal newspapers for 15 consecutive days, or for 30 consecutive days in the case of a patent for an imported article. 

Time and validity.-Patents for invention or improvement are granted for 3 to 12 years, depending upon the importance of the invention or improvement, and may be valid for the entire country or for a specific district. Patents for imported articles also are granted for 3 to 12 years, depending upon the amount of capital invested, and they are valid only in the Province in which installation is to be made. In the case of patents for imports, when application is made, the Ministry of Economy grants a provisional patent for 18 months, within which period a plant must be installed or the grant expires. Patents may not be renewed.

Modification. The law provides for the modification of patents. Anyone who has obtained a patent for an invention may request a certificate for a change, improvement, or addition in the original invention. The procedure for obtaining a certificate is the same as for obtaining the origi- 143

nal patent. Certificates granted patentees are issued only for the period covered by the main patent. After the first year of a patent concession, anyone may apply for a patent which changes or improves the registered patent. The new or improved patent may not be used during the concession period of the first patent unless arrangements are made between the patentees or other holders of the rights. The use of improved patents may be made obligatory at any time, however, if the improvements contribute to the safety of workers or are of acknowledged public value. 

Expiration. Patents expire at the termination of the concession period and also if they are not used within 2 years from date of grant and if their use is suspended for 2 years without just cause.

Patents for imports expire if installation of the machinery or apparatus is not carried out within the prescribed period of 18 months or if their use is suspended for more than 6 months without just cause. 

Fees. Patent fees in 1955 ranged from 150 to 

1,500 sucres ($10 to $100) on inventions and improvements and from 400 to 2,000 sucres ($26 to $132) on imported articles, depending upon the number of years for which the patent was granted. Trademarks 

The use, ownership, and registry of trademarks are governed by the Ley de Marcas de Fabrica (Trademarks Law) of September 21, 1928, and its amendments.

The use of a trademark is optional. It may, however, be required if the public good so demands. Only the manufacturer or producer, or an attorney authorized to act for him, may register a trademark to protect the articles he produces. If a trademark is used without having been registered, the user is not entitled to the rights conceded by the law to registered trademarks.

Registration of trademarks is under the direction of the Ministry of Economy. The application for registration must include a detailed description of the trademark and must be accompanied by 20 copies and a cliche of the trademark.

If the application is in order and if the trademark does not contravene the law, notice of the application is then published in the Registro 

Oficial 4 times at intervals of not more than 10 days. During the publication period, anyone may file an objection to the registration if he believes that he has a better right by reason of prior registration and use, or if he believes that the trademark contravenes the law. If the Ministry finds such an objection to be justified, the case is referred to a Provincial court for judicial settlement.

If no objection is made, the Ministry of Econ omy orders registration of the trademark. The decree of registration is transcribed verbatim in the Registro Oficial, including the date. Registration is completed by a certificate from the 

Under Secretary of the Ministry, indicating the serial number of the registration, the number of pages, and the fact that the decree has been authentically recorded.

Registration of a trademark is valid for an initial period of 20 years, after which it will lapse if not renewed. Renewals may be obtained indefinitely for additional periods of 15 years. 

Trademarks may be inherited or transferred.

Unless there is a stipulation to the contrary, any agreement for the transfer of a business or industry will grant to the transferee the right to use of the marks employed in that business or industry. 

Registration fees for foreign trademarks in 

1955 were 750 sucres ($49.50) for the initial registration and 375 sucres ($24.75) for renewals. 

Fees for registering national trademarks were 50 percent lower. 

Trade Names 

The name of a manufacturer or producer, firm name, name of a corporation, and the sign or designation of a firm or establishment doing business in specific articles or products constitute property under the law. 

Any person intending to engage in industry, business, or branch thereof already exploited by another person with the same name or conventional designation must adopt some modification which will render such name or designation visibly distinct from that used by the previously existing establishment. 

If a party injured by the use of a name fails to protest within 1 year from the date such use became publicly known, his right to a claim for damages is lost. Claim must be made by suit in court. 144

CHAPTER XIV Tax System 

1 Ecuador's tax system is complicated, but in general there has been little indication that the tax burden is a deterrent to foreign investment. 

Many of the country's indirect taxes, i. e., customs duties, production, sales, and social welfare taxes, and a host of minor taxes affecting business, in practice become elements of operating costs, and the direct taxes paid on business income rarely range higher than 15 to 25 percent.

In various Government reports the tax system of Ecuador has been criticized. Complaint is made that the taxing authority is too diffused, that there are too many taxes, that much of the tax burden falls on the lower income groups, that tax collection is wasteful, and that tax evasion is widespread. It has been pointed out that in 1951 about 85 percent of the total tax intake was in the form of indirect taxes and that in recent times the National Treasury has had control over less than 40 percent of all Government revenues. 

The tax system now in effect permits the 

National Government, the Provincial govern ments, and the cities to levy taxes. It has also been the practice to create special taxes earmarked for a large number of semiautonomous public institutions and for special emergencies. In some cases these taxes are most minute. In 1953, it was estimated that the number of separate taxes or surtaxes levied in the country reached 250. In 

1954, the National Government alone levied 116 different taxes on 50 sources of taxation.2 Export and import transactions, for example, were subject to some 85 different levies or service charges, 1 Detailed information on Ecuador's taxes is presented in the following works, which, although not covering the subject completely, are the only readily available current sources: Rentas 

Descentralizadas de la Nación, 1951, Suplemento No. 1 (1953),

Rafael Reyna Drouet, Talleres Gráficos de Servicios de Sumin istros, Quito, Ecuador; Esteban Amador Navarro, Leyes Mercantiles y Tributarias de la República del Ecuador, Segunda 

Edición, 1955, Reed & Reed Co., Guayaquil, Ecuador; Ley 

Orgánica de Aduanas, Ley Arancelaria y Arancel de Aduanas, 1953, Ministerio del Tesoro, Quito, Ecuador; Codificacion de Leyes 

Tributarias del Ecuador Hasta Junio-1957, Contraloria General de la Nacion. Talleres Gráficas del Servicio de Suministros, Quito, Ecuador. References to any taxes not covered in these compilations probably can be found only in the daily issues of the Government's official register, Registro Oficial, or in offices of the various Government Departments which deal with tax matters. 

2 Costa, Jasper S., Taxes in Ecuador, Quito, 1956. Published in Informe a la Nacion, 1956, Ministerio del Tesoro, Quito, 1956. all requiring an inordinate amount of paper work. 

Since 1953 about half of the customs taxes have been unified, and the Ministry of Treasury has plans for further unification and centralization of other taxes.

Tax changes, however, ordinarily require congressional approval. Because of the confused state of fiscal, budget, and tax affairs, and the traditional rivalry in tax matters between the richer coastal section and the less commercial mountain area of the country, a thorough job of tax reform may take some time. An economic mission known as the Curran Mission, which was organized by the Ecuadoran Government with staff assistance of the World Bank and of the International

Monetary Fund and of the United Nations in early 1958, was working on a basic revision of 

Ecuador's tax laws.

National income taxes are levied at varying scales depending primarily upon the source of income.

For the purpose of this tax a firm or person will be considered to have a fixed establishment if he has a head office, branch, mine, oil well, plantation, factory, shop, agency, installation, or consulting office in Ecuador. Business transactions conducted through an independent agent are outside the rule; however, transactions are within the rule if the dealings with such agent are continuous and such agent is established permanently in the country and has authority to act in the name of the firm.

Source: Contraloria General de la Nacion, Codificacion de Leyes Tributarias del Ecuador, Hasta Junio-1957, Quito, 1957, pages 48 and 49.

While corporation income is basically taxed to the individual stockholder, tax-collection problems have caused the lawmakers to create a patchwork of rules which permit the assessment of taxes at the time the corporation earns the income. 

To prevent both excessive accumulation of profits and tax evasion the corporation is taxed on undivided profits at the graduated scale but with the highest rate stopping at 16 percent instead of 22 percent. However, taxes paid on such undivided profits are credited to the individual stockholder when those profits are eventually distributed to him. The corporation must also withhold a tax on declared dividends in accordance with the rate applicable to the recipient of the dividend if such stockholder is an Ecuadoran or a foreigner resident in Ecuador, but the individual taxpayer must make a declaration at the end of the year and his income is adjusted to include all income derived from capital and is taxed according to the graduated scale. However, if the dividend-receiving stockholder is a foreigner resident abroad the tax withheld is at a flat rate of 16 percent.5 Net income of a branch of a foreign corporation also is taxed at the flat 16-percent rate. Dividends on bearer shares pay a minimum tax of 20 percent, if the recipient is a national or foreigner residing in Ecuador.

Firms which enter into special contracts with the Government are subject to income taxes as stipulated in the respective contract. 

Eighteen percent when including a national defense tax of 2 percent, see Leyes Mercantiles y Tributarias, page 144.

But when such stockholder receives other income produced in Ecuador, derived from capital, and the total income falls within a higher tax bracket of table 69, the higher tax shall be paid. 

Another variation on the taxing scheme is that mining firms which are subject to production taxes pay income taxes on net income, as follows: 13 percent, if production taxes are less than 7 percent; 12 percent, if production taxes are between 

7 and 8 percent; 11 percent, if production taxes are between 8 and 10 percent; and 10 percent, if production taxes are more than 10 percent.

A branch operation of a foreign organization operating a business is permitted the usual business expenses and depreciation in arriving at the net income figure subject to tax. Similarly, the domestic business entity may deduct business expenses and depreciation from gross income to arrive at the net income figure for the year. One tax deduction worth noting provides that up to 25 percent of the amounts spent by industrial enterprises in improving or increasing their installations may be deducted from gross income for the year.

In most cases, the payer of any income subject to the tax is responsible for withholding the applicable tax, particularly if the income is paid to a nonresident.

Individuals receiving income under this category, i. e., "income derived from capital," must pay an additional tax of 7 percent on any amount of net income exceeding 12,000 sucres per year and a 10-percent surtax on the total income tax assessment. As a substitute for this schedule, foreign branches pay an additional tax of 2 percent on all net income and a surtax of 10 percent on the total income tax assessment. However, if the foreign firm with a branch in Ecuador declares dividends to Ecuadorans, a deduction in the amount of such dividends may be taken in establishing the amount of net income subject to the 

2-percent additional tax.

Again in 1954, still another tax was assessed against income derived from capital; the rate was 

1 percent on the amount of income in excess of 

20,000 sucres of net or taxable income."

In the case of the partnership the tax is assessed on the income earned by each partner, although the partnership entity must make an annual return and must withhold and pay to the tax authorities the amount of tax owed by each partner. The following deductions are allowed: (1) 6,000 sucres as personal deduction, (2) 3,000 sucres as marital deductions, (3) 750 sucres for each dependent close relative under 21 years of age, (4) 1,000 sucres each for first and second child and 2,000 sucres for each child thereafter, and

Regulations. A tax return must be filed annually, but employers by law must withhold taxes monthly and make quarterly payments to the tax authorities. 

Remuneration of any type paid to foreigners for their services in Ecuador as commercial travelers, representatives, or employees of firms domiciled abroad and with no branches in Ecuador is exempt from the tax, provided such employees do not remain in Ecuador more than 6 months every year. Notwithstanding, should the total period of residence in Ecuador exceed 6 months in 2 consecutive years that income is subject to 50 percent of the tax. A foreigner employed by a firm domiciled abroad and who occasionally works in a branch of the firm in Ecuador is subject to the tax even if he resides in the country less than 6 months.

Individuals may not leave the country without clearance from the tax authorities.

TAXES ON CAPITAL 

All businesses are assessed a variable tax based on the amount of capital used by the firm. The proceeds from this tax are earmarked for the maintenance of children's homes, homes for the poor, and other welfare institutions. The tax was imposed by Supreme Decree No. 22 of January 27, 1938, and amended several times.10 tax at the highest rates approximates 2.5 sucres for each 1,000 sucres of capital employed.

OTHER TAXES 

Transfer tax."-The municipalities levy a tax on the sale or transfer of realty and vessels, or certain rights thereto, located within their jurisdiction.

The tax, including special supplementary taxes, ranges from 2.5 to 5.5 percent of the value of the property transferred. In some Provinces, property transfers valued at more than 20,000 sucres pay still other additional taxes of 1 or 2 percent. 

National sales tax.12-A long list of businesses are subject to a sales tax based on the gross value of sales with certain minor deductions. The tax amounts to 1 percent on the first 10,000 sucres and 

1.5 percent on the remainder up to 60,000 sucres. 

If sales are over 60,000 sucres, the tax amounts to 

875 sucres plus 1.5 percent of the excess over 60,000 sucres. 

To avoid double taxation, the tax is collected from producers, principal establishments, or agencies. Establishments which resell the product are not subject to the tax, except for restaurants and bars. Manufacturing establishments may deduct from their gross sales the c. i. f. cost of imported materials under certain circumstances. Sales of most export products are exempted completely. 

This tax is collected quarterly. 

Stamp taxes.18-Almost all commercial and legal papers as well as petitions and forms filed with the Government administrative or registration agencies must be made on so-called stamped paper; or special stamps, also sold by the Government, must be affixed to whatever other paper is used for the transaction. Some transactions must carry stamps on an ad valorem basis. ample, a transaction involving 1 million sucres pays 7,000 sucres in stamps. Many transactions, however, have a fixed stamp tariff which ranges, in the main, from less than 1 sucre to 500 sucres. 

For ex- 

Realty tax.¹-Realty taxes are assessed by the cities. The tax on urban property is regulated by city ordinances every 4 years. No tax may be levied higher than 10 sucres per 1,000 of the assessed value and this maximum rate may be applied only to assessments of over 1 million sucres.

Each taxpayer is assessed on the aggregate value of the properties he owns within the jurisdiction of the city, and the tax rate applicable to the total valuation is then applied proportionately to each separate piece of property. As much as 40 percent of the amount of a mortgage may be subtracted from the assessed valuation. The assessed 

11 Leyes Mercantiles y Tributarias, pages 230 through 237. 

12 Law 1239, October 24, 1941, as amended, and decree 619, November 24, 1944, compiled in Leyes Mercantiles y Tributarias, pages 151 through 163 and 171 through 176; legislative decree, November 2, 1949; decree of January 7, 1950. 

18 Leyes Mercantiles y Tributarias, pages 186 through 229. 

14 Leyes Mercantiles y Tributarias, pages 240 through 267. 

valuation may not exceed 60 percent of the commercial value.

The tax on rural property is graduated. The highest rate applies to property valued at over 1 million which carries a tax of 12,400 sucres sucres, plus 15 sucres for each 1,000 sucres of valuation.

As in the case of urban property, each taxpayer pays at the rate applicable to the combined value of all the rural property he owns. 

Both for urban and rural property a number of tax deductions and exemptions are allowed. For urban property additional taxes of 1 or 1.5 percent of valuation must be paid for the use of the fire departments. 

Estate, inheritance, and gift taxes.15-Estates, as such, are not taxed.

Taxes are levied on gifts inter vivos and on testate or intestate inheritances at rates ranging from 1.69 percent on amounts less than 10,000 sucres to 7.80 percent on amounts of more than 

1 million sucres, when the recipients are parents, children, or spouses. Collaterals and nonrelatives are assessed on an ascending scale in accordance with the degree of relationship and the amount involved. On 10,000 sucres, second-, third-, fourth-, and fifth-degree collaterals pay 5.2, 9.1, 14.3, and 19.5 percent, respectively, and on more than 700,000 sucres, 14.3, 22.1, 32.5, and 39 percent, respectively. The rates on nonrelatives range from 24.7 percent on 10,000 sucres to 52 percent on more than 700,000 sucres. 

Grandparents and grandchildren pay a surtax of 10 percent and other ascendants or descendants, a surtax of 20 percent. A spouse pays no tax on common property acquired during marriage. 

Each heir or legatee is allowed a deduction of 2,000 sucres or 500 sucres, respectively. Other deductions are permitted in certain cases. Additional taxes on inheritances are imposed by some of the Provinces.

Some municipalities also levy surtaxes on inheritances. Guayaquil, for example, levies a tax of 25 percent on the amount of tax paid to the 

National Government. 

If estates are not distributed within 5 years or 

7 years, a tax penalty of 10 or 15 percent, respectively, is imposed. 

Special rules govern taxes on bequests which take the form of what Ecuadoran law calls Fidei comiso, Usufructo, Uso y la Habitación, Rentas 

Perpetuas, temporales y vitalicias. Pensiones 

Alimenticias, and Asignaciones Condicionales and for which there are no exact counterparts in the United States, but which in part resemble life estates, estate for years, and trust arrangements. 

15 Codified law of December 11, 1950, and amendments as found in Leyes Mercantiles y Tributarias, pages 268 through 291. 

148

Tax System 

In general, inheritances are valued at market value, and provision is made for the use of experts in making valuations of property with no ready market value.

All property existing in Ecuador at the time of administration is subject to tax without taking into account the place of death or the nationality of the deceased. Credits or assets are considered to exist in Ecuador when payable in Ecuador, or when backed up with collateral in Ecuador, or when corresponding to contracts relating to realty in Ecuador, or when relating to contracts verified in Ecuador between Ecuadorans, as well as deposits which Ecuadorans may have abroad. In administrations involving foreigners, deposits and other movable assets left abroad will be taken into account in the computation of the tax of a beneficiary domiciled in Ecuador.

U. S. TAX INCIDENCE 

Income earned abroad by United States citizens or corporations is generally subject to U. S. taxes. 

However, in most cases, U. S. taxpayers may take a deduction or a credit for income taxes paid to a foreign government.16

The U. S. tax consequences on income earned by 

U. S. taxpayers from the operation of a business abroad vary with the form of organization employed to conduct the business. United States corporations, for example, usually have a choice of operating a direct investment aboard as (1) a branch of the U. S. corporation, (2) a branch of

U. S. subsidiary corporation of the U. S. corporation, and (3) a foreign subsidiary corporation, wholly or partly owned by the U. S. corporation.

In each of these cases the tax effect is different (for list of U. S. companies operating in Ecuador, see appendix C).

To make an accurate appraisal, each particular case must be studied individually but, in general, the return after taxes to the parent corporation from foreign investment income is about the same as for domestic income, if the investment is conducted as a direct branch or agency of the parent corporation; it is substantially greater if it is conducted as a branch or agency of a U. S. subsidiary corporation qualifying as a Western Hemisphere 

16 U. S. Internal Revenue Code (1954), sections 164 and 

901-905. 17 corporation or as a foreign subsidiary corporation; and it is less if it is conducted as a branch or an agency of an ordinary U. S. subsidiary corporation of the parent corporation. on The tax effect is different also in connection with the deferral of U. S. income tax foreign earnings. The income from the branch operation is taxed when it is earned,18 while the income earned abroad by the foreign subsidiary is taxed only when dividends are declared to the U. S. corporation. Therefore, to the extent that the foreign tax rates and rules permit, the foreign subsidiary is in a better position than the branch to accumulate profits abroad to hold or reinvest in its enterprises.

For the mining and petroleum industry, another important U. S. tax factor is that the branch of the U. S. corporation may take advantage of the depletion deduction permitted by the U. S. tax law but the foreign corporation, being a foreign entity, cannot avail itself of this privilege. 

United States tax factors concerning operating losses and liquidations as between the branch and the foreign corporation should also be examined by_the prospective investor.

Income earned abroad by U. S. citizens and residents, whether as individuals or as partners, is subject to U. S. taxes and, in general, at the same rates and under the same rules applicable to domestic income.19 Usually a deduction or a credit may be taken for taxes paid to foreign governments.

The individual citizen residing abroad, however, is given an important tax benefit in that he may exclude from his gross income at least a substantial portion of amounts received for personal services performed outside the United States under certain conditions. 

17 Section 921 of the U. S. Internal Revenue Code specifies that a Western Hemisphere Trade Corporation is a United States corporation which: (1) Does all of its business (other than incidental purchases) in the Western Hemisphere; (2) derives at least 95 percent of its gross income from sources outside the United States; and (3) derives at least 90 percent of its gross income from the active conduct of a trade or business. Section 

922 specifies that a Western Hemisphere corporation may take certain deductions which in effect in some cases represent a percentage of return of income after taxes of 62 percent as compared with the usual rate of return of 48 percent for domestic income. 

Owing to the intercorporate dividends tax the net return may be reduced to some 57 percent when the Western Hemisphere corporation income is passed on to the parent corporation in the form of dividends, or to some 53 percent if the foreign country from which the Western Hemisphere corporation derives its income imposes a tax on dividends paid nonresidents at about a 15percent rate. 

18 Certain exceptions are made if, because of foreign exchange controls, the taxpayer is unable to remit foreign profits to the 

United States.

Abroad, U. S. Treasury Department Internal Revenue Service,

APPENDIX A Industrial Encouragement 

Law 

On June 27, 1957, there was published in Ecuador's

Registro Oficial a law decreed by the Constitutional President of the Republic pursuant to article 80 of the 

Political Constitution. 

According to the preliminary statement by President

Camelio Ponce Enriquez, the law was decreed because- "The country requires, urgently, a legal regime technically oriented to promote its industrial development; "The Industrial Protection Law, dictated in the year 1921, does not answer the actual and modern requirements of so complex a subject; "It is necessary to create a climate attractive for domestic and foreign capital which may be invested in 

Ecuadoran industrial production; "A properly directed industrial development will bring undeniable benefits for the economy of Ecuador and its consumer majorities; "As a result of prohibitions contained in the Legislative Decree of November 7, 1956, industrial development has been virtually paralyzed; 

"The National Economic Council (Consejo Nacional de 

Economia) has issued its favorable opinion expressed in 

Communication (Oficio) No. 16-P, of June 5, 1957."

An unofficial translation of this industrial encourage ment law follows:

CHAPTER I Introduction 

Article 1. The purpose of this law is to encourage the economic development of the country, in conformity with the requirements of general economic growth, stimulating national and foreign investments in existing industry and in those which may be established in the future. 

Article 2.-The provisions of this law are applicable only to permanent industrial activities which are for the purpose of transforming, either in shape or composition, raw material or semimanufactured products into finished or semifinished articles more suitable for human needs. 

Also included among these activities are those of simple installation and assembly.

When industrial transformation enterprises carry on, simultaneously, activities directed toward obtaining raw materials without the necessity of industrial processing, or for the retail distribution of their products, the present law will be applied only to the industrial transformation activities and its resultants, and none of its provisions shall be applied to cover the above-cited other complementary activities. 

Article 3.-For the purposes of this law, those activities which, by reason of their characteristics, may be classified as those of the artisan in conformity with that provided in article 1 of the Law for the Defense of Artisanry.¹ 

1 

Article 4.-Expressly excluded from the regime estab lished by this law are construction and hotel activities; those extractive industries embraced in article 4 of Emergency Decree-Law No. 5 of January 3, 1957; the transport industry; and the extractive or capture phase of fishing. 

Article 5.-All those industries which effect an important transformation of raw material or a semimanufactured product which can be utilized in the industrial process, and whose objectives and management are conducive to the national economic development, are eligible for the benefits of this law.

For legal purposes existing industries are distinguished from new industries, it being understood that new industries are those established after the effective date of this law. These latter are eligible for its benefits when, in addition to fulfilling the conditions of the first paragraph of this article, they are destined to produce: a. Articles not yet being manufactured in the country at the time of its establishment; b. Articles which, although manufactured in the country at the time of its establishment, still have available markets because of deficiencies in the quantity or quality, or because of the high price of existing national production; c. Merchandise for export.

Existing or new industrial enterprises which fulfill the requirements set forth in this article shall enjoy the concessions provided by this law, according to the category applicable to them in the classification contained in its chapter II. 

Classification of Industries 

Article 6.-Depending upon their importance to the general economic development of the country, the industries which comply with that provided in the previous article will be classified in three categories: "A," "B," and "C.”

Those industries which legally fall in one of these categories will be denominated in this law as classified industries. 

Article 7.—The following will be considered as industries in category "A": 

1 October 27, 1953, Registro Oficial, Quito, November 5, 1953. 151

a. Those which manufacture raw material, semimanufactured products, intermediate manufactured products, tools, machinery, accessories and repair parts, scientific apparatus or vehicles, for use in the productive processes of agricultural, forestry, industrial, mining, and fishing activities; whenever the National Board of Economic Planning and Coordination (Junta Nacional de 

Planificacion y Coordinacion Economica) shall consider the industry to be basic for the economic development of the country. b. Those industries whose production has substituted for, or is capable of substituting for, merchandise useful to the population and important for the country, which normally had been imported, provided the following conditions are observed:

I. That the value of the imports which have been substituted or for which partial or complete substitution is proposed, represents an average of at least 1 percent of the f. o. b. value of the total imports of the country in the 3 years prior to the presentation of the application; or those which, though not reaching this percentage, will nevertheless be imports which, because of their characteristics, should be substituted in a preferential manner, in accordance with criteria of the National Board of Economic Planning and Coordination. The regulations to this law shall determine the method to be followed for estimating the value of imports substituted by existing industries; 

II. That the value of imported raw material and materials incorporated in the product or sold with it does not represent a proportion greater than 15 percent of the cost of production. c. Industries which, having satisfied the internal market, export or show reasonable possibilities for exporting the greater part of their production, provided that the value of the imports of raw material and materials which are incorporated in the product or sold with it does not represent a proportion greater than 30 percent of the factory export sales price. In the case of new industries, if after 3 years of operation the enterprise does not justify the original prospects for exports, it shall no longer be considered within this category.

Article 8.-The following will be considered as industries in category "B": a. Those which produce or process goods destined to satisfy those fundamental needs upon which depend the subsistence and health of the population, such as food, clothing, health, and hygiene, including industries which produce construction materials; b. Industries which, although having the characteristics delineated in subsection (a) of article 7 of this law, have not been considered as basic by the National Board of Economic Planning and Coordination; c. Industries whose production has replaced or is capable of replacing merchandise imports considered to be useful for the population and of importance to the country, and which have normally been effected, provided that the value of the imports of raw material and of materials incorporated in the product or sold with it do not represent a proportion greater than 40 percent of the cost of production; d. Those effecting simple installation or assembly, when the value of the imported parts or pieces does not represent a proportion greater than 50 percent of the cost of production; and e. Industries which, having satisfied the internal market, export or offer reasonable possibilities for exporting a part of their production, provided that the value of imports of raw material and materials incorporated into the product or sold with it does not represent a proportion greater than 50 percent of the factory export sales price. In the case of new industries, if after 3 years of operation the industry does not justify the original prospects for exports, it shall no longer be considered within this category.

Article 9.-All those industries which cannot be included in the two previous categories, although having the characteristics fixed in article 5, will be considered as category "C" industries.

Article 10.-For the purposes of classification, it will be understood that the cost of production is composed of the following items: a. Wages paid to workers and salaries of technical personnel; b. Cost of raw material and of materials incorporated into the product or sold with it; c. Normal amortization of machinery and accessories, and cost of maintaining such machinery and accessories; d. Cost of fuel, lubricants, and power;

The regulations to this law shall delineate, with such detail as necessary, all the items of expense which must be taken into account to obtain the components of the cost of production cited in this article. 

Article 11. When the industrial enterprise manufactures or proposes to manufacture various articles, the predominant activity must be taken into account for the purposes of classification; the predominant activity shall be understood to be the one with a value of sales representing, or which could possibly represent, a minimum of 50 percent of the total value of the sales of the enterprise.

The regulations to this law will establish the criteria which must be observed for classification when it is not possible to determine the predominant activity in conformity with the preceding paragraph; but, in all cases, special attention must be directed to the relationship between the total value of imports of raw material or materials which are to be incorporated into the separate products or sold with them, and the overall cost of production or, if applicable, the total value of the exports of the enterprise.

Article 12.-The industrial enterprises whose production is for export or is destined for export, and which were classified in conformity with this law, may only effect such exports after satisfying national needs for raw materials, semimanufactured or intermediate products, as well as for finished products, as the case may be. The factory sale price of these goods for the national market may not be higher than that charged for the foreign market. In order to effect exports under conditions distinct from those established in this article, the specific authorization of the Ministry of Economy will be required, and it will be granted only when in the national interest. 

CHAPTER III 

Machinery, Accessories, and Spare Parts

Article 13. For the purposes of this law, industrial machinery shall only and exclusively be understood to be that which brings about directly industrial processes of transformation, as well as laboratory, testing, and general investigation apparatus, provided that such capital goods are embraced in any of the customs classifications which are set forth in the regulations to this law. 

Article 14.-Only the following shall be considered as accessories for industrial machinery: a. The mechanism for production, conversion, and transmission of motive power; b. The mechanism for transport of raw material, products being manufactured or finished products, when such mechanism is functionally specified for the corresponding industry; c. Pressure systems for air and liquids and accessories, provided that their use is justified; d. Electronic systems and mechanism for automatic control of processes; 152

Industrial Encouragement Law e. Materials necessary for the distribution of electrical energy, except those destined for illumination, in conformity with that provided in the regulations to this law; f. Refractory and anticorrosive materials and thermic insulators for temperatures greater than 100°C.; g. The maintenance machinery absolutely necessary for the industrial process; and h. Fire prevention installations and the systems and apparatus for protection and safeguarding against the hazards of industrial work.

Article 15.-Only those parts or pieces of machinery or accessories subject to movement or corrosion, whose periodic replacement is precisely scheduled by the firm manufacturing such equipment as a prerequisite for its efficient and normal operation, shall be considered as spare parts; nevertheless, for legal purposes, there will also be included as spare parts those parts or pieces which are in need of replacement, although not in conformity with the anticipated replacement schedule, when proven to be attributable to force majeure or fortuitous circumstance.

CHAPTER IV Applications for Industrial Development 

Article 16.-Every person, enterprise, or company wishing to obtain the benefits which this law grants for the installation of a new industry or for the efficient operation of existing ones must present the appropriate industrial development application to the Ministry of Economy, as well as completing the questionnaires provided for in the corresponding regulations.

Article 17.—In the tenor of the preceding article and depending upon the situation of the industry and the benefits being sought, interested parties must submit, in every case, for the consideration of the Ministry of Economy, the appropriate application from among those listed below: a. Industrial development application for establishment of the industry; b. Industrial development application for existing industries which wish to be classified or for those which petition for the benefits granted to new enterprises which are the source of a ruinous competition; c. Application for import benefits submitted by existing enterprises which have been classified and which wish to import machinery, accessories, spare parts and, if applicable, raw material, according to the concessions which correspond to their category in conformity with this law; d. Industrial development application to obtain reclassification, when a change in the characteristics of the enterprise or in operating conditions justifies the transfer to a category other than the one earlier assigned; e. Application for qualification of the efficiency of the industry, necessary for the application of the provisions on tax benefits in chapter VI of this law. 

The Ministry of Economy will inform the Ministry of the Treasury of the application submitted under paragraphs (a), (b), (c), and (d) of this article for the processing of the respective Ministerial Accord (Acuerdo).

Article 18.-The interested party must submit to the 

National Board of Economic Planning and Coordination a copy of its application, certified by the Directorate Gen eral of Industries and Commerce of the Ministry of Eco nomy; applications for classification under paragraph (e) of the previous article, the processing of which does not require a report from the cited office, are excepted. 

Article 19.-The National Board of Economic Planning and Coordination, by its reports, must advise the Minis- 470366-59—11 tries of Economy and Treasury on decisions on applications of the industrial enterprises of transformation. The report must be issued within a term of 30 days from the date on which the certified copy of the corresponding application is submitted.

Article 20. The reports of the National Board of Eco nomic Planning and Coordination shall study the applications with the care necessary to orient the decisions of the Ministries of Economy and of the Treasury regarding the appropriateness or inappropriateness of the application, as well as all and each of the aspects which, in conformity with this law and its regulations, must be embraced in the corresponding Ministerial Accord if the application is accepted.

In every case, the National Board of Economic Planning and Coordination shall include in its report its judgment as to the importance of the industry for the economic development of the country, an analysis of its technical and economic possibilities, and an opinion as to the truth or reality of the data contained in the application.

Article 21.-If the application is resolved favorably, the 

Ministries of Economy and of the Treasury will issue the corresponding Ministerial Accord within a term of 

30 days from the date of the presentation of the report of the National Board of Economic Planning and Coordination; therein, according to the kind of application, they must determine the bases and conditions whereby the applicant enterprise may come under this law and they will specify the benefits to which, as a result, it will be entitled. Within the 30 days following the issuance of the respective Ministerial Accord, the enterprise may request that the Ministries of Economy and of the Treasury reconsider their decision.

The regulations shall establish the decisions that must be embraced in the various kinds of Ministerial Accords which the administration of this law requires. 

Article 22.-When, in the judgment of the Ministries of 

Economy and of the Treasury, the operation of an industry does not conform to the characteristics which served as the basis for its earlier classification and, even without application from the interested enterprise, these Ministries may proceed to reclassify such industries by Ministerial Accord, or to withdraw the benefits granted as a result of the original classification, if the enterprise has not already fulfilled the conditions necessary for classification.

When there is no fraud on the part of the interested enterprise, the changes in the classification or the withdrawal of the benefits, to which the previous paragraph refers, shall be made effective 60 days after notice of the matter to the interested party, within which time the interested enterprise may request reconsideration of the 

Reclassification Accord or of the withdrawal of benefits. 

Article 23.-The Ministerial Accords treated in article 21 

of this law will be sufficient for the industries to be considered as having the right to the enjoyment of the benefits specified in this law, excepting those which require, in addition, a special proceeding before the Ministry of the Treasury in conformity with the pertinent provisions of this law.

Article 24.-The classification of the efficiency of an in dustry to which article 40 of this law refers will require a resolution from the Ministry of Economy, duly communicated to the Ministry of the Treasury for the corresponding effects.

Article 25.-For the information of the interested parties and for the purpose of making possible the presentation of their claims to the Ministry of Economy, a resume of the applications described in paragraphs (a), (b), and (d) of article 17, must be published, at the expense of the applicant, for 3 consecutive days in the newspapers of wider circulation of Quito, Guayaquil, and if it has one, of the Province, within the territorial limits of which the industry might be located or is to be located. This 153

publication must be begun within 8 days following the presentation of the application.

In order to provide the time necessary for the observations of the interested parties, the Ministerial Accords may be issued only after the completion of a term of 15 days from the last publication of the resume of the corresponding application. Also at the expense of the applicant, the resume of the Ministerial Accords must be published, for 3 consecutive days, under the terms established in the preceding paragraph for the resume of the application. The complete text of these Ministerial Accords will be published in the Registro Oficial. 

CHAPTER V Concessions on Duties and Fees 

Article 26.-Industries classified in category "A," on the importation of the machinery, accessories, spare parts, and raw material specified in the respective Ministerial Accord will enjoy exemption from the payment of customs duties and the surcharges thereon, except the temporary stamp tax on importation created by Emergency 

Decree-Law No. 1 of December 21, 1956, and a reduction of 50 percent in the attendant consular fees. 

Article 27.-Industries classified in category "B," on the importation of the machinery, accessories, and spare parts specified in the respective Ministerial Accord, will enjoy exemption from the payment of customs duties and the surcharges thereon, except the temporary stamp tax on importation created by Emergency Decree-Law 

No. 1 of December 21, 1956, and a reduction of 50 percent in the attendant consular fees. Only those industries which might be of the kind treated in paragraph (e) of article 8 of this law will enjoy similar exemptions and reductions for the importation of raw material, solely for the quantity of raw material utilized in the manufacture of the product exported. 

Article 28.-Industries classified in category "C," on the importation of the machinery, accessories, and spare parts specified in the respective Ministerial Accord, will enjoy a 70-percent reduction in the payment of the customs duties and the surcharges thereon, except the temporary stamp tax on importation created by Emergency Decree-Law 

No. 1 of December 21, 1956, and a 30-percent reduction in the attendant consular fees.

Article 29. In that which refers to export industries in categories "A" and "B," in special cases and after a report of the Foreign Trade Council (Consejo de Comer cio Exterior), the Ministries of Economy and of the Treasury are empowered to increase, to the extent necessary, the concessions which this law fixes for the payment of the duties on the importation of raw material, and to establish exemptions or reductions in export duties, when such concessions coincide with the national interest and will be imperative for there to be placement of the product of national enterprises in the foreign market; but in this case, these concessions may only be extended to the raw material utilized in the product exported or to the part of production actually exported, respectively. 

Article 30.-The exemptions and reductions on the importation of raw material, to which articles 26 and 27 of this law refer, may be made effective half-yearly provided that the enterprise proves the amount of raw material which has actually been employed in production, if the industry is classified in category "A," or in the production exported, is of the kind treated in paragraph (e) of article 8 of this law, corresponding to category "B." 

The beneficiary enterprises may elect either of the two following systems for the administration of such concessions: 

1. The computation and payment of the customs duties and attendant consular fees on the importation of raw material shall be subject to the normal regime, but the Ministry of the Treasury, on the basis of application, will put into effect the exemption from customs duties and consular fees paid on the importation of the quantity of raw material used, within the terms of the first paragraph of this article, and will deliver to the interested party a credit note for the corresponding value, which the Ministry of the Treasury shall receive in payment of the taxes owed by the enterprise. Any balance owed to the latter will be refunded in money of legal tender; 

2. The beneficiary enterprises may withdraw the imported raw material from the customhouse after the acceptance by the Ministry of the Treasury of a 1-year renewable bank guaranty, which will cover the amount of the customs duties and consular fees resulting from this importation in conformity with the normal regime. 

The Ministry of the Treasury, on the basis of application, will put into effect the exemption from customs duties and consular fees corresponding to that part of the raw material utilized, within the terms of the first paragraph of this article; it will proceed to collect the duties resulting from the importation of the raw material not utilized in the production exported, if the industry is of the kind treated in paragraph (e) of article 8 of this law, and shall authorize the consequent modifications in the bank guaranty.

The Ministry of the Treasury shall establish the procedures which must be followed for the implementation of this article; and this Ministry and that of Economy are empowered to resolve all those special cases which originate in the regime for exemptions for the importation of raw material.

Article 31. The concessions in the payment of import duties to which this chapter refers may only be granted when the machinery, accessories, spare parts, and raw material are not produced in the country or are produced in such conditions of quantity and quality that their use may adversely affect the technical and economical efficiency of the industry to the prejudice of the national interest.

Article 32. The prior authorization of the Ministry of 

Economy will be necessary in order to loan, rent, exchange, or sell all or part of the machinery, accessories, and spare parts imported with the concessions, to which this chapter refers, during the time embraced between the issuance of the Ministerial Accord which determined these concessions and a date 5 years after that on which these capital goods were first used. 

In the event of bankruptcy or liquidation of an industrial enterprise, the receiver must communicate this fact to the Ministry of Economy for the purposes of the authorization treated in the previous paragraph, without prejudice to such other effects provided for in this law for similar cases. 

Article 33.-Classified enterprises which wish to employ the machinery, accessories, and spare parts imported with the benefits established by this law for a use other than that declared in the application must request the prior authorization of the Ministry of Economy. If the new characteristics of the industry justify it, the Ministries of Economy and of the Treasury shall proceed to reclassify it or withdraw the concessions authorized as a result of this law, subject to that provided in article 22 of this law. 

Article 34.-If all or part of the machinery, accessories or spare parts, imported with tax concessions resulting from this law, are, with the authorization of the Ministry of Economy and within the period of time referred to in article 32 of this law, leased, loaned, or sold to classified industrial enterprises, are exchanged for other assets of these enterprises or they are employed for a purpose other than that declared in the application, the aforementioned Ministry and that of the Treasury, in con- 154

Industrial Encouragement Law formity with the benefit which this negotiation may produce for the enterprise, taking into account its technical and economic conditions, shall decide whether there shall be a computation and collection of all or part of the import duties which the enterprise did not pay by reason of this law.

When the negotiations for lease, loan, sale, or exchange, to which the previous paragraph refers, are realized with nonclassified industries or nonindustrial enterprises, with prior authorization of the Ministry of Economy and within the time indicated in the preceding paragraph, the enterprise which has benefited from concessions in the payment of import duties for the goods negotiated must pay the full amount which it did not pay because of such concessions, when the transfer of ownership or use takes place in the year of importation, and, if the negotiation occurs in the following years, this amount less 

20 percent for each of the succeeding years.
